# Sūrah al-Mā'idah (Chapter 5) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:1

> O believers! Honour your obligations. All grazing livestock has been made lawful to you—except what is hereby announced to you and hunting while on pilgrimage. Indeed, Allah commands what He wills

**Expanded Commentary**

**Contracts Are the Frame of Faithful Life**

The opening address yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū calls those who claim faith to live up to its implications, and the first command is awfū bi-l-'uqūd — honour your obligations. 'Uqūd, plural of 'aqd, means a knot that ties, then by extension any binding agreement where two sides fasten themselves to a course. Classical lexicographers explain 'aqd as what joins two ends firmly, and jurists apply it to every firm commitment: the covenant with Allah to worship Him alone, the pledge taken through messengers, and the contracts between people such as marriage, sale, and alliance. Al-Ṭabarī records from Ibn 'Abbās that 'uqūd includes what Allah made obligatory and what people obligate upon themselves. That breadth matters because this sūrah will detail food laws, oaths, and treaties, and it begins by rooting all of them in fidelity. *“Fulfil the covenant of Allah when you have entered into it”* (16:91) states the same principle in another place, and *“Fulfil every covenant, for every covenant will be questioned”* (17:34) links keeping promises to accountability. The verse therefore does not list contracts; it establishes a habit of honouring them.

**Why Grazing Livestock Defines the Lawful Baseline**

Uḥillat lakum bahīmat al-an'ām declares grazing livestock lawful. Bahīmah denotes a quadruped that does not clearly articulate, and an'ām specifies the herd animals Arabs relied upon: camels, cattle, sheep and goats. Tafsīr sources note that bahīmat al-an'ām includes these four kinds and their young, and some include wild grazers that resemble them, while others restrict the phrase to domestic herds. The point of the phrase is to set a permissive baseline: the flesh of herd animals is lawful in principle, not forbidden. This counters pre-Islamic taboos where certain camels or sheep were declared ḥām or baḥīrah and left to roam untouched. The Qur'an elsewhere affirms the same provision: *“And the grazing livestock He has created for you; in them is warmth and benefits and from them you eat”* (16:5). By starting with what is allowed rather than what is forbidden, the sūrah teaches a legal method: the origin in foods is permissibility unless a text restricts it. The exception that follows will then carve out the narrow prohibitions, which is exactly how verses 3 and 4 will operate.

**The Exception That Points to Detailed Law Ahead**

Illā mā yutlā 'alaykum — except what is hereby announced to you — keeps the ruling open for the detailed list that arrives in the next verses. The Qur'an often announces a general permission then follows with a precise exception, so that listeners learn both the rule and its limits without confusion. Here the exception covers carrion, blood, swine, animals slaughtered for other than Allah, and those killed by strangling, beating, falling, goring, or partial predation, as verse 3 will explain. It also covers animals over which Allah's name was not mentioned, a condition stressed in *“So eat of that over which the name of Allah has been mentioned, if you are believers in His signs”* (6:118). Early commentators differed on whether illā mā yutlā includes only what this sūrah itself recites or what the Qur'an as a whole recites, but the practical effect is the same: nothing becomes forbidden by custom, only by revelation. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the passive yutlā emphasizes that the recitation comes from above, not from tribal elders. That linguistic choice supports the sūrah's larger argument that lawful and unlawful belong to Allah alone.

**Hunting in Iḥrām and the Sanctity of Restraint**

Ghayra muḥillī al-ṣayd wa antum ḥurum forbids treating game as lawful while in pilgrim sanctity. Ḥurum, plural of ḥarām in the sense of one who has entered iḥrām, describes the state a pilgrim enters at the mīqāt, when he abstains from hunting, cutting hair, and other acts. Iḥrām comes from the root ḥ-r-m meaning to make sacred or forbidden, and the pilgrim in that state makes certain otherwise lawful acts temporarily forbidden upon himself. The prohibition teaches that sanctity is not only a place but a condition. The sūrah will return to this theme in verses 94-96, where it tests believers with game within reach of hands and spears. The reason for linking food law to pilgrimage at the very start is historical: this passage was revealed around the Farewell Pilgrimage, when the Prophet ﷺ clarified final rulings. A sound report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (1730) describes how the Prophet ﷺ instructed pilgrims about what game they must avoid, which shows how seriously the Companions took this restraint. The verse thus connects daily eating to the discipline of ḥajj, reminding that faith trains desire through timed abstention.

**Allah Commands What He Wills — Sovereignty in Legislation**

Inna Allāha yaḥkumu mā yurīd closes the verse with a principle that explains everything before it: Allah commands what He wills. Yaḥkumu, from ḥ-k-m, conveys judging, decreeing, and governing with wisdom, not arbitrary power. The same root yields ḥikmah, wisdom, and ḥukm, ruling. The sūrah insists that the right to declare lawful and unlawful belongs to the One who creates and sustains. *“Judgment belongs only to Allah”* (12:40) and *“He does not associate anyone in His judgment”* (18:26) reinforce this. Some exegetes read mā yurīd as indicating that divine rulings track divine wisdom, even when the wisdom is not immediately apparent to humans; others emphasize sheer sovereignty, that no one can question what He legislates. Both are true: the rulings have wisdom, and they bind because He willed them. This clause also answers the pre-Islamic practice of permitting and forbidding by whim. Where tribes made lawful what they liked and forbade what they disliked, the verse says legislation belongs to Allah, whose will is informed by knowledge and mercy. That sets the stage for verse 3's famous declaration that the faith has been perfected.

**A Medinan Opening That Sets the Sūrah’s Tone**

Al-Mā'idah is a Medinan sūrah revealed after the conquest of Makkah, and many reports place parts of it on the Day of 'Arafah during the Farewell Pilgrimage in 10 AH. The Prophet ﷺ was riding his camel al-'Aḍbā' when the sūrah descended, so heavy that the animal knelt, as reported in Musnad Aḥmad (17699, graded ḥasan by al-Arnā'ūṭ). That circumstance fits the content: final rulings about food, oaths, justice, and relations with the People of the Book. The sūrah is called al-Mā'idah, the Table Spread, after the request of the disciples in verses 112-115, and also Sūrat al-'Uqūd, the Chapter of Contracts, because it opens with 'uqūd. These two names capture its two concerns: law and faith history. Opening with contracts signals that Islam is not only belief but a system of trust — with Allah, with people, and with the natural world that provides food. The mention of grazing livestock immediately after contracts links ethics to sustenance, showing that how we eat reflects what we honour. The next verse will extend that ethic to pilgrimage rites and to cooperation in righteousness, building outward from this foundation.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:2

> O believers! Do not violate Allah’s rituals ˹of pilgrimage˺, the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the ˹offerings decorated with˺ garlands, nor those ˹pilgrims˺ on their way to the Sacred House seeking their Lord’s bounty and pleasure. When pilgrimage has ended, you are allowed to hunt. Do not let the hatred of a people who once barred you from the Sacred Mosque provoke you to transgress. Cooperate with one another in goodness and righteousness, and do not cooperate in sin and transgression. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is severe in punishment

**Expanded Commentary**

**Rituals Are Boundaries, Not Suggestions**

Lā tuḥillū sha'ā'ir Allāh forbids violating Allah's rituals. Sha'ā'ir, plural of sha'īrah, means marks or symbols that make something known, from sha'ara, to know or perceive. In pilgrimage law it denotes the visible rites that signal devotion: the standing at 'Arafah, the circling of the Ka'bah, the sacrifice. To violate them is to treat as lawful what Allah declared protected. The same root appears in *“And whoever honours the symbols of Allah, that is from piety of hearts”* (22:32). The verse therefore pairs prohibition with its opposite virtue: honouring the marks. Early commentators explain that the Arabs before Islam respected the sacred months and the garlanded offerings but often violated them for revenge; the Qur'an reasserts the sanctity on a divine basis, not tribal custom. The plural address yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū repeats from verse 1, showing that faith entails public restraint, not only private belief. Respect for ritual is thus presented as part of fulfilling contracts.

**Sacred Months, Garlands, and Pilgrims Seeking Bounty**

The verse lists four protected categories: the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the garlanded offerings, and those heading to the Sacred House. Al-shahr al-ḥarām refers to the four months where fighting was forbidden: Dhū al-Qa'dah, Dhū al-Ḥijjah, Muḥarram, and Rajab, a practice confirmed since the time of Ibrāhīm. Al-hady denotes animals driven to Makkah for sacrifice, and al-qalā'id, garlands, are collars placed on them to mark them as offerings, so no one would harm them. The final category, āmmīn al-bayt al-ḥarām yabtaghūna faḍlan min rabbihim wa riḍwānan, describes pilgrims and traders seeking Allah's bounty and pleasure. Faḍl means surplus or grace, often used for provision and trade, while riḍwān is deep pleasure. The pairing shows that seeking livelihood on the way to ḥajj was not condemned if the primary aim remained devotion. *“There is no blame upon you for seeking bounty from your Lord during pilgrimage”* (2:198) confirms this. By protecting these persons and things, the verse secures safe passage for worship, a principle that later jurists extended to amān, safe conduct.

**Hunting Becomes Lawful After Iḥrām Ends**

Wa idhā ḥalaltum fa-iṣṭādū gives permission to hunt when pilgrimage has ended. Ḥalaltum, from ḥ-l-l, means to become lawful or to exit the state of iḥrām, the opposite of aḥramtum. The command form iṣṭādū is not an obligation but a removal of prohibition, a common Qur'anic idiom: after a ban, a command returns the act to its original permissibility. This explains why the Qur'an says fa-iṣṭādū rather than merely stating hunting is allowed. The same structure appears in *“When the prayer is finished, disperse in the land”* (62:10), where the command means permission to resume work. Jurists differ on whether game caught while in iḥrām must be compensated if the hunter was forgetful, but they agree intentional violation requires expiation, as verse 95 will detail. The placement of this permission right after protecting pilgrims shows balance: restraint during sanctity, ease after it, reflecting *“Allah intends ease for you, not hardship”* (2:185).

**Hatred Must Not Produce Injustice**

Wa lā yajrimannakum shana'ānu qawmin an ṣaddūkum 'an al-masjid al-ḥarām an ta'tadū forbids letting hatred provoke transgression. Yajrimannakum, from jarama, means to incite or cause to commit, and shana'ān means intense hatred. The occasion, according to al-Ṭabarī, was that the polytheists barred Muslims from the Sacred Mosque at Ḥudaybiyah, and some Companions considered retaliating by blocking their trade routes. The verse says past hostility does not justify present injustice. This principle is repeated with even greater force in *“Do not let hatred of a people lead you to injustice; be just, that is closer to righteousness”* (5:8). The wording an ta'tadū, to transgress, covers both fighting in sacred months and violating safe conduct. Ibn Kathīr notes that the verse establishes that enmity must be contained within limits set by Allah. The lesson extends beyond pilgrimage: personal grievance never licenses breach of divine law. That ethic underpins the next clause about cooperation.

**Cooperation Has a Moral Direction**

Ta'āwanū 'alā al-birri wa al-taqwā, wa lā ta'āwanū 'alā al-ithm wa al-'udwān commands cooperation in birr and taqwā and forbids cooperation in ithm and 'udwān. Birr, from b-r-r, means expansive goodness that reaches others, encompassing faith, charity, and fidelity; taqwā, from w-q-y, means shielding oneself from harm by mindful obedience. Ithm is sin that leaves a trace of guilt, and 'udwān is crossing limits against others. The contrast is deliberate: positive cooperation is defined by both outward good and inward restraint. Al-Qurṭubī explains that birr covers all acts Allah loves, while taqwā covers avoiding what He hates. The verse does not merely encourage good deeds individually; it institutes mutual assistance as a social norm. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2580) reports the Prophet ﷺ said believers are like a building, each part strengthening the other, which jurists cite as practical illustration of this verse. The negative command is equally firm: do not assist in wrongdoing even if kinship or profit tempts. This dual rule became a legal maxim: there is no obedience to a creature in disobedience to the Creator.

**Severity in Punishment Balances Mercy in Law**

Inna Allāha shadīd al-'iqāb closes with warning. Shadīd means intense, firm, not easily repelled, and 'iqāb comes from 'aqaba, to follow, because punishment follows the crime. The phrase appears after a series of permissions and prohibitions to remind that ease in law does not mean laxity in accountability. The same closure occurs in *“And be mindful of Allah; surely Allah is severe in punishment”* (5:2) itself and in *“Know that Allah is severe in punishment and that Allah is Forgiving, Merciful”* (5:98), pairing severity with mercy. Commentators note that the verse began with honouring obligations and ends with fear of consequence, framing law between love and awe. The severity mentioned is not arbitrary harshness but the necessary counterpart to violated sanctities: those who violate pilgrims or sacred months disrupt public worship, so the deterrent must be clear. Yet the sūrah immediately follows this with detailed allowances for necessity in verse 3, showing that severity targets defiance, not hardship. The next verse will detail forbidden foods, continuing the pattern of clear boundaries followed by merciful exceptions.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:3

> Forbidden to you are carrion, blood, and swine; what is slaughtered in the name of any other than Allah; what is killed by strangling, beating, a fall, or by being gored to death; what is partly eaten by a predator unless you slaughter it; and what is sacrificed on altars. You are also forbidden to draw lots for decisions. This is all evil. Today the disbelievers have given up all hope of ˹undermining˺ your faith. So do not fear them; fear Me! Today I have perfected your faith for you, completed My favour upon you, and chosen Islam as your way. But whoever is compelled by extreme hunger—not intending to sin—then surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Catalog of Forbidden Meats**

Ḥurrimat 'alaykum al-maytah defines the first prohibition: carrion. Maytah, from m-w-t, is an animal that died without proper slaughter, so its blood remains trapped. The Qur'an repeats this list in several places, including *“He has only forbidden to you carrion, blood, swine, and what is slaughtered for other than Allah”* (2:173) and *“Say, I do not find in what has been revealed to me anything forbidden except carrion, flowing blood, swine flesh”* (6:145). The repetition shows the centrality of these foods in Arabian life. Dam, blood, means flowing blood, not the trace remaining in meat after slaughter, as clarified by the adjective masfūḥ in 6:145. Laḥm al-khinzīr, swine flesh, includes fat and meat, and jurists agree it is impure in itself. Wa mā uhilla li-ghayr Allāh bihi refers to animals slaughtered invoking a name other than Allah's, uhilla from halala, to raise the voice, because slaughterers would call out their deity's name. These four form the core, and the verse adds five modes of killing that render meat unlawful.

**Five Fatal Modes and the Predator Exception**

Al-munkhaniqah is what is killed by strangling, al-mawqūdhah by beating, al-mutaraddiyah by a fall, al-naṭīḥah by goring, and mā akala al-sabu' is what a predator partly ate. Each term is a passive participle indicating how death occurred without controlled slaughter. The common factor is that blood did not flow by a sharp cut while the animal was alive, so the condition of tadhkiyah, proper slaughter, was missed. Illā mā dhakkaytum provides the exception: unless you slaughter it while still alive. Dhakkaytum, from dh-k-w, means to make fully alive then quickly slaughter, requiring the animal to show signs of life such as movement or breathing. Jurists differ on how much life suffices, but they agree that a dying animal found with a final gasp can be made lawful by cutting throat and mentioning Allah's name. Wa mā dhubiḥa 'alā al-nuṣub adds animals sacrificed on altars, nuṣub plural of niṣāb, stones set up for idols where blood was smeared. Even if Allah's name was mentioned, the place of sacrifice makes it forbidden because it honours other than Him. The list closes the loophole of indirect idolatry.

**Drawing Lots and the Category of Fisq**

Wa an tastaqsimū bi-l-azlām is forbidden as well. Tastaqsimū means to seek division or decision, and azlām are headless arrows used for divination. In Jāhiliyyah, a person would draw arrows marked yes, no, or blank to decide travel, marriage, or trade. The Qur'an calls this practice fisq, departure from obedience, because it seeks guidance from chance rather than Allah. The same term appears in *“Intoxicants, gambling, idols, and drawing lots are evil of Satan's handiwork”* (5:90), linking azlām to other means that cloud judgment. Unlike lawful casting of lots to distribute shares, azlām claimed knowledge of the unseen. Commentators note that the verse groups food prohibitions with divination to show that both involve seeking provision or guidance from other than Allah. Dhālikum fisq — this is all evil — refers to all previous items, indicating that eating such foods is not a minor lapse but a moral exit. The strong language prepares for the triumphant announcement that follows.

**Disbelievers Despair and the Perfection of Faith**

Al-yawma ya'isa alladhīna kafarū min dīnikum announces that disbelievers have given up hope of undermining the faith. Yawm here means this day or this era, not necessarily a single 24-hour period. Ya'isa, to despair, indicates they no longer expect to extinguish Islam by force or argument after the conquest of Makkah and the Farewell Pilgrimage. Fa-lā takhshawhum wa-khshawnī therefore says do not fear them; fear Me. The shift from third to first person — fear Me — intensifies the command. Then comes the most quoted clause: al-yawma akmaltu lakum dīnakum wa atmamtu 'alaykum ni'matī wa raḍītu lakum al-islām dīnan. Akmaltu, from k-m-l, means to make complete in parts, while atmamtu, from t-m-m, means to complete in favour and continuity. The faith is perfected as a system of guidance, and favour is completed as lived blessing. Raḍītu, I have approved or chosen with pleasure, shows divine acceptance. A report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (45) mentions a Jew telling 'Umar that if this verse had been revealed to them they would have taken its day as a festival; 'Umar replied he knew where and when it was revealed — on 'Arafah on a Friday. That date is verified as 9 Dhū al-Ḥijjah 10 AH.

**Necessity Lifts Prohibition Without Lifting Piety**

Fa-man iḍṭurra fī makhmaṣatin ghayra mutajānif li-ithm provides the final exception: whoever is compelled by extreme hunger, not inclining to sin, may eat. Iḍṭurra, passive of ḍarara, means forced by necessity, and makhmaṣah, from kh-m-ṣ, means emptiness of belly, extreme hunger that hollows the stomach. Ghayra mutajānif li-ithm means not leaning toward sin, not seeking to enjoy the forbidden or exceed the amount needed to survive. Jurists derive the principle al-ḍarūrāt tubīḥ al-maḥẓūrāt, necessities permit prohibitions, with the condition of not transgressing. Fa-inna Allāha Ghafūr Raḥīm closes with two names that soften the severity earlier: All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. The same pair appears after other food allowances in *“But whoever is forced by necessity, not desiring nor transgressing, then surely Allah is Forgiving, Merciful”* (2:173). The structure teaches that law is firm but not cruel; it anticipates hardship and provides exit. The next verse will answer a question about what is lawful, continuing the same merciful pedagogy.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:4

> They ask you, ˹O Prophet,˺ what is permissible for them ˹to eat˺. Say, “What is good and lawful. Also what is caught by your hunting animals and birds of prey which you have trained as instructed by Allah. So eat what they catch for you, but mention the Name of Allah over it ˹first˺.” And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is swift in reckoning

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question About Food Becomes a Principle**

Yas'alūnaka mādhā uḥilla lahum shows the Companions asking what is made lawful for them to eat. The question follows a long list of prohibitions in verse 3, so the natural concern is what remains. The Qur'an often answers such questions by giving a principle rather than an exhaustive list, so the answer can cover new cases. Qul uḥilla lakum al-ṭayyibāt — say, what is good and lawful has been made lawful. Al-ṭayyibāt, plural of ṭayyib, means what is wholesome, pleasant, and pure, both in taste and in moral sense. It contrasts with khabā'ith, impure things, mentioned in *“He makes lawful for them the good things and forbids for them the impure”* (7:157). The term therefore includes both legal permissibility and natural wholesomeness. Early exegetes explain that ṭayyibāt covers all foods that Arabs considered good and that revelation did not forbid, excluding carrion and filth. By framing the answer this way, the verse teaches that the default is purity and goodness, not suspicion. The lawful is not a narrow island in a sea of prohibitions; it is the wide land, with only a few fenced areas.

**Hunting Animals as Trained Partners**

Wa mā 'allamtum min al-jawāriḥ mukallibīn adds what trained hunting animals catch. Jawāriḥ, plural of jāriḥ, comes from jarḥa, to wound, because these animals wound prey when catching it. It includes dogs, cheetahs, falcons, and hawks. Mukallibīn, from kalaba, means training dogs for hunting, but grammarians extend it to all predatory trainers. Tu'allimūnahunna mimmā 'allamakum Allāh — you have trained as instructed by Allah — indicates that training itself is knowledge Allah taught humans. The condition for permissibility is training: the animal must be taught to catch for its master, not for itself, and to respond to release and recall. Jurists differ on how many times an animal must demonstrate obedience, with some requiring three successful returns without eating from the catch. The requirement that training be from what Allah taught elevates a practical skill into a religious ethic: even using animals involves guidance. A report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (5483) and Muslim (1929) about 'Adī ibn Ḥātim asking about hunting with dogs confirms this rule, where the Prophet ﷺ said to mention Allah's name and eat what the dog holds for you.

**Mentioning Allah's Name Over the Catch**

Fa-kulū mimmā amsakna 'alaykum wa-dhkurū ism Allāh 'alayhi permits eating what they catch for you and commands mentioning Allah's name over it. Amsakna, they held, implies the hunting animal held the prey for its owner, not consumed it. If the dog or falcon eats part, that indicates it hunted for itself, and the catch is not lawful, as the same ḥadīth of 'Adī clarifies. Wa-dhkurū ism Allāh 'alayhi refers to saying bismillāh at sending the animal and at slaughter if the prey is still alive when reached. If the prey is already dead from the hunting animal's bite, the mention at sending suffices. This mention transforms a natural act into worship, linking provision to remembrance. *“So eat of that over which Allah's name has been mentioned, if you believe in His signs”* (6:118) and *“Do not eat of that over which Allah's name has not been mentioned”* (6:121) establish the same link for domestic slaughter. The verse thus unites hunting and slaughter under one devotional formula, showing that lawful eating always involves consciousness of the Giver.

**Swift Reckoning After Ease**

Wa-ittaqū Allāh, inna Allāha sarī' al-ḥisāb closes with mindfulness and swift reckoning. Ittaqū, from w-q-y, means to shield yourselves by obedience, and sarī' al-ḥisāb, swift in reckoning, means Allah accounts quickly and precisely. The pairing appears after a permission, warning not to abuse ease by neglecting conditions. The same closing appears in *“And be mindful of Allah; surely Allah is swift in reckoning”* (5:4) itself and in *“Be mindful of Allah and know that Allah is severe in punishment”* (2:196) during pilgrimage rulings. Speed here does not imply haste but thoroughness without delay; nothing escapes count. Al-Rāzī notes that swift reckoning encourages prompt obedience because the audit is near. For hunters, it means not to hunt in iḥrām, not to use untrained animals, and not to forget the name of Allah. The phrase also balances the earlier ease: food is abundant and good, but accountability is immediate. The next verse will expand lawful foods to include those of the People of the Book, continuing the theme of broad permission with clear boundaries.

**An Extra Verified Detail About Training**

Classical lexicons verify that mukallibīn originally described dog trainers, then by extension any trainer of beasts of prey, because kalab means dog. Lisān al-'Arab notes that taklīb is training a dog to hunt, and mukallab is a dog trained to catch. This linguistic point clarifies why the verse uses a dog-specific form to cover falcons and cheetahs: Arab culture knew dogs best as hunting partners, so the verb for dog training became generic. The verse's phrase mimmā 'allamakum Allāh also has a legal implication: training must involve withholding the animal from eating the catch, which is a learned restraint, not instinct. Al-Qurṭubī records that the trained dog should leave food even when hungry when its master calls, which is the sign of instruction. That detail shows how the Qur'an transforms a cultural practice into a moral lesson about discipline. The permission to eat game caught this way was a mercy for communities dependent on hunting, and the conditions ensure that mercy does not become a loophole for eating carrion-like prey.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:5

> Today all good, pure foods have been made lawful for you. Similarly, the food of the People of the Book is permissible for you and yours is permissible for them. And ˹permissible for you in marriage˺ are chaste believing women as well as chaste women of those given the Scripture before you—as long as you pay them their dowries in wedlock, neither fornicating nor taking them as mistresses. And whoever rejects the faith, all their good deeds will be void ˹in this life˺ and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Today Marks a Completion of Food Law**

Al-yawma uḥilla lakum al-ṭayyibāt repeats the permission with emphasis on today. Al-yawma, today, points to the time of revelation, likely during the Farewell Pilgrimage when final rulings were clarified. The repetition of ṭayyibāt, good pure foods, from verse 4 stresses that the baseline remains wholesomeness. By saying today, the verse signals that previous restrictions tied to earlier communities or to pre-Islamic taboos are now settled. The same today appears in verse 3: *“Today I have perfected your faith for you”* (5:3), linking perfection of religion to expansion of lawful foods. Jurists note that al-yawma here does not limit the permission to one day but marks the moment when the ruling became definitive. The good foods include all that is not explicitly forbidden and that sound nature finds pleasant. Al-Ṭabarī explains ṭayyibāt as what is lawful and whose harm does not outweigh its benefit. This principle allowed later scholars to address new foods by asking whether they are ṭayyib or khabīth, pure or impure, rather than seeking a specific text for each item.

**Food of the People of the Book and Mutual Permission**

Wa ṭa'ām alladhīna ūtū al-kitāb ḥillun lakum wa ṭa'āmukum ḥillun lahum declares mutual permissibility of food. Ṭa'ām here is interpreted by most early commentators as slaughtered meat, not all foods like bread or fruit, because the issue is slaughter. Ibn 'Abbās, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī in commentary form, said ṭa'ām means their slaughtered animals. The permission rests on the fact that People of the Book invoke Allah's name, even if with different theology, and they practice slaughter that drains blood. The mutuality — your food is permissible for them — acknowledges that they may also eat Muslim slaughter, though their own laws may restrict them. This verse therefore opens social relations: sharing meals builds trust. *“The food of those given the Scripture before you is lawful for you”* (5:5) is cited as basis for eating their meat when the conditions of proper slaughter and absence of explicit prohibition are met. Some scholars restrict it to meat slaughtered according to their law, not what they themselves consider forbidden, and they exclude what was slaughtered for other than Allah.

**Marriage to Chaste Women Among Believers and People of the Book**

Wa al-muḥṣanāt min al-mu'mināt wa al-muḥṣanāt min alladhīna ūtū al-kitāb permits marriage to chaste women from both groups. Muḥṣanāt, from ḥ-ṣ-n, means fortified, protected, and can mean chaste, free, or married depending on context; here it means chaste free women who guard their chastity. The verse requires dowry, uǧūr, plural of ajr, meaning compensation or gift, given in wedlock, muḥṣinīn, fortifying yourselves through marriage, ghayra musāfiḥīn wa lā muttakhiḏī akhdān, neither fornicating nor taking secret lovers. Musāfiḥ, from s-f-ḥ, means one who pours out lust without commitment, and akhdān, plural of khidn, means secret lover. The conditions emphasize public, committed marriage over casual relations. Jurists differ on whether muḥṣanāt from People of the Book must also be chaste in practice or merely free, but the stronger view requires actual chastity. The permission does not extend to polytheists, as *“Do not marry polytheist women until they believe”* (2:221) forbids that. This marriage allowance, like food allowance, facilitates social coexistence while preserving moral boundaries.

**Dowry, Chastity, and the Form of Marriage**

Idhā ātaytumūhunna ujūrahunna conditions the permission on paying dowries. Ujūr here means mahr, bridal gift, not wage, and its plural shows it is due for each marriage. Muḥṣinīn describes men who seek fortification through marriage, protecting themselves and their wives from sin. The contrast with musāfiḥīn and muttakhiḏī akhdān condemns both public promiscuity and private affairs, covering all extramarital forms. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“Give women their dowries as a free gift”* (4:4) and *“Marry them with permission of their families and give them their dowries in fairness”* (4:25). The verse therefore ties interfaith marriage to the same ethics as intra-faith marriage: consent, dowry, public commitment, and chastity. Some commentators note that the verse was revealed when some Companions asked about marrying Jewish and Christian women in Madinah, where such unions were socially relevant. The answer permits but frames with responsibility. The children of such marriages are raised as Muslims, which is why the permission is given to Muslim men, not the reverse, according to classical jurisprudence.

**Rejection of Faith Voids Deeds**

Wa man yakfur bi-l-īmān fa-qad ḥabiṭa 'amaluhu wa huwa fī al-ākhirati min al-khāsirīn warns that whoever rejects faith, his deeds are void and he is among losers in Hereafter. Yakfur bi-l-īmān, to reject faith, means to cover or deny the truth after it has become clear, not merely to lack knowledge. Ḥabiṭa 'amaluhu, his deeds are void, uses ḥ-b-ṭ meaning to swell and then collapse, like a sick animal that appears large but is empty, a vivid image for deeds that look good but lack foundation. The same verb appears in *“And whoever rejects faith, his work has failed”* (5:5) itself and in *“If you associate others with Allah, your work will surely fail”* (39:65). The loss is described as in this life and Hereafter: in this life because acts lose moral weight, in Hereafter because they do not save. The clause closes a passage of expanding permissions with a firm boundary: ease in food and marriage does not mean ease in doctrine. The sūrah's next verses will detail purification for prayer, shifting from social law to worship, but the same principle remains — external permission requires internal faith.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:6

> O believers! When you rise up for prayer, wash your faces and your hands up to the elbows, wipe your heads, and wash your feet to the ankles. And if you are in a state of ˹full˺ impurity, then take a full bath. But if you are ill, on a journey, or have relieved yourselves, or have been intimate with your wives and cannot find water, then purify yourselves with clean earth by wiping your faces and hands. It is not Allah’s Will to burden you, but to purify you and complete His favour upon you, so perhaps you will be grateful

**Expanded Commentary**

**Washing for Prayer Establishes a Physical Threshold**

Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū idhā qumtum ilā al-ṣalāh fa-ghsilū wujūhakum wa aydiyakum ilā al-marāfiq defines wuḍū'. Qumtum, you rise, indicates intention to pray, not merely standing, and fa-ghsilū commands washing. Wajh, face, is what faces others, from forehead to chin and ear to ear. Aydī, hands, includes forearms up to marāfiq, elbows, plural of mirfaq, joint where arm bends. The verse orders washing face and hands, wiping head, and washing feet to ankles. Wamsaḥū bi-ru'ūsikum — wipe your heads — uses bi- for partitive or for contact, which led to juristic differences on how much of head to wipe. Wa arjulakum ilā al-ka'bayn — feet to ankles — ka'bayn, dual of ka'b, means ankle bones. The majority read arjulakum as accusative, connected to washing, while a variant reading with genitive connects to wiping, leading to difference on whether feet are washed or wiped over socks. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (163) warns those who are negligent in washing feet with “woe to heels from Fire,” supporting washing. The verse thus sets a clear physical threshold for prayer.

**Full Impurity and the Full Bath**

Wa in kuntum junuban fa-iṭṭahharū addresses major impurity. Junub, from j-n-b, means distant, because the impure person is distant from prayer places; it covers after intercourse or seminal emission. Iṭṭahharū, purify yourselves, means ghusl, full bath, pouring water over entire body with intention. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“Do not approach prayer while intoxicated until you know what you say, nor in a state of major impurity except passing through, until you have bathed”* (4:43). The command to purify completely shows that prayer requires full readiness, not partial. Jurists explain ghusl requires intention and water reaching all skin, including hair roots. The verse places ghusl after wuḍū' to show two levels of purification: minor and major, both with water as origin. The mention of junub here also prepares for the concession that follows: when water is absent or harmful, another purification replaces it. This structure mirrors earlier pattern of law followed by ease, seen in verses 3 and 4.

**Illness, Travel, and the Search for Water**

Wa in kuntum marḍā aw 'alā safar aw jā'a aḥadun minkum min al-ghā'iṭ aw lāmastum al-nisā' fa-lam tajidū mā'an fa-tayammamū ṣa'īdan ṭayyiban lists excuses that permit dry ablution. Marḍā, ill, includes wounds where water would harm; safar, journey, implies lack of access. Ghā'iṭ originally meant low ground where people relieved themselves, then came to mean relieving itself. Lāmastum al-nisā', you touched women, is interpreted by Ibn 'Abbās as intercourse, while others take it as touching with desire, leading to difference on what breaks wuḍū'. Fa-lam tajidū mā'an — then you cannot find water — is the trigger for tayammum. Tayammamū, from y-m-m, means to intend or head toward, here intending clean earth. Ṣa'īdan ṭayyiban means clean earth, surface soil that is pure and dusty enough to leave trace on hands. The procedure is wiping faces and hands, famsaḥū bi-wujūhikum wa aydīkum minhu, showing that tayammum is symbolic purification, not physical cleaning, yet legally sufficient.

**Allah Does Not Intend Hardship**

Mā yurīdu Allāhu li-yaj'ala 'alaykum min ḥaraj states divine intent: Allah does not want to burden you. Ḥaraj, from ḥ-r-j, means narrowness, constriction, hardship that tightens chest. The negation mā yurīdu li-yaj'ala emphasizes that hardship is not the goal of law. Wa lākin yurīdu li-yuṭahhirakum wa li-yutimma ni'matahu 'alaykum — but He wills to purify you and complete favour upon you — gives the positive purpose: ṭahārah, purification, and itmām al-ni'mah, completing favour. Purification here is both physical and spiritual, removing dirt and sins; a ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (244) says when a believer washes face, sins leave with water. Completing favour refers to guidance that makes worship easy and accessible even without water. La'allakum tashkurūn — so perhaps you will be grateful — links purification to gratitude. Gratitude, shukr, is recognizing favour and using it obediently. The verse thus frames wuḍū' not as burden but as gift that prepares body and heart for standing before Allah.

**A Verse That Summarizes Legal Principles**

This verse is called by some scholars the verse of wuḍū' and tayammum, and it is one of the most cited in fiqh. It establishes that water is the primary purifier, that earth is a substitute when water is absent or harmful, and that law considers excuse. It also establishes that commands are tied to ability, a principle later formulated as al-mashaqqah tajlib al-taysīr, hardship brings ease. The order — face, hands, head, feet — became the obligatory sequence for many schools, while others allow some flexibility. The mention of elbows and ankles defines limits precisely, removing doubt. A verified linguistic note: marāfiq is plural of mirfaq or marfiq, both meaning elbow, and ka'bayn is dual of ka'b, ankle bone, which is the protruding bone at side of foot. Classical dictionaries confirm these anatomical meanings, showing the Qur'an's precision. The verse ends with gratitude, not fear, unlike verse 2 which ended with severe punishment, because purification is invitation to nearness. The next verse will remind of covenant and hearing and obeying, connecting outward washing to inward commitment.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:7

> Remember Allah’s favour upon you and the covenant He made with you when you said, “We hear and obey.” And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart

**Expanded Commentary**

**Favour and Covenant Pair in Memory**

Wa-dhkurū ni'mat Allāh 'alaykum wa mīthāqahu alladhī wāthaqakum bihi commands remembering favour and covenant. Dhikr, remembering, means both mental recall and verbal mention, and ni'mah, favour, covers guidance, security, and provision given to Madinan community. Mīthāq, from w-th-q, means a firm bond, stronger than 'aqd, a covenant fastened by oath. Wāthaqakum bihi — He bound you with it — emphasizes Allah as the one who secured the covenant, not merely humans promising each other. The covenant refers to the pledge of allegiance given to the Prophet ﷺ, particularly the Bay'at al-'Aqabah and Bay'at al-Riḍwān, where Companions pledged to hear and obey in ease and hardship. Some exegetes extend it to the primordial covenant mentioned in *“And when your Lord took from children of Adam their descendants and made them testify, Am I not your Lord? They said, Yes”* (7:172). The pairing of ni'mah and mīthāq teaches that favour precedes obligation: Allah gives first, then asks fidelity, so remembrance of favour motivates keeping covenant.

**We Hear and Obey as a Formula of Commitment**

Idh qultum sami'nā wa aṭa'nā recalls their words: we hear and we obey. Sami'nā, we heard, means we understood and accepted, not merely heard sound, and aṭa'nā, we obeyed, from ṭ-w-', means we followed willingly. The phrase is the believers' motto, contrasted with the Israelites' saying *“We hear and we disobey”* (2:93). It appears again as ideal response: *“The only response of believers when called to Allah and His Messenger to judge between them is to say, We hear and we obey”* (24:51). The past tense qultum indicates a historic utterance, but its moral remains present: faith is listening that leads to action. Al-Ṭabarī notes that this was said when the Prophet ﷺ took pledge that they would obey in what they liked and disliked. The covenant is therefore not abstract theology but concrete commitment to follow revelation even when it restricts food, hunting, and marriage as previous verses did. Remembering that utterance checks hypocrisy, because claiming faith then refusing its rulings contradicts one's own words.

**Mindfulness of Allah as Inward Guard**

Wa-ittaqū Allāh commands mindfulness. Ittaqū, from w-q-y, means to place a shield between yourself and what you fear, here Allah's displeasure. It follows remembrance because memory without mindfulness becomes nostalgia; mindfulness turns memory into caution. The same command closed verses 2, 4, and 8, forming a refrain in this opening. In each place it has slightly different shade: after food laws it means fearing to violate limits, after wuḍū' it means desiring purification, here it means honouring covenant. The repetition shows that taqwā is not one act but a posture accompanying all law. A ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (1987, graded ḥasan) says taqwā is here, pointing to heart, indicating that outward compliance must be rooted inward. The verse therefore moves from external favour to external covenant to internal shield, a progression from what Allah gave to what humans said to what they must maintain.

**Allah Knows What Hearts Conceal**

Inna Allāha 'alīm bi-dhāt al-ṣudūr closes with divine knowledge of what is in breasts. 'Alīm, intensely knowing, and dhāt al-ṣudūr, what possesses the breasts or what is hidden inside chests, means inner thoughts, intentions, and resentments. The phrase appears in several places, such as *“Indeed Allah knows what is in the breasts”* (3:119) and *“He knows what is in the breasts”* (64:4). Its placement after covenant is deliberate: humans may utter we hear and obey while harbouring reluctance, but Allah knows the difference. Al-Rāzī explains that this is both reassurance and warning: reassurance that sincere intention is known even if action falls short, and warning that pretense is exposed. In context of earlier verse about not letting hatred lead to injustice (5:8), knowing what is in hearts reminds that justice must be inward before outward. The clause also prepares for verse 8's command to be just even against those you dislike, because the One who knows hearts will judge fairness.

**A Medinan Reminder After Purification**

The sequence from verse 6 to 7 is purposeful: after detailing physical purification, the Qur'an reminds of spiritual purification through remembering covenant. Washing face and hands symbolizes cleaning what people see, while remembering favour cleans what Allah sees. The verse is short but comprehensive, linking theology, history, and ethics. A verified historical note: the covenant referenced is linked by many early sources to the pledge at Ḥudaybiyah in 6 AH, when 1400 Companions pledged under a tree to not flee, an event called Bay'at al-Riḍwān, mentioned in *“Allah was pleased with believers when they pledged to you under the tree”* (48:18). That pledge was about obedience unto death, matching sami'nā wa aṭa'nā. Remembering it after food and purification laws tells the Madinan community that law is not a list of rules but a living pledge they already made. The next verse will apply that pledge to justice, showing how covenant translates into conduct toward others.

**Why Hearing Must Precede Obeying**

Sami'nā wa aṭa'nā orders hearing before obeying, indicating that understanding precedes action. Sam', hearing, in Qur'anic usage includes comprehension and acceptance, not just auditory perception, as in *“If they had listened or reasoned, we would not be among companions of Blaze”* (67:10). Obedience without hearing is blind imitation, while hearing without obedience is knowledge without benefit. The covenant therefore joins both. Early jurists used this phrase to argue that a Muslim must first learn ruling then act, not act without knowledge. Al-Qurṭubī notes that sami'nā implies they heard the Prophet ﷺ recite revelation and understood its demand, then committed. This sequence protects against two errors: zeal without knowledge and knowledge without zeal. In context of this sūrah, which details many specific rulings, the reminder that believers already said we hear and obey encourages them to receive new details with readiness rather than resistance. It also explains why the verse ends with knowledge of hearts: Allah knows whether hearing was sincere and obedience willing.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:8

> O believers! Stand firm for Allah and bear true testimony. Do not let the hatred of a people lead you to injustice. Be just! That is closer to righteousness. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do

**Expanded Commentary**

**Standing Firm for Allah as Witnesses of Justice**

Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū kūnū qawwāmīn li-Llāh shuhadā'a bi-l-qisṭ commands being firm upholders for Allah and witnesses with justice. Qawwāmīn, intensive plural of qā'im, means those who stand constantly, upholding, maintaining. It is stronger than qā'imīn, indicating persistence and effort. Li-Llāh, for Allah, specifies motive: standing for His sake, not tribe or gain. Shuhadā'a bi-l-qisṭ, witnesses with equity, means bearing testimony that is fair and balanced. Qisṭ, from q-s-ṭ, means justice that gives each his due, and is used in *“O believers, stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah”* (4:135) with similar wording. The verse pairs two roles: qawwāmīn, active maintainers of justice, and shuhadā', those who testify when called. The first is broader, maintaining justice in all affairs; the second is specific, giving true testimony in disputes. Together they describe a community that both lives justly and speaks justly, with Allah as reference point. This is the practical meaning of the covenant mentioned in verse 7.

**Hatred Must Not Bend the Scale**

Wa lā yajrimannakum shana'ānu qawmin 'alā allā ta'dilū forbids letting hatred cause injustice. The phrasing repeats verse 2's warning but sharpens it: there it was about not transgressing against those who barred you from the Sacred Mosque; here it is about justice in testimony and judgment generally. Yajrimannakum, to incite or drive you, and shana'ān, intense hatred, show that emotion can push a person to tilt the scale. 'Allā ta'dilū, to not be just, from 'a-d-l, to make equal, straighten. The Qur'an acknowledges that hatred exists; it does not command you to feel love for enemies, but commands that feeling does not produce injustice. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this is among the strongest verses on impartiality, because it addresses the hardest case: being just toward those you hate. The principle became a legal maxim: justice is obligatory even toward a disbeliever, and injustice is forbidden even if for a friend. The verse therefore separates emotion from action, allowing dislike while forbidding wrong.

**Justice Is Closer to Righteousness**

I'dilū huwa aqrabu li-l-taqwā — be just, that is closer to righteousness — gives the reason. I'dilū, be just, imperative, and huwa, it, refers to justice, aqrabu li-l-taqwā, nearer to mindfulness and protection. Taqwā, as before, is shielding oneself from Allah's displeasure. Justice is closer to taqwā than injustice, meaning the path to piety passes through fairness. The comparison aqrabu suggests degrees: among actions, justice brings you nearest to God-consciousness. Some exegetes read it as justice is the way to attain taqwā, because habitual fairness trains heart to fear Allah. Others read it as justice itself is a form of taqwā, so being just is being mindful. The verse does not say justice is taqwā, but closer to it, indicating that taqwā includes more than justice, yet justice is its nearest companion. The same idea appears in *“Indeed Allah commands justice and excellence”* (16:90), pairing justice with higher virtue. By linking justice to taqwā, the verse makes impartiality a spiritual act, not merely civic.

**Mindfulness and Awareness of Deeds**

Wa-ittaqū Allāh, inna Allāha khabīr bi-mā ta'malūn closes with mindfulness and divine awareness. Khabīr, from kh-b-r, means fully aware of inner realities and outward acts, more penetrating than 'alīm in some contexts because it implies experience of hidden details. Bi-mā ta'malūn, of what you do, covers testimony, judgment, and cooperation mentioned earlier. The pairing of taqwā and khabīr appears often after justice commands, such as *“Be mindful of Allah; surely Allah is Aware of what you do”* (5:8) itself. It reminds that while humans may not detect biased testimony, Allah does. Al-Rāzī notes that khabīr is appropriate here because injustice often hides in subtle wording or selective memory, which only One aware of details can uncover. The command to be mindful after commanding justice shows that justice needs continual self-watch. The next verses will promise forgiveness for believers and punishment for deniers, linking justice in this world to recompense in next.

**A Verse That Grounds Law in Character**

This verse is among the most quoted ethical principles in Islamic law and is cited in constitutions and judicial oaths. Its structure — address to believers, command to stand firm, prohibition of bias, command to be just, link to taqwā, reminder of divine awareness — models how to legislate character, not only rules. A verified report: the Prophet ﷺ said in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (1827) that those who are just will be on pulpits of light near Allah, those who are just in their judgments and families. That ḥadīth illustrates the high rank given to 'adl. Linguistically, qawwāmīn li-Llāh appears only twice in Qur'an, here and 4:135, both in contexts of justice despite personal interest, showing deliberate emphasis. The verse also corrects a common human tendency to reserve justice for friends; it insists justice is owed to all, even those whose shana'ān, hatred, you feel. The following verse promises forgiveness and great reward for those who believe and do good, indicating that justice is among the good deeds that earn forgiveness.

**Why Qisṭ and 'Adl Both Appear in Qur'anic Justice**

Qisṭ and 'adl both translate as justice but carry different shades. Qisṭ, used here, emphasizes giving each party its exact share, balancing scales precisely, while 'adl emphasizes straightness and equality. The Qur'an uses qisṭ for testimony and commercial fairness, as in *“Give full measure and weigh with a just balance”* (17:35) where qisṭās, from same root, means scales. Using qisṭ here suggests justice that measures carefully, not approximate fairness. Al-Rāghib explains that qisṭ is justice that is manifest and measured, while 'adl is more general. The verse commands shuhadā'a bi-l-qisṭ, witnesses with measured justice, implying testimony must be precise, not vague. This precision matters when hatred tempts to exaggerate or minimize. The next clause I'dilū uses 'adl, broader justice, showing that specific measured testimony leads to general justice. The pairing teaches that justice begins with accurate words then extends to fair actions. Classical jurists used this verse to require that a witness be 'adl, upright, and that testimony be free of bias, forming basis for rules of evidence.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:9

> Allah has promised those who believe and do good ˹His˺ forgiveness and a great reward

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Promise Attached to Faith and Righteous Action**

Wa'ada Allāhu alladhīna āmanū wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāt lahum maghfirah wa ajr 'aẓīm states a divine promise. Wa'ada, He promised, from w-'-d, is a binding assurance, and when Allah promises, it is true without fail, as *“Indeed the promise of Allah is true”* (30:60) affirms. Alladhīna āmanū, those who believe, denotes those who affirm inwardly and outwardly, and 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāt, do good deeds, shows that faith is not claim alone but produces action. Ṣāliḥāt, plural of ṣāliḥah, means sound, wholesome, beneficial deeds that align with revelation. The combination īmān plus 'amal ṣāliḥ appears over fifty times in Qur'an, establishing that one without the other is incomplete. Lahum maghfirah, for them is forgiveness, means covering of sins so they do not lead to punishment, and ajr 'aẓīm, great reward, means compensation beyond measure. The promise is structured as forgiveness first, then reward, indicating that removal of harm precedes granting of good. The verse is short but comprehensive, summarizing the outcome of the justice and covenant commands that preceded it.

**Forgiveness as Covering and Reward as Amplification**

Maghfirah comes from gh-f-r, to cover, as a helmet covers head. In theological usage it means Allah covers sin so it does not expose its owner to disgrace or punishment. Ajr, reward, from a-j-r, originally means payment for work, but divine reward exceeds work, as *“Whoever comes with a good deed will have ten times like it”* (6:160) shows. 'Aẓīm, great, describes reward as immense in quantity and quality, beyond human calculation. The pairing of maghfirah and ajr appears in many verses, such as *“Those who believe and do good, theirs is forgiveness and great reward”* (5:9) itself and *“For them is forgiveness and noble provision”* (8:74). Some exegetes note that maghfirah relates to past sins, ajr to future life, so the promise covers both. Others note that maghfirah is needed because even good deeds are imperfect, so forgiveness cleanses them, then ajr honours them. The promise is not conditional on perfection but on faith and striving, which is why taqwā and justice in previous verses lead naturally to this assurance.

**Why This Promise Follows Justice Commands**

The placement after verse 8's command to be just even toward enemies is deliberate. Justice is difficult when emotion pulls toward bias, so Allah promises forgiveness for shortcomings and great reward for effort. The sequence teaches that ethical commands are not left without motivation: they are tied to divine recompense. In sūrah al-Mā'idah, this pattern repeats — law, then reminder of favour or reward — to show that law is not burden but path to mercy. Al-Ṭabarī links this verse to verse 7's covenant: those who said we hear and obey and then act justly are the ones promised forgiveness. The verse also contrasts with next verse, which threatens residents of Hellfire for disbelief and denial, creating a clear binary. The contrast is not meant to despair but to clarify choice. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6410) says Allah says: I am as My servant thinks of Me, encouraging hope alongside fear. The promise here nourishes that hope for those who strive for justice.

**Faith and Good Deeds as Inseparable Pair**

Alladhīna āmanū wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāt is more than a formula; it defines what counts as faith in Qur'anic discourse. Īmān includes belief in Allah, His messengers, and Last Day, while 'amal ṣāliḥ includes prayer, charity, justice, and fulfilling contracts mentioned in opening verses. The two are linked by wa, and, indicating both are required. Some early groups tried to separate them, claiming faith alone suffices, but verses like this and *“By time, indeed humanity is in loss, except those who believe and do righteous deeds”* (103:1-3) refute that. Al-Rāzī argues that 'amal ṣāliḥ is the fruit of īmān, so mentioning both emphasizes that true faith necessarily yields good. In this sūrah, good deeds include honouring contracts (5:1), respecting pilgrimage rites (5:2), eating lawful (5:3-5), purifying for prayer (5:6), and being just (5:8). The promise therefore gathers all previous commands into one outcome. It also implies that forgiveness is needed even for believers, because no one's deeds are perfect, so maghfirah precedes ajr.

**Great Reward Beyond Description**

Ajr 'aẓīm is left undefined, which is itself rhetorical: its greatness is beyond enumeration. The Qur'an describes great reward elsewhere as Gardens under which rivers flow, as in *“Their reward with their Lord is Gardens of Eden”* (98:8), and as pleasure of Allah, *“And Allah's pleasure is greater”* (9:72). By keeping it general here, the verse allows all those images to be included. The indefinite maghfirah and ajr also suggest abundance: a forgiveness and a great reward, not limited to one type. Classical commentators note that 'aẓīm is used for Allah's throne, *“Lord of the Mighty Throne”* (27:26), and for Qur'an, *“We have given you seven oft-repeated and the mighty Qur'an”* (15:87), so using it for reward elevates it. The promise is made by Allah, whose promise never fails, unlike human promises. That is why the verse begins with wa'ada Allāh, Allah has promised, placing His name first for emphasis. The next verse will show the opposite promise for those who disbelieve, completing the picture of recompense.

**Why Faith Must Be Named Before Deeds**

The verse names īmān before 'amal ṣāliḥ to show that deeds derive value from faith, not faith from deeds. In Arabic rhetoric, what is mentioned first is foundation. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that alladhīna āmanū is the subject, and wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāt is conjunct describing them, so good deeds are attribute of believers, not separate group. This matters because some might do outward good without faith, but Qur'an does not promise them ajr 'aẓīm in same sense. *“And whoever does righteous deeds, male or female, while being a believer, those will enter Paradise”* (4:124) makes same condition explicit. The promise of maghfirah also indicates that believers still need forgiveness, so faith does not mean sinlessness but orientation toward Allah that seeks pardon. The verse therefore balances hope and realism: it promises great reward but begins with forgiveness, acknowledging human imperfection while encouraging striving. That balance mirrors opening of sūrah, where permissions are wide but boundaries remain firm.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:10

> As for those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are the residents of the Hellfire

**Expanded Commentary**

**Disbelief and Denial as Two Layers of Rejection**

Wa alladhīna kafarū wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā describes those who disbelieve and deny Our signs. Kafarū, from k-f-r, means to cover, to hide, to be ungrateful, and in theology to reject truth after it has become clear. Kadhdhabū, from k-dh-b, means to declare false, to deny by speech and action. The pairing shows two stages: inward covering and outward rejection. Āyāt, signs, includes Qur'anic verses, prophetic miracles, and natural indicators of divine power. Bi-āyātinā, Our signs, attributes signs to Allah, emphasizing that denial is not of a human idea but of divine communication. The same pairing appears in *“Those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are companions of Fire”* (5:10) and in *“Those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, for them is a humiliating punishment”* (22:57). The verse follows promise of forgiveness for believers, so the contrast is immediate: belief and good deeds lead to forgiveness and great reward; disbelief and denial lead to Fire. The structure mirrors many Qur'anic passages that present two paths side by side to clarify choice.

**Residents of the Hellfire as Permanent Abode**

Ulā'ika aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm — they are residents of Hellfire. Aṣḥāb, companions or residents, from ṣ-ḥ-b, means those who accompany something constantly, implying permanence, not brief visit. Jaḥīm, from j-ḥ-m, means intense blazing fire that is deep and fierce. The term appears often for punishment of deniers, as in *“They will be companions of Hellfire”* (2:39). Using aṣḥāb rather than merely inhabitants suggests intimacy with punishment, as if Fire is their companion that does not leave. The verse does not detail duration, but other verses clarify that for those who die in persistent disbelief, abode is lasting, while Allah's mercy remains with forgiveness for those who repent before death. The brevity of the verse is rhetorical: after detailed laws and promises, the outcome for persistent denial is stated starkly without elaboration, letting its severity speak. The Qur'an often pairs concise threat with expansive promise, as here verse 9 had maghfirah and ajr 'aẓīm, while verse 10 has single clause aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, showing that mercy is elaborated, punishment summarized.

**Why Signs Are Central to Accountability**

Bi-āyātinā emphasizes that denial is not excused by lack of evidence. Āyāt include revelation, as in *“We have sent down clear signs”* (2:99), and natural signs, as in *“Indeed in creation of heavens and earth are signs for people of understanding”* (3:190). To deny them is to reject guidance that is clear and accessible. Early exegetes explain that āyāt here refers primarily to Qur'anic verses that clarify lawful and unlawful, which this sūrah has been detailing, so denial means refusing to accept the perfection of faith announced in verse 3. The verse therefore connects law to theology: refusing food laws or justice commands because one denies their source is not minor disagreement but kufr that leads to Fire. At same time, Qur'an distinguishes between those who deny out of ignorance and those who deny after knowledge; the latter is kadhdhabū, active denial. The sūrah's audience includes People of the Book who had received earlier signs, so reminder that denying Our signs has consequence is relevant to them as well as to polytheists.

**Contrast as Pedagogical Method**

Placing verse 9 and 10 together illustrates Qur'anic method of tarhīb and targhīb, warning and encouraging. Promise of forgiveness and great reward encourages good, while warning of being companions of Fire deters evil. The two verses are short, balanced in length, and opposite in outcome, making them easy to remember. This pairing appears throughout Qur'an, such as *“Indeed those who believe and do good, for them is forgiveness and great reward; but those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are companions of Hellfire”* (5:9-10) as a unit. Al-Rāzī notes that mentioning believers first shows priority of mercy over wrath. The contrast also serves justice: it would be unjust to mention reward without mentioning consequence for rejection, or vice versa. By stating both, Qur'an leaves no ambiguity about where each path leads. The next verse returns to addressing believers with reminder of favour when a people sought to harm them, showing that divine protection in this world precedes recompense in next.

**A Verse That Closes the Opening Ethical Section**

Verses 1-10 form an opening unit: contracts, pilgrimage rites, food laws, purification, covenant, justice, then promise and threat. Verse 10 closes that unit with eschatological outcome. Its brevity after detailed law suggests that law's purpose is to lead to these outcomes, not to be studied for its own sake. A verified historical note: jaḥīm is described in ḥadīth literature as one of names of Hell, with varying levels, and is mentioned as intensely hot fire whose fuel is people and stones, as in *“Fear Fire whose fuel is people and stones”* (2:24). The term jaḥīm appears 26 times in Qur'an, often paired with aṣḥāb, indicating consistent usage for persistent deniers. Linguistically, jaḥīm comes from root meaning to burn fiercely, and its form fa'īl indicates intensity. The verse's starkness also serves as transition: after establishing law and its ultimate results, sūrah will turn to history of Children of Israel and Christians, showing how previous communities responded to similar covenants. Their stories will illustrate both fulfilment and breach of contracts.

**The Pairing of Kufr and Takdhīb**

Kufr and takdhīb often appear together to show that rejection has inward and outward dimensions. Kufr alone can be passive covering, while takdhīb is active denial, accusing signs of falsehood. By combining both, verse 10 describes those who not only hide truth but campaign against it, as Quraysh did when they called Qur'an magic or poetry. *“Those who disbelieve and deny Our signs”* (5:10) therefore is stronger than merely alladhīna kafarū. Al-Rāzī notes that takdhīb implies they heard signs clearly then declared them lies, which is worse than ignoring them. The consequence, aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, fits the crime: persistent denial after clear signs warrants lasting companionship with Fire. The verse does not mention repentance, but other verses clarify that repentance before death is accepted, as in *“Say to those who disbelieve, if they cease, what has passed will be forgiven”* (8:38). The brevity here is intentional to close section with clarity, while mercy remains open elsewhere. The next verse returns to believers with reminder of protection, showing that divine favour continues despite human denial.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:11

> O believers! Remember Allah’s favour upon you: when a people sought to harm you, but He held their hands back from you. Be mindful of Allah. And in Allah let the believers put their trust

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Favour of Protection From Immediate Harm**

Udhkurū ni'mat Allāh 'alaykum idh hamma qawmun an yabsuṭū ilaykum aydiyahum fa-kaffa aydiyahum 'ankum recalls a specific favour: a group planned to stretch hands against you, but Allah held their hands back. Hamma, from h-m-m, means to intend or resolve, and yabsuṭū aydiyahum, to extend hands, idiom for attack or seizure. Kaffa, to withhold or restrain, shows divine intervention that stopped harm before it landed. Early reports differ on occasion: some say Quraysh at Ḥudaybiyah planned to attack Muslims, others say a Jewish tribe in Madinah plotted against Prophet ﷺ while he was sitting with companions, and Allah informed him. Al-Ṭabarī records several chains, indicating the verse may refer to more than one incident, which is why wording is general: qawm, a people, not named. The generality allows every believer to recall personal moments when harm was averted. The favour is not only physical safety but demonstration that Allah controls hands that would harm. *“And remember when you were few and oppressed, fearing people would snatch you, but He sheltered you”* (8:26) gives similar memory. Remembering such protection builds trust.

**Holding Hands Back as Divine Action**

Fa-kaffa aydiyahum 'ankum attributes restraint to Allah directly. Kaffa means to pull back, to stop from reaching, used elsewhere for *“It is Allah who withheld their hands from you and your hands from them”* (48:24) after Ḥudaybiyah. The image is vivid: hands stretched to harm are frozen mid-air. This is not merely that attackers changed mind; Allah acted to prevent. The verb fa-kaffa with fa- shows immediacy: they intended, then Allah immediately held back. The verse thus teaches that security ultimately comes from divine kafa, not from numbers or walls. For Madinan community that had faced boycott, battles, and plots, this reminder was tangible. It also explains why the next command is ittaqū Allāh, be mindful of Allah: the One who can restrain enemies deserves mindfulness. The favour is past, but its lesson is present: the same Allah who held hands then can hold them now. That leads naturally to tawakkul, trust, at end.

**Mindfulness and Trust as Twin Responses**

Wa-ittaqū Allāh, wa 'alā Allāhi fa-l-yatawakkali al-mu'minūn pairs mindfulness with trust. Ittaqū, shield yourselves by obedience, and tawakkul, from w-k-l, to entrust affairs to a wakil, representative who handles matters. The command wa 'alā Allāhi fa-l-yatawakkali places 'alā Allāh first for emphasis: upon Allah alone let believers trust. The particle fa- indicates that trust should follow mindfulness; those who are mindful find trusting easier because they know whom they trust. Al-Rāzī notes that tawakkul does not mean abandoning means but relying on Allah after taking means, as Prophet ﷺ took precautions yet trusted. The verse appears after a military favour, so trust here includes strategic trust: plan, but rely on Allah to block what you cannot control. *“And whoever puts trust in Allah, He will be enough for him”* (65:3) promises sufficiency. The verse therefore moves from memory to mindfulness to trust, a progression from past favour to present attitude to future reliance.

**Why This Verse Follows Promise and Threat**

Verse 9 promised forgiveness and great reward for believers, verse 10 warned of Hellfire for deniers, and verse 11 returns to believers with reminder of worldly protection. The sequence shows that divine care covers both worlds: forgiveness in next, safety in this. The reminder also prepares for verses 12-14 about covenants of Israelites and Christians, because those covenants also included promise of divine presence: *“I am truly with you”* (5:12). For Muslims, presence was shown by holding back hands; for earlier communities, presence was conditional on prayer, alms, and supporting messengers. The contrast will show that Allah's support is real but tied to covenant. The verse's brevity, like verses 9 and 10, makes it memorable, yet it carries historical depth. A verified detail: the phrase yabsuṭū ilaykum aydiyahum appears also in 60:2 describing enemies who would stretch hands and tongues with evil if they overcame you, showing consistent idiom for aggression. The remedy is same: divine kafa and human tawakkul.

**A Community Learning to Attribute Safety Correctly**

The Madinan community could have attributed safety to their own vigilance or alliances, but Qur'an says remember Allah's favour. Ni'mat Allāh 'alaykum appears repeatedly in this sūrah — verses 7, 11, 20 — forming a refrain that trains believers to attribute good to Allah. This attribution is itself worship, called shukr, gratitude. The verse ends with al-mu'minūn, believers, emphasizing that trust is characteristic of faith, not optional extra. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6416) says if you ask, ask Allah, and if you seek help, seek help from Allah, illustrating tawakkul. Linguistically, kaffa aydiyahum 'ankum uses 'an to show hands were diverted away from you, not merely stopped, indicating complete removal of threat. The verse thus teaches that protection is not just absence of harm but active turning away of harm. The next verse will show covenant with twelve leaders, expanding from immediate protection to long-term responsibility.

**Extra Verified Note on Occasion**

Classical sources link this verse to incident at Dhāt al-Riqā' when a polytheist named Ghawrath ibn al-Ḥārith drew sword over Prophet ﷺ while he rested under tree, then sword fell and Prophet ﷺ held it, saying who will protect you now, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (2910) and Muslim (843). Another link is to Ka'b ibn al-Ashraf's plot. The multiplicity of reports suggests verse is general, covering all cases where Allah protected Muslims from surprise attack. The general wording allows it to apply beyond one event, which is why exegetes like al-Ṭabarī list several possibilities without insisting on one. This generality is itself a mercy, allowing every generation to see its own protection in verse. The command to remember favour therefore remains relevant whenever believers face intimidation.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:12

> Allah made a covenant with the Children of Israel and appointed twelve leaders from among them and ˹then˺ said, “I am truly with you. If you establish prayer, pay alms-tax, believe in My messengers, support them, and lend to Allah a good loan, I will certainly forgive your sins and admit you into Gardens under which rivers flow. And whoever among you disbelieves afterwards has truly strayed from the Right Way.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Covenant With Twelve Leaders as Structure**

Wa laqad akhadha Allāhu mīthāq banī Isrā'īl wa ba'athnā minhum ithnay 'ashara naqīban recalls covenant with Children of Israel and twelve naqībs. Mīthāq, firm covenant, and akhadha, He took, shows Allah as initiator who takes pledge. Banī Isrā'īl, Children of Israel, refers to descendants of Ya'qūb, whose name Israel means servant of Allah. Naqīb, from n-q-b, means one who looks into affairs, inspects, represents, like a captain or chieftain. Twelve leaders correspond to twelve tribes, ensuring each tribe had representation and responsibility. The number twelve appears also for twelve springs for Moses' people in *“We split them into twelve tribes”* (7:160) and twelve months in *“Indeed number of months with Allah is twelve”* (9:36), indicating organized community. Ba'athnā, We sent or appointed, shows divine selection, not merely tribal choice. This structure teaches that covenant needs organization: faith is not only individual but communal, with leaders accountable for their groups.

**Divine Presence Conditional on Deeds**

Wa qāla Allāhu innī ma'akum — Allah said I am truly with you — gives assurance of divine companionship. Ma'akum, with you, means with support, knowledge, and aid, not spatial proximity. The same assurance was given to Moses and Aaron: *“Indeed I am with you both, hearing and seeing”* (20:46). Here it is conditional, followed by la-in aqamtum al-ṣalāh wa ātaytum al-zakāh. La-in, if indeed, emphasizes condition. Aqamtum al-ṣalāh, establish prayer, means perform it properly with its pillars and humility, not merely pray. Ātaytum al-zakāh, pay alms-tax, zakāh from z-k-w, to purify and grow, means giving portion of wealth to purify remainder. These two are pillars of practical faith. Wa āmantum bi-rusulī wa 'azzartumūhum adds belief in messengers and supporting them. 'Azzartumūhum, from '-z-r, means to support with strength and honour, to prevent humiliation. Wa aqraḍtum Allāha qarḍan ḥasanan — lend to Allah good loan — means spend in His way, charity that will be returned multiplied. Qarḍ ḥasan appears also in *“Who will lend to Allah a good loan so He multiplies it”* (2:245).

**Forgiveness and Gardens as Reward for Covenant Fulfilment**

La-ukaffiranna 'ankum sayyi'ātikum wa la-udkhilannakum jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār promises forgiveness and entry to Gardens. Ukffiranna, from k-f-r, to cover, here means to cover sins so they are erased, with emphatic nūn for certainty. Sayyi'āt, evil deeds, plural of sayyi'ah, small sins or misdeeds that accumulate. Jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār, Gardens under which rivers flow, is standard Qur'anic image of Paradise, where water flows beneath, indicating abundance and permanence. The promise is conditional but assured with double emphasis la- ... -anna. This mirrors promise to Muslims in verse 9: forgiveness and great reward for faith and good deeds. The covenant with Israelites therefore had same core: prayer, alms, belief in messengers, support, charity, leading to forgiveness and Paradise. The difference is not in principle but in response, as next verse will show breach. The verse thus establishes continuity of divine method across communities.

**Straying From Right Way After Guidance**

Fa-man kafara ba'da dhālika minkum fa-qad ḍalla sawā' al-sabīl warns that whoever disbelieves after that has strayed from right way. Ba'da dhālika, after that, means after covenant and clear conditions. Kafara here means rejected or covered truth after knowing it. Ḍalla, strayed, from ḍ-l-l, to lose way, and sawā' al-sabīl, even or middle of path, means right way that is straight and balanced. The phrase appears in *“Whoever exchanges faith for disbelief has strayed from right way”* (2:108). The warning shows that after covenant, disbelief is worse because it is betrayal, not ignorance. The verse uses minkum, among you, addressing Israelites directly, indicating that straying is individual responsibility even within chosen community. The covenant was communal, but accountability is personal. This principle applies to Muslims as well, as verse 7 reminded of their own covenant. The verse therefore serves as both history and admonition: having leaders and promises does not guarantee success; fulfilment does.

**Continuity Between Communities**

This verse begins a historical section that explains why previous covenants were broken and why new covenant with Muslims matters. The twelve naqībs parallel twelve chiefs among Anṣār at 'Aqabah, showing similar structure in early Islam. The conditions — prayer, zakāh, belief in messengers, support, good loan — are same as Islamic pillars, indicating continuity of religion. The difference lies in response: Israelites broke covenant, as verse 13 will say. A verified linguistic note: naqīb is also used for leader of group who investigates and ensures compliance, and its plural nuqabā' appears in ḥadīth about twelve leaders of Anṣār. Lisān al-'Arab defines naqīb as one who is responsible for knowledge of his people and their affairs. The number twelve is thus not arbitrary but represents complete representation. The verse ends with warning that straying after guidance is clear loss, preparing for next verse's description of hardening hearts as consequence of breach.

**Extra Verified Historical Detail**

The appointment of twelve leaders is linked to Moses selecting one leader per tribe before entering Holy Land, as narrated in Numbers 13-14 and reflected in Qur'an 5:20-26. Al-Ṭabarī records that Moses ordered each naqīb to ensure his tribe's obedience. This historical context explains why verse mentions twelve before describing entry into Holy Land in verse 21. The covenant's conditions also match Deuteronomy's calls to keep commandments, support prophets, and give charity. The Qur'anic retelling emphasizes that covenant was not merely law but relationship: I am with you. That phrase ma'akum appears for Muslims in *“Indeed Allah is with those who are mindful and those who do good”* (16:128), showing same companionship offered to all who fulfil conditions.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:13

> But for breaking their covenant We condemned them and hardened their hearts. They distorted the words of the Scripture and neglected a portion of what they had been commanded to uphold. You ˹O Prophet˺ will always find deceit on their part, except for a few. But pardon them and bear with them. Indeed, Allah loves the good-doers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Breaking Covenant Leads to Curse and Hard Hearts**

Fa-bi-mā naqḍihim mīthāqahum la'annāhum wa ja'alnā qulūbahum qāsiyah explains consequence: for breaking covenant, We cursed them and made hearts hard.
