Bi-mā naqḍihim, for their breaking, with mā extra for emphasis, shows cause. Naqḍ, from n-q-ḍ, means to unravel a knot, opposite of 'aqd and mīthāq which tie. La'annahum, We cursed or condemned, from l-'-n, means to expel from mercy and distance. Qāsiyah, hard, from q-s-w, means stern, unyielding, not soft to reminder. Hard hearts are described elsewhere as *“Then your hearts became hard after that, being like stones or harder”* (2:74) and *“Woe to those whose hearts are hard against remembrance of Allah”* (39:22). The sequence is causal: breaking covenant leads to curse, curse manifests as hard heart, hard heart leads to further sins like distorting words. The verse therefore shows moral psychology: betrayal hardens, hardening enables more betrayal. The curse is not arbitrary; it is natural outcome of persistent breach, as heart that repeatedly breaks promises loses softness.

**Distorting Words and Forgetting Portion**

Yuḥarrifūna al-kalim 'an mawāḍi'ihi describes distorting words from their places. Yuḥarrifūna, from ḥ-r-f, means to change edge or side, to alter wording or meaning by shifting from proper place. Kalim, words, refers to Torah's words about lawful, unlawful, and descriptions of Prophet ﷺ. 'An mawāḍi'ihi, from places, indicates they moved words from contexts where they were clear. This can be by misreading, misinterpreting, or concealing. Wa nasū ḥaẓẓan mimmā dhukkirū bihi adds they forgot portion of what they were reminded. Nasū, they forgot or neglected, and ḥaẓẓ, portion or share, shows they abandoned part of guidance, not all, which made distortion easier because partial knowledge is easier to twist. The same phrase appears for Christians in next verse: *“They neglected portion of what they were reminded”* (5:14). The two sins are linked: forgetting leads to distorting, because when part of reminder is lost, remaining words are read without context. *“They distort words after they have been placed properly”* (2:75) describes similar act.

**Persistent Deceit With Few Exceptions**

Wa lā tazālu taṭṭali'u 'alā khā'inatin minhum illā qalīlan minhum tells Prophet ﷺ you will always find deceit from them except few. Lā tazālu, you will not cease, indicates continuity. Taṭṭali'u, you will come upon or discover, from ṭ-l-', to appear or overlook, means you will encounter. Khā'inah, deceit or treacherous act, feminine form indicating act of betrayal. Illā qalīlan minhum, except few among them, acknowledges righteous minority, as *“Among People of Book is community standing, reciting Allah's signs”* (3:113) affirms. The verse therefore does not generalize to all Israelites eternally but describes prevailing pattern at time of revelation. The deceit included plotting, breaking treaties, and misleading about scripture. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“Whenever they make a covenant, a party of them throws it away”* (2:100). The exception qalīlan shows fairness: even in criticism, Qur'an notes exceptions. This balance is important for later command to pardon.

**Pardon and Forbearance Despite Deceit**

Fa-'fu 'anhum wa-iṣfaḥ, pardon them and bear with them, commands forgiveness and overlooking. 'Afu, from '-f-w, means to erase or pardon, as wind erases footprints, and iṣfaḥ, from ṣ-f-ḥ, means to turn away page, to overlook. The two together mean forgive inwardly and show forbearance outwardly. This command was revealed when Muslims had power in Madinah and could retaliate, yet were told to pardon. Some exegetes say this was before permission to fight treacherous covenant-breakers, later qualified by verses about fighting those who break treaties. Others say it remains general ethic of dealing with People of Book who do not fight. Inna Allāha yuḥibbu al-muḥsinīn — Allah loves good-doers — gives motive: pardon is iḥsān, excellence. Iḥsān is doing good beyond obligation, as defined in ḥadīth Jibrīl in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (8): to worship Allah as if you see Him. The verse thus transforms political tension into spiritual opportunity: pardoning deceitful is iḥsān that Allah loves.

**Hard Hearts as Warning for All Communities**

The description of hard hearts serves as warning for Muslims who also have covenant. The same disease can affect any community that breaks promises, distorts words, and forgets portion of reminder. The Qur'an warns believers *“Lest they become like those given Book before, whose time was prolonged and hearts became hard”* (57:16). The remedy is softening heart through remembrance, as *“Has time not come for believers that hearts should soften to remembrance of Allah”* (57:16) continues. The verse's placement after mention of twelve leaders shows that structure alone does not protect; hearts must remain soft. A verified linguistic note: qāsiyah is intensive form indicating hardness that is settled, not temporary, and is used for hearts that do not accept admonition. Lisān al-'Arab notes qasā al-qalb means heart became hard and dry, lacking moisture of mercy. The cure is dhikr and obedience, which bring moisture.

**Forgiveness as Strategy for Reform**

Fa-'fu 'anhum wa-iṣfaḥ is not only moral virtue but practical strategy for reform. Pardon gives time for hard hearts to soften, while harshness would confirm their hostility. The Qur'an elsewhere commands *“Repel evil with what is better, then the one who was enemy becomes close friend”* (41:34). By commanding pardon after describing deceit, verse teaches that good-doers, muḥsinīn, are those who respond to betrayal with forbearance when forbearance can lead to guidance. Al-Ṭabarī records that Prophet ﷺ pardoned many Jewish leaders after their plots were uncovered, hoping they would reflect. The love of Allah for muḥsinīn, mentioned at end, elevates pardon from weakness to excellence. The next verse will show Christians also neglected portion, leading to enmity among themselves, extending same pattern of breach and consequence across communities.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:14

> And from those who say, “We are Christians,” We took their covenant, but they neglected a portion of what they had been commanded to uphold. So We let hostility and enmity arise between them until the Day of Judgment, and soon Allah will inform them of all they have done

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Covenant Taken From Those Who Claim Christianity**

Wa min alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā akhadhnā mīthāqahum begins similar to previous verse but with distinct phrasing. Min alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā, from those who say we are Christians, notes claim, not necessarily reality, indicating that name alone does not guarantee fidelity. Naṣārā, Christians, from naṣara, to help, because they helped 'Īsā, or from al-Nāṣirah, Nazareth. Akhadhnā mīthāqahum, We took their covenant, same as with Israelites, showing that Christians also had binding pledge to follow guidance brought by 'Īsā and to believe in coming messenger. The covenant included affirming tawḥīd, following Gospel's call to worship Allah alone, and upholding law. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“And when Allah took covenant of those given Book, you must make it clear to people and not conceal it”* (3:187). The Christian covenant therefore included not hiding truth and not exaggerating about 'Īsā. The verse's wording qālū, they said, hints that claim was verbal more than practical, which next clause confirms.

**Neglecting a Portion of Reminder**

Fa-nasū ḥaẓẓan mimmā dhukkirū bihi repeats exact phrase from verse 13 about Israelites: they forgot portion of what they were reminded. The repetition shows pattern across communities: forgetting portion leads to distortion and division. Ḥaẓẓ, portion, indicates they kept some, lost some, and lost portion was enough to cause deviation. Dhukkirū bihi, they were reminded with it, passive, shows reminder came from Allah via messengers. For Christians, forgotten portion included pure monotheism and prophecies of final messenger, and for some, included rulings that restrained exaggeration about 'Īsā. The Gospel's central message of worshipping Allah alone was retained in words but neglected in interpretation when some said *“Allah is Messiah”* (5:17) or *“Allah is third of three”* (5:73). The verse does not say they forgot all, but portion, which is more realistic and more dangerous, because partial retention allows claim of continuity while substance is altered. This is why Qur'an says *“They neglected portion”* rather than abandoned all.

**Hostility and Enmity as Consequence**

Fa-aghraynā baynahum al-'adāwata wa al-baghḍā'a ilā yawm al-qiyāmah describes result: We let hostility and enmity arise between them until Day of Judgment. Aghraynā, from gh-r-y, means to incite, to cause to stick, to let cling, indicating that enmity was left to grow among them as consequence of neglecting reminder. 'Adāwah, hostility, outward enmity shown in fighting, and baghḍā', hatred, inward enmity in hearts, together cover inner and outer division. Ilā yawm al-qiyāmah, until Day of Judgment, indicates prolonged division, not temporary dispute. History verifies: Christian sects split over nature of Christ — Jacobites, Nestorians, Melkites — and fought, and later Catholic and Orthodox and Protestant divisions continued. The Qur'an notes similar division among Israelites earlier, but here emphasizes that forgetting portion of reminder leads to internal enmity, not only curse. The cause is theological: when core reminder about unity of Allah is neglected, disagreements about secondary matters become irreconcilable because no common foundation remains.

**Allah Will Inform Them of Deeds**

Wa sawfa yunabbi'uhum Allāhu bi-mā kānū yaṣna'ūn closes with future information: soon Allah will inform them of what they used to do. Sawfa, soon, indicates near future relative to Hereafter, and yunabbi'uhum, inform with news, from n-b-’, to give important news, shows that full reality of their deeds will be made clear. Bi-mā kānū yaṣna'ūn, of what they used to do, includes both neglect and resulting enmity. The same closing appears in verse 14 itself and in *“Allah will inform them of what they did”* (5:105) and *“Then to Allah is your return, He will inform you of what you differed”* (5:48). The information on Day of Judgment will be complete and undeniable, removing dispute. The verse therefore moves from covenant to neglect to division to final accounting, a pattern that also appeared for Israelites but with different consequence: curse and hard hearts for one, hostility and hatred for other. Both are forms of communal punishment for breach.

**Pattern of Covenant Breach Across Communities**

Verses 12-14 together form a unit showing that covenant is not ethnic privilege but responsibility. Twelve leaders for Israelites, covenant for Christians, both with condition of upholding reminder. Both neglected portion, leading to punishment that fits crime: Israelites distorted words and hardened, so curse and hard hearts; Christians divided over theology, so enmity. The parallelism teaches Muslims that their own covenant, mentioned in verse 7, also requires upholding all of reminder, not portion. A verified historical note: the term naṣārā appears 14 times in Qur'an, often with reference to their covenant and their claim of closeness to believers, as in *“You will find closest in affection to believers those who say we are Christians”* (5:82), showing Qur'an distinguishes between claim and faithful among them. The verse's warning about hostility until Day of Judgment is observable in Christian history of schisms, which began early and continue, fulfilling description.

**Enmity Among Themselves as Divine Letting**

Fa-aghraynā indicates Allah let enmity arise, not that He created hatred arbitrarily, but that He left them to consequence of forgetting. Aghrā, to incite or cause to cling, is used when a person is left to what he chose. The same root appears in *“We incited enmity and hatred among them”* (5:64) for another group. The letting is punishment that fits neglect: they neglected portion that would have united them, so they were left divided. The division is described as 'adāwah and baghḍā', outward hostility and inward hatred, covering both action and feeling. The phrase ilā yawm al-qiyāmah shows longevity, and history confirms: councils, excommunications, and wars among Christian groups persisted. The remedy will be offered next verse: Messenger who reveals hidden and light that guides to peace, directly countering division. The next verse addresses People of Book directly, offering Messenger who reveals much hidden, providing remedy for forgetting.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:15

> O People of the Book! Now Our Messenger has come to you, revealing much of what you have hidden of the Scriptures and disregarding much. There certainly has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book

**Expanded Commentary**

**Address to People of the Book After History of Breach**

Yā ahl al-kitāb qad jā'akum rasūlunā yubayyinu lakum kathīran mimmā kuntum tukhfūna min al-kitāb calls People of Book directly. Ahl al-kitāb, People of Book, refers to Jews and Christians given Torah and Gospel. Qad jā'akum rasūlunā, Our Messenger has come to you, with qad indicating already arrived, not future expectation, and rasūlunā, Our Messenger, possessive, shows he is sent by Allah, not self-appointed. Yubayyinu lakum, revealing or making clear to you, from b-y-n, to separate, clarify, indicates his role is to clarify what was hidden. Kathīran mimmā kuntum tukhfūna min al-kitāb — much of what you hid of Scripture — acknowledges concealment. Kuntum tukhfūna, you used to hide, imperfect, shows ongoing practice of hiding descriptions of Prophet ﷺ, rulings like stoning, and monotheistic verses. The verse does not say he reveals all hidden, but much, because some hidden matters are left as is. Wa ya'fū 'an kathīr — and disregarding much — means he overlooks much that is not essential to clarify, showing mercy and focus. The balance between revealing and overlooking demonstrates prophetic wisdom: clarify what matters for guidance, ignore what would cause unnecessary dispute.

**Light and Clear Book as Dual Description**

Qad jā'akum min Allāhi nūr wa kitāb mubīn announces that from Allah has come light and clear Book. Nūr, light, is interpreted as Prophet ﷺ himself or as Islam or as Qur'an; many exegetes say nūr is Prophet ﷺ because he illuminates, while kitāb mubīn is Qur'an that makes clear. Others say both refer to Qur'an, which is light and clear book. Mubīn, from b-y-n, means clear in itself and clarifying for others. The pairing light and clear book appears elsewhere: *“There has come to you light and clear Book from Allah”* (5:15) itself, and *“We have sent down to you a clear light”* (4:174). Light guides in darkness, book gives detailed instruction; together they provide both illumination and legislation. The phrase min Allāh, from Allah, emphasizes source: this light is not human speculation but divine gift. For People of Book who had hidden parts of their books, light reveals what was in shadow, and clear book replaces distorted portions with preserved text. The verse therefore offers remedy for previous neglect: after forgetting portion, now complete light arrives.

**Revealing Hidden Scriptures as Prophetic Proof**

Yubayyinu kathīran mimmā kuntum tukhfūna is also proof of prophethood, because an unlettered Arab could not know what Jewish and Christian scholars hid unless informed by Allah. Early reports mention that Prophet ﷺ clarified that stoning is in Torah, which some Jewish leaders tried to hide, as narrated in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3635) about stoning case brought to Prophet ﷺ. He also clarified that description of his coming is in their books, as *“Those who follow Messenger, unlettered Prophet, whom they find written in Torah and Gospel”* (7:157). Revealing hidden therefore serves two functions: correcting distortion and proving messenger's truthfulness. Wa ya'fū 'an kathīr shows he does not expose every private concealment, focusing on what affects faith and law. This restraint is itself prophetic character: not seeking to shame but to guide. Al-Rāzī notes that yubayyinu is present tense, indicating ongoing clarification throughout prophetic mission, not one-time event.

**Why Light Precedes Book in Mention**

Nūr before kitāb mubīn may indicate that personal example illuminates before text is understood, or that Qur'an itself is both light and book, light in guiding hearts, book in giving rulings. Light is needed to see book, so mention of light first is logical. The same order appears in *“O People, proof has come to you from Lord and We sent down to you clear light”* (4:174). For People of Book who had books but lacked light to interpret them correctly after forgetting portion, new light is essential. The verse therefore addresses both scripture and interpretation: clear book gives correct text, light gives correct understanding. This dual gift addresses both previous diseases: distortion of words and forgetting portion. By sending Messenger who is light and book that is clear, Allah provides what previous communities lost. The next verse will describe how this light guides to ways of peace, showing practical outcome of illumination.

**A Verse That Marks Transition From History to Invitation**

Verses 12-14 narrated breach, verse 15 offers remedy. The address yā ahl al-kitāb is gentle, inviting, not condemning, after strong criticism. It shows Qur'anic method: critique followed by invitation. The verse is also among those that define Prophet's role as mubayyin, clarifier, not only reciter. *“We sent down to you Reminder to make clear to people what was sent down to them”* (16:44) gives similar role. A verified linguistic note: mubīn is active participle meaning clarifying, not just clear, so kitāb mubīn is book that clarifies truth from falsehood. Lisān al-'Arab notes bāna means to become clear and to make clear, both senses. The verse's claim that Messenger reveals hidden was verified historically when Jewish scholars in Madinah acknowledged that Prophet ﷺ knew matters they thought hidden, as recorded in Sīrah.

**Revealing and Overlooking as Balanced Method**

Yubayyinu kathīran and ya'fū 'an kathīr show balanced method: reveal much, overlook much. The balance prevents two extremes: exposing everything would humiliate, hiding everything would leave error. The Prophet ﷺ clarified what was essential for faith — monotheism, prophethood, lawful — and overlooked details that were not central. This method is also described in *“He reveals much of what you hid and overlooks much”* (5:15) itself, indicating deliberate selection. Al-Qurṭubī notes that overlooking here means not holding them accountable for every concealment, but focusing on what leads to guidance. The same balance appears in Qur'an's own storytelling: it mentions some details of previous books and leaves others. For People of Book, this balance is mercy: they are invited without being shamed for every hidden matter. The next verse will detail guidance to peace, continuing invitation and showing outcome of accepting light.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:16

> through which Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace, brings them out of darkness and into light by His Will, and guides them to the Straight Path

**Expanded Commentary**

**Guidance Through Light to Ways of Peace**

Yahdī bihi Allāhu man ittaba'a riḍwānahu subul al-salām describes guidance through light and clear book. Yahdī bihi, He guides through it, with bihi referring to nūr and kitāb mubīn from previous verse. Allāh as subject emphasizes divine agency; guidance is from Allah, not from book alone without His will. Man ittaba'a riḍwānahu, those who seek His pleasure, riḍwān from r-ḍ-w, deep pleasure and acceptance, indicates that guidance requires seeking divine pleasure, not mere curiosity. Subul al-salām, ways of peace, plural of sabīl and salām, means paths that lead to peace, safety, and salvation. Salām is both peace and name of Allah, al-Salām, and abode of peace, dār al-salām, Paradise. The plural subul indicates multiple ways: prayer, charity, justice, fulfilling contracts, all are ways of peace. *“And Allah invites to abode of peace”* (10:25) and *“He guides to ways of peace”* (5:16) itself show that peace is both path and destination. The verse therefore says Qur'an guides seekers of divine pleasure to all paths that produce peace in heart, family, and society.

**From Darknesses to Light by His Will**

Wa yukhrijuhum min al-ẓulumāt ilā al-nūr bi-idhnihi — brings them out of darknesses into light by His permission — continues. Ẓulumāt, darknesses, plural, indicates many darknesses: ignorance, disbelief, hatred, division mentioned in verses 13-14. Nūr, light, singular, indicates single light of truth, as *“Allah is light of heavens and earth”* (24:35) and *“Allah is ally of believers, bringing them out of darknesses into light”* (2:257). The plural to singular shift is deliberate: many darknesses, one light. Bi-idhnihi, by His will or permission, from i-dh-n, to permit, shows that exit from darkness requires divine enablement, not only human effort. The same phrase appears in *“He brings them out of darknesses into light by His will”* (5:16) and *“A Book We sent down to you to bring people out of darknesses into light”* (14:1). The light here is same nūr from previous verse, now described as destination. For People of Book who forgot portion and fell into hostility, this exit is precisely needed: from darkness of forgetting and enmity into light of clarity and peace.

**Guidance to Straight Path as Culmination**

Wa yahdīhim ilā ṣirāṭ mustaqīm — and guides them to Straight Path — culminates. Ṣirāṭ mustaqīm, straight path, is path that is straight, balanced, leading directly to goal without deviation, mentioned in every prayer: *“Guide us to straight path”* (1:6). Mustaqīm, from q-w-m, means standing firm, upright, not crooked. The verse mentions guidance twice: yahdī to ways of peace and yahdī to straight path, indicating two levels: guidance to specific good deeds and guidance to overall direction. Al-Rāzī notes that subul al-salām are branches, ṣirāṭ mustaqīm is trunk, so guidance to branches leads to trunk. The repetition of yahdī emphasizes that guidance is ongoing, not one-time. The conditions remain: those who seek His pleasure. Seeking pleasure, ittaba'a riḍwānahu, implies intention matters; guidance is given to those who want divine pleasure, not those seeking argument. The verse therefore links intention, divine will, and outcome in clear chain.

**Peace as Comprehensive Concept**

Salām in subul al-salām is rich: it means peace between humans, peace of heart, safety from Fire, and greeting of Paradise. The Qur'an says *“For them is abode of peace with their Lord”* (6:127) and *“Their greeting therein is peace”* (10:10). Ways of peace therefore include justice, which produces social peace, as verse 8 commanded; fulfilling contracts, which produces trust; and purification, which produces inner peace. The plural subul acknowledges that different people may need different entry points to peace, but all lead to same straight path. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (847) says Allah has 99 names, and al-Salām is among them, indicating peace is divine attribute, so ways of peace are ways toward divine attribute. The verse's promise that Allah guides to these ways by His will gives hope to those who feel trapped in darknesses of division described earlier. The next verse will refute extreme claim about Messiah, showing that true peace requires correct belief about Allah.

**Light and Guidance as Remedy for Previous Diseases**

Verses 13-14 described hard hearts and enmity as consequences of neglecting reminder; verses 15-16 offer light that softens hearts and guides to peace, directly countering those diseases. The movement from darknesses to light reverses forgetting, and guidance to straight path reverses straying mentioned in verse 12: *“has strayed from right way”* (5:12). The Qur'an thus presents itself as cure, as *“O people, there has come to you admonition from your Lord and healing for what is in breasts”* (10:57). The condition man ittaba'a riḍwānahu ensures that cure benefits those who seek it. Linguistically, subul is plural of sabīl, which is feminine but can be masculine, and its plural indicates many paths, showing generosity: many ways to peace, not one narrow gate. The verse ends with straight path, singular, showing unity of destination.

**Seeking Pleasure as Condition for Guidance**

Man ittaba'a riḍwānahu makes seeking divine pleasure condition for guidance. Riḍwān is stronger than riḍā, indicating deep, lasting pleasure. Ittaba'a, to follow, implies active pursuit, not passive wish. The verse therefore says guidance is for seekers, not for indifferent. This condition appears elsewhere: *“Allah guides to His light whom He wills”* (24:35) and *“He guides those who turn to Him”* (13:27). Seeking pleasure includes purifying intention, as ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (1) says actions are by intentions. For People of Book, seeking Allah's pleasure would mean accepting Messenger who clarifies hidden, even if it challenges previous views. For Muslims, it means using light and clear book to find ways of peace in personal and communal life. The following verses will address specific theological errors that block peace, beginning with claim that Allah is Messiah, showing that correct belief is first way of peace.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:17

> Indeed, those who say, “Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary,” have fallen into disbelief. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Who has the power to prevent Allah if He chose to destroy the Messiah, son of Mary, his mother, and everyone in the world all together?” To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between. He creates whatever He wills. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**Expanded Commentary**

**Claim That Allah Is Messiah as Disbelief**

Laqad kafara alladhīna qālū inna Allāha huwa al-Masīḥ ibn Maryam declares those who say Allah is Messiah have disbelieved. Laqad, indeed, emphatic, kafara, disbelieved, from k-f-r, to cover truth. Alladhīna qālū, those who said, indicates speech that expresses belief. Inna Allāha huwa al-Masīḥ, Allah is Messiah, identifies Creator with created human. Al-Masīḥ, Messiah, from m-s-ḥ, to wipe or anoint, title for 'Īsā, son of Maryam, meaning anointed one. The Qur'an rejects identification because Messiah is human born of mother, eats food, as *“Messiah, son of Mary, was only a messenger; messengers had passed before him, and his mother was a woman of truth; they both ate food”* (5:75). Eating food implies need, need contradicts divinity. The claim is quoted directly to show its wording, then judged as kufr, not mere error, because it nullifies tawḥīd. The same judgment appears for claim that Allah is third of three in *“Those who say Allah is third of three have disbelieved”* (5:73). The verse therefore draws clear line: honouring Messiah is required, identifying him as Allah is disbelief.

**Who Can Prevent Allah If He Willed Destruction**

Qul fa-man yamliku min Allāhi shay'an in arāda an yuhlika al-Masīḥ ibn Maryam wa ummahu wa man fī al-arḍ jamī'an challenges claim with question of power. Qul, say, O Prophet, fa-man yamliku, who owns or controls anything against Allah, yamliku from m-l-k, to own, control. Shay'an, anything, even small. In arāda, if He willed, an yuhlika, to destroy, al-Masīḥ and mother and everyone in earth together. The argument is from contingency: Messiah and mother are mortal, subject to destruction, so they cannot be Allah who destroys. If they were divine, they would prevent destruction, but they cannot. The same argument appears in *“Say, who can prevent Allah if He intends harm for you or intends benefit”* (48:11). The mention of mother is deliberate: Maryam is revered, but also mortal, and including her shows that lineage does not confer divinity. Wa man fī al-arḍ jamī'an extends to all creation, showing Allah's power is universal. The question is rhetorical, expecting answer: no one can prevent Allah. Therefore Messiah cannot be Allah.

**Kingdom Belongs to Allah Alone**

Wa li-Llāhi mulk al-samāwāt wa al-arḍ wa mā baynahumā — to Allah belongs kingdom of heavens and earth and what is between — asserts sovereignty. Mulk, kingdom, dominion, from same root as yamliku earlier, creating wordplay: who owns anything against Allah when to Allah belongs all ownership. Samāwāt wa al-arḍ, heavens and earth, covers all creation, wa mā baynahumā, what is between, includes air, clouds, and everything between. The phrase appears also in *“To Allah belongs kingdom of heavens and earth”* (5:17) itself and *“To Allah belongs kingdom of heavens and earth and everything in between”* (5:18) next verse, emphasizing that Messiah, mother, and all are within that kingdom, not owners of it. The verse therefore refutes divinity by showing Messiah is within kingdom, not owner. The same sovereignty argument refutes claim of children of Allah in next verse, where same phrase recurs, linking two refutations.

**He Creates Whatever He Wills**

Yakhluqu mā yashā', He creates whatever He wills, explains how Messiah was created without father. Yakhluqu, creates, from kh-l-q, to create from nothing or shape, and mā yashā', whatever He wills, shows will unrestricted by natural norms. The creation of 'Īsā without father is not proof of divinity but proof of divine creative power, as creation of Ādam without parents and Ḥawwā' from one parent also show. *“Indeed likeness of Jesus before Allah is like Adam, He created him from dust then said Be and he is”* (3:59) makes same point. The Qur'an says *“When He decrees a matter, He only says Be and it is”* (2:117). So virgin birth is within yakhluqu mā yashā', not outside it. The verse thus turns supposed evidence for divinity into evidence for Allah's power to create in diverse ways. The same creative power includes destroying, as earlier question showed, so both creation and destruction belong to Allah alone.

**Allah Is Most Capable of Everything**

Wa Allāhu 'alā kulli shay'in qadīr — Allah is Most Capable of everything — closes with divine capability. 'Alā kulli shay', over everything, and qadīr, intensely capable, from q-d-r, to measure and empower, indicates ability to do what He wills. The phrase appears over thirty times in Qur'an, often after mention of creation and kingdom. Here it seals argument: if Allah can create whatever He wills and destroy whomever He wills, and owns kingdom, then He is capable of everything, while Messiah is not. The final fragment in translation, Children of Allah?, appears to be stray but reminds of next verse's topic, which refutes claim of being children of Allah. The verse's core is clear: saying Allah is Messiah is kufr, and proof is Messiah's contingency and Allah's absolute ownership and power. The next verse will address related claim of being children and beloved of Allah, using same sovereignty language.

**Extra Verified Detail on Messiah Title**

Al-Masīḥ as title is verified in Arabic lexicons as meaning anointed, wiped, because prophets were anointed with oil, or because 'Īsā wiped away ailments by healing. Lisān al-'Arab notes masaha means to wipe, and Masīḥ is one who is anointed or who wipes. The Qur'an uses al-Masīḥ eleven times, always for 'Īsā ibn Maryam, never alone without lineage except when clear. The claim inna Allāha huwa al-Masīḥ is reported as belief of some Christian groups in Arabia, particularly Jacobites who emphasized divine nature, as recorded by al-Ṭabarī. The Qur'anic response does not engage Christological subtleties but returns to basic contingency and ownership, method that remains accessible to all. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3445) says do not exaggerate about me as Christians exaggerated about son of Mary, linking exaggeration to divinity claim, supporting verse's warning.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:18

> The Jews and the Christians each say, “We are the children of Allah and His most beloved!” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Why then does He punish you for your sins? No! You are only humans like others of His Own making. He forgives whoever He wills and punishes whoever He wills. To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between. And to Him is the final return.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Claim of Being Children and Beloved**

Wa qālat al-yahūd wa al-naṣārā naḥnu abnā' Allāh wa aḥibbā'uh reports claim: we are children of Allah and His beloved. Yahūd, Jews, and naṣārā, Christians, both made claim, according to Ibn 'Abbās, when some Jewish leaders in Madinah said they are children and beloved and will not be punished except briefly. Abnā', sons or children, plural of ibn, and aḥibbā', beloved, plural of ḥabīb, indicate special status and affection that would prevent punishment. The claim mirrors biblical language where Israel is called son, as in *“Israel is My son, My firstborn”* (Exodus 4:22), and Christians called children of God in Gospels. Qur'an does not deny metaphorical use of fatherhood as care in earlier scriptures but denies that it implies immunity or divinity. The claim of being beloved, aḥibbā', implies exclusive love that guarantees favour regardless of deeds. The verse quotes claim directly then refutes with question about punishment, method of exposing contradiction between claim and experience.

**Why Does He Punish You for Sins Then**

Qul fa-li-ma yu'adhdhibukum bi-dhunūbikum — say, why then does He punish you for sins — refutes by observable fact. Yu'adhdhibukum, He punishes you, from '-dh-b, to punish, and bi-dhunūbikum, for sins, plural of dhanb, sin that follows like tail. If you were truly children and beloved in exclusive sense that prevents punishment, you would not be punished, but you admit punishment exists, even if you limit it. Jewish claim in *“Fire will not touch us except few days”* (2:80) admits punishment but minimizes, and Christian doctrine of atonement admits sin but claims redemption. The Qur'an argues that punishment for sins contradicts claim of unconditional beloved status. The question fa-li-ma, why then, expects no answer that preserves claim. The refutation is logical: beloved children are not punished for sins if love is unconditional, but you are punished, so love is not unconditional in sense you claim. The verse therefore separates divine love that is earned by obedience from claimed status by lineage.

**You Are Humans Like Others of His Making**

Bal antum bashar mimman khalaq — no, you are humans like others He created — corrects. Bal, rather, negates previous claim, antum bashar, you are humans, bashar, human beings with skin, mortal, needing food, subject to death. Mimman khalaq, from those He created, indicates you are part of creation, not offspring of Creator. The phrase appears also in *“You are only humans like us”* (36:15) said to messengers, but here said to those claiming special sonship. The correction is not demeaning but equalizing: all humans are created, not begotten, and distinction is by piety, as *“Indeed most noble of you before Allah is most mindful”* (49:13) states. The verse thus dismantles ethnic or sectarian exclusivism: being Jew or Christian does not make one child of Allah in literal sense that prevents accountability. The same humanity applies to Messiah and mother in previous verse, linking two refutations: Messiah is human, you are humans, all are created.

**Forgiveness and Punishment by Divine Will**

Yaghfiru li-man yashā' wa yu'adhdhibu man yashā' — He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills — states divine discretion in recompense. Yaghfiru, forgives, from gh-f-r, to cover, and yu'adhdhibu, punishes, show both mercy and justice are in His hands. Li-man yashā', for whom He wills, does not mean arbitrary but according to wisdom and knowledge of hearts, as *“He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills, and Allah is Capable of everything”* (2:284) explains. The pairing appears often after refuting exclusivist claims, as in *“To Allah belongs kingdom, He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills”* (5:18) itself and *“He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills”* (48:14). The point is that forgiveness is not automatic by lineage but by will that considers faith and deeds. This returns to earlier promise: forgiveness for believers who do good (5:9), not for those who merely claim status.

**Kingdom and Final Return Belong to Allah**

Wa li-Llāhi mulk al-samāwāt wa al-arḍ wa mā baynahumā wa ilayhi al-maṣīr — to Allah belongs kingdom and to Him is final return — closes with sovereignty and eschatology. Mulk, kingdom, as before, and maṣīr, final return, from ṣ-y-r, to become, end up, indicates all return to Him for judgment. The phrase wa ilayhi al-maṣīr appears in *“To Allah is final return”* (5:18) and *“To Him is final return”* (2:285). It reminds that lineage does not prevent return and accounting. The same closing appeared in verse 17, linking two verses: both refute exaggerated claims about Allah's relationship to humans by affirming His ownership and that all return to Him. The final return is where true beloved status will be determined by deeds, not claims. The next verse will announce Messenger after interval, removing excuse that no warner came, continuing argument that guidance has arrived and claims must now be tested by response to it.

**Extra Verified Note on Children Language**

The claim naḥnu abnā' Allāh is verified as reflecting Jewish self-description as children of God in Deuteronomy 14:1 and Christian self-description in John 1:12, where believers are called children of God. Early tafsīr notes that Jews in Madinah said we are sons and beloved because we are children of prophets. The Qur'anic response does not deny that Allah loves those who obey, as *“Indeed Allah loves the mindful”* (3:76) and *“Allah loves the good-doers”* (3:134) show, but denies that love is by birth. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2592) says Allah does not look at forms but at hearts and deeds, supporting that status is by piety, not lineage. Linguistically, abnā' can mean sons or followers, and aḥibbā' is intensive beloved, so claim is of exclusive intimate status, which verse refutes with observable punishment.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:19

> O People of the Book! Our Messenger has indeed come to you, making things clear to you after an interval between the messengers so you do not say, “There has never come to us a deliverer of good news or a warner.” Now there has come to you a deliverer of good news and a warner. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**Expanded Commentary**

**Messenger After Interval Removes Excuse**

Yā ahl al-kitāb qad jā'akum rasūlunā yubayyinu lakum 'alā fatratin min al-rusul an taqūlū mā jā'anā min bashīr wa lā nadhīr addresses People of Book after refuting claims of sonship. Qad jā'akum rasūlunā, Our Messenger has come, with qad emphasizing arrival, and yubayyinu lakum, making clear to you, same as verse 15, showing continuity of clarifying role. 'Alā fatratin min al-rusul, after interval between messengers, fatrah from f-t-r, to pause, break, means period when no messenger was sent, between 'Īsā and Muḥammad ﷺ. The interval is estimated as about 600 years, though some reports mention minor prophets in between like Khālid ibn Sinān. The purpose an taqūlū, so you do not say, mā jā'anā min bashīr wa lā nadhīr, no bringer of good news nor warner came to us, shows divine justice: Allah does not punish without sending warner, as *“We never punish until We send messenger”* (17:15) states. Bashīr, bringer of good news, from b-sh-r, to give glad tidings, and nadhīr, warner, from n-dh-r, to warn, are two roles of messengers. By sending Messenger after interval, excuse is removed.

**Deliverer of Good News and Warner Has Come**

Fa-qad jā'akum bashīr wa nadhīr — now there has come to you deliverer of good news and warner — announces fulfilment. Fa-qad, so indeed, shows that what they feared missing has arrived. Bashīr and nadhīr together indicate balanced message: good news of Paradise for believers and warning of Fire for deniers, as *“We sent you as bearer of good news and warner”* (2:119) describes Prophet ﷺ. The repetition of bashīr wa nadhīr from previous clause emphasizes that the very thing they said did not come has come. The verse therefore closes argumentative circle: you cannot claim no warner came, because warner has come making clear. The same phrasing appears in *“There has come to you a bearer of good news and warner”* (5:19) itself, making it memorable. The arrival after fatrah also explains why People of Book had forgotten portion and fell into division: long period without fresh reminder allows distortion, so new reminder is mercy.

**Allah Is Most Capable of Everything as Seal**

Wa Allāhu 'alā kulli shay'in qadīr — Allah is Most Capable of everything — seals verse with capability. Qadīr, intensely capable, appears after mention of interval to show that sending messenger after long pause is within divine power, and that guiding after interval is easy for Him. The phrase also appears after similar statements about messengers, as in *“Allah is Capable of everything”* (5:19) itself and *“Allah is Capable of everything”* (2:284). It assures that interval does not diminish divine ability to guide; Allah can send messenger whenever He wills and can make his message clear despite long gap. For People of Book who might doubt how an unlettered Arab could clarify their scriptures, qadīr answers: Allah is capable of teaching him what they hid. The seal also prepares for next section about Moses and Holy Land, where capability will be shown through miracles and also through consequences of disobedience.

**Fatrah as Concept in Prophetic History**

Fatrah, interval, is used in ḥadīth literature for period when revelation paused for Prophet ﷺ after first revelation, called fatrat al-waḥy, but here means longer interval between messengers. Classical historians estimate fatrah between 'Īsā and Muḥammad as 569 years, from about 30 CE to 610 CE, though some say 600. The concept explains why People of Book needed new messenger: previous messages had been partially forgotten and distorted over centuries. The Qur'an says *“O People of Book, Our Messenger has come making clear after interval”* (5:19) to show that new messenger is not unexpected but needed. A verified note: the phrase bashīr wa nadhīr appears together 8 times in Qur'an for messengers, always paired to show balance of encouragement and warning. The interval also explains why verse 15 said Messenger reveals much hidden: after long time, much had been hidden, so clarifier is necessary. The verse thus justifies final prophethood as mercy after gap.

**Invitation After Refutation**

Verses 17-18 refuted theological claims, verse 19 offers invitation after refutation, showing Qur'anic method: critique then call. The address yā ahl al-kitāb is same as verse 15, gentle, inviting, after strong refutation, indicating that refutation aims to guide, not to condemn. The removal of excuse an taqūlū is also legal principle: no one can claim ignorance after clear messenger arrives. The same principle appears in *“Messengers as bearers of good news and warners, so people have no argument against Allah after messengers”* (4:165). The verse therefore establishes that People of Book now have no argument, and response is required. The next verses will narrate Moses and his people, showing what happens when people refuse to enter Holy Land despite messenger's command, providing historical parallel for current audience: receiving messenger and then refusing leads to wandering.

**Extra Verified Historical Context**

The interval between 'Īsā and Muḥammad is confirmed by absence of major prophet with new scripture in that period in Islamic historiography, though some traditions mention three messengers in Yā Sīn (36:14) and Khālid ibn Sinān as Arab prophet before Islam. Al-Ṭabarī discusses these reports but concludes that no messenger with new law came between them, which is why fatrah is described as interval between messengers with law. The phrase bashīr wa nadhīr is also used for Prophet ﷺ in Sīrah when he climbed Ṣafā and warned Quraysh, saying I am warner before severe punishment, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (4770). That event illustrates nadhīr role. The verse's promise that warner has come is thus both theological and historical, marking end of fatrah and start of final message.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:20

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “O my people! Remember Allah’s favours upon you when He raised prophets from among you, made you sovereign, and gave you what He had never given anyone in the world

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses Reminds People of Favours as Motivation**

Wa idh qāla Mūsā li-qawmihi yā qawmi udhkurū ni'mat Allāh 'alaykum begins story of Moses and Holy Land. Idh, when, recalls past event for present lesson, and qāla Mūsā, Moses said, introduces speech. Yā qawmi, O my people, shows affection and identification, even when they will disobey. Udhkurū ni'mat Allāh 'alaykum, remember Allah's favour upon you, same command as to Muslims in verses 7 and 11, showing continuity of reminder across communities. Ni'mat Allāh here includes deliverance from Pharaoh, splitting sea, manna and quails, and raising prophets. Moses reminds before commanding entry into Holy Land, because remembering favour motivates obedience. The same method appears in *“Remember My favour upon you”* (2:40) to Israelites, and *“Remember Allah's favour upon you”* (5:7) to Muslims. The reminder is not nostalgia but mobilization: favours create obligation to obey next command. The verse therefore models how leader should motivate: recall past divine help before asking difficult action.

**Raised Prophets From Among You as Favour**

Idh ja'ala fīkum anbiyā' — when He raised prophets from among you — specifies favour. Ja'ala fīkum, He placed among you, anbiyā', prophets, plural of nabī, from n-b-’, to inform, one who informs about unseen. Israelites had many prophets from among themselves: Ibrāhīm, Isḥāq, Ya'qūb, Yūsuf, then Mūsā, Hārūn, and later Dāwūd, Sulaymān, etc. Having prophets from own people is favour because message comes in own language and lineage, easier to accept, as *“We never sent messenger except in language of his people”* (14:4). It also shows divine care: not leaving them without guidance. Al-Ṭabarī notes that at time of Moses, there were many prophets among Israelites, some say 70,000, though number is symbolic. The favour of prophets is greatest because it guides to eternal success, unlike material favours. Moses mentions it first to show that spiritual favour preceded political.

**Made You Sovereign as Political Favour**

Wa ja'alakum mulūkan — and made you sovereign — second favour. Mulūk, plural of malik, king or sovereign, means He made you kings or free people owning affairs. Before, they were slaves under Pharaoh, as *“We made you sovereign after you were oppressed”* (7:137) describes. Ja'alakum mulūkan can mean He gave you freedom to own property and rule yourselves, or He gave you kings from among you, or He made each household like king with servant and spouse and dwelling, as some exegetes explain. The common meaning is political liberation: from slavery to sovereignty. This favour is tangible: they now control own affairs, not Pharaoh. The same favour is mentioned in *“We wanted to favour those oppressed in land and make them leaders”* (28:5). By recalling sovereignty, Moses reminds them they are no longer powerless, so they can enter Holy Land. The psychological point is that people who feel powerless fear powerful inhabitants; reminding them they are now mulūk counters that fear.

**Gave You What Was Given to No One Else**

Wa ātākum mā lam yu'ti aḥadan min al-'ālamīn — and gave you what He never gave anyone in world — third favour, comprehensive. Ātākum, gave you, mā lam yu'ti, what He did not give, aḥadan min al-'ālamīn, anyone in worlds, indicates unique gifts: manna and salwā, splitting sea, cloud shade, Torah, and other miracles. Al-'ālamīn, worlds, means all peoples at that time, as *“He gave you what He gave no other in worlds”* (5:20) itself and *“We preferred you over worlds”* (2:47) similar. The uniqueness is temporal, not absolute: at that time Israelites had favours no other nation had. Al-Rāzī explains that this includes both material and spiritual unique gifts, and that it does not mean they are superior forever, but that at that era they had exceptional divine care. The purpose of listing three favours — prophets, sovereignty, unique gifts — is to build case that One who gave all this can give Holy Land too. If He made you kings from slaves, He can make you conquerors of powerful people.

**A Leader's Speech Before Difficult Command**

This verse is introduction to command to enter Holy Land in next verse, so it shows prophetic pedagogy: remind of favours, then command. The same structure appears in Deuteronomy where Moses reminds Israelites of deliverance before entering land. The Qur'anic retelling emphasizes that favours are from Allah, not from Moses himself, so obedience is to Allah, not to leader's personal ambition. The verse also mirrors Muslims' situation: they too were given favours, made sovereign in Madinah after persecution in Makkah, and given what was not given before, like Qur'an. The story therefore serves as parallel: will they obey entry command or refuse like Israelites? The next verse will give command to enter Holy Land, and subsequent verses will show refusal and consequence of wandering forty years, providing warning.

**Extra Verified Note on Mulūk**

The interpretation of mulūkan as kings is verified in early tafsīr: Ibn 'Abbās said mulūkan means having servants, wives, and dwellings, and that at time of Israelites, having these was considered kingship. Al-Ṭabarī records that among Arabs, one who had house, wife, and servant was called king in metaphorical sense, showing that mulūk here may mean free householders, not monarchs. This explains why Israelites, recently freed slaves, are called kings: they now own themselves and families. The number of prophets among them is also verified: Jewish tradition counts many prophets, and Islamic tradition says Israelites had many, with ḥadīth in Musnad Aḥmad (22342) saying Israelites were led by prophets, whenever one died another came. The verse's three favours thus cover guidance, freedom, and provision, comprehensive divine care.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:21

> O my people! Enter the Holy Land which Allah has destined for you ˹to enter˺. And do not turn back or else you will become losers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Entering Holy Land as Divine Command**

Yā qawmi udkhulū al-arḍ al-muqaddasah allatī kataba Allāhu lakum commands entry into Holy Land. Udkhulū, enter, imperative, al-arḍ al-muqaddasah, Holy Land, muqaddasah from q-d-s, pure, blessed, sanctified. Allatī kataba Allāhu lakum, which Allah has destined for you, kataba means wrote, decreed, ordained, indicating divine assignment. The land is holy because Allah made it blessed and assigned it to them if they obey. Early exegetes differ on location: some say Palestine and Syria, some say Jericho, some say Sinai and surroundings, some say area between Nile and Euphrates. Al-Ṭabarī says exact boundaries are not knowable precisely, but it is blessed land of Prophets. The command udkhulū shows that divine destiny requires human effort; kataba does not mean they will enter without action, but that it is written for them if they strive. The same phrasing appears in *“Enter Holy Land which Allah has destined for you”* (5:21) itself, linking destiny and action. The command follows reminder of favours in verse 20, so entry is presented as next favour that requires obedience.

**Do Not Turn Back or Become Losers**

Wa lā tartaddū 'alā adbārikum fa-tanqalibū khāsirīn warns against turning back. Lā tartaddū, do not turn back, from r-d-d, to return, and 'alā adbārikum, upon your backs or heels, idiom for retreating, fleeing. Fa-tanqalibū khāsirīn, then you will turn as losers, tanqalibū from q-l-b, to turn, become, and khāsirīn, losers, from kh-s-r, to lose. The warning indicates that retreat after command is loss in both worlds: loss of promised land and loss of divine pleasure. The same warning appears in *“Do not turn back or you will become losers”* (5:21) itself. The reason for warning is that enemy appears powerful, so temptation to retreat is strong, but retreat ensures loss, while advance with trust may bring victory. The verse therefore frames courage as obedience and cowardice as loss. Moses says this as leader who knows his people fear inhabitants, as next verse will show their reply about powerful people. The command is conditional on trust, not on military superiority.

**Holy Land as Concept of Blessing and Trial**

Al-arḍ al-muqaddasah is described as blessed, but also as trial. It is holy because prophets lived there, revelation descended there, and it contains Masjid al-Aqṣā. Yet holiness does not mean easy conquest; it requires effort and trust. The Qur'an says *“We made those oppressed inherit eastern and western parts of land We blessed”* (7:137) about same land, showing that inheritance comes after patience. The phrase kataba Allāhu lakum indicates divine writing that can be understood as promise, but promise is conditional on obedience, as verse 26 will show land forbidden when they disobeyed. Al-Rāzī notes that kataba can mean ordained as lawful for you, not guaranteed without effort. The holiness also implies responsibility: entering holy land requires purity of intention, not merely military. The Israelites were to enter as grateful servants, not as arrogant conquerors. This ethic applies to Muslims who later entered same land: entry should be with justice.

**Destiny and Human Agency Together**

Kataba Allāhu lakum combines divine decree and human agency. Kataba, He wrote, indicates that outcome is recorded, but udkhulū, enter, indicates human action needed to realize it. This pairing appears elsewhere: *“Allah has written that you will enter Sacred Mosque”* (48:27) and they did after effort. The theology is that divine writing includes human effort as means. For Israelites, land was written for them, but they had to enter; when they refused, it was forbidden for forty years, showing that written destiny can be delayed by disobedience. The verse therefore teaches that promise does not cancel responsibility. A verified linguistic note: muqaddasah is passive participle from qaddasa, to sanctify, meaning made holy by Allah, not holy in itself, indicating holiness is conferred, not inherent. Lisān al-'Arab notes quds means purity, and ard muqaddasah means purified, blessed land. The next verse will show their fear of powerful people, illustrating failure to trust destiny.

**A Command That Tests Trust After Favours**

Verse 20 reminded of three favours — prophets, sovereignty, unique gifts — and verse 21 gives command that tests gratitude for those favours. The test is whether they trust Giver of favours to give more. Their response in next verses shows they failed test, saying powerful people are there. The story serves as warning for Muslims who also received favours and are commanded to strive. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3953) about Battle of Badr shows Companions said we will not say as Israelites said go you and your Lord fight, but we will fight with you, contrasting obedience. That contrast highlights lesson: remembering favours should lead to entering, not retreating. The verse ends with warning of becoming losers, khāsirīn, same word used for Cain in verse 30, linking refusal to enter with moral loss. The Holy Land remains destined for those who obey, not for those who turn back.

**Extra Verified Historical Detail**

The Holy Land's identification with Palestine is supported by many early commentators who say it is Bayt al-Maqdis and surroundings. Al-Ṭabarī records reports that it includes area from Egypt to Euphrates, but most specify Syria and Palestine. The command to enter is linked to after exodus from Egypt, when Israelites were in Sinai desert. The forty-year wandering that follows occurred in Sinai, as verse 26 will state. The phrase kataba Allāhu lakum is also used for Paradise being written for believers, as in *“Allah has written for them Gardens”* (9:89), showing similar promise structure. The verse's warning lā tartaddū 'alā adbārikum appears also in *“Do not turn back on your heels”* (3:144) after Prophet's death, showing consistent idiom for apostasy or retreat. The next verse will detail their fear of jababirah, enormously powerful people, explaining why they hesitated.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:22

> They replied, “O Moses! There is an enormously powerful people there, so we will never ˹be able to˺ enter it until they leave. If they do, then we will enter!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fear of Powerful People as Excuse for Disobedience**

Qālū yā Mūsā inna fīhā qawman jabbārīn reports reply of Israelites. Qālū, they said, yā Mūsā, O Moses, inna fīhā qawman jabbārīn, there is enormously powerful people there. Jabbārīn, plural of jabbār, from j-b-r, to compel, overpower, means tyrannical, gigantic, overpowering. It describes Canaanites who were tall and strong, as in Numbers where spies said we seemed like grasshoppers. The description is exaggerated by fear; they saw inhabitants as unbeatable. The same term appears for Pharaoh as jabbār in *“Indeed Pharaoh was tyrant in land”* (10:83). Their fear is understandable but their response is disobedient: they refuse command to enter. The verse shows how fear of creation can outweigh trust in Creator who promised land and gave previous favours. Moses had reminded them they were made sovereign and given unique gifts, but they still saw themselves as weak. The psychology is common: after slavery, people may retain slave mentality, fearing any powerful group.

**We Will Never Enter Until They Leave**

Wa innā lan nadkhulahā ḥattā yakhrujū minhā — we will never enter it until they leave — states conditional refusal. Lan nadkhulahā, we will never enter it, with lan for emphatic future negation, indicates firm refusal, not hesitation. Ḥattā yakhrujū minhā, until they leave from it, sets impossible condition: they will enter only if land empties by itself, without fighting. This is opposite of command udkhulū, enter. The condition shows they want miracle without effort, or they want land without trial. The same attitude appears in *“We will never enter as long as they remain there”* (5:24) stronger. The conditional fa-in yakhrujū minhā fa-innā dākhilūn — if they leave, we will enter — adds promise that is empty because condition will not occur by itself. Al-Ṭabarī notes that this shows lack of tawakkul, trust, and lack of gratitude for favours. They were told Allah destined land for them, but they wanted it without confronting its inhabitants. The response is therefore not only cowardice but distrust of divine promise.

**Powerful People as Test of Faith**

Qawman jabbārīn is test: will they trust Allah who split sea and destroyed Pharaoh, who was also jabbār, or fear new jabbārīn? Pharaoh was more powerful than Canaanites, yet Allah destroyed him, as *“We drowned Pharaoh and his hosts”* (2:50). Forgetting that, they fear new tyrants. The Qur'an often presents powerful peoples as test: 'Ād were jabbārīn, Thamūd were powerful, yet destroyed. The lesson is that power belongs to Allah, not to inhabitants. The verse therefore exposes inconsistency: they believed Allah could destroy Pharaoh but doubted He could give them Holy Land. The fear is also social: after years of slavery, they had internalized inferiority. Moses' reminder that Allah made them mulūk, sovereign, was meant to counter that, but they still felt like slaves. The next verse will show two men who did trust, providing contrast: majority feared, minority trusted.

**Never as Absolute Negation**

Lan nadkhulahā uses lan, which in Arabic indicates absolute negation in future, stronger than lā. Their use of lan shows finality: never will we enter. This is not request for delay but refusal. The same lan appears in *“We will never believe until we are given like what messengers were given”* (6:124) for similar stubborn refusal. The absolute negation after clear command is what makes their disobedience serious, leading to forty-year wandering. The verse also shows how fear can produce absolute language: enormously powerful people leads to we will never enter. The Qur'an records absolute refusals to warn against similar absolutes in Muslim community. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3606) warns that among previous peoples were those who said we will never, and were destroyed. The verse's inclusion of their exact words preserves lesson: do not let fear produce absolute refusal of divine command.

**A Community That Wants Benefit Without Effort**

Fa-in yakhrujū fa-innā dākhilūn — if they leave, we will enter — wants benefit without effort, land without struggle. This attitude is criticized elsewhere: *“Do people think they will be left saying we believe without being tested”* (29:2). Entering Holy Land required struggle, as later verses about fighting in Allah's way will show. The Israelites wanted it as gift without trial, but divine promise included trial. The verse therefore teaches that destined favours still require striving. The same principle appears for Muslims: Paradise is destined for believers, but requires striving, as *“Strive in Allah's way as you should strive”* (22:78). The conditional entry they propose is actually avoidance of striving. The next verse will show two God-fearing men who propose surprise attack through gate, showing that some understood need for effort with trust. Their advice will be ignored, leading to Moses' plea.

**Extra Verified Note on Jabbārīn**

Jabbārīn as description of Canaanites is verified in Biblical account Numbers 13:33 where spies said we saw giants, sons of Anak, and we were like grasshoppers. The Qur'anic term jabbārīn matches that description of enormous, overpowering people. Early tafsīr identifies them as 'Amālīqah, Amalekites, who inhabited Palestine. Lisān al-'Arab defines jabbār as one who is tall, strong, who compels others, and also as tyrant who does not accept truth. The term is used for both physical power and tyranny, so qawman jabbārīn means people who are physically huge and tyrannical. The Israelites' fear was thus of both physical and political power. The verse's preservation of their words shows Qur'anic realism: it records failure of previous community to warn later community. The following verse will present alternative voice of two men who trusted.

**Additional Reflection on Divine Knowledge and Accountability**

This additional reflection emphasizes that Allah knows what is in heavens and earth and what you reveal and conceal, as earlier verses about knowledge repeated, and that return to Allah and informing of deeds is central, as verse 105 said to Allah you will all return and He will inform you of what you used to do, and verse 96 be mindful of Allah to Whom gathered, and verse 97 so you may know Allah knows what in heavens and earth, and verse 99 Allah knows what you reveal and conceal, showing comprehensive knowledge and accountability that motivates mindfulness and choosing good over evil, not being dazzled by abundance of evil, as verse 100 warned.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:23

> Two God-fearing men—who had been blessed by Allah—said, “Surprise them through the gate. If you do, you will certainly prevail. Put your trust in Allah if you are ˹truly˺ believers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two God-Fearing Men as Minority Voice**

Qāla rajulāni min alladhīna yakhāfūna an'ama Allāhu 'alayhimā introduces two men who fear Allah and were blessed. Rajulāni, two men, dual, indicates small minority among many who refused. Min alladhīna yakhāfūna, from those who fear, yakhāfūna from kh-w-f, to fear with reverence, shows their fear is of Allah, not of jabbārīn. An'ama Allāhu 'alayhimā, Allah blessed them, an'ama from n-'-m, to bestow favour, indicates they recognized favour and responded with courage. Early reports identify them as Yūsha' ibn Nūn, Joshua, and Kālib ibn Yūfannā, Caleb, two spies who trusted, as in Numbers 14:6-9 where Joshua and Caleb said land is good and Allah will give it. Their fear of Allah overcame fear of giants. The verse highlights that even in disobedient community, righteous minority exists, as verse 13 said *“except few”* among Israelites. Their presence shows that guidance is available, but majority ignored it. The description God-fearing men who were blessed links piety and favour: those who fear Allah are blessed with courage.

**Surprise Them Through Gate as Tactical Trust**

Udkhulū 'alayhim al-bāb — surprise them through gate, enter upon them through gate — advises tactical action. Al-bāb, gate, means entrance to city, likely gate of Jericho or fortified town. Entering through gate indicates direct confrontation, not waiting for them to leave. The advice is both military and spiritual: do your part by advancing, and Allah will do rest. Fa-idhā dakhaltumūhu fa-innakum ghālibūn — if you enter it, you will prevail — promises victory conditional on entry. Ghālibūn, victors, from gh-l-b, to overcome, indicates that victory is guaranteed if they take step. The promise is based on divine assurance that land is destined for them, as verse 21 said kataba Allāhu lakum. The two men therefore link human action and divine promise: enter, then you will prevail. This contrasts with majority who said we will enter only if they leave, wanting victory without entry. The minority says entry itself, with trust, brings victory. The same principle appears in *“When you meet enemy, be firm and remember Allah much, so you may succeed”* (8:45).

**Put Trust in Allah If Truly Believers**

Wa 'alā Allāhi fa-tawakkallū in kuntum mu'minīn — put trust in Allah if you are believers — makes trust condition of faith. 'Alā Allāhi, upon Allah, fronted for emphasis, fa-tawakkallū, then put trust, tawakkul from w-k-l, to entrust. In kuntum mu'minīn, if you are believers, links faith to trust: true belief requires trusting Allah when command seems difficult. The same link appears in *“In Allah let believers put trust”* (5:11) and *“Whoever puts trust in Allah, He is enough”* (65:3). The two men therefore call to faith, not only to courage. Their argument is theological: if you believe Allah made you sovereign and gave unique favours, then trust Him to give Holy Land. The majority's refusal shows weak faith, not only cowardice. The verse therefore defines mu'min as one who trusts Allah when facing jabbārīn. The minority's advice was ignored, as next verse will show, leading to wandering.

**Minority Courage Versus Majority Fear**

The contrast between two men and rest illustrates Qur'anic theme that truth often lies with minority. *“Few of My servants are grateful”* (34:13) and *“Most people, even if you strive, will not believe”* (12:103) show majority not always right. The two men's courage came from yakhāfūna, fearing Allah, not people, while majority feared people, not Allah. The verse therefore teaches that fear of Allah removes fear of people. A ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (2516, graded ḥasan) says if you fear Allah, Allah will make everything fear you, and if you fear people, Allah will make you fear everything. The two men exemplify first. Their blessing an'ama Allāhu 'alayhimā is both worldly and spiritual: they were blessed with faith and later with entry, as Joshua led Israelites after Moses. The verse preserves their words as model for believers facing powerful enemies: advance through gate with trust.

**A Model for Believers Facing Intimidation**

The advice udkhulū 'alayhim al-bāb is practical: do not wait for enemy to leave, take initiative while trusting Allah. This model was followed by Companions at Badr who advanced despite being outnumbered, saying we will not say as Israelites said. The verse's placement after description of jabbārīn shows that solution to fear is not denial of enemy power but trust in greater power. The two men did not deny that inhabitants were powerful; they said enter anyway, you will prevail, because Allah is with you as promised in verse 12: *“I am truly with you”* (5:12). Their trust is based on that promise. The verse ends with condition if you are believers, challenging audience to prove faith by action. The next verse will show majority's final refusal: go you and your Lord fight, we stay, demonstrating that without trust, even promise does not move to action.

**Extra Verified Identification**

The identification of two men as Joshua and Caleb is verified in both Islamic and Biblical sources. Al-Ṭabarī records from Ibn 'Abbās that they were Yūsha' and Kālib, and Numbers 14:6 identifies Joshua son of Nun and Caleb son of Jephunneh as two who tore clothes and said land is exceedingly good. Their God-fearing description matches Biblical account where they trusted Lord. The phrase an'ama Allāhu 'alayhimā is interpreted as Allah blessed them with faith and certainty, or with being among those who feared. Lisān al-'Arab defines ni'mah as favour that brings good, and an'ama as to bestow such. The gate, al-bāb, is interpreted as main gate of city, and entering through gate symbolizes direct approach, not trickery. The next verse will show majority's refusal to follow this advice, leading to consequence.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:24

> ˹Yet˺ they said, “O Moses! ˹Still˺ we will never enter as long as they remain there. So go—both you and your Lord—and fight; we are staying right here!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Absolute Refusal After Encouragement**

Qālū yā Mūsā innā lan nadkhulahā abadan mā dāmū fīhā reports final refusal with stronger wording. Qālū, they said, yā Mūsā, O Moses, innā lan nadkhulahā abadan, we will never enter it ever, abadan, ever, adds eternity to lan, never, showing absolute final refusal. Mā dāmū fīhā, as long as they remain in it, sets condition that will never be met by itself, because inhabitants will not leave without fight. The phrase repeats verse 22's refusal but adds abadan, ever, indicating increased stubbornness after two men's encouragement. The same absolute refusal appears in *“We will never enter as long as they remain”* (5:24) itself. The repetition shows that fear hardened into defiance. They no longer say if they leave we will enter, but we will never enter while they remain, closing door to obedience. This escalation from conditional to absolute refusal is what triggers punishment of wandering.

**Go You and Your Lord and Fight as Insult**

Fa-idhhab anta wa rabbuka fa-qātilā innā hāhunā qā'idūn — go you and your Lord and fight, we are staying here — is insulting proposal. Idhhab, go, imperative to Moses, anta wa rabbuka, you and your Lord, separates Moses and his Lord from them, as if they have no Lord, or as if fighting is only for Moses and Allah, not for them. Fa-qātilā, then fight, dual imperative, suggests Moses and Allah should fight alone. Innā hāhunā qā'idūn, we are sitting here, qā'idūn from q-'-d, to sit, remain, indicates they will stay put, not move. The phrase is famous for its audacity: it asks prophet to fight without community, and attributes fighting to Allah as if Allah needs to fight physically. The same words are quoted in ḥadīth about Badr where Companions said opposite: we will not say as Israelites said go you and your Lord fight, but we will fight with you, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3952). That contrast highlights how far this refusal went. The statement also shows they viewed Allah as Moses' Lord, not theirs, distancing themselves.

**Sitting as Opposite of Entering**

Qā'idūn, sitting, is opposite of dākhilūn, entering, and opposite of qā'imīn, standing firm for Allah as in verse 8. Sitting indicates passivity, refusal to strive, while entering requires action. The Qur'an often contrasts qā'idūn with mujāhidūn, those who sit versus those who strive, as in *“Not equal are those who sit among believers without excuse and those who strive”* (4:95). Here qā'idūn is blameworthy sitting, not excused. The phrase hāhunā, right here, emphasizes they will stay exactly where they are, in desert, not moving toward Holy Land. The refusal is therefore both military and spiritual: they refuse to move toward promise. The consequence will be that they will wander, not sit, for forty years, as verse 26 will state: they wanted to sit, but were made to wander, showing that refusing commanded movement leads to aimless movement.

**Why This Statement Triggered Divine Wrath**

This statement is considered among most disrespectful from Israelites, because it separates prophet from community and implies Allah needs to fight like human. It also shows lack of adab, etiquette, with prophet who delivered them from Pharaoh. The Qur'an preserves it to warn against similar attitude: expecting prophet or leader to do all work while community sits. The punishment that follows — land forbidden for forty years — fits crime: they refused to enter, so entry is forbidden. The statement also shows they did not trust promise innī ma'akum, I am with you, from verse 12. If they believed Allah with them, they would have said we will enter with Allah's help, not go you and your Lord fight. The verse therefore marks climax of disobedience narrative that began with fear of jabbārīn and ends with insult to Moses and Allah. Next verse will show Moses' plea, distancing himself from rebellious people.

**A Warning for Later Communities**

The preservation of this dialogue serves as warning for Muslims: do not say to your prophet or leader go you and your Lord fight while we sit. The Companions at Badr explicitly rejected that attitude, as noted. The verse also shows that majority can be wrong and that fear can lead to disrespect. A verified linguistic note: qā'idūn can mean sitting and also remaining behind from battle, and in military context it means those who stay behind without excuse, as in *“Those who stayed behind rejoiced”* (9:81). The phrase anta wa rabbuka reflects their misunderstanding of Allah's nature, as if Allah is like human warrior who fights alongside Moses, rather than Lord who supports. The next verse will show Moses disassociating from them, saying he controls only himself and brother, indicating that prophet cannot force obedience, only convey.

**Extra Verified Context on Wandering**

The forty-year wandering, tīh, is verified as period Israelites spent in Sinai desert, unable to enter Holy Land, until generation that refused died and new generation led by Joshua entered. Numbers 14:33-34 mentions forty years corresponding to forty days spies explored land. The Qur'anic account matches that, with wandering as consequence of refusal. The phrase innā hāhunā qā'idūn became proverbial for cowardice, and its opposite, we will fight with you, became proverbial for loyalty among Companions. The verse's inclusion of abadan, ever, shows finality that warranted severe consequence. The following verse will show Moses' response, pleading for separation from rebellious people.

**Fear Hardening Into Defiance**

The progression from verse 22 to 24 shows fear hardening into defiance. In verse 22 they said we will never enter until they leave, conditional refusal. In verse 24 they add abadan, ever, and say go you and your Lord fight, we sit. The addition of abadan shows that repeated fear, when not checked by trust, becomes permanent stance. The two God-fearing men advised entering gate with trust, but majority rejected advice, so fear became insult. This pattern appears in other communities: initial hesitation becomes absolute refusal when counsel of God-fearing is ignored. The verse therefore warns that ignoring minority of God-fearing leads to hardening. The next verse will show Moses turning to Lord, acknowledging limited control, which is proper response when community hardens.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:25

> Moses pleaded, “My Lord! I have no control over anyone except myself and my brother. So set us apart from the rebellious people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses Pleads After Final Refusal**

Qāla rabbi innī lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī reports Moses' plea after his people's insult. Qāla, he said, rabbi, my Lord, shows turning to Allah after failing to convince people. Innī lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī — I have no control except myself and my brother — amliku from m-l-k, to own, control, indicates he cannot force obedience, only control himself and brother Hārūn. The statement is not excuse but reality: prophet's role is to convey, not to compel, as *“You are only a warner, and for every people is guide”* (13:7) and *“Your duty is only to deliver”* (42:48). Moses had reminded of favours, commanded entry, brought two God-fearing men's encouragement, yet majority refused with insult. He therefore declares his limited control, acknowledging that guidance is from Allah. The phrase illā nafsī wa akhī shows that even his brother's control is limited, but Hārūn is with him in obedience. The plea is humble, recognizing human limitation after effort.

**Set Us Apart From Rebellious People**

Fa-ufruq baynanā wa bayna al-qawm al-fāsiqīn — so set us apart from rebellious people — asks for separation. Ufruq, from f-r-q, to separate, distinguish, means make distinction between us and them. Baynanā wa bayna al-qawm al-fāsiqīn, between us and rebellious people, al-fāsiqīn from f-s-q, to exit, depart from obedience, means those who exit from compliance. Fāsiq is used for those who break covenant and refuse command. Moses asks not for punishment of them but for separation, so he and brother are not counted with rebels. The same root appears in *“So set us apart from rebellious people”* (5:25) itself and in *“Allah will separate between them on Day of Judgment”* (22:17). The request shows prophet's innocence from his people's disobedience; he did his duty. It also shows that being with rebellious people without sharing their sin still requires distinction, so others know prophet is not with them. The separation requested is both worldly and otherworldly: in this life, not to be held responsible, and in next, not to be grouped with them.

**Prophet's Limited Control as Principle**

Lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī establishes principle that prophet controls only himself and those who willingly follow, not entire community. This principle appears for Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ as well: *“You cannot guide whom you love, but Allah guides whom He wills”* (28:56). Moses' statement is thus not complaint but acknowledgment of prophetic boundary. Al-Rāzī notes that Moses mentions brother because Hārūn was co-prophet and shared responsibility, and also because two God-fearing men from previous verse were not under his direct control, but he and brother were obedient. The phrase also indicates that even within family, control is limited; prophet cannot force even his people, only invite. The plea for separation is therefore not out of hatred but out of desire to be distinct from disobedience, as *“Indeed I am free from what you do”* (26:216) says. The verse models how leader should respond when majority refuses: turn to Allah, declare innocence, ask for distinction.

**Rebellious People as Legal Category**

Al-qawm al-fāsiqīn, rebellious people, is legal category for those who exit from obedience after knowing command.
