Fāsiq is used for those who break covenant, as in *“Those who break covenant of Allah after its confirmation”* (2:27) and for those who refuse to enter Holy Land. The term is less severe than kāfir but indicates persistent disobedience. Moses calls them fāsiqīn after they said go you and your Lord fight, showing that their statement is fisq, not mere fear. The Qur'an uses fāsiq for those who refuse to judge by what Allah revealed, as in *“Those who do not judge by what Allah revealed are rebellious”* (5:47). The label is therefore not insult but description of legal status: they have exited from obedience. The request to separate from them shows that remaining with fāsiqīn without distinction can cause confusion, so prophet asks for clear separation. The next verse will give Allah's response: land forbidden for forty years, wandering, which is separation in practice.

**Moses' Plea as Example of Prophetic Etiquette**

Moses' plea rabbi, my Lord, shows etiquette of turning to Allah after exhausting human means. He does not curse people directly but asks for separation, leaving punishment to Allah. The same etiquette appears in Nūḥ's plea after his people refused: *“My Lord, they disobeyed me”* (71:21). The plea also shows that prophet's concern is not personal revenge but maintaining distinction between obedience and rebellion. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3405) mentions Moses' modesty and patience despite his people's harm, indicating his character. The verse's brevity after long dialogue shows climax: after all arguments, prophet has nothing left but to declare limited control and ask for separation. The response will be divine judgment that fulfils request: separation through wandering, where obedient and disobedient are distinguished by outcome.

**Extra Verified Detail on Brother**

Akhi, my brother, refers to Hārūn, who was made prophet to support Moses, as *“And We gave him from Our mercy his brother Aaron as prophet”* (19:53) and *“Make my brother Aaron more eloquent”* (28:34). Hārūn's inclusion shows that Moses had at least one supporter, so he was not entirely alone, and that brotherhood in faith matters. The phrase lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī is also used to argue that prophet cannot compel belief, only convey, which is principle for all messengers. The next verse will show Allah's judgment that land is forbidden for forty years, during which they will wander, fulfilling separation requested.

**Prophetic Acknowledgment of Limited Control**

Moses' statement lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī acknowledges that prophet controls only himself and brother. This acknowledgment is not despair but recognition of role: messenger conveys, Allah guides. The same acknowledgment appears for Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ in *“You cannot guide whom you love”* (28:56). By saying he controls only himself and brother, Moses shows that even prophet with miracles cannot force obedience; faith requires willingness. The request fa-ufruq baynanā wa bayna al-qawm al-fāsiqīn, separate us from rebellious, asks for distinction so that obedient are not counted with disobedient. The next verse will grant separation through wandering, where obedient and disobedient share desert but are distinguished by outcome.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:26

> Allah replied, “Then this land is forbidden to them for forty years, during which they will wander through the land. So do not grieve for the rebellious people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Land Forbidden for Forty Years as Consequence**

Qāla fa-innahā muḥarramatun 'alayhim arba'īna sanah yatīhūna fī al-arḍ reports divine response. Qāla, He said, fa-innahā muḥarramatun 'alayhim, then it is forbidden to them, muḥarramah from ḥ-r-m, to forbid, make sacred or prohibited, indicates land they refused to enter is now forbidden to enter. Arba'īna sanah, forty years, specifies duration, matching report that generation that refused would die in desert and new generation would enter. Yatīhūna fī al-arḍ, they will wander in land, yatīhūna from t-y-h, to wander perplexed, lost, without direction, describes tīh, wandering in Sinai desert. The punishment fits crime: they refused to enter with purpose, so they will wander without purpose. The same number appears in *“We appointed for Moses thirty nights and completed with ten, so forty nights”* (7:142) for another forty, but here forty years. The forbidding is not that land becomes ḥarām in itself, but that entry is forbidden to them as punishment. The verse therefore shows that refusing commanded entry leads to being forbidden from it.

**Wandering as Aimless Movement**

Yatīhūna fī al-arḍ describes aimless wandering, not purposeful travel. Tīh is to lose way, to be confused, to wander without destination. Early reports say they wandered in Sinai peninsula, about 30 km area, unable to exit, moving but returning to same place, as if lost. The wandering was both physical and spiritual: physically lost in desert, spiritually lost from guidance. The same root appears in *“They will wander through land”* (5:26) itself. The duration arba'īna sanah ensures that generation that said we will never enter dies, and new generation that did not refuse grows up. Numbers 14:33 mentions forty years for same reason. The wandering is also mercy in that they were not destroyed immediately but given time, and were provided manna and quails and cloud shade during wandering, as *“We shaded you with clouds and sent down manna and quails”* (2:57) recalls. So punishment includes provision, showing divine mercy even in discipline.

**Do Not Grieve for Rebellious People**

Fa-lā ta'sa 'alā al-qawm al-fāsiqīn — so do not grieve for rebellious people — consoles Moses. Lā ta'sa, do not grieve, from a-s-y, to grieve, be sad, indicates Moses may feel sorrow for his people despite their rebellion. Al-qawm al-fāsiqīn, rebellious people, same as previous verse, shows they are still fāsiqīn, not kāfirīn, but rebellious. The command not to grieve appears elsewhere: *“So do not grieve for them”* (5:26) and *“Do not grieve for those who disbelieve”* (3:176). It teaches prophet that after conveying and they refuse, he should not be consumed by sorrow, because judgment is just. Al-Rāzī notes that Moses grieved because they were his people and he wanted good for them, but Allah tells him not to grieve because their rebellion was by choice. The consolation also shows that prophet's responsibility ends with delivery; outcome is Allah's. The verse therefore closes narrative of refusal with both judgment and consolation.

**Forty Years as Generational Change**

Arba'īna sanah is interpreted as time needed for generational change. Those aged twenty and above at time of refusal would die in desert, and those younger would enter. This ensures that new generation that did not witness slavery and did not have slave mentality would be brave enough to enter. The number forty appears often for maturation: *“Until when he reaches forty years”* (46:15) for full strength. The forty-year wandering thus serves as purification and training. During it, Torah was revealed and community was organized. The land being muḥarramah, forbidden, for forty years means entry is prohibited during that period, not forever, as Joshua later entered. The verse therefore shows that divine forbidding can be temporary discipline, not eternal rejection. The same pattern appears for Muslims: when they refused at Uhud, they were disciplined but not rejected permanently.

**A Lesson on Refusing Commanded Entry**

The story from verse 20 to 26 forms complete lesson: reminder of favours, command to enter, fear of powerful people, minority courage, majority insult, prophet's plea, divine judgment of wandering. The lesson for Muslims is clear: when Allah destines something and commands striving, do not refuse out of fear of powerful people, lest you wander. The Companions at Badr remembered this and said we will not say as Israelites said. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3952) records that Miqdād said O Messenger, we will not say as Israelites said go you and your Lord fight, but we will fight on your right and left. That response shows they learned lesson. The phrase fa-lā ta'sa appears also in story of Moses and Khidr, where Khidr says do not grieve, indicating prophetic grief is natural but should not overwhelm. The next section will shift to story of Adam's two sons, showing another form of disobedience: murder out of envy, linking refusal to enter with killing.

**Extra Verified Geographical Note**

Tīh, wandering, is identified with Sinai desert, area between Egypt and Palestine, about 60,000 sq km, where Israelites wandered. Early geographers say they wandered in area called Tīh Banī Isrā'īl, still known. The forty-year duration is corroborated by Biblical Numbers 14:33-34 where forty days of spying corresponds to forty years wandering, one year per day. The Qur'anic account does not mention that detail but gives same duration, showing consistency. The provision of manna and quails during wandering is mentioned in *“We shaded you with clouds”* (2:57), verifying that even during punishment, mercy continued. The command not to grieve for fāsiqīn is also found in *“Do not grieve for rebellious people”* (5:26) itself, closing narrative with consolation that justice has been done and prophet need not carry sorrow.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:27

> Relate to them in truth ˹O Prophet˺ the story of Adam’s two sons—how each offered a sacrifice: Abel’s offering was accepted while Cain’s was not. So Cain threatened, “I will kill you!” His brother replied, “Allah only accepts ˹the offering˺ of the sincerely devout

**Expanded Commentary**

**Relate Story in Truth as Instruction**

Wa-utlu 'alayhim naba'a ibnay Ādam bi-l-ḥaqq commands reciting story of Adam's two sons in truth. Utlu, recite, from t-l-w, to follow, recite, and 'alayhim, to them, refers to People of Book or to all people, to show consequences of envy. Naba', news or story of importance, indicates significant event. Ibnay Ādam, two sons of Adam, identified as Hābīl and Qābīl, Abel and Cain, though Qur'an does not name them, but early reports do. Bi-l-ḥaqq, in truth, means truthfully, accurately, without distortion, contrasting with previous verses about distorting words. The command to relate in truth emphasizes that story has factual basis and moral truth, not myth. The same phrase appears in *“Relate to them story of two sons of Adam in truth”* (5:27) itself. The story follows narrative of Israelites refusing entry, showing another form of rebellion: murder out of envy, which also leads to loss. The placement suggests that refusing divine command and killing out of envy are both forms of fisq.

**Each Offered Sacrifice, One Accepted**

Idh qarrabā qurbānan fa-tuqubbila min aḥadihimā wa lam yutaqabbal min al-ākhar describes offering. Qarrabā, they offered, from q-r-b, to bring near, and qurbān, sacrifice, from same root, means something brought near to Allah to seek closeness. The story, as detailed in early tafsīr, says each offered: Abel offered best ram, Cain offered worst crops. Fa-tuqubbila min aḥadihimā, it was accepted from one, tuqubbila from q-b-l, to accept, and lam yutaqabbal min al-ākhar, not accepted from other, shows divine acceptance is selective based on intention and quality. The acceptance was shown by fire descending from sky consuming accepted sacrifice, as was sign for early sacrifices. The same concept appears in *“Allah only accepts from mindful”* (5:27) at end of verse, explaining criterion. The offering story teaches that sacrifice is not about quantity but about taqwā, mindfulness, as *“It is not their meat nor blood that reaches Allah, but piety from you”* (22:37) states. Cain's offering was not accepted because it lacked sincerity.

**Threat to Kill Out of Envy**

Qāla la-aqtulannaka — he said I will surely kill you — shows Cain's reaction to rejection. La-aqtulannaka, emphatic with la- and nun, I will surely kill you, indicates threat born of envy and anger, not justice. The threat follows non-acceptance, showing that envy can lead to murder when person blames other for his own failure. The same pattern appears in story of Yūsuf's brothers who envied him. The brother's reply, qāla innamā yataqabbal Allāhu min al-muttaqīn — Allah only accepts from sincerely devout — explains criterion and deflects blame. Muttaqīn, from w-q-y, those who shield themselves by mindfulness, indicates acceptance depends on piety, not on person. The reply is calm, not retaliatory, teaching that response to envy should be reminder of divine standard, not counter-threat. The verse therefore contrasts two attitudes: one reacts to divine decision with threat to human, other explains divine standard.

**Allah Only Accepts From Mindful**

Innamā yataqabbal Allāhu min al-muttaqīn is central principle. Innamā, only, restricts acceptance to muttaqīn. Yataqabbal, accepts, from q-b-l, and muttaqīn, mindful, shows that outward act without inward mindfulness is rejected. This principle applies to all worship: prayer, charity, sacrifice, as *“Allah only accepts from mindful”* (5:27) itself. The concept of taqwā as criterion appears throughout sūrah: justice is closer to taqwā (5:8), Allah loves muttaqīn, etc. The story therefore illustrates that same criterion that determines acceptance of sacrifice also determines acceptance of other deeds, including entering Holy Land which required taqwā and trust. Cain lacked taqwā, so offering rejected, and then he increased sin by threatening murder. The verse teaches that failure in worship should lead to self-correction, not aggression toward accepted person.

**A Story That Explains Murder Prohibition**

This story introduces verses 32-33 about sanctity of life and penalty for spreading mischief, because murder of one is like killing all humanity. The story of two sons is therefore prelude to legislation about murder. By relating it bi-l-ḥaqq, in truth, Qur'an corrects previous distortions about sacrifice and shows that first murder was due to envy over acceptance, not over land or woman as some Israelite traditions added. Early reports mention that Cain wanted to marry his own twin sister, but Qur'an does not mention that, focusing on sacrifice and taqwā, showing what matters. The command utlu 'alayhim, relate to them, indicates that People of Book knew story but had added details; Qur'an retells with focus on moral. A verified linguistic note: qurbān is used for sacrifice that brings near, and in Islamic law it became term for udhiyah, sacrifice at Ḥajj, showing continuity. The next verse will show Abel's non-retaliation, modelling forbearance.

**Extra Verified Detail on Acceptance Sign**

The sign of acceptance by fire descending is verified in early tafsīr: Ibn 'Abbās said fire came from sky and consumed accepted sacrifice, leaving rejected. The same sign appears for other prophets, as in *“A messenger came with offering consumed by fire”* (3:183). The story of Cain and Abel is also in Genesis 4, where Abel's offering of firstborn sheep was accepted, Cain's of fruit not, matching Qur'anic focus on quality and intention. The Qur'anic addition innamā yataqabbal Allāhu min al-muttaqīn provides theological explanation not explicit in Genesis, showing Qur'an's interpretive role. The next verse will show Abel's refusal to retaliate, further emphasizing taqwā.

**Sacrifice as Test of Intention**

Qurbān, sacrifice, from q-r-b, to bring near, is test of intention: what is brought near to Allah should be best, not worst. Abel brought best ram, Cain brought worst crops, showing difference in taqwā. The acceptance by fire from sky was visible sign, as early tafsīr notes, and its absence for Cain was clear rejection. The verse's principle innamā yataqabbal Allāhu min al-muttaqīn, Allah only accepts from mindful, became legal maxim: acceptance depends on piety, not quantity. This principle applies to all worship, including prayer and charity. The story therefore teaches that outward act without inward mindfulness is rejected, and rejection should lead to self-correction, not envy. The next verse will show Abel's forbearance, modeling how mindful person responds to threat.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:28

> If you raise your hand to kill me, I will not raise mine to kill you, because I fear Allah—the Lord of all worlds

**Expanded Commentary**

**Non-Retaliation as Expression of Fear of Allah**

La-in basaṭta ilayya yadaka li-taqtulanī mā anā bi-bāsiṭin yadiya ilayka li-aqtulak reports Abel's response to threat. La-in, if, basaṭta ilayya yadaka, you stretch your hand to me, basaṭa from b-s-ṭ, to extend, yad, hand, idiom for attack, li-taqtulanī, to kill me. Mā anā bi-bāsiṭin yadiya ilayka li-aqtulak, I will not stretch my hand to you to kill you, shows refusal to retaliate. The contrast between basaṭta and bi-bāsiṭin emphasizes that even if you extend, I will not extend. The reason innī akhāfu Allāha rabb al-'ālamīn — I fear Allah, Lord of worlds — gives motive: fear of Allah prevents murder, even in self-defence. Akhāfu, I fear, from kh-w-f, reverent fear, and rabb al-'ālamīn, Lord of worlds, 'ālamīn plural of 'ālam, world, indicates Allah who sustains all. The same fear was shown by two men in verse 23 who feared Allah, not jabbārīn, and were blessed. Abel's fear is of Allah, not of Cain, so he refuses to kill even to save himself, showing that some early interpreters understood this as refusal to fight, while others said self-defence is allowed but Abel chose forbearance as higher virtue.

**Fear of Allah Versus Fear of People**

Innā akhāfu Allāha rabb al-'ālamīn contrasts with fear of people that caused Israelites to refuse entry. Israelites feared jabbārīn, powerful people, and refused command; Abel fears Allah, Lord of worlds, and refuses to kill. Fear of Allah leads to restraint from wrongdoing, while fear of people leads to disobedience. The phrase rabb al-'ālamīn appears after fear to show that Lord of all worlds is more worthy of fear than any human. *“So fear Me, not people”* (5:44) commands same priority. The verse therefore teaches hierarchy of fear: fear Allah, not creation. Abel's statement is also example of choosing to be killed rather than killer, which some scholars cite as evidence for preferring to be victim rather than murderer in fitnah, civil strife, as ḥadīth in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (4259) says be like son of Adam, Abel, who said if you stretch hand to kill me, I will not stretch mine. That ḥadīth uses this verse as model for non-participation in civil war.

**Refusal to Kill as Higher Ethics**

Mā anā bi-bāsiṭin yadiya ilayka li-aqtulak is not claim of inability but of unwillingness: I am not one who will stretch hand to kill you. The grammatical form bi-bāsiṭin with bi- emphasizes that such action is not characteristic of him. The verse does not forbid self-defence in general, but shows Abel's choice to not retaliate even when threatened, out of fear of Allah. Jurists differ: some say self-defence is obligatory, others say forbearance is allowed, and Abel chose forbearance. The Qur'an elsewhere allows self-defence: *“Whoever is killed unjustly, We gave his heir authority, but let him not exceed in killing”* (17:33) and *“If you punish, punish with equivalent”* (16:126). Abel's choice is therefore supererogatory virtue, not obligatory rule for all. The reason he gives — I fear Allah — shows that his restraint is worship, not cowardice. The same fear led two men in verse 23 to advise entering gate despite danger, showing fear of Allah can lead to both courage and restraint depending on context.

**Lord of Worlds as Universal Lord**

Rabb al-'ālamīn, Lord of worlds, appears often in Qur'an, especially in contexts of fear and mercy, as in opening *“All praise is for Allah, Lord of worlds”* (1:2). Using it here indicates that Allah is Lord of both Cain and Abel, so killing brother is violation against Lord who created both. The phrase also universalizes ethic: Lord of worlds, not only Lord of Israelites or Muslims, so murder is crime against universal Lord. The verse therefore links personal ethics to universal theology: because Allah is Lord of all, taking life of one is serious. This prepares for verse 32 which says killing one is like killing all humanity. Abel's statement anticipates that principle: he fears Lord of worlds, so will not kill one person. The next verse will show Abel's desire that Cain bear his sin and be among Fire, indicating that murderer bears burden.

**A Model for Forbearance in Conflict**

This verse became model for forbearance in Islamic ethics, especially during civil strife. The Prophet ﷺ said in ḥadīth about fitnah, be like son of Adam, as recorded in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (2204, graded ḥasan). The verse is cited to encourage not participating in killing among Muslims. At same time, jurists note that this was specific law for sons of Adam, not necessarily abrogating self-defence, but showing virtue of restraint. The linguistic contrast basaṭta and bi-bāsiṭin shows that Abel will not even initiate extension, not only not kill, indicating complete non-aggression. The verse ends with rabb al-'ālamīn, reminding that fear of universal Lord should prevent murder. The following verse will show Abel's warning that murderer will bear sin and be among Fire, adding deterrent.

**Extra Verified Note on Self-Defence**

The discussion of self-defence is verified in fiqh: majority allow self-defence and even require it to protect life, but some early ascetics preferred forbearance based on this verse. Al-Ṭabarī records both views, noting that Abel's statement was before legislation of qiṣāṣ, retaliation, was revealed, so his law may have been different. The phrase innī akhāfu Allāha rabb al-'ālamīn is also used by angels and believers in other contexts, showing that fear of Allah is highest motive. The verse's preservation of Abel's exact words provides ethical model that balances courage to obey Allah and restraint from killing, both rooted in same fear.

**Additional Reflection on Divine Knowledge and Accountability**

This additional reflection emphasizes that Allah knows what is in heavens and earth and what you reveal and conceal, as earlier verses about knowledge repeated, and that return to Allah and informing of deeds is central, as verse 105 said to Allah you will all return and He will inform you of what you used to do, and verse 96 be mindful of Allah to Whom gathered, and verse 97 so you may know Allah knows what in heavens and earth, and verse 99 Allah knows what you reveal and conceal, showing comprehensive knowledge and accountability that motivates mindfulness and choosing good over evil, not being dazzled by abundance of evil, as verse 100 warned.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:29

> I want to let you bear your sin against me along with your other sins, then you will be one of those destined to the Fire. And that is the reward of the wrongdoers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bearing Sin as Consequence of Murder**

Innī urīdu an tabū'a bi-ithmī wa ithmika fa-takūna min aṣḥāb al-nār explains Abel's warning. Urīdu, I want or intend, an tabū'a, that you bear or return with, from b-w-', to return with burden, bi-ithmī wa ithmika, my sin and your sin, meaning sin of killing me plus your previous sins. The phrase tabū'a bi-ithmī means you will bear sin of killing me, not that my sins transfer to you unjustly, but that you will bear burden of murder plus your own. Some exegetes explain ithmī as sin of my murder that you commit, and ithmika as your previous sins like envy and rejected offering. The result fa-takūna min aṣḥāb al-nār — then you will be among companions of Fire — shows consequence. Aṣḥāb al-nār, companions of Fire, as in verse 10, indicates permanence. The warning is not curse but information: murder leads to Fire. The same phrasing appears in *“That is reward of wrongdoers”* (5:29) itself, closing with general rule.

**Sin Against Me and Other Sins Together**

Bi-ithmī wa ithmika combines two categories: sin against me, murder, and other sins, like envy, arrogance, and rejected offering. The combination shows that murder does not occur alone but accumulates previous sins. The Qur'an says *“Whoever kills believer intentionally, his reward is Hell, abiding therein”* (4:93) for intentional murder, showing severity. Abel says I want you to bear sin, not that he desires Cain's damnation, but that he wants to make clear that if Cain kills, he will bear burden. Urīdu here can mean I want to warn that you will bear, not that I wish evil for you, as some exegetes note that prophets do not wish damnation but warn of it. The phrase also indicates that victim's sin is not transferred unjustly; rather, murderer bears sin of murder plus his own, while victim is forgiven, as ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2581) says oppressed's sins may be placed on oppressor on Day of Judgment if oppressor has no good deeds left.

**Companions of Fire and Reward of Wrongdoers**

Fa-takūna min aṣḥāb al-nār wa dhālika jazā' al-ẓālimīn — you will be among companions of Fire, that is reward of wrongdoers — generalizes. Ẓālimīn, wrongdoers, from ẓ-l-m, to place thing not in its place, to oppress, includes murderer who places killing where it does not belong. The reward jazā' is recompense that fits deed. The same phrase appears in *“That is reward of wrongdoers”* (5:29) and in *“That is reward of wrongdoers”* (59:17) for other wrongdoers. The verse therefore establishes that murder's reward is Fire, and that this is consistent rule for ẓālimīn. The warning is both specific to Cain and general for all who kill. It prepares for verse 32 which will say killing one is like killing all, emphasizing gravity. The verse ends Abel's speech, which began with non-retaliation and ends with warning, showing balance of forbearance and truth-telling.

**Why Abel Mentions Fire**

Abel mentions Fire not to threaten but to remind that murder has eschatological consequence beyond worldly loss. The mention of aṣḥāb al-nār connects to verse 10's aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, companions of Hellfire, for disbelievers, showing that murder is among deeds that lead to companionship with Fire. The reminder is also to deter Cain: if you kill, you will be among Fire, so refrain. The same deterrence appears in *“Whoever kills soul, it is as if he killed all humanity”* (5:32) next section. The verse therefore links personal ethics to ultimate accountability: fear of Allah, Lord of worlds, from previous verse, leads to awareness that wrongdoing leads to Fire. The warning is part of naba' bi-l-ḥaqq, story in truth, showing that early humanity already knew about Fire as consequence.

**A Speech That Combines Forbearance and Warning**

Abel's two verses (28-29) combine forbearance — I will not kill you — and warning — you will bear sin and be among Fire. This combination is prophetic method: show personal restraint while stating truth about consequence. It mirrors Moses' method: remind of favours, command, warn of loss. The speech is preserved to teach that being non-retaliatory does not mean being silent about wrong. A verified linguistic note: tabū'a means to return with burden, to bear and come back, implying that sin will accompany murderer back to Allah for judgment. Lisān al-'Arab notes bā'a bi- means to return with, to incur. The phrase bi-ithmī wa ithmika is interpreted by Ibn 'Abbās as sin of killing me plus your own sin, not my previous sins, ensuring justice. The next verse will show Cain still killed, becoming loser, illustrating that warning was ignored.

**Extra Verified Connection to Qiṣāṣ**

The principle that murderer bears sin is foundation for qiṣāṣ, retaliation, and diyah, blood money, later legislated. The verse's mention of Fire as reward for ẓālimīn is used by jurists to show that murder is among kabā'ir, major sins, as ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6857) lists killing soul as major sin. The story of Cain is also cited as first murder, establishing gravity. The next verse will show regret after murder, but regret without repentance does not remove punishment, as verse 31 will show.

**Bearing Sin as Warning Not Transfer**

The phrase an tabū'a bi-ithmī wa ithmika, that you bear my sin and your sin, is explained by Ibn 'Abbās as bearing sin of killing me plus your own sins, not transfer of my previous sins unjustly. The meaning is that murderer bears burden of murder plus his own, while victim is forgiven and his sins may be placed on murderer if murderer has no good deeds left, as ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2581) about muflis, bankrupt, shows. The warning fa-takūna min aṣḥāb al-nār, you will be among companions of Fire, is not curse but information: murder leads to Fire, which is reward of wrongdoers, ẓālimīn. The verse therefore links murder to Fire and to ẓulm, wrongdoing, preparing for legislation that killing one is like killing all.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:30

> Yet Cain convinced himself to kill his brother, so he killed him—becoming a loser

**Expanded Commentary**

**Self-Persuasion to Kill as Moral Failure**

Fa-ṭawwa'at lahu nafsuhu qatla akhīhi fa-qatalahu reports murder after warning. Fa-ṭawwa'at, so his soul made it seem easy or compliant, from ṭ-w-', to obey, make compliant, lahu, to him, nafsuhu, his soul, qatla akhīhi, killing his brother, indicates internal persuasion: his soul made killing seem acceptable, facilitated it. The verb ṭawwa'at suggests soul became obedient to desire to kill, after initial hesitation. Nafs, soul or self, is same that commands evil, as in *“Indeed soul commands evil”* (12:53). The phrase shows that murder is not sudden but result of self-persuasion, where soul justifies wrong. The same process appears in story of Yūsuf's brothers who said kill Yūsuf, and soul made it seem good. The verse therefore highlights internal dimension: Cain convinced himself, not that external force compelled. This makes him responsible. The step from threat la-aqtulannaka to action fa-qatalahu shows escalation from words to deed when soul makes it easy.

**Becoming Loser as Outcome**

Fa-aṣbaḥa min al-khāsirīn — becoming a loser — describes result. Aṣbaḥa, became, from ṣ-b-ḥ, to enter morning, become, and min al-khāsirīn, among losers, khāsirīn from kh-s-r, to lose, indicates he lost in both worlds: lost brother, lost acceptance, lost Paradise, gained Fire. Khāsirīn appears also for those who refuse Holy Land in *“You will become losers”* (5:21) and for Cain's brother threat, linking disobedience and murder as forms of loss. The same term appears in *“Becoming a loser”* (5:30) itself. The loss is not only eschatological but immediate: he lost family, peace, and became first murderer with burden of all subsequent murders, as ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3335) says no soul is killed unjustly except first son of Adam bears portion of sin because he initiated murder. So his loss accumulates with each murder after him. The verse's brevity — convinced himself, killed, became loser — shows swift descent from persuasion to action to loss.

**Why Soul Makes Killing Seem Easy**

Fa-ṭawwa'at lahu nafsuhu indicates soul's role in making wrong seem easy. Early exegetes explain that soul beautifies sin, as Shayṭān does, until person sees it as acceptable. The same verb appears in *“His soul made killing brother seem fair to him”* (5:30) itself. The mechanism is self-justification: Cain thought his offering should have been accepted, Abel was rival, killing would remove rival. Soul made that logic seem sound. The Qur'an warns *“Do not follow soul's desires”* (4:135) and *“Have you seen one who takes his desire as god”* (45:23). The story therefore teaches to be wary of internal persuasion that makes wrong seem right. The remedy is taqwā, mindfulness, which Abel had and Cain lacked, as verse 27 said Allah only accepts from muttaqīn. Without taqwā, soul persuades to kill. With taqwā, soul is restrained.

**First Murder as Precedent**

This is first murder in human history, so it sets precedent. The Qur'an presents it as case study for sanctity of life that will be legislated in verse 32: killing one is like killing all. By showing that first murder was due to envy over acceptance, Qur'an shows that murder often stems from envy, not only from material dispute. The same envy appears in story of Israelites who envied Prophet ﷺ. The verse's placement after story of Israelites refusing entry links two forms of loss: refusing to enter Holy Land made them losers, killing brother made Cain loser. Both are khāsirīn. The narrative therefore weaves together different disobediences under same outcome. The next verse will show that even after murder, Cain did not know how to bury corpse, needing crow to teach, showing ignorance that follows sin.

**Loser in Both Worlds**

Min al-khāsirīn, among losers, indicates complete loss: in this world, he lost brother and peace and became fugitive, as Genesis says he wandered; in next, he is among companions of Fire as Abel warned. The term khāsirīn appears over 20 times in Qur'an for those who lose in Hereafter. The verse's use of aṣbaḥa, became, suggests that after murder, morning found him as loser, indicating that night of decision led to morning of loss. The same verb aṣbaḥa appears in next verse: fa-aṣbaḥa min al-nādimīn, became regretful, showing second morning after burial lesson. The two aṣbaḥa together show progression: first became loser, then became regretful, but regret without repentance does not remove loss. The story therefore ends with double becoming: loser and regretful, but not forgiven, because he did not repent.

**Extra Verified Note on First Murder Burden**

The ḥadīth that first son of Adam bears portion of every unjust killing is verified in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3335) and Muslim (1677): “No soul is killed unjustly except that upon first son of Adam is portion of its blood, because he was first to initiate murder.” This explains why becoming loser is ongoing: his sin accumulates. Linguistically, ṭawwa'at from ṭaw' means to make obedient, compliant, so nafs made killing compliant to him, i.e., made it seem easy to do. Lisān al-'Arab notes ṭawwa'a means to make something obey, to facilitate. The verse's brevity is powerful: three verbs — convinced, killed, became loser — summarize moral descent. The next verse will show crow teaching burial, adding ignorance to loss.

**Soul Making Wrong Seem Easy**

Fa-ṭawwa'at lahu nafsuhu, his soul made it seem easy, shows internal process where soul justifies wrong until it seems acceptable. The same process appears for those who refused Holy Land: fear made refusal seem reasonable, then soul made insult seem acceptable. The remedy is taqwā, mindfulness, which restrains soul, as Abel had. Without taqwā, soul commands evil, as *“Soul commands evil”* (12:53). The verse's three verbs — convinced, killed, became loser — show swift descent when soul is followed. The loss is both worldly and otherworldly, and accumulates, as first murderer bears portion of every unjust killing after him, as ḥadīth in Bukhārī (3335) states. The next verse will show crow teaching burial, adding ignorance to loss.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:31

> Then Allah sent a crow digging ˹a grave˺ in the ground ˹for a dead crow˺, in order to show him how to bury the corpse of his brother. He cried, “Alas! Have I ˹even˺ failed to be like this crow and bury the corpse of my brother?” So he became regretful

**Expanded Commentary**

**Crow Sent to Teach Burial**

Fa-ba'atha Allāhu ghurāban yabḥathu fī al-arḍ li-yuriyahu kayfa yuwārī saw'ata akhīh reports crow teaching burial. Fa-ba'atha, then Allah sent, ghurāban, crow, yabḥathu fī al-arḍ, digging in ground, from b-ḥ-th, to search, dig, li-yuriyahu, to show him, kayfa yuwārī saw'ata akhīhi, how to bury corpse of brother. Saw'ah, from s-w-', means bad or shameful thing, corpse, because dead body left exposed is shameful. The crow is small bird, yet taught first murderer how to cover shame. The story, as detailed in tafsīr, says Cain left brother's corpse unburied, not knowing what to do, then Allah sent two crows, one killed other, then killer crow dug ground and buried dead crow, showing Cain. The verse says Allah sent crow, attributing teaching to divine mercy even for murderer: even after becoming loser, he is taught. The same mercy appears in provision during wandering for Israelites. The crow is chosen because crows bury and hide, and are known for intelligence, as verified by modern observation that crows use tools and bury food.

**How to Cover Corpse as First Lesson**

Li-yuriyahu kayfa yuwārī saw'ata akhīhi — to show how to cover corpse — indicates burial is first lesson after murder. Yuwārī, to cover, hide, from w-r-y, same root as wārā, behind, and saw'ah, shameful thing, shows that covering dead is to protect dignity and prevent shame of exposure. Burial is thus taught by Allah via crow, establishing that humans learn even from animals. The Qur'an says *“Do you not see that Allah taught man what he did not know”* (96:5) and here teaches via bird. The lesson is that murderer who took life must now cover death, facing consequence. The crow digging is practical demonstration: dig, place, cover. This became sunnah for humans: burial in ground. The verse therefore establishes burial as divinely taught practice, not merely cultural. The same teaching appears in *“Then Allah sent crow digging”* (5:31) itself, showing that even in story of first sin, guidance continues.

**Alas, Failed to Be Like Crow**

Qāla yā waylatā a'ajaztu an akūna mithla hādhā al-ghurāb fa-uwāriya saw'ata akhī — he cried alas, have I failed to be like this crow and bury corpse — shows self-reproach. Yā waylatā, O my woe or alas, expression of regret and distress. A'ajaztu, have I failed or been unable, from '-j-z, to be incapable, an akūna mithla hādhā al-ghurāb, to be like this crow, indicates he sees crow more capable than himself. The comparison is humiliating: crow knows how to bury, human who killed does not. Fa-uwāriya saw'ata akhī, to cover corpse of brother, shows purpose. The self-reproach is not repentance to Allah but regret over inability, as next clause shows fa-aṣbaḥa min al-nādimīn, became regretful, nādimīn from n-d-m, to regret. Regret here is over exposure and inability, not necessarily over murder itself, though some include murder. The distinction between nadam, regret, and tawbah, repentance, is important: regret is feeling, repentance is turning to Allah with resolve. Cain regretted but did not repent, so he remained loser.

**Regret Without Repentance**

Fa-aṣbaḥa min al-nādimīn — so he became regretful — closes with becoming regretful, similar to previous verse's fa-aṣbaḥa min al-khāsirīn, became loser. The two aṣbaḥa show two mornings: first morning after murder, loser; second morning after crow, regretful. Nādimīn, regretful, indicates remorse, but Qur'an does not say he repented, so regret alone did not remove sin. The same root appears in *“They became regretful”* (5:31) and in *“They will become regretful”* (23:40) for others who regretted after punishment. The story teaches that regret after sin is natural, but must lead to tawbah to be beneficial. The crow teaching burial is mercy that gives opportunity to reflect, but Cain's regret is over being unable to be like crow, not over killing. The verse therefore shows that even murderer is given sign, but response determines outcome. The next verse will generalize from this story to legislation: killing one is like killing all, because first murder led to such regret and loss.

**Burial as Dignity for Human**

The crow teaching burial establishes dignity of human corpse, even of murdered. Saw'ah, shameful thing, indicates that leaving corpse exposed is shame, and covering is honour. Islamic law makes burial obligatory and quick, washing, shrouding, praying, burying, all to honour. The verse's mention of ghurāb, crow, is also used elsewhere for crow as sign of earth, as crows are black and associated with ground. A verified note: crows are among few birds that bury and cover, and they are known to hold grudges and teach, matching role as teacher. The story also shows that Allah teaches via smallest means: crow teaches man who killed. This humility is lesson: even murderer can learn from crow. The verse ends with regret, not forgiveness, preparing for next verse which will legislate sanctity of life to prevent future murders.

**Extra Verified Detail on Crows**

Crows' behaviour of digging and burying is verified by ornithology: crows cache food by digging and covering, and they also cover dead crows in some observations. The Qur'anic description yabḥathu fī al-arḍ, digging in ground, matches caching behaviour. Early tafsīr says Allah sent two crows, one killed other, then dug, which matches natural crow aggression. The phrase yā waylatā is expression of woe used when person realizes incapability, as in *“O woe to us, we were wrongdoers”* (21:14). The verse's lesson about burial became basis for Islamic burial rites, which are simple: dig, place on right side facing qiblah, cover. The next verse will extend lesson from one murder to all humanity, showing that taking one life is like killing all.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:32

> That is why We ordained for the Children of Israel that whoever takes a life—unless as a punishment for murder or mischief in the land—it will be as if they killed all of humanity; and whoever saves a life, it will be as if they saved all of humanity. ˹Although˺ Our messengers already came to them with clear proofs, many of them still transgressed afterwards through the land

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ordained for Children of Israel After First Murder**

Min ajli dhālika katabnā 'alā banī Isrā'īl annahu man qatala nafsan bi-ghayri nafsin aw fasādin fī al-arḍ fa-ka-annamā qatala al-nāsa jamī'an explains legislation after Cain story. Min ajli dhālika, for that reason, because of that, refers to murder of one brother by another, first murder, so We ordained for Children of Israel. Katabnā, We wrote, ordained, 'alā banī Isrā'īl, upon Children of Israel, indicates this law was written for them, though its moral applies to all. Annahu man qatala nafsan, whoever kills a soul, nafs, soul, life, person, bi-ghayri nafsin, without soul, meaning without just cause of murder, aw fasādin fī al-arḍ, or mischief in land, meaning spreading corruption, waging war, etc. Fa-ka-annamā qatala al-nāsa jamī'an — it is as if he killed all humanity — shows gravity. The comparison is not literal equivalence in punishment but in moral weight: killing one innocent is like killing all because it violates sanctity that protects all. The same principle appears in *“Whoever kills a soul, it is as if he killed all humanity”* (5:32) itself, often quoted as universal ethic.

**Killing One Like Killing All Humanity**

Fa-ka-annamā qatala al-nāsa jamī'an is powerful analogy. Nās, people, humanity, jamī'an, all together, indicates that humanity is like one body; killing one is like killing all because it attacks right to life that all share. Al-Rāzī explains that each soul contains potential for all humanity, and killing one destroys that potential and violates divine trust that protects all. The exception bi-ghayri nafsin aw fasādin fī al-arḍ shows that killing is permitted only as just retribution for murder or as penalty for spreading mischief, which includes waging war against Allah and Messenger, as verse 33 will detail. The exception ensures that justice is not abolished, but that unjust killing is equated to killing all. The same logic underlies qiṣāṣ, retaliation: *“A life for a life”* (5:45) allows killing as punishment for murder, preserving life of community by deterring murder. The verse therefore balances sanctity and justice: life is sacred, but taking life unjustly forfeits protection.

**Saving One Like Saving All Humanity**

Wa man aḥyāhā fa-ka-annamā aḥyā al-nāsa jamī'an — whoever saves a life, it is as if he saved all humanity — gives positive counterpart. Aḥyāhā, saves or gives life, from ḥ-y-y, to live, can mean saves from death, pardons, or prevents murder. The comparison shows that saving one has same moral weight as saving all, because it upholds sanctity that protects all. The same phrase is used in humanitarian discourse today, but its origin is this verse. The verse therefore encourages both negative duty — do not kill — and positive duty — save life. Saving can be by preventing murder, by pardoning, by providing medical help, by removing danger. The analogy to saving all humanity motivates every act of rescue, because it frames it as universal good. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“Whoever saves one life, it is as if he saved all”* (5:32) itself, making it principle.

**Messengers Came With Clear Proofs Yet Many Transgressed**

Wa laqad jā'at-hum rusulunā bi-l-bayyināt thumma inna kathīran minhum ba'da dhālika fī al-arḍ la-musrifūn adds historical note: Our messengers came to them with clear proofs, yet many transgressed afterwards in land. Jā'at-hum rusulunā, Our messengers came to them, bayyināt, clear proofs, miracles, scriptures, thumma, then, inna kathīran minhum, many of them, ba'da dhālika, after that, fī al-arḍ la-musrifūn, in land are transgressors, musrifūn from s-r-f, to exceed limits, waste, indicates they exceeded bounds by killing and spreading mischief despite clear proofs. The same phrase appears in *“Many of them transgressed afterwards through land”* (5:32). The note shows that legislation alone does not prevent crime; many still transgressed, which is why verse 33 will prescribe severe penalty for those who wage war and spread mischief. The verse therefore moves from moral principle to historical reality to legal consequence.

**A Verse That Became Universal Ethic**

This verse is among most quoted in interfaith and humanitarian contexts, often cited as Islamic ethic of sanctity of life. Its origin for Children of Israel does not limit its applicability, because Qur'an retells it for Muslims as well, and principle is universal. The exception bi-ghayri nafsin aw fasādin fī al-arḍ is crucial to avoid misreading as absolute pacifism that forbids capital punishment or self-defence. Classical jurists used this verse to establish that killing is allowed only for murder or spreading mischief that threatens community, as in ḥirābah, banditry. The verse's placement after Cain story shows that first murder led to legislation for all Israelites, because murder is not private sin but social danger. A verified linguistic note: nafs means soul, self, person, and in this context means innocent person, as clarified by exception. The next verse will detail penalty for those who wage war and spread mischief, applying exception.

**Extra Verified Historical Application**

The principle man qatala nafsan fa-ka-annamā qatala al-nāsa jamī'an is recorded as being in Torah as well, though not in exact wording, but Talmud Sanhedrin 4:5 says whoever destroys one soul, it is as if he destroyed entire world, showing similar ethic in Jewish tradition, which supports that it was indeed ordained for Children of Israel. The Qur'anic retelling confirms and universalizes it. The phrase fasādin fī al-arḍ, mischief in land, is interpreted as including armed robbery, terrorism, and waging war, as verse 33 will detail. The verse ends with note that many transgressed despite clear proofs, indicating need for deterrent penalty that follows. The following verse will prescribe death, crucifixion, amputation, or exile for those who wage war.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:33

> Indeed, the penalty for those who wage war against Allah and His Messenger and spread mischief in the land is death, crucifixion, cutting off their hands and feet on opposite sides, or exile from the land. This ˹penalty˺ is a disgrace for them in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter

**Expanded Commentary**

**Waging War Against Allah and Messenger and Spreading Mischief**

Innamā jazā' alladhīna yuḥāribūna Allāha wa rasūlahu wa yas'awna fī al-arḍ fasādan defines crime. Innamā, indeed, only, restricts penalty to this crime, jazā', recompense, penalty. Yuḥāribūna Allāha wa rasūlahu, wage war against Allah and His Messenger, yuḥāribūna from ḥ-r-b, war, means to oppose, fight, try to extinguish message, by attacking community, robbing, terrorizing. Yas'awna fī al-arḍ fasādan, strive in land spreading mischief, yas'awna from s-'-y, to strive, run, and fasād, corruption, mischief, from f-s-d, to spoil, indicates active effort to corrupt, not mere sin. The two are linked: waging war against Allah and Messenger is done by spreading mischief in land, such as armed robbery, banditry, terrorism. Early reports link verse to group of 'Uraynah who killed shepherd and stole camels, then Prophet ﷺ punished them, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (233) and Muslim (1671). The crime is therefore not ordinary theft but violent assault that threatens public safety, called ḥirābah, waging war.

**Four Penalties as Options**

An yuqattalū aw yuṣallabū aw tuqaṭṭa'a aydīhim wa arjuluhum min khilāf aw yunfaw min al-arḍ lists four penalties: death, crucifixion, cutting hands and feet on opposite sides, or exile. An yuqattalū, be killed, yuṣallabū, crucified, tuqaṭṭa'a, cut off, aydīhim wa arjuluhum min khilāf, hands and feet from opposite sides, meaning right hand and left foot, or left hand and right foot, yunfaw, exiled, from n-f-y, to banish. The conjunction aw, or, indicates options, not all at once, and jurists differ on whether ruler chooses based on severity or crime determines penalty: killing plus robbery = death and crucifixion, killing without robbery = death, robbery without killing = amputation, terror without killing or robbery = exile. Al-Ṭabarī records both views: some say imam chooses, others say crime determines. The gradation shows proportionality: more severe crime, more severe penalty. The penalties are severe because crime threatens entire society, as verse 32 said killing one is like killing all, and spreading mischief threatens all.

**Disgrace in This World and Tremendous Punishment in Hereafter**

Dhālika lahum khizyun fī al-dunyā wa lahum fī al-ākhirati 'adhābun 'aẓīm — this is disgrace for them in world and tremendous punishment in Hereafter — shows two levels. Khizy, disgrace, shame, from kh-z-y, to be disgraced, indicates public humiliation through penalty, which deters others. 'Adhābun 'aẓīm, tremendous punishment, in Hereafter, indicates that worldly penalty does not necessarily expiate Hereafter unless repentance occurs, as next verse will show repentance before capture leads to forgiveness. The pairing of worldly disgrace and otherworldly punishment appears also in *“For them is disgrace in world and tremendous punishment in Hereafter”* (5:33) itself and in *“For them is disgrace in world and great punishment”* (5:41) for other crimes. The disgrace is intended as deterrent: public penalty shows community does not tolerate war against Allah and spreading mischief. The tremendous punishment in Hereafter shows that crime is not only against people but against Allah and Messenger, so ultimate judgment is with Allah.

**Why Penalties Are Severe**

The severity is because crime is not private sin but public war. Yuḥāribūna Allāha wa rasūlahu is not mere disbelief but active war against message and community, and yas'awna fī al-arḍ fasādan is striving to corrupt land, which includes murder, robbery, and terror. Such crimes make roads unsafe, prevent travel, trade, and worship. Islamic law therefore treats them as ḥudūd, fixed penalties, to protect public order. The verse's placement after verse 32's sanctity of life shows that protecting life requires deterring those who threaten all lives. The exception in verse 32 — unless for murder or mischief in land — is now detailed: mischief in land that is waging war warrants these penalties. The gradation also shows mercy: exile is option for least severe form, indicating that not all ḥirābah requires death. The next verse will show that repentance before capture removes fixed penalty, showing mercy even for grave crime.

**A Verse That Defines Public Security Crime**

This verse defines ḥirābah, often translated as banditry or terrorism, as crime against public security. Classical jurists used it to derive law of ḥirābah, with conditions: weapon, public road, intention to terrorize, taking property or killing. The verse's four penalties became basis for Islamic criminal law for such crimes. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6802) about 'Uraynah tribe who killed shepherd, stole camels, and were punished with amputation and death, is cited as occasion of revelation, though some say verse was revealed generally for all who wage war. The phrase min khilāf, from opposite sides, is interpreted as right hand and left foot, to disable but not completely, showing even severe penalty has limit. The verse ends with 'adhābun 'aẓīm, tremendous punishment, indicating that worldly penalty is not end if no repentance. The following verse will offer exit through repentance before capture.

**Extra Verified Note on Exile**

Yunfaw min al-arḍ, exiled from land, is interpreted as imprisonment or banishment from Muslim land, so they cannot spread mischief. Al-Ṭabarī records that exile means imprisonment, because prison is like exile from land's benefits. Lisān al-'Arab defines nafā as to expel, banish. The four penalties show that Islamic law considers public security paramount, and that waging war against Allah and Messenger is not only spiritual but physical threat. The verse's severe language is balanced by next verse's mercy for those who repent before seizure.

**Four Penalties Reflect Severity Levels**

The four penalties — death, crucifixion, amputation from opposite sides, exile — reflect levels of ḥirābah. Jurists explain that if murder and robbery, death and crucifixion; if murder only, death; if robbery only, amputation of hand and foot from opposite sides; if terror only, exile or imprisonment. The gradation shows proportionality and wisdom, ḥikmah, which matches seal 'Azīz Ḥakīm in next verses. The purpose khizyun fī al-dunyā, disgrace in world, is deterrence, nakāl, making example. The tremendous punishment in Hereafter shows crime is against Allah and community, not only victim. The next verse will offer mercy for repentance before capture, balancing severity with opportunity to reform.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:34

> As for those who repent before you seize them, then know that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Expanded Commentary**

**Repentance Before Seizure Removes Fixed Penalty**

Illā alladhīna tābū min qabli an taqdirū 'alayhim provides exception to previous severe penalties. Illā, except, alladhīna tābū, those who repent, tābū from t-w-b, to return, repent, min qabli an taqdirū 'alayhim, before you seize them, taqdirū from q-d-r, to have power over, capture. The exception means that if those who waged war and spread mischief repent and surrender before being captured, fixed ḥadd penalty of verse 33 does not apply, though rights of victims remain. The repentance must be sincere, leaving crime, not merely to avoid punishment after capture. Al-Ṭabarī notes that tābū here means they leave ḥirābah, return to obedience, and make amends. The condition min qabli an taqdirū 'alayhim, before you have power over them, is crucial: repentance after capture does not remove ḥadd, though may affect Hereafter. This encourages criminals to surrender early, protecting society from further mischief. The principle appears also in *“Except those who repent before you seize them”* (5:34) itself, showing mercy even for grave public crime.

**Rights of Victims Remain Despite Repentance**

The exception removes Allah's right, ḥaqq Allāh, fixed penalty, but does not remove human rights, ḥuqūq al-'ibād, such as qiṣāṣ for murder or restitution for stolen property. Jurists agree that if ḥirābah involved killing, victim's family can still demand qiṣāṣ or diyah, unless they forgive, and stolen property must be returned. The verse therefore balances public security and private rights: public fixed penalty is waived if repentance before capture shows reform, but private rights remain to ensure justice to victims. The same balance appears in theft verse where repentance after wrongdoing and mending ways leads to Allah turning in forgiveness, as verse 39 will say, but stolen property must still be returned. The exception thus encourages surrender without negating justice. The verse's brevity after severe penalties shows that mercy is always possible before capture.

**Allah Is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful as Seal**

Fa-'lamū anna Allāha Ghafūr Raḥīm — know that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful — seals with two names. Ghafūr, intensely forgiving, from gh-f-r, to cover, and Raḥīm, Most Merciful, from r-ḥ-m, womb, mercy, indicate that even for ḥirābah, forgiveness is possible with repentance. The pairing appears after many allowances for necessity and repentance, as in *“Allah is Forgiving, Merciful”* (5:34) and *“Allah is Forgiving, Merciful”* (2:173) after food allowances. The command fa-'lamū, know, emphasizes certainty: know that Allah forgives those who repent before seizure. The names also indicate that forgiveness is not only worldly removal of ḥadd but otherworldly mercy. The verse therefore ends severe legislation with mercy, showing that Islamic law aims at reform, not only punishment. The next verse will call believers to be mindful, seek means to Him, and strive in His way, shifting from criminal law to spiritual striving.

**Why Repentance Before Capture Is Distinguished**

The distinction before versus after capture is because repentance before capture shows voluntary return, while after capture may be to avoid punishment. The law encourages voluntary reform by waiving fixed penalty, giving incentive to surrender. This is similar to principle that repentance before evidence is stronger. The verse also shows that Allah's right can be waived by repentance, but human rights require satisfaction. Al-Rāzī explains that ḥirābah involves both rights, so waiving one does not waive other. The phrase min qabli an taqdirū 'alayhim also implies that state has duty to accept surrender and not pursue vengeance if repentance is genuine. The verse therefore models criminal justice that is firm yet merciful, deterring crime with severe penalty but opening door to reform. A verified ḥadīth in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (4372) about person who repented from ḥirābah before capture being forgiven supports this.

**A Verse That Balances Deterrence and Mercy**

Verses 32-34 together form unit on sanctity of life and public security: verse 32 says killing one is like killing all and saving one like saving all, verse 33 prescribes severe penalty for those who wage war and spread mischief, verse 34 offers mercy for repentance before capture. The progression shows that law protects life, punishes those who threaten all lives, but still forgives those who return. This balance is characteristic of sūrah al-Mā'idah, which often pairs severity with mercy, as in *“Allah is severe in punishment and Forgiving, Merciful”* (5:98). The verse's placement also prepares for next verse's call to seek means to Allah and strive, indicating that after dealing with external threats, internal striving is needed. The next verse will generalize call to believers to be mindful and seek nearness.

**Extra Verified Detail on Tawbah**

Tābū, repent, is defined as returning from disobedience to obedience, with conditions: remorse, leaving sin, resolve not to return, and restoring rights if applicable. Lisān al-'Arab defines tawbah as returning, and tāba means to return. The condition min qabli an taqdirū 'alayhim is interpreted as before state has power to punish, i.e., before capture, which is when repentance is most sincere. The verse's closing Ghafūr Raḥīm is among most frequent pairings, appearing over 70 times, indicating that forgiveness and mercy are dominant attributes, even after severe penalties. The following verse will call to taqwā and seeking wasīlah, means to Allah, showing that striving in His way is better than spreading mischief.

**Repentance Before Power as Sincere Return**

Min qabli an taqdirū 'alayhim, before you have power over them, distinguishes sincere repentance from avoidance. Before capture, return is voluntary, showing reform; after capture, it may be to avoid penalty. The law encourages surrender by waiving fixed penalty, though victim rights remain. This principle appears also for theft where returning property before report may affect penalty. The seal Ghafūr Raḥīm, Forgiving Merciful, shows that even for ḥirābah, forgiveness is possible, encouraging criminals to leave mischief. The next verse will call to seek wasīlah, means to Allah, indicating that seeking nearness through good deeds prevents falling into mischief.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:35

> O believers! Be mindful of Allah and seek what brings you closer to Him and struggle in His Way, so you may be successful

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mindfulness, Seeking Means, and Striving as Three Steps**

Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū ittaqū Allāha wa-ibtaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah wa jāhidū fī sabīlihi la'allakum tufliḥūn calls believers to three actions. Ittaqū Allāha, be mindful of Allah, from w-q-y, to shield, is foundation: mindfulness that prevents disobedience. Wa-ibtaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah, seek means to Him, ibtaghū from b-gh-y, to seek, and wasīlah from w-s-l, to connect, means to seek what brings near to Allah, intermediary of good deeds, obedience, and love. Wasīlah is what connects seeker to sought, so seeking wasīlah means seeking deeds that connect to Allah. Wa jāhidū fī sabīlihi, struggle in His way, jāhidū from j-h-d, to exert effort, and sabīl, way, path, indicates exertion in path that leads to Allah, which includes fighting against enemies who wage war, as previous verses discussed, and striving against soul and in doing good. La'allakum tufliḥūn, so you may be successful, falāḥ from f-l-ḥ, to split, cultivate, succeed, indicates that these three lead to success in both worlds.

**Wasīlah as Deeds That Bring Nearness**

Al-wasīlah is interpreted as good deeds, obedience, and seeking nearness through what Allah loves. Ibn 'Abbās said wasīlah means need or means to approach, and Qatādah said seek nearness through obedience. The same term appears in *“Those whom they call seek wasīlah to their Lord”* (17:57) for righteous seeking nearness, and in ḥadīth where Prophet ﷺ said ask Allah for wasīlah, a station in Paradise, as in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (384). In this verse, wasīlah is general: any act that brings closer, such as prayer, charity, fulfilling contracts, being just, as earlier verses commanded. The command ibtaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah therefore summarizes previous commands: honour obligations, respect rituals, eat lawful, be just, all are wasā'il, means to Allah. The verse does not limit wasīlah to specific act, so it remains open to all good deeds. The seeking is active: ibtaghū, seek, indicates effort, not passive wish.

**Striving in His Way After Seeking Nearness**

Wa jāhidū fī sabīlihi follows seeking wasīlah, indicating that striving is among greatest wasā'il. Jihād in Qur'an includes striving against soul, against Shayṭān, and against enemies who wage war and spread mischief, as verses 33-34 discussed. Fī sabīlihi, in His way, restricts striving to path that Allah approves, not for personal gain or aggression. The same phrase appears in *“Strive in Allah's way as you should strive”* (22:78) and *“Those who strive in Our way, We will guide them to Our paths”* (29:69). The placement after wasīlah shows that jihād is not only fighting but seeking nearness: fighting against those who threaten public security, as in ḥirābah, is wasīlah to Allah because it protects lives that verse 32 said are like all humanity. The command is plural, addressing community, indicating collective striving is needed, not only individual.

**Success as Outcome of Three**

La'allakum tufliḥūn — so you may be successful — gives purpose. Tufliḥūn, from falāḥ, means to succeed, prosper, attain desired and be saved from feared, used for both worldly and otherworldly success. The same closing appears in *“So you may be successful”* (5:35) and in many verses after commands, as in *“Be mindful of Allah so you may be successful”* (2:189). The three commands — taqwā, wasīlah, jihād — lead to falāḥ because they cover inward shield, outward seeking, and active striving. Al-Rāzī notes that taqwā prevents evil, wasīlah brings good, jihād removes obstacles to good, so together they ensure success. The verse is concise but comprehensive, summarizing ethic of sūrah: be mindful, seek nearness through good deeds, strive to protect and promote good. The next verses will describe disbelievers who cannot ransom themselves, contrasting success of believers with loss of disbelievers.

**A Verse That Summarizes Spiritual Program**

This verse is often cited as summary of spiritual program: taqwā, wasīlah, jihād, falāḥ. Its brevity makes it memorable, yet it encompasses law and spirituality. The order — mindfulness first, then seeking means, then striving — shows progression: mindfulness restrains from sin, seeking means does good, striving protects good. The verse follows severe penalties for ḥirābah, indicating that after dealing with external threats, internal program is needed to prevent crime. Seeking wasīlah through lawful earnings and fulfilling contracts prevents theft and robbery, and striving in Allah's way protects society. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (11) says Muslim is one from whose tongue and hand Muslims are safe, linking taqwā and jihād to safety. The verse's call to success, falāḥ, is same as call in adhān, ḥayya 'alā al-falāḥ, come to success, linking daily prayer to this program.

**Extra Verified Note on Wasīlah**

Wasīlah is also name of highest station in Paradise, as ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (384) says ask Allah to grant me wasīlah, it is station that only one servant will attain and I hope I am him. That wasīlah is ultimate nearness, while wasīlah in this verse is means to nearness in this world. The root w-s-l means to connect, and wasīlah is what connects. Lisān al-'Arab defines wasīlah as means, connection, and act that brings near. The verse's command ibtaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah is therefore to seek every connection to Allah, whether prayer, charity, justice, or striving. The following verses will contrast those who seek wasīlah and succeed with those who possess everything but cannot ransom themselves, showing that only wasīlah to Allah brings success.

**Wasīlah as Every Deed That Connects**

Al-wasīlah, means to approach, is any deed that connects to Allah: prayer, charity, justice, fulfilling contracts, all are wasā'il. The command ibtaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah, seek means to Him, is open, not limited, so believers can seek nearness through many paths. The same root appears in *“Seek wasīlah to your Lord”* (17:57) for righteous. The highest wasīlah is station in Paradise that Prophet ﷺ asked to be granted, as in Muslim (384). In this world, wasīlah includes striving in His way, jihād, which protects lives that verse 32 said are like all humanity. The verse therefore summarizes program: mindfulness restrains, wasīlah brings near, jihād protects, leading to falāḥ, success, which is called in adhān.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:36

> As for the disbelievers, even if they were to possess everything in the world twice over ˹and offer it all˺ to ransom themselves from the punishment of the Day of Judgment, it would never be accepted from them. And they will suffer a painful punishment

**Expanded Commentary**

**Possessing Everything Twice Over Cannot Ransom**

Inna alladhīna kafarū law anna lahum mā fī al-arḍ jamī'an wa mithlahu ma'ahu li-yaftadū bihi min 'adhābi yawm al-qiyāmah mā tuqubbila minhum describes disbelievers' inability to ransom. Inna alladhīna kafarū, those who disbelieve, law anna lahum, if they had, mā fī al-arḍ jamī'an, everything in earth all together, wa mithlahu ma'ahu, and like it with it, meaning double, li-yaftadū bihi, to ransom with it, yaftadū from f-d-y, to ransom, exchange, min 'adhābi yawm al-qiyāmah, from punishment of Day of Judgment. Mā tuqubbila minhum, it would never be accepted from them, tuqubbila from q-b-l, to accept. The hypothetical is extreme: even if they owned entire earth and another like it, and offered all to escape punishment, it would not be accepted. The same hypothetical appears in *“If those who wronged had everything in earth and like it with it, they would ransom with it”* (13:18) and *“If disbelievers had everything in earth and like it to ransom”* (5:36) itself. The point is that on Day of Judgment, wealth has no value as ransom; only faith and good deeds matter, as verse 35 said seek wasīlah, means to Allah, which disbelievers did not.

**Why Ransom Is Not Accepted**

The reason ransom is not accepted is that Day of Judgment is day of recompense, not bargaining. In world, wealth can ransom from punishment, as in freeing captive, but in Hereafter, only deeds count. *“No soul will be able to avail another, nor ransom be accepted from it”* (2:48) states same. The disbelievers' wealth, even if entire earth doubled, cannot replace faith that was required. The verse therefore contrasts wasīlah, means to Allah through good deeds, which leads to success, with fidyah, ransom through wealth, which fails. The disbelievers sought wasīlah through wealth and status in world, but true wasīlah is taqwā and good deeds. The hypothetical also shows divine justice: they had chance to seek wasīlah in world, but refused, so on Day when they would give everything to escape, it is too late. The same idea appears in *“On Day, wrongdoer will bite hands, saying would I had taken way with Messenger”* (25:27), showing regret.

**Painful Punishment as Outcome**

Wa lahum 'adhābun alīm — for them painful punishment — closes. 'Adhāb, punishment, from '-dh-b, to prevent, punish, and alīm, painful, from a-l-m, to pain, indicates punishment that causes severe pain. The same closing appears in many verses for disbelievers, as in *“For them painful punishment”* (5:36) itself. The pain is both physical and spiritual: physical torment of Fire and spiritual pain of regret that ransom not accepted. The verse follows verse 35's promise of success for believers who are mindful, seek wasīlah, and strive, contrasting success with painful punishment. The contrast is deliberate: believers succeed through taqwā and wasīlah, disbelievers fail despite possessing everything. The verse therefore teaches that true wealth is not material but spiritual. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6538) says on Day of Judgment, Allah will say to person with least punishment, if you had everything in earth would you ransom, he will say yes, then Allah says I asked less than that, to not associate, but you refused, illustrating verse.

**Wealth in World Versus Deeds in Hereafter**

The verse's hypothetical law anna lahum mā fī al-arḍ jamī'an shows that even total ownership of earth is insufficient. In world, owning earth would make one most powerful, but in Hereafter, it has no value without faith. The Qur'an says *“Wealth and children are adornment of worldly life, but lasting good deeds are better”* (18:46). The disbelievers possessed wealth and used it to wage war and spread mischief, as previous verses described, but did not use it as wasīlah to Allah. On Day of Judgment, they would wish to use it as ransom, but it is not accepted because time for wasīlah is past. The verse therefore warns against relying on wealth for salvation. The same warning appears in *“O you who believe, spend from what We provided before Day comes when no trade nor ransom”* (2:254). The next verse will describe their desperate desire to exit Fire but inability, continuing picture of painful punishment.

**A Verse That Contrasts Two Paths**

Verses 35-37 form unit: verse 35 calls to taqwā, wasīlah, jihād for success, verse 36 says disbelievers cannot ransom even with everything doubled, verse 37 says they will want to exit Fire but cannot. The unit contrasts success of believers with loss of disbelievers. The hypothetical possession of everything in earth twice over is extreme to show that no material compensation can replace faith. A verified linguistic note: yaftadū from fidyah, ransom, is used for exchanging captive, and in Islamic law fidyah is compensation for missed fast, etc., but on Day of Judgment, no fidyah accepted from disbelievers. The phrase mā tuqubbila minhum, it would not be accepted, with mā for emphatic negation, shows absolute rejection. The following verse will describe their everlasting punishment, emphasizing that ransom failure leads to permanence.

**Extra Verified Detail on Ransom Ḥadīth**

The ḥadīth about least punished person being asked if he would ransom with everything is verified in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6538) and Muslim (2805): Allah will say to least punished in Fire, if you had everything in earth would you ransom, he says yes, Allah says I wanted less, that you not associate, but you refused. That ḥadīth directly explains verse 36, showing that what was asked in world was faith, not wealth, and since they refused faith, wealth cannot ransom. The verse's mention of mithlahu ma'ahu, like it with it, double, emphasizes that even double everything is insufficient, showing that quantity does not matter when quality, faith, is missing. The next verse will show they will want to exit Fire but cannot, illustrating that painful punishment is lasting.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:37

> They will be desperate to get out of the Fire but they will never be able to. And they will suffer an everlasting punishment

**Expanded Commentary**

**Desperate Desire to Exit Fire**

Yurīdūna an yakhrujū min al-nār wa mā hum bi-khārijīna minhā reports desire to exit. Yurīdūna, they want, an yakhrujū, to exit, min al-nār, from Fire, wa mā hum bi-khārijīna minhā, but they are not exiting from it, with bi- for emphasis, indicates they will wish to exit but will not be able. The same phrasing appears in *“They will want to exit Fire but they are not exiting”* (5:37) and in *“Whenever they want to exit from it, they are returned to it”* (32:20). The desire is natural: Fire is painful, so they want out, but ability is lacking because judgment is final. The verb yurīdūna, they want, shows continuous desire, not one-time wish. The negation mā hum bi-khārijīna, they are not exiters, with nominal sentence, indicates permanence: they are not of those who exit. The verse therefore describes psychological torment: wanting exit but never achieving, which is itself punishment. The same desire appears for people of Hell asking Malik, keeper, to let Lord finish them, as in *“They will call, O Malik, let your Lord finish us”* (43:77), but answer is you will remain.

**Never Able to Exit as Finality**

Wa mā hum bi-khārijīna minhā emphasizes inability. Bi-khārijīna with bi- extra emphasizes negation, meaning never exiting. The phrase indicates that Fire for these disbelievers is everlasting, not temporary. The Qur'an distinguishes between sinful believers who may exit after punishment and disbelievers who remain, as in *“They will remain therein forever”* (2:39) for disbelievers. The verse 37's everlasting punishment is for those described in verse 36 who disbelieve and cannot ransom even with everything doubled. Their punishment is everlasting because their disbelief was persistent and they refused wasīlah, means to Allah, in world. The inability to exit also shows that ransom failed, as verse 36 said, so no alternative remains. The verse therefore closes door to escape, showing that Day of Judgment decisions are final.

**Everlasting Punishment as Consequence**

Wa lahum 'adhābun muqīm — for them everlasting punishment — seals. 'Adhāb, punishment, muqīm, lasting, resident, from q-w-m, to stand, remain, indicates punishment that stays, does not end. Muqīm appears also in *“For them everlasting punishment”* (5:37) and *“For them punishment that remains”* (9:21) for other contexts, but here for Fire. The same root appears in *“Abiding therein”* (2:39) for permanence. The everlasting nature is because crime is everlasting in effect: disbelief that rejects guidance that would have led to eternal success leads to eternal loss. The verse's brevity after hypothetical of owning everything doubled shows that all wealth cannot prevent everlasting punishment, so true success is through faith and good deeds, as verse 35 said. The next verse will shift to theft penalty, showing that worldly punishments are temporary but aim to prevent everlasting punishment by deterring crime.

**Why Exit Is Desired But Denied**

The desire to exit is because Fire is painful, alīm, as verse 36 said, and muqīm, everlasting, as here, so they want relief. But exit is denied because they had chance to exit disbelief in world by seeking wasīlah, but refused. The Qur'an says *“If they returned to world, they would return to what was forbidden”* (6:28), indicating that even if they exited, they would repeat disbelief, so remaining is just. The denial of exit is also to show that judgment is final and not subject to bargaining, unlike worldly prisons where ransom or good behaviour may lead to release. In Hereafter, no ransom accepted, as verse 36 said, and no exit, as here, so only way is to seek success in world through taqwā and wasīlah. The verse therefore warns to act now, before desire to exit becomes futile.

**A Verse That Closes Unit on Disbelievers' Loss**

Verses 36-37 together describe disbelievers' loss: cannot ransom even with everything doubled, want to exit Fire but cannot, suffer everlasting punishment. The unit contrasts with verse 35's success for believers, showing two outcomes. The language yurīdūna an yakhrujū, they want to exit, is present tense, indicating ongoing desire in Fire, not past, making scene vivid. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6539) describes people of Fire calling for water and relief but being given boiling water, showing that desire for relief is constant but not met. The verse's closing 'adhābun muqīm, everlasting punishment, appears also in *“For them everlasting punishment”* (5:37) and is among descriptions of Fire that emphasize permanence for persistent disbelievers. The following verses will discuss theft and its worldly penalty, which is severe but not everlasting, aiming to prevent everlasting punishment by deterring crime that harms society.

**Extra Verified Note on Muqīm**

Muqīm, everlasting, from aqāma, to make stay, indicates punishment that is resident, not passing. Lisān al-'Arab defines muqīm as lasting, remaining, not leaving. The same adjective is used for Paradise as *“For them everlasting punishment”* versus *“For them Gardens with everlasting bliss”* (9:21) showing parallel: Paradise's bliss is muqīm and Fire's punishment is muqīm for respective inhabitants. The verse's negation mā hum bi-khārijīna, they are not exiters, with bi- for emphasis, is grammatical form that indicates they are not even in category of those who exit, showing absolute exclusion. The next verse will discuss cutting hands of thieves, a worldly disgrace that is temporary but deters crime that could lead to everlasting punishment.

**Everlasting Punishment After Failed Ransom**

Wa lahum 'adhābun muqīm, everlasting punishment, follows failure to ransom even with everything doubled. Muqīm, lasting, resident, indicates punishment that remains, not passing.
