The same adjective is used for Paradise bliss, showing parallel: Paradise bliss is muqīm for believers, Fire punishment muqīm for persistent disbelievers. The desire yurīdūna an yakhrujū, they want to exit, is continuous, but mā hum bi-khārijīna, they are not exiters, shows finality. The verse closes unit on disbelievers' loss, contrasting with believers' success through taqwā and wasīlah. The next verse will discuss theft penalty, which is severe but temporary, aiming to prevent everlasting punishment by deterring crime.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:38

> As for male and female thieves, cut off their hands for what they have done—a deterrent from Allah. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise

**Expanded Commentary**

**Male and Female Thieves as Equal in Penalty**

Wa al-sāriqu wa al-sāriqatu fa-iqṭa'ū aydiyahumā prescribes penalty for theft. Sāriq, male thief, and sāriqah, female thief, both mentioned to show equality in law, no gender distinction. Sāriq from s-r-q, to steal secretly, taking property of other stealthily without permission. Fa-iqṭa'ū aydiyahumā, cut off their hands, iqṭa'ū imperative plural, addressing community or authority, aydiyahumā, their hands, dual, indicates hand of each, though some interpret as right hand of each. The penalty is severe because theft violates sanctity of property that verse 32 said is like all humanity in importance, and because theft, if unchecked, leads to spreading mischief, as verse 33 discussed. The same penalty appears in *“Cut off hands of male and female thieves”* (5:38) itself, and is among ḥudūd, fixed penalties. The mention of both genders shows that law applies equally, not only to men.

**For What They Have Done as Just Recompense**

Jazā'an bi-mā kasabā — recompense for what they earned — explains reason: penalty is for what they earned, kasabā from k-s-b, to earn, acquire, indicates theft is earning through unlawful means, so penalty is recompense that fits earning. The phrase appears also in *“Recompense for what they earned”* (5:38) itself, emphasizing justice: they earned sin by stealing, so they earn penalty. The concept of kasb, earning, appears in *“Every soul will be recompensed for what it earned”* (2:281), showing that worldly and otherworldly recompense both follow earning. The penalty is therefore not arbitrary but jazā', recompense that matches crime. The theft must meet conditions: property taken secretly from secure place, ḥirz, with minimum value, niṣāb, and without doubt, as jurists detailed from Sunnah. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6783) says hand cut for quarter dinar or more, establishing niṣāb. So cutting is not for trivial theft but for significant breach of trust.

**Deterrent From Allah as Purpose**

Nakālan min Allāh — deterrent from Allah — gives purpose: nakāl, deterrent, exemplary punishment that deters others, from n-k-l, to make example, prevent. Min Allāh, from Allah, indicates deterrent is divinely ordained, not human invention, and that its wisdom is divine. The same term appears in *“Deterrent from Allah”* (5:38) itself. The purpose of severe penalty is deterrence, not revenge: by making example of thief, others are deterred from theft, protecting property of all. The verse therefore shows that ḥudūd aim at protecting society, as verse 32 said saving one life is like saving all, similarly protecting one property protects all. The deterrent is from Allah, so it carries authority and wisdom, not cruelty. The same purpose appears for other ḥudūd, such as for ḥirābah which is disgrace in world to deter. The deterrent is public, so that people see consequence.

**Allah Is Almighty, All-Wise as Seal**

Wa Allāhu 'Azīz Ḥakīm — Allah is Almighty, All-Wise — seals with two names. 'Azīz, Almighty, from '-z-z, to be mighty, invincible, indicates Allah has power to legislate and enforce, and that no one can prevent His penalty. Ḥakīm, All-Wise, from ḥ-k-m, to judge with wisdom, indicates penalty is wise, not harsh without purpose. The pairing appears after many ḥudūd verses, as in *“Allah is Almighty, All-Wise”* (5:38) and *“Allah is Almighty, All-Wise”* (5:118) at end of sūrah, showing that might and wisdom go together: might ensures law enforced, wisdom ensures law is just. Al-Rāzī notes that 'Azīz is appropriate because cutting hand requires power to enforce against criminal's resistance, and Ḥakīm because cutting must be done with conditions that ensure justice, not arbitrary. The seal therefore reassures that penalty, though severe, is from One who is both powerful and wise.

**A Verse That Protects Property**

This verse protects property, which is among five essentials that Islamic law protects: religion, life, intellect, lineage, property. Theft violates property, so penalty protects it. The verse's placement after verses about ḥirābah, which is theft with violence, shows gradation: ḥirābah is more severe, theft is less but still serious. The condition of cutting hand is debated: majority say right hand for first theft, left foot for second if repeats, etc., but all agree that repentance after theft does not remove ḥadd if reported to authority, though repentance before report may affect, as next verse will show Allah turns to those who repent and mend. The verse also shows equality: male and female thieves same penalty, indicating that property rights are equal. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6788) about woman from Makhzūm who stole and Prophet ﷺ ordered hand cut despite intercession, saying if Fatimah stole I would cut hand, shows that law applies even to noble.

**Extra Verified Detail on Niṣāb**

The niṣāb, minimum value for cutting, is verified as quarter dinar or three dirhams, based on ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6789) and Muslim (1684) that Prophet ﷺ cut hand for quarter dinar. The secure place, ḥirz, is place where property is normally kept, like house, shop, safe, and taking from non-secure place may not warrant cutting but ta'zīr, discretionary punishment. The hand cut is right hand from wrist, as practiced. The verse's purpose nakālan min Allāh is also used for other deterrent penalties, showing consistent philosophy: make example to prevent. The following verse will show that repentance after wrongdoing and mending leads to forgiveness, balancing severity with mercy.

**Equality and Deterrence in Theft Law**

Wa al-sāriqu wa al-sāriqatu, male and female thieves, shows equality: both genders same penalty, indicating property rights equal. Sāriq from s-r-q, to steal secretly, requires taking from secure place, ḥirz, with minimum value, niṣāb, quarter dinar as ḥadīth in Bukhārī (6783) establishes. The penalty iqṭa'ū aydiyahumā, cut hands, is deterrent, nakālan min Allāh, from Allah, making example to protect all property. The seal 'Azīz Ḥakīm, Almighty All-Wise, shows might to enforce and wisdom to condition, so cutting is not for trivial theft but significant breach. The next verse will offer forgiveness for repentance and mending, balancing deterrence with mercy.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:39

> But whoever repents after their wrongdoing and mends their ways, Allah will surely turn to them in forgiveness. Indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Expanded Commentary**

**Repentance After Wrongdoing and Mending**

Fa-man tāba min ba'di ẓulmihi wa aṣlaḥa provides way after theft penalty. Fa-man, whoever, tāba, repents, from t-w-b, to return, min ba'di ẓulmihi, after his wrongdoing, ẓulm from ẓ-l-m, to place thing not in its place, wrong, here theft, wa aṣlaḥa, and mends, aṣlaḥa from ṣ-l-ḥ, to make sound, correct, indicates repentance must include mending: returning stolen property, making amends, correcting behaviour. The same combination appears in *“Whoever repents after wrongdoing and mends”* (5:39) itself and in *“Whoever repents and does good”* (25:71). The condition shows that tawbah is not only remorse but action: leave sin, regret, resolve not to return, and restore rights. For thief, mending includes returning property or compensating, as jurists agree that repentance does not remove obligation to return stolen goods. The verse therefore offers hope after severe penalty: even thief can repent and mend, and Allah will forgive.

**Allah Will Turn to Them in Forgiveness**

Fa-inna Allāha yatūbu 'alayhi — Allah will surely turn to them in forgiveness — promises acceptance of repentance. Yatūbu 'alayhi, He turns to him, from t-w-b, same root as tāba, indicates Allah returns to servant with mercy when servant returns to Allah with repentance. The phrase appears also in *“Allah will turn to them”* (5:39) and in *“Allah turns to those who repent”* (2:222). The emphatic fa-inna, indeed, shows certainty. The turning is both worldly and otherworldly: in world, repentance may affect punishment if before capture, and in Hereafter, forgiveness removes sin. The verse does not say worldly ḥadd is automatically removed after repentance if crime already reported, but says Allah will turn in forgiveness, indicating otherworldly forgiveness is assured with sincere repentance and mending. The same promise appears for ḥirābah in verse 34 where repentance before seizure removes fixed penalty, showing consistency: repentance before authority has power leads to waiving ḥadd, but after, forgiveness is still possible in Hereafter.

**All-Forgiving, Most Merciful as Seal**

Inna Allāha Ghafūr Raḥīm — Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful — seals with mercy after severity. Ghafūr, intensely forgiving, and Raḥīm, Most Merciful, as before, indicate that even after cutting hand, forgiveness is possible. The pairing appears after many repentance verses, as in *“Allah is Forgiving, Merciful”* (5:39) itself and *“Allah is Forgiving, Merciful”* (5:34) for ḥirābah. The seal shows that Islamic law's purpose is not to brand sinner forever but to reform: penalty deters, repentance and mending restores. The verse therefore balances verse 38's severity with mercy, showing that law is firm but not hopeless. Al-Rāzī notes that Ghafūr relates to covering sin so it does not lead to punishment in Hereafter, and Raḥīm relates to granting mercy and good after forgiveness. The combination after theft shows that thief who repents and mends is not only forgiven but shown mercy.

**Why Mending Is Required Alongside Repentance**

Wa aṣlaḥa, and mends, is required because theft harms victim, so repentance must include restoring right. The same requirement appears for other crimes: for slander, repentance requires clarification, for mischief, requires leaving mischief. The Qur'an says *“Except those who repent, mend, and make clear”* (2:160) for those who concealed knowledge, showing that mending depends on crime. For thief, mending is returning property, as ḥadīth in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (3565) says hand of thief not cut if he returns property before report, indicating that mending before authority can affect. The verse's placement after theft penalty shows that even after worldly penalty, mending is still needed for otherworldly forgiveness. The concept of iṣlāḥ, mending, is broad: it includes correcting behaviour, making amends, and doing good to compensate, as *“Good deeds remove evil deeds”* (11:114) states.

**A Verse That Offers Hope After Deterrent**

Verses 38-39 together show deterrence and hope: verse 38 prescribes cutting as deterrent from Allah, Almighty, All-Wise, verse 39 offers forgiveness for those who repent and mend, All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. The two seals — 'Azīz Ḥakīm and Ghafūr Raḥīm — show two aspects of divine: might and wisdom in legislation, forgiveness and mercy in accepting repentance. The balance is characteristic of sūrah: severity for public crime, mercy for repentant. The verse also applies beyond theft to all wrongdoing, as wording is general: whoever repents after wrongdoing and mends, Allah will turn to him. It therefore generalizes principle: repentance plus mending leads to forgiveness. A verified linguistic note: ẓulm here means wrongdoing, specifically theft, but general term shows that any wrong can be repented. The next verse will remind that kingdom belongs to Allah, He punishes and forgives whom He wills, reinforcing that forgiveness is by divine will, not automatic.

**Extra Verified Note on Return of Property**

Return of stolen property as part of mending is verified in fiqh: thief must return property even after hand cut, because cutting is ḥaqq Allāh, right of Allah, while property is ḥaqq al-'abd, right of person, and one does not waive other. Al-Ṭabarī records that Prophet ﷺ said thief must return property, and jurists agree. The phrase fa-inna Allāha yatūbu 'alayhi, Allah will turn to him, is also used for those who repent from ḥirābah before capture, showing that Allah's turning is promise for sincere repentance. The following verse will remind that kingdom belongs to Allah, He punishes and forgives whom He wills, showing that forgiveness is by His will and wisdom.

**Mending as Part of Repentance**

Wa aṣlaḥa, and mends, requires returning stolen property and correcting behaviour, because theft harms victim. Tawbah without iṣlāḥ is incomplete. The same requirement appears for those who concealed knowledge: repent, mend, make clear (2:160). For thief, mending is returning goods, as jurists agree cutting is ḥaqq Allāh, right of Allah, while property is ḥaqq al-'abd, right of person, and one does not waive other. The promise fa-inna Allāha yatūbu 'alayhi, Allah will turn to him, shows that with sincere repentance and mending, forgiveness is assured, and seal Ghafūr Raḥīm, Forgiving Merciful, shows mercy after severity. The next verse will remind that kingdom belongs to Allah, He punishes and forgives whom He wills, Most Capable.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:40

> Do you not know that the kingdom of the heavens and the earth belongs to Allah ˹alone˺? He punishes whoever He wills and forgives whoever He wills. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**Expanded Commentary**

**Kingdom of Heavens and Earth Belongs to Allah**

A-lam ta'lam anna Allāha lahu mulk al-samāwāt wa al-arḍ asks rhetorical question. A-lam ta'lam, do you not know, ta'lam from '-l-m, to know, indicates that knowledge is expected, and question is to affirm. Anna Allāha lahu mulk al-samāwāt wa al-arḍ, that to Allah belongs kingdom of heavens and earth, mulk as before, kingdom, dominion, ownership. The same phrase appears in *“To Allah belongs kingdom of heavens and earth”* (5:17, 18, 40) repeated, showing that sovereignty is basis for legislation about theft, ḥirābah, and forgiveness. Lahu, to Him, fronted for emphasis: to Allah alone belongs kingdom, not to thieves or tyrants. The question a-lam ta'lam appears also in *“Do you not know that kingdom belongs to Allah”* (5:40) itself, prompting reflection. The knowledge that kingdom belongs to Allah implies that He can legislate as He wills, as verse 1 said *“Allah commands what He wills”* (5:1), and that He can punish and forgive as He wills, as next clause says.

**He Punishes Whom He Wills and Forgives Whom He Wills**

Yu'adhdhibu man yashā' wa yaghfiru li-man yashā' — He punishes whom He wills and forgives whom He wills — states discretion. Yu'adhdhibu, punishes, yaghfiru, forgives, man yashā', whom He wills, li-man yashā', for whom He wills, shows that punishment and forgiveness are by divine will that is wise, not arbitrary. The same pairing appears in *“He punishes whom He wills and forgives whom He wills”* (5:40) and in *“He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills”* (5:18) with order reversed, showing both are possible. The order here — punish then forgive — matches context of theft penalty followed by forgiveness for repentant, showing that punishment is mentioned first because verse 38 prescribed cutting, then forgiveness for repentant in verse 39, now general principle that both are by His will. The will is not random but based on wisdom: He punishes those who persist in wrongdoing and forgives those who repent and mend, as previous verses showed. The phrase therefore explains why some are punished and some forgiven: it is by will that considers repentance and mending.

**Allah Is Most Capable of Everything as Seal**

Wa Allāhu 'alā kulli shay'in qadīr — Allah is Most Capable of everything — seals with capability, as in verses 17, 19, and 40 itself. Qadīr, intensely capable, indicates that punishing and forgiving are within capability, and that legislating severe penalties and accepting repentance are both possible for Him. The phrase appears over thirty times, often after mention of kingdom and will, as here, showing that capability is basis for sovereignty. The seal also reassures that forgiveness is not due to inability to punish, and punishment is not due to inability to forgive, but both are by capable will. The verse therefore ties together sovereignty, will, and capability: because kingdom belongs to Allah, He can punish and forgive whom He wills, and He is capable of everything, so His decisions are enforceable and wise.

**Why Question Form Is Used**

A-lam ta'lam, do you not know, is rhetorical question that expects yes, you know, and also rebukes those who act as if they do not know. Those who steal act as if kingdom belongs to them to take, and those who despair of forgiveness act as if Allah cannot forgive, but question reminds that kingdom belongs to Allah, so He can punish theft and forgive repentance. The same question appears in *“Do you not know that Allah has power over everything”* (2:106) and *“Do you not know that Allah knows what is in heavens and earth”* (22:70), always to affirm knowledge that should affect behaviour. Here it affirms that Allah's kingdom includes heavens and earth, so His law covers all, and His will in punishing and forgiving is part of kingdom. The question also invites reflection that leads to taqwā, mindfulness, as earlier verses commanded.

**A Verse That Links Legislation to Sovereignty**

Verses 38-40 form unit on theft: verse 38 prescribes cutting as deterrent, Almighty, All-Wise, verse 39 offers forgiveness for repentance and mending, All-Forgiving, Most Merciful, verse 40 explains that kingdom belongs to Allah, He punishes and forgives whom He wills, Most Capable. The unit shows that legislation comes from sovereign, punishment and forgiveness are by His will, and capability ensures enforcement. The repetition of mulk al-samāwāt wa al-arḍ in this sūrah — verses 17, 18, 40, 120 — emphasizes that all rulings, whether about food, ḥirābah, or theft, stem from same sovereignty. A verified linguistic note: mulk means kingdom, dominion, ownership, and lahu mulk indicates exclusive ownership, as lām indicates possession. The phrase yu'adhdhibu man yashā' wa yaghfiru li-man yashā' appears with both orders in Qur'an, showing that neither punishment nor forgiveness is default, both are by will. The next verse will address Prophet's grief over those who race to disbelief, showing that even with sovereignty and capability, some still choose disbelief.

**Extra Verified Note on Punishment and Forgiveness**

The pairing punish whom He wills and forgive whom He wills is verified as reflecting divine justice and mercy: will is based on wisdom, not arbitrary, as *“He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills, and Allah is Capable”* (48:14) and other verses show. The order punish then forgive here matches context of theft where punishment is prescribed then forgiveness offered, while order forgive then punish in verse 18 matches context of claim of being beloved where forgiveness is hoped then punishment warned. The variation shows rhetorical fit. The verse's closing qadīr, capable, appears also at end of sūrah: *“Allah is Capable of everything”* (5:120), framing sūrah with capability. The following verse will discuss those who race to disbelief, showing that despite capability, some choose disbelief.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:41

> O Messenger! Do not grieve for those who race to disbelieve—those who say, “We believe” with their tongues, but their hearts are in disbelief. Nor those among the Jews who eagerly listen to lies, attentive to those who are too arrogant to come to you. They distort the Scripture, taking rulings out of context, then say, “If this is the ruling you get ˹from Muḥammad˺, accept it. If not, beware!” Whoever Allah allows to be deluded, you can never be of any help to them against Allah. It is not Allah’s Will to purify their hearts. For them is disgrace in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter

**Expanded Commentary**

**Do Not Grieve for Those Who Race to Disbelief**

Yā ayyuhā al-rasūl lā yaḥzunka alladhīna yusāri'ūna fī al-kufr consoles Messenger. Lā yaḥzunka, do not grieve you, yaḥzun from ḥ-z-n, to grieve, and yusāri'ūna fī al-kufr, race to disbelief, from s-r-', to be quick, race, indicates they hasten to disbelief, competing in it. The phrase appears also in *“Do not grieve for those who race to disbelief”* (5:41) itself and in *“Those who race to disbelief will not harm Allah”* (3:176). The racing shows eagerness, not reluctance. Two groups are described: those who say we believe with tongues but hearts disbelieve, munāfiqūn, hypocrites, and Jews who listen to lies. The first group says āmannā bi-afwāhihim wa lam tu'min qulūbuhum — we believe with mouths but hearts did not believe — showing contradiction between tongue and heart. The second, samā'ūna li-l-kadhib, eager listeners to lies, from s-m-', to listen, and kadhib, lie, indicates they listen to falsehood and accept it. The verse tells Prophet ﷺ not to grieve because their racing does not harm Allah or Messenger, only themselves.

**Listening to Lies and Attentive to Absent Arrogant**

Samā'ūna li-l-kadhib samā'ūna li-qawmin ākharīna lam ya'tūka describes Jewish leaders who listen to lies and are attentive to others who do not come to Prophet. Samā'ūna li-l-kadhib, listeners to falsehood, indicates they accept lies about Torah and about Prophet, and samā'ūna li-qawmin ākharīna, listeners to other people who did not come to you, indicates they act as spies or intermediaries for absent leaders who are too arrogant to come. Early reports say Jewish leaders in Khaybar sent people to Madinah to ask Prophet about stoning, with instruction if he says stoning, accept, if not, beware. They distorted scripture about stoning, hiding it, and wanted to follow desire. The verse therefore describes two layers of listening to falsehood: listening to lies about scripture and listening to absent arrogant who instruct them. The same pattern appears for hypocrites who listen to enemies. The phrase lam ya'tūka, did not come to you, shows arrogance: they are too proud to come themselves, sending others.

**Distorting Scripture Taking Rulings Out of Context**

Yuḥarrifūna al-kalim min ba'di mawāḍi'ihi — they distort words after they have been placed — similar to verse 13 but with min ba'di, after places, indicating distortion after proper placement. They take rulings out of context, then say in ūtītum hādhā fa-khudhūhu wa in lam tu'tawhu fa-iḥdharū — if you are given this, take it, if not, beware — showing they pick and choose rulings based on desire, not truth. The example is stoning: Torah says stoning for adultery, but they wanted lighter penalty, so they said if Muḥammad gives lighter, accept, if says stoning, beware. This is precisely taking ruling out of context and following desire, as verse 48 will warn *“Do not follow their desires”* (5:48). The verse therefore exposes method of distortion: hide true ruling, then present alternative, then instruct followers to accept only if it matches desire. The same method is used by those who follow caprice in judging.

**Whoever Allah Allows to Be Deluded Cannot Be Helped**

Wa man yuridi Allāhu fitnatahu fa-lan tamlika lahu min Allāhi shay'an — whoever Allah allows to be deluded, you cannot help them against Allah — states limit of prophetic help. Fitnah here means trial, delusion, punishment that leads astray, from f-t-n, to test with fire. Man yuridi Allāhu fitnatahu, whom Allah wills to put in trial, indicates that persistent following of lies and distortion leads to divine letting them be deluded as consequence. Fa-lan tamlika lahu min Allāhi shay'an, you will never have power for him against Allah anything, tamlika from m-l-k, to own, control, shows Prophet cannot protect them from Allah's judgment if Allah wills their delusion as result of their choice. The same phrase appears in *“You will never have power for him against Allah”* (5:41). The verse therefore balances consolation with warning: do not grieve, but also understand that some hearts are not purified because they chose lies, as next clause says.

**Hearts Not Purified, Disgrace and Tremendous Punishment**

Ulā'ika alladhīna lam yuridi Allāhu an yuṭahhira qulūbahum — they are those whose hearts Allah did not will to purify — indicates that purification of heart requires divine will plus human effort; when humans persistently choose lies and distortion, Allah does not will to purify. Qulūb, hearts, purification is needed because heart is source of belief, as *“In hearts is disease”* (2:10) for hypocrites. Lahum fī al-dunyā khizyun wa lahum fī al-ākhirati 'adhābun 'aẓīm — for them disgrace in world and tremendous punishment in Hereafter — shows two-level consequence: khizy, disgrace, in world, through exposure of lies and failure, and 'adhāb 'aẓīm, tremendous punishment, in Hereafter. The same closing appears in verse 33 for ḥirābah and here for distortion, showing that distorting scripture and racing to disbelief is also grave. The verse therefore consoles Messenger that their disgrace is already beginning, and tremendous punishment awaits, so grief is unnecessary.

**Extra Verified Note on Stoning Incident**

The stoning incident is verified as occasion of revelation: Jewish couple committed adultery, Jewish leaders brought them to Prophet ﷺ hoping for lighter penalty, Prophet asked about Torah ruling, they hid stoning verse, 'Abdullāh ibn Salām exposed it, Prophet ordered stoning, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3635) and Muslim (1699). The verse's description yuḥarrifūna al-kalim min ba'di mawāḍi'ihi matches that hiding. The phrase samā'ūna li-l-kadhib, listeners to lies, is interpreted as listening to false rabbis who permit lies. The verse's consolation lā yaḥzunka is also in *“Do not grieve for those who race to disbelief”* (3:176) for similar context, showing consistent prophetic consolation. The next verse will discuss listening to falsehood and consuming forbidden gain, continuing description of same group.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:42

> They eagerly listen to falsehood and consume forbidden gain. So if they come to you ˹O Prophet˺, either judge between them or turn away from them. If you turn away from them, they cannot harm you whatsoever. But if you judge between them, then do so with justice. Surely Allah loves those who are just

**Expanded Commentary**

**Listening to Falsehood and Consuming Forbidden Gain**

Samā'ūna li-l-kadhib akkālūna li-l-suḥt describes same group as previous verse. Samā'ūna li-l-kadhib, eager listeners to falsehood, repeated for emphasis, and akkālūna li-l-suḥt, consumers of forbidden gain, akkālūna from a-k-l, to eat, consume, and suḥt from s-ḥ-t, to peel, strip, means unlawful gain that strips blessing, such as bribery, usury, and taking payment for distorting scripture. Suḥt is described as gain that is peeled away from good, leaving no barakah. The same phrase appears in *“They listen to falsehood and consume forbidden gain”* (5:42) itself. The two sins are linked: listening to lies leads to consuming forbidden gain, because when truth is distorted for desire, gain follows. Early reports say Jewish leaders took bribes to change rulings, such as taking payment to hide stoning. The verse therefore diagnoses corruption: falsehood in knowledge and forbidden gain in wealth, both stemming from following desire.

**Option to Judge or Turn Away**

Fa-in jā'ūka fa-uḥkum baynahum aw a'riḍ 'anhum — if they come to you, judge between them or turn away — gives Prophet option. Jā'ūka, they come to you, indicates they seek judgment from Prophet despite having Torah, as next verse will question. Fa-uḥkum baynahum, judge between them, or a'riḍ 'anhum, turn away from them, shows that Prophet is not obligated to judge between People of Book who have own scripture, but may if he chooses. The option was later understood as abrogated by verse 49 which commands judging by what Allah revealed, but some say option remains when they seek judgment. Al-Ṭabarī notes that turning away is allowed when they come seeking desire-based ruling, not sincere justice. The choice gives Prophet discretion to avoid being used for their games. The same option appears for other cases where People of Book seek Muslim judgment.

**If You Turn Away They Cannot Harm You**

Wa in tu'riḍ 'anhum fa-lan yaḍurrūka shay'an — if you turn away from them, they cannot harm you at all — reassures. Tu'riḍ, turn away, yaḍurrūka, harm you, from ḍ-r-r, to harm, shay'an, anything, indicates that their plots cannot harm Prophet if he turns away, because Allah protects, as *“Allah will protect you from people”* (5:67) promises. The reassurance is needed because Jewish leaders were powerful and could slander, but verse says no harm if he turns away. The same assurance appears in *“They cannot harm you whatsoever”* (5:42) itself. The protection is both physical and reputational: turning away does not make him look unjust, because they have Torah. The verse therefore gives Prophet freedom without fear.

**If You Judge, Judge With Justice**

Wa in ḥakamta fa-uḥkum baynahum bi-l-qisṭ — if you judge, judge between them with justice — commands justice when judging. Ḥakamta, you judge, uḥkum, judge, bi-l-qisṭ, with equity, qisṭ as in verse 8, measured justice that gives each due. The command shows that even for those who listen to falsehood and consume forbidden gain, justice is required, not bias. The same command appears in *“Judge between them with justice”* (5:42) itself and in *“Be just, that is closer to righteousness”* (5:8). The justice required is not based on their desire but on what Allah revealed, as verse 48 will clarify: judge by what Allah revealed. Al-Qurṭubī notes that qisṭ here means justice that is apparent and measured, requiring evidence, not whim. The verse therefore teaches that justice is owed even to corrupt, and that judge must not be influenced by their listening to lies or consuming forbidden gain.

**Allah Loves Those Who Are Just**

Inna Allāha yuḥibbu al-muqsiṭīn — Allah loves those who are just — seals with love for just. Muqsiṭīn, from q-s-ṭ, those who do qisṭ, justice, indicates that justice is beloved. The same seal appears in *“Allah loves those who are just”* (5:42) and in *“Allah loves just”* (49:9) and *“Allah loves those who are just”* (60:8) for dealing with non-Muslims justly. The love for just is motive: judge justly because Allah loves just, not only to be fair to them. The verse therefore elevates justice from civic duty to act of worship that earns divine love. The pairing of justice and love appears after many justice commands, as in verse 8 where justice is closer to taqwā. Here justice earns love, which is higher than closeness. The verse therefore encourages Prophet to judge justly if he judges, because that earns love, and reassures that turning away also causes no harm.

**Extra Verified Note on Suḥt**

Suḥt, forbidden gain, is defined as bribery and taking payment for changing rulings, as verified in tafsīr: Ibn Mas'ūd said suḥt is bribery, and al-Ḥasan said it is taking payment for judging. Lisān al-'Arab defines suḥt as unlawful gain that is stripped of blessing, and as something that is peeled away. The same term appears in *“They consume forbidden gain”* (5:42, 62) for same group. The option to judge or turn away is discussed in fiqh as whether Muslim judge must judge between dhimmīs, People of Book under protection, when they seek judgment; majority say he may but should judge justly by Islamic law if he does. The next verse will question why they come for judgment when they have Torah containing Allah's judgment, exposing inconsistency.

**Why Option Was Given**

The option fa-uḥkum aw a'riḍ, judge or turn away, was given because People of Book have own book with Allah's judgment, so Prophet not obligated to judge their internal disputes unless they seek him. The option also tests their sincerity: if they seek judgment hoping for desire-based ruling, turning away exposes insincerity. The later command in verse 49 to judge by what Allah revealed shows that when they do come, judgment must be by revelation, not desire. The reassurance fa-lan yaḍurrūka shay'an, they cannot harm you, shows that turning away does not cause harm because Allah protects. The next verse questions why they come when Torah has Allah's judgment, exposing inconsistency and showing that seeking judgment from Prophet while having Torah is not out of belief but desire.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:43

> But why do they come to you for judgment when they ˹already˺ have the Torah containing Allah’s judgment, then they turn away after all? They are not ˹true˺ believers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why Seek Judgment When Torah Contains Judgment**

Wa kayfa yuḥakkimūnaka wa 'indahum al-tawrāt fīhā ḥukm Allāh questions inconsistency. Kayfa, how, yuḥakkimūnaka, they make you judge, wa 'indahum al-tawrāt, while they have Torah, fīhā ḥukm Allāh, in it is Allah's judgment, shows they have scripture containing ruling, yet seek Prophet's judgment hoping for lighter. The question kayfa is rhetorical, expressing amazement at inconsistency: they have Torah with stoning ruling, yet come to you. The same question appears in *“Why do they come to you for judgment when they have Torah”* (5:43) itself. The Torah's ḥukm Allāh, Allah's judgment, refers to stoning for adultery and other rulings, which they hid. Their coming to Prophet is not out of belief in his prophethood but out of desire for easier ruling, as verse 41 described they said if you are given this, take it, if not, beware. The verse therefore exposes that their seeking judgment is not sincere search for truth but search for desire.

**Then They Turn Away After That**

Thumma yatawallawna min ba'di dhālika — then they turn away after that — shows that even after Prophet judges with justice by what Allah revealed, which matches Torah's true ruling, they turn away. Yatawallawna, turn away, from w-l-y, to turn, indicates they turn away from judgment when it does not match desire. Min ba'di dhālika, after that, after judgment or after having Torah, indicates persistent turning. The same phrase appears in *“They turn away after all”* (5:43). The turning away shows they are not seeking truth but desire, because if they sought truth, they would accept judgment that matches their own book's true ruling. Al-Ṭabarī notes that they turned away after Prophet ordered stoning, which matched Torah, showing they did not want true Torah ruling either, but wanted to avoid it. The verse therefore shows double inconsistency: they have Torah but do not follow it, and they seek Prophet's judgment but turn away when it matches Torah.

**They Are Not True Believers**

Wa mā ulā'ika bi-l-mu'minīn — they are not believers — concludes. Mā ulā'ika bi-l-mu'minīn, they are not believers, indicates they are not true believers in Torah nor in Qur'an, because believer follows judgment of his book. The negation is strong: they are not in category of mu'minīn at all. The same phrase appears in *“They are not true believers”* (5:43) itself. The verse therefore judges their faith claim: having Torah and not following it, seeking Prophet and turning away, shows lack of belief. The same judgment appears for those who say we believe with mouths but hearts disbelieve in verse 41. The verse's placement after option to judge or turn away shows that even if Prophet judges justly, they will turn away, so turning away from them causes no harm, as verse 42 said. The next verse will praise Torah as containing guidance and light, showing that true judgment is in Torah, which they neglected.

**Inconsistency as Sign of Following Desire**

The inconsistency — having Torah with Allah's judgment yet seeking other judgment, then turning away when judgment matches Torah — is sign of following desire, hawā, not truth. The Qur'an warns *“Do not follow desire, lest it lead you astray from Allah's way”* (38:26) and *“Do not follow their desires over truth that has come to you”* (5:48). The Jews' behaviour exemplifies following desire: they wanted lighter penalty for adultery, so they hid stoning and sought Prophet hoping for lighter, but when Prophet gave stoning, which is true Torah ruling, they turned away, showing they did not want Torah either, only desire. The verse therefore teaches that following desire leads to inconsistency: one has scripture but does not follow, seeks other judge but does not accept. The remedy is to judge by what Allah revealed, as verses 44-48 will command.

**A Verse That Exposes Selective Seeking of Judgment**

This verse exposes selective seeking of judgment, where people seek judge only when they expect favourable ruling, not when they seek truth. The same behaviour appears today when people seek fatwā shopping, looking for scholar who gives desired answer. The verse's question kayfa, how, expresses amazement that should lead to reflection: why seek other judge when you have book with Allah's judgment. The answer is desire. The verse also shows that having book is not enough; following it matters. The Torah contained guidance and light, as next verse will say, but they neglected portion and distorted, so having it did not benefit. The same applies to Muslims who have Qur'an but do not judge by it. The next verse will praise Torah as containing guidance and light, by which prophets judged, showing what true following looks like.

**Extra Verified Detail on Torah Judgment**

The Torah's ḥukm Allāh, Allah's judgment, for adultery is stoning, as verified by Torah Deuteronomy 22:22-24 and by ḥadīth in Bukhārī (3635) where Prophet asked Jewish scholars about stoning in Torah, they said we blacken faces and flog, but 'Abdullāh ibn Salām said Torah says stoning, and they found it. The verse's question kayfa yuḥakkimūnaka, how do they make you judge, is also recited with different reading yuḥakkimūnaka, they make you judge, indicating they appoint you judge. The phrase wa mā ulā'ika bi-l-mu'minīn, they are not believers, is used also for those who do not judge by what Allah revealed in verses 44, 45, 47, showing that true belief requires judging by revelation. The following verse will detail Torah's guidance and light and role of prophets, rabbis, scholars as keepers.

**Seeking Judgment as Desire Shopping**

The question kayfa yuḥakkimūnaka, how do they make you judge, expresses amazement at selective seeking. They have Torah with ḥukm Allāh, Allah's judgment, yet seek Prophet hoping for lighter. When Prophet judges with justice that matches Torah's true ruling, they turn away, showing they do not want true Torah either, only desire. This is fatwā shopping, seeking scholar who gives desired answer. The verse's judgment wa mā ulā'ika bi-l-mu'minīn, they are not believers, shows that true belief requires following revelation, not shopping. The next verse will praise Torah as guidance and light, showing what true following looks like: prophets and rabbis judged by it, not trading for small price.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:44

> Indeed, We revealed the Torah, containing guidance and light, by which the prophets, who submitted themselves to Allah, made judgments for Jews. So too did the rabbis and scholars judge according to Allah’s Book, with which they were entrusted and of which they were made keepers. So do not fear the people; fear Me! Nor trade my revelations for a fleeting gain. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the disbelievers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Torah Revealed Containing Guidance and Light**

Innā anzalnā al-tawrāt fīhā hudan wa nūr affirms Torah's divine origin and content. Innā anzalnā, We revealed, anzalnā from n-z-l, to send down, indicates divine sending, al-tawrāt, Torah, fīhā hudan wa nūr, in it guidance and light, hudā from h-d-y, to guide, and nūr, light, as in verse 15-16 for Qur'an, showing that Torah also had guidance and light, not only Qur'an. The same phrase appears for Qur'an as *“Light and clear Book”* (5:15) and for Torah here, indicating continuity: both contain guidance and light. The Torah's guidance includes rulings like stoning, qiṣāṣ, and monotheism, and its light illuminates path to Allah. The verse therefore praises Torah, even while criticizing those who distorted it, showing that criticism is of people, not of original book. The revelation is attributed to Allah, innā anzalnā, We revealed, showing that Torah is from same source as Qur'an.

**Prophets Who Submitted Judged for Jews**

Yaḥkumu bihā al-nabiyyūn alladhīna aslamū li-lladhīna hādū — by it prophets who submitted judged for Jews — describes who judged by Torah. Yaḥkumu bihā, judge by it, al-nabiyyūn, prophets, alladhīna aslamū, who submitted, aslamū from s-l-m, to submit, indicates prophets who submitted to Allah, i.e., all prophets, not only Muslim prophets, but prophets who were muslim in sense of submitting. Li-lladhīna hādū, for Jews, hādū from h-w-d, to be Jewish, to return, indicates those who are Jews. The prophets judged for Jews by Torah, showing that prophetic role includes judging by revelation. The same appears in *“Prophets who submitted judged for Jews”* (5:44) itself. The phrase alladhīna aslamū emphasizes that true prophets submit to Allah, not to desire, so their judgment is by revelation. The verse therefore shows ideal: prophets judged by Torah containing guidance and light, not by desire.

**Rabbis and Scholars as Entrusted Keepers**

Wa al-rabbāniyyūn wa al-aḥbār bi-mā istuḥfiẓū min kitāb Allāh wa kānū 'alayhi shuhadā' adds rabbis and scholars. Rabbāniyyūn, rabbis, from r-b-b, lord, those who nurture knowledge and people, and aḥbār, scholars, plural of ḥibr, scholar, from ḥ-b-r, to beautify, ink, indicates learned who write and explain. Bi-mā istuḥfiẓū min kitāb Allāh, with what they were entrusted of Allah's Book, istuḥfiẓū from ḥ-f-ẓ, to guard, entrusted to guard, indicates they were given responsibility to preserve and judge by Book. Wa kānū 'alayhi shuhadā', and were witnesses over it, shuhadā' from sh-h-d, to witness, indicates they were supposed to witness truth and not conceal. The verse therefore describes two tiers of judges: prophets and then rabbis/scholars who were entrusted as keepers. The entrusting shows that preservation is human responsibility, and failure to preserve, as in distorting and forgetting portion, is breach of trust.

**Do Not Fear People, Fear Me, Do Not Trade Revelations**

Fa-lā takhshaw al-nāsa wa-khshawnī wa lā tashtarū bi-āyātī thamanan qalīlan commands fearing Allah, not people, and not trading revelations for small price. Lā takhshaw al-nāsa, do not fear people, takhshaw from kh-sh-y, to fear with reverence, and wa-khshawnī, fear Me, shows that fear of people leads to distorting for their pleasure, while fear of Allah leads to judging by truth. Wa lā tashtarū bi-āyātī thamanan qalīlan, do not trade My revelations for small price, ishtarā from sh-r-y, to buy, sell, trade, and thamanan qalīlan, small price, indicates worldly gain like bribery, status, or favour, which is small compared to Hereafter. The same warning appears in *“Do not trade My revelations for small price”* (2:41) to Israelites. The verse therefore warns scholars not to distort for fear of people or for worldly gain, which is exactly what samā'ūna li-l-kadhib akkālūna li-l-suḥt in verse 42 did: listened to lies and consumed forbidden gain, trading revelations for small price.

**Those Who Do Not Judge by What Allah Revealed Are Disbelievers**

Wa man lam yaḥkum bi-mā anzala Allāhu fa-ulā'ika hum al-kāfirūn gives severe judgment. Man lam yaḥkum, whoever does not judge, bi-mā anzala Allāhu, by what Allah revealed, fa-ulā'ika hum al-kāfirūn, they are disbelievers, indicates that refusing to judge by revelation out of rejection or belief that other judgment is better is disbelief. The same phrasing appears with variations for ẓālimūn, wrongdoers, in verse 45 and fāsiqūn, rebellious, in verse 47, showing degrees: disbelief if one denies obligation to judge by revelation, wrongdoing if one judges unjustly, rebellion if one exits obedience. Early exegetes like Ibn 'Abbās said this is not general disbelief that expels from Islam for every sin, but disbelief that is kufr dūna kufr, lesser disbelief, when one judges by other than revelation while believing revelation is better but following desire. The verse therefore warns against both denying revelation and following desire over it.

**Extra Verified Note on Rabbāniyyūn and Aḥbār**

Rabbāniyyūn is defined as those who nurture people with knowledge, from rabb, to nurture, and aḥbār as scholars who write, from ḥibr, ink, showing two roles: nurturing and writing. The phrase istuḥfiẓū min kitāb Allāh, entrusted with Book, is used to show that preservation of Torah was entrusted to scholars, unlike Qur'an which Allah promised to preserve Himself, as *“We will preserve it”* (15:9). The difference explains why Torah was distorted: entrusted to humans who failed, while Qur'an is preserved by Allah. The command lā takhshaw al-nāsa wa-khshawnī appears also in *“Do not fear people, fear Me”* (5:44) itself, and is principle for scholars: fear Allah, not people, when judging. The next verse will detail qiṣāṣ in Torah, showing example of Allah's judgment that they hid.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:45

> We ordained for them in the Torah, “A life for a life, an eye for an eye, a nose for a nose, an ear for an ear, a tooth for a tooth—and for wounds equal retaliation.” But whoever waives it charitably, it will be atonement for them. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the wrongdoers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Life for Life, Eye for Eye in Torah**

Wa katabnā 'alayhim fīhā anna al-nafs bi-l-nafs wa al-'ayn bi-l-'ayn wa al-anf bi-l-anf wa al-udhun bi-l-udhun wa al-sinn bi-l-sinn wa al-jurūḥ qiṣāṣ reports ordainment in Torah. Katabnā 'alayhim, We ordained for them, katabnā from k-t-b, to write, ordain, fīhā, in it, Torah, anna al-nafs bi-l-nafs, life for life, nafs, soul, life, bi-l-nafs, for life, indicates retaliation for murder: life taken for life taken unjustly. Wa al-'ayn bi-l-'ayn, eye for eye, 'ayn, eye, and so on for nose, anf, ear, udhun, tooth, sinn, and jurūḥ, wounds, qiṣāṣ, retaliation, equal. The same law appears in Exodus 21:23-25 and Leviticus 24:19-20, showing Torah indeed contained qiṣāṣ. The verse therefore proves that Torah had just retaliation law, which Jewish leaders in Madinah tried to avoid by seeking lighter penalty for adultery and by having differential penalties for killing based on status, as some reports say they had different diyah for different tribes. The ordainment shows that justice requires equality: life for life regardless of status, eye for eye regardless of tribe.

**Wounds Equal Retaliation as Precision**

Wa al-jurūḥ qiṣāṣ — wounds equal retaliation — generalizes: all wounds have equal retaliation, qiṣāṣ from q-ṣ-ṣ, to follow, track, because retaliation follows crime exactly. The phrase indicates that retaliation must be equal, not excessive, as *“If you punish, punish with equivalent of what you were harmed”* (16:126) states. The precision — eye for eye, nose for nose — shows that Torah's law was detailed and just, not vague. The same precision appears in *“A life for a life, eye for eye”* (5:45) itself. The law of qiṣāṣ is both deterrent and just: it deters murder and injury by threatening equal, and it satisfies victim's family with justice. The Qur'an confirms qiṣāṣ for Muslims as well in *“O believers, qiṣāṣ is prescribed for you in murder”* (2:178) and *“In qiṣāṣ is life for you”* (2:179), showing that retaliation preserves life by deterring killing. The Torah's ordainment therefore is same principle that Qur'an confirms.

**Waiving Charitably as Atonement**

Fa-man taṣaddaqa bihi fa-huwa kaffāratun lahu — whoever waives it charitably, it is atonement for him — offers alternative: waiving retaliation as charity is atonement. Taṣaddaqa bihi, waives charitably, from ṣ-d-q, to give charity, be truthful, indicates forgiving qiṣāṣ as act of charity, not as obligation. Fa-huwa kaffāratun lahu, it is atonement for him, kaffārah from k-f-r, to cover, atonement that covers sin, lahu, for him, for waiving person. The same concept appears in *“But whoever waives charitably, it is atonement for them”* (5:45) and in *“If you pardon, it is closer to righteousness”* (2:237). The verse therefore encourages forgiveness as higher virtue, though qiṣāṣ is allowed. The atonement is for waiving person, meaning his sins are covered because he waived right, showing that forgiving is charity that benefits forgiver in Hereafter. Early reports say that when victim's family waives qiṣāṣ, it is expiation for their sins.

**Those Who Do Not Judge by Revelation Are Wrongdoers**

Wa man lam yaḥkum bi-mā anzala Allāhu fa-ulā'ika hum al-ẓālimūn — those who do not judge by what Allah revealed are wrongdoers — gives judgment. Man lam yaḥkum, whoever does not judge, bi-mā anzala Allāhu, by what Allah revealed, fa-ulā'ika hum al-ẓālimūn, they are wrongdoers, ẓālimūn from ẓ-l-m, to wrong, place thing not in place. This is second in trio: verse 44 said kāfirūn, disbelievers, for those who deny obligation to judge by revelation, verse 45 says ẓālimūn, wrongdoers, for those who judge unjustly, not giving equal retaliation, and verse 47 will say fāsiqūn, rebellious, for those who exit obedience. The three describe different aspects of same failure: disbelief in rejecting revelation, wrongdoing in giving unequal judgment, rebellion in exiting obedience. Al-Ṭabarī notes that ẓālimūn here refers to those who judge with inequality, such as giving different penalties based on status, which Jewish leaders did. The verse therefore condemns unequal justice.

**A Verse That Shows Torah's Justice and Encourages Forgiveness**

This verse shows that Torah contained just law of equality, which Jewish leaders violated by having unequal penalties, and that Qur'an confirms same law but encourages forgiveness as charity that is atonement. The balance between qiṣāṣ and forgiveness appears also in *“A life for a life, but whoever waives charitably, it is atonement”* (5:45) itself, showing that law allows both justice and mercy. The verse's placement after verse 44's praise of Torah as guidance and light shows example of that guidance: life for life, eye for eye. The next verse will discuss Gospel confirming Torah and containing guidance and light, continuing chain of revelation: Torah, then Gospel, then Qur'an, each confirming previous and containing guidance. The verse also prepares for verse 48 which will say to each We ordained law and way, and to compete in good deeds.

**Extra Verified Note on Qiṣāṣ**

Qiṣāṣ, retaliation, is defined as following crime with equal, from qaṣṣa, to follow, track, because avenger follows criminal's act. Lisān al-'Arab defines qiṣāṣ as retaliation that follows exactly. The law of eye for eye is also in Ḥammurabi code, but Torah's version is more just because it limits retaliation to equal, not excessive. The Qur'anic confirmation of qiṣāṣ for Muslims in 2:178-179 shows continuity. The encouragement to waive as charity is verified in ḥadīth where Prophet ﷺ said no one waives retaliation seeking Allah's pleasure except Allah raises him in honour, as recorded in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (1391, graded ḥasan). The next verse will discuss Gospel as confirmation of Torah.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:46

> Then in the footsteps of the prophets, We sent Jesus, son of Mary, confirming the Torah revealed before him. And We gave him the Gospel containing guidance and light and confirming what was revealed in the Torah—a guide and a lesson to the God-fearing

**Expanded Commentary**

**In Footsteps of Prophets, Jesus Sent Confirming Torah**

Wa qaffaynā 'alā āthārihim bi-'Īsā ibn Maryam muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi min al-tawrāt describes Jesus following previous prophets. Qaffaynā, We sent following, from q-f-w, to follow footsteps, 'alā āthārihim, on their traces, indicates Jesus came after prophets who judged by Torah, following their path, not bringing entirely new law that abrogates all, but confirming. Bi-'Īsā ibn Maryam, with Jesus son of Mary, lineage emphasizes humanity and virgin birth, muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi min al-tawrāt, confirming what was before him of Torah, muṣaddiq from ṣ-d-q, to confirm truth, indicates Gospel confirms Torah's truth, not contradicts. The same phrasing appears for Qur'an confirming previous scriptures in *“Confirming what was before it”* (3:3) and for Jesus here. The confirmation shows continuity: Torah, then Gospel, then Qur'an, each confirms previous, so those who have Torah should recognize Gospel, and those who have both should recognize Qur'an.

**Gospel Given Containing Guidance and Light**

Wa ātaynāhu al-injīl fīhi hudan wa nūr — We gave him Gospel in it guidance and light — repeats description used for Torah in verse 44: fīhā hudan wa nūr, in it guidance and light, now fīhi hudan wa nūr for Gospel, showing that Gospel also contains guidance and light, not only Torah. Injīl, Gospel, from Greek euangelion, good news, contains guidance for monotheism and ethics and light that illuminates path. The same description appears in *“We gave him Gospel containing guidance and light”* (5:46) itself. The Gospel's guidance includes call to worship Allah alone, to be mindful, and to prepare for coming messenger, as *“And when Jesus said, O Children of Israel, I am messenger confirming Torah and bringing good news of messenger after me, Aḥmad”* (61:6) states. The light in Gospel illuminates, but over time, as verses 13-14 said, portion was forgotten and words distorted, so light dimmed, requiring new light and clear book, Qur'an, as verses 15-16 said.

**Confirming Torah, Guide and Lesson for God-Fearing**

Wa muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi min al-tawrāt wa hudan wa maw'iẓah li-l-muttaqīn — confirming Torah, guide and lesson for God-fearing — adds two more roles. Muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi, confirming what was before it, shows Gospel confirms Torah, not abrogates its monotheism, and hudan, guide, and maw'iẓah, lesson, admonition, from w-'-ẓ, to admonish, for muttaqīn, God-fearing, indicates that Gospel guides and admonishes those who fear Allah. The same phrase hudan wa maw'iẓah appears for Qur'an as *“Guide and admonition for God-fearing”* (3:138). The restriction li-l-muttaqīn, for God-fearing, shows that guidance benefits those who fear, not those who follow desire, as previous verses about distorting and consuming forbidden gain showed. The Gospel's confirmation of Torah also means that true followers of Torah should accept Gospel, and true followers of both should accept Qur'an, which confirms both.

**Chain of Revelation as Confirmation**

The sequence Torah, Gospel, Qur'an as each confirming previous shows chain of revelation: each confirms truth of previous and adds. The chain is mentioned in *“We sent down Torah, then Gospel, then Qur'an”* (5:44-48) as unit, showing continuity. The confirmation is not that later book copies earlier, but that it confirms its core: monotheism, justice, accountability. The differences are in law, sharī'ah, and way, minhāj, as verse 48 will say to each We ordained law and way. The Gospel's law was somewhat lighter than Torah's, easing some restrictions, as *“I have come to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden”* (3:50) says Jesus said. The Qur'an then confirms both and is protector, muhaymin, over them, as verse 48 will say. The verse therefore places Jesus in prophetic chain, not as divine, but as messenger confirming Torah and bringing Gospel with guidance and light.

**A Verse That Defines Jesus' Role**

This verse defines Jesus' role as messenger in footsteps of prophets, confirming Torah, bringing Gospel with guidance and light, guide and lesson for God-fearing. It counters claim that Allah is Messiah (5:17) and that Messiah is third of three (5:73) by showing Jesus as messenger with book, not divine. The same definition appears in *“Messiah, son of Mary, was only messenger”* (5:75). The verse also shows that Gospel, like Torah, contains guidance and light, so People of Book have had light, but neglected portion, leading to enmity and distortion, as earlier verses said. The remedy is new light and clear book, Qur'an, that guides to ways of peace, as verses 15-16 said. The next verse will command people of Gospel to judge by what Allah revealed in it, showing that they too must judge by revelation, not desire.

**Extra Verified Note on Qaffaynā**

Qaffaynā 'alā āthārihim, We sent following on their traces, is used for sending messenger after messenger, following footsteps, indicating continuity. The same verb appears in *“Then We sent following on their traces Our messengers”* (57:27). The name Injīl is Arabicized from Greek, and is used in Qur'an 12 times, always as book given to Jesus, containing guidance and light. The phrase hudan wa nūr appears for Torah, Gospel, and Qur'an, showing that all three share description, but Qur'an is also described as nūr and kitāb mubīn, light and clear book, emphasizing its clarity. The next verse will command people of Gospel to judge by what Allah revealed, completing parallel to Torah judgment.

**Gospel as Good News and Confirmation**

Injīl, Gospel, from euangelion, good news, is good news of monotheism and of coming messenger Aḥmad, as *“Bringing good news of messenger after me, Aḥmad”* (61:6) says. The Gospel confirming Torah means it confirms monotheism and justice, not abrogating core. The description fīhi hudan wa nūr, in it guidance and light, same as Torah, shows continuity. The light in Gospel was meant to guide, but over time portion forgotten and words distorted, as verses 13-14 said, so new light and clear book, Qur'an, came to guide to ways of peace. The verse therefore places Jesus as messenger in chain, not divine, countering claim Allah is Messiah. The next verse will command people of Gospel to judge by what Allah revealed, completing parallel.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:47

> So let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah has revealed in it. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the rebellious

**Expanded Commentary**

**People of Gospel Must Judge by What Allah Revealed**

Wa li-yaḥkum ahl al-injīl bi-mā anzala Allāhu fīh commands people of Gospel to judge by revelation. Li-yaḥkum, let them judge, or so that they judge, ahl al-injīl, people of Gospel, Christians, bi-mā anzala Allāhu fīh, by what Allah revealed in it, indicates that Gospel contains Allah's judgment that they must follow, just as Torah contained Allah's judgment that Jews must follow, as verse 44 said. The command is parallel to previous commands to judge by Torah, showing that each community must judge by its own book as long as it is preserved and not abrogated. The Gospel's judgments include monotheism, justice, and preparation for coming messenger. The verse therefore establishes that People of Gospel have responsibility to judge by what Allah revealed in Gospel, not by distorted interpretations that claim divinity for Messiah. The same principle appears in *“Let people of Gospel judge by what Allah revealed”* (5:47) itself, making it explicit.

**Those Who Do Not Judge Are Rebellious**

Wa man lam yaḥkum bi-mā anzala Allāhu fa-ulā'ika hum al-fāsiqūn — those who do not judge by what Allah revealed are rebellious — third in trio of judgments. Man lam yaḥkum, whoever does not judge, bi-mā anzala Allāhu, by what Allah revealed, fa-ulā'ika hum al-fāsiqūn, they are rebellious, fāsiqūn from f-s-q, to exit, rebel, indicates exiting from obedience. The trio is: verse 44 kāfirūn, disbelievers, for denying obligation, verse 45 ẓālimūn, wrongdoers, for judging unjustly, verse 47 fāsiqūn, rebellious, for exiting obedience. The three describe same act from different angles: disbelief in rejecting revelation, wrongdoing in giving unjust judgment, rebellion in exiting obedience. Early exegetes like Ibn 'Abbās said these are not three separate groups but three descriptions of those who do not judge by revelation, with severity depending on belief and action. The verse therefore warns Christians, like Jews before, that not judging by Gospel is fisq, rebellion.

**Judging by Gospel Means Following Its Monotheism**

Judging by what Allah revealed in Gospel means following its call to worship Allah alone and to follow messenger who confirms Torah, not following later doctrines that claim Allah is Messiah or third of three. The Gospel's true judgment includes *“Hear, O Israel, Lord our God is One”* (Mark 12:29) and Jesus saying *“I am going to my Father and your Father”* (John 20:17) indicating distinction, and *“Worship Lord your God”* (Matthew 4:10). The Qur'an says *“They did not follow Gospel as it should be followed”* (57:27) and *“They neglected portion”* (5:14). Judging by Gospel therefore means returning to its monotheistic core and to its prophecy of coming messenger, Aḥmad, as in *“Bringing good news of messenger after me, Aḥmad”* (61:6). The command li-yaḥkum ahl al-injīl is therefore call to follow true Gospel, not distorted.

**A Verse That Completes Parallel for Two Communities**

Verses 44-47 form parallel: verse 44 Torah with guidance and light, prophets and rabbis judged by it, do not fear people, fear Me, do not trade revelations, those who do not judge are disbelievers; verse 45 ordained life for life etc., waiving is atonement, those who do not judge are wrongdoers; verse 46 Jesus confirming Torah, Gospel with guidance and light, guide and lesson for God-fearing; verse 47 let people of Gospel judge by what Allah revealed, those who do not are rebellious. The parallel shows that both communities have same responsibility and same failure, and same categories of judgment for failure. The next verse will bring Qur'an as confirmation and protector over previous books, commanding Prophet to judge by what Allah revealed and not follow desires, completing chain: Torah, Gospel, Qur'an, each must be judged by, but Qur'an is final protector.

**Why Fāsiqūn Here**

Fāsiqūn, rebellious, is used here for People of Gospel who do not judge by Gospel, while kāfirūn was used for People of Torah who do not judge by Torah in verse 44, and ẓālimūn for those who violate qiṣāṣ in verse 45. The variation may indicate that for Christians, not judging by Gospel is fisq, exiting from obedience, because Gospel's main call is to follow and prepare for final messenger, and exiting that is rebellion. Al-Rāzī notes that fisq is exiting from obedience after knowing, which fits Christians who knew Gospel's monotheism but exited to trinity. The three terms together cover all who do not judge by revelation, regardless of community. The verse therefore warns that true belief requires judging by revelation, not by desire.

**Extra Verified Note on Ahl al-Injīl**

Ahl al-injīl, people of Gospel, appears only here in Qur'an, showing specific address to Christians to judge by Gospel. The command li-yaḥkum can be read as imperative, let them judge, or as purpose, so that they judge, both meaning they must judge by it. The phrase fa-ulā'ika hum al-fāsiqūn, they are rebellious, appears also in *“Those who do not judge are rebellious”* (5:47) itself, and is among verses used to discuss whether ruling by other than revelation is major or minor kufr, with Ibn 'Abbās explaining it can be kufr dūna kufr, lesser disbelief, if done while believing revelation is better but following desire. The next verse will introduce Qur'an as confirmation and protector, commanding Prophet to judge by it.

**Trio of Kāfirūn, Ẓālimūn, Fāsiqūn as Descriptions**

The trio kāfirūn in verse 44, ẓālimūn in 45, fāsiqūn in 47 are three descriptions of those who do not judge by revelation, with severity depending on belief: disbelief if denies obligation, wrongdoing if judges unjustly, rebellion if exits obedience. Ibn 'Abbās said these can be kufr dūna kufr, lesser disbelief, when done while believing revelation better but following desire. The variation shows different angles: rejecting revelation is kufr, unequal judgment is ẓulm, exiting obedience is fisq. For Christians, not judging by Gospel is fisq because Gospel's main call is to follow and prepare for final messenger. The command li-yaḥkum ahl al-injīl, let people of Gospel judge, shows responsibility. The next verse will bring Qur'an as confirmation and protector, commanding Prophet to judge by it.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:48

> We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ this Book with the truth, as a confirmation of previous Scriptures and a supreme authority on them. So judge between them by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires over the truth that has come to you. To each of you We have ordained a code of law and a way of life. If Allah had willed, He would have made you one community, but His Will is to test you with what He has given ˹each of˺ you. So compete with one another in doing good. To Allah you will all return, then He will inform you ˹of the truth˺ regarding your differences

**Expanded Commentary**

**Book With Truth as Confirmation and Protector**

Wa anzalnā ilayka al-kitāb bi-l-ḥaqq muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi min al-kitāb wa muhayminan 'alayh introduces Qur'an. Anzalnā ilayka al-kitāb, We revealed to you Book, bi-l-ḥaqq, with truth, indicates Qur'an is revealed with truth, containing truth, and for truth. Muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi min al-kitāb, confirming what was before it of Book, muṣaddiq as before, confirming Torah and Gospel's truth, and muhayminan 'alayh, protector, guardian, trustee over them, muhaymin from h-y-m-n, to protect, watch over, also name of Allah al-Muhaymin, Guardian. Muhaymin means Qur'an is guardian that testifies to truth of previous books and preserves and judges over them, so what agrees with Qur'an is true, what contradicts is distorted. The same description appears in *“Confirming previous and protector over them”* (5:48) itself. The Book with truth is Qur'an, which confirms previous scriptures' monotheism and justice, and protects by clarifying what was hidden and distorted, as verses 15 and 41 described.

**Judge Between Them by What Allah Revealed**

Fa-uḥkum baynahum bi-mā anzala Allāh commands Prophet to judge between People of Book by what Allah revealed, i.e., Qur'an, not by their distorted desires. Fa-uḥkum, so judge, baynahum, between them, bi-mā anzala Allāh, by what Allah revealed, indicates that when they come for judgment, as in verses 42-43, he should judge by Qur'an, which contains true judgment that matches Torah and Gospel's original. Wa lā tattabi' ahwā'ahum 'ammā jā'aka min al-ḥaqq — do not follow their desires over truth that came to you — warns against following hawā, desire, 'ammā jā'aka min al-ḥaqq, away from truth that came to you, ḥaqq, truth, from ḥ-q-q, to be true, real. The same warning appears in *“Do not follow their desires”* (5:48, 49) repeated, showing that following desire is main danger when judging between People of Book who seek desire-based rulings, as verse 41 said they said if you are given this, take it, if not, beware. The command to judge by revelation and not follow desire is core of previous verses about not trading revelations for small price.

**To Each We Ordained Law and Way**

Li-kullin ja'alnā minkum shir'atan wa minhājan — to each of you We ordained law and way — explains plurality of laws. Li-kullin, to each, community, ja'alnā, We made, ordained, minkum, among you, shir'atan, law, from sh-r-', to legislate, open way, and minhājan, way, clear path, from n-h-j, to be clear, open, indicates that each community has its own law and way, though core tawḥīd is same. Shir'ah is law, sharī'ah, and minhāj is way that is clear and smooth. The verse acknowledges that different communities have different ritual and legal formulations appointed by Allah, as part of divine wisdom, not as contradiction. Al-Ṭabarī notes that shir'ah and minhāj refer to distinct laws for each messenger, while religion, dīn, is one. The same idea appears in *“For each We appointed law and way”* (5:48) itself. The plurality is intentional, not accidental, as next clause explains.

**If Allah Willed He Would Make You One Community**

Wa law shā'a Allāhu la-ja'alakum ummatan wāḥidah — if Allah willed, He would make you one community — states that diversity is by divine will, not inability. Law shā'a, if He willed, la-ja'alakum, He would make you, ummatan wāḥidah, one community, indicates that Allah could have made all one community with one law, but did not, for wisdom. The same counterfactual appears in *“If Allah willed, He would make you one community”* (5:48) and in *“If Allah willed, He would make you one community, but He leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills”* (16:93) and *“Had Allah willed, He would have made you one community”* (42:8). The will not to make one community is to test, as next clause says. The test is not to create division for its own sake but to test obedience within diversity, as *“We made you into nations and tribes to know one another”* (49:13) explains diversity as means to know, not to despise.

**Test With What He Gave and Competition in Good Deeds**

Wa lākin li-yabluwakum fī mā ātākum — but to test you in what He gave you — gives purpose: li-yabluwakum, to test you, from b-l-w, to test, try, fī mā ātākum, in what He gave you, indicates that different laws and ways are test of obedience and gratitude. Fa-istabiqū al-khayrāt — so compete in good deeds — commands competition in khayrāt, good deeds, plural of khayr, good, from kh-y-r, to be good, choose. The competition is in good deeds, not in theology, indicating that while laws differ, good deeds are common ground where communities can compete. The same command appears in *“Compete in good deeds”* (2:148) and *“Compete in good deeds”* (5:48) itself. Ilā Allāhi marji'ukum jamī'an fa-yunabbi'ukum bi-mā kuntum fīhi takhtalifūn — to Allah is your return all together, He will inform you of what you differed — shows that final resolution of theological differences is with Allah in Hereafter, not to be forced in world. The return marji', from r-j-', to return, and yunabbi'ukum, inform, from n-b-’, to give important news, indicates that Allah will clarify differences on Day of Judgment.

**Extra Verified Note on Muhaymin**

Muhaymin, protector, guardian, is verified as meaning witness, keeper, trustworthy guardian, from root h-y-m-n, to protect. Lisān al-'Arab defines muhaymin as guardian who watches, and al-Muhaymin as name of Allah who watches over creation. The description of Qur'an as muhayminan 'alayh, protector over previous books, means it is criterion that judges what is true in them. The phrase li-kullin ja'alnā shir'atan wa minhājan is used in discussions of religious pluralism, as it acknowledges distinct laws and ways appointed by Allah. The verse's call fa-istabiqū al-khayrāt, compete in good deeds, is cited as basis for interfaith competition in virtue, not conflict. The next verse will repeat command to judge by revelation and warn against being lured away, continuing emphasis on judging by truth, not desire.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:49

> And judge between them ˹O Prophet˺ by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires. And beware, so they do not lure you away from some of what Allah has revealed to you. If they turn away ˹from Allah’s judgment˺, then know that it is Allah’s Will to repay them for some of their sins, and that many people are indeed rebellious

**Expanded Commentary**

**Judge by What Allah Revealed, Do Not Follow Desires**

Wa an iḥkum baynahum bi-mā anzala Allāh wa lā tattabi' ahwā'ahum repeats command from previous verse with emphasis. An iḥkum, and judge, baynahum, between them, bi-mā anzala Allāh, by what Allah revealed, wa lā tattabi' ahwā'ahum, do not follow their desires, ahwā' plural of hawā, desire, whim, shows that judging must be by revelation, not by desire of People of Book who seek favorable rulings. The repetition from verse 48 — *“Judge between them by what Allah revealed and do not follow their desires”* (5:48) — emphasizes importance, because temptation to follow desires is strong when judging between powerful groups. The command is to Prophet ﷺ but also to all judges after him: judge by revelation, not desire. The same warning appears in *“Do not follow desire, lest it lead you astray from Allah's way”* (38:26) for Dāwūd. The verse therefore reinforces that justice requires following revelation, not desire.

**Beware They Do Not Lure You Away**

Wa-iḥdharhum an yaftinūka 'an ba'ḍi mā anzala Allāhu ilayk — beware, lest they lure you away from some of what Allah revealed to you — warns of attempt to lure. Iḥdharhum, beware of them, from ḥ-dh-r, to be cautious, an yaftinūka, lest they tempt or lure you, yaftinūka from f-t-n, to test with trial, to lure away, 'an ba'ḍi mā anzala Allāhu ilayk, from some of what Allah revealed to you, indicates they will try to make you leave part of revelation, not necessarily all, by suggesting compromise: accept part, leave part. Early reports say Jewish leaders tried to persuade Prophet to not order stoning or to give lighter penalty, offering to follow him if he did, as in verse 41's if you are given this, take it. The warning an yaftinūka 'an ba'ḍi shows that even leaving some of revelation for desire is dangerous, because revelation is interconnected. The same warning appears in *“Beware, lest they tempt you away from some of what Allah revealed”* (5:49) itself. The command to beware shows that even Prophet needs caution against being lured, so others need more.

**If They Turn Away, Allah Will Repay for Sins**

Fa-in tawallaw fa-'lam anna-mā yurīdu Allāhu an yuṣībahum bi-ba'ḍi dhunūbihim — if they turn away, know that Allah wills to repay them for some sins — explains consequence of turning away. Tawallaw, turn away, from w-l-y, to turn, indicates they turn away from judgment by revelation. Fa-'lam, know, anna-mā yurīdu Allāhu, that Allah wills, an yuṣībahum, to afflict them, yuṣībahum from ṣ-w-b, to hit, afflict, bi-ba'ḍi dhunūbihim, for some sins, indicates that turning away leads to punishment for some sins in this world, as part of recompense, with more in Hereafter. The phrase bi-ba'ḍi dhunūbihim, for some sins, shows that not all sins are punished immediately, but some are, as warning. The same phrasing appears in *“Allah wills to afflict them for some sins”* (5:49) itself. The affliction can be through defeat, exposure, or other worldly punishment. The verse therefore says if they turn away from your just judgment, know that their turning away is itself cause for Allah to punish them for some sins, so you need not grieve.

**Many People Are Rebellious**

Wa inna kathīran min al-nāsi la-fāsiqūn — many people are rebellious — notes prevalence of fisq, rebellion. Kathīran min al-nāsi, many people, la-fāsiqūn, indeed rebellious, fāsiqūn as before, those who exit obedience, indicates that many, not only People of Book, are rebellious, so turning away is not surprising. The same phrase appears in *“Many people are rebellious”* (5:49) and in *“Many people are rebellious”* (5:81) for similar context. The observation explains why some turn away: because many are rebellious, following desire rather than revelation. The verse therefore consoles Prophet that turning away is common, not personal failure. It also warns believers not to be among many rebellious, but among few who judge by revelation and compete in good deeds, as verse 48 said.

**A Verse That Repeats Command for Emphasis**

This verse repeats command to judge by revelation and not follow desires for third time in two verses (48 and 49), showing emphasis. The repetition is not redundant but adds new element: beware lest they lure you away from some of what was revealed, and if they turn away, know Allah will punish for some sins. The addition shows progression: first command to judge by revelation, then warning not to follow desires, then warning to beware of being lured from some, then consequence if they turn away. The structure mirrors previous warnings about distorting and forgetting portion: small deviation leads to larger. The verse's closing inna kathīran min al-nāsi la-fāsiqūn, many are rebellious, appears also after similar warnings, indicating that rebellion is widespread, so adherence to revelation requires firmness. The next verse will question whether they seek judgment of ignorance, showing that alternative to revelation is jāhiliyyah.

**Extra Verified Note on Yaftinūka**

Yaftinūka, lure you, from fitnah, trial that tempts away, is used for attempts to make Prophet compromise, as in *“They almost tempted you away from what We revealed”* (17:73) for similar attempt by polytheists to make Prophet compromise. The phrase an yaftinūka 'an ba'ḍi mā anzala Allāhu, lest they lure you from some of what Allah revealed, shows that even partial compromise is fitnah. Early tafsīr reports that Jewish leaders said if you judge with stoning, we will not follow, but if you judge with flogging, we will follow, trying to lure Prophet to leave part of revelation. The verse warns against that. The next verse will ask if they seek judgment of ignorance, contrasting revelation with jāhiliyyah.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:50

> Is it the judgment of ˹pre-Islamic˺ ignorance they seek? Who could be a better judge than Allah for people of sure faith

**Expanded Commentary**

**Is It Judgment of Ignorance They Seek**

A-fa-ḥukm al-jāhiliyyah yabghūn questions whether they seek judgment of ignorance. A-fa-, is it, interrogative with fa-, so, ḥukm al-jāhiliyyah, judgment of ignorance, jāhiliyyah from j-h-l, ignorance, era before Islam marked by ignorance of divine guidance, tribal custom, following desire. Yabghūn, they seek, from b-gh-y, to seek, desire, indicates they desire judgment of ignorance instead of Allah's judgment. The question is rhetorical, expressing disapproval: do they seek jāhiliyyah judgment after having Torah, Gospel, and Qur'an with guidance and light. The same phrase appears in *“Is it judgment of ignorance they seek”* (5:50) itself and in *“They sought judgment of ṭāghūt”* (4:60) for similar seeking of non-divine judgment. Jāhiliyyah judgment is characterized by following desire, tribal bias, and inequality, as earlier verses showed Jewish leaders had differential penalties based on status, which is jāhiliyyah. The verse therefore asks if after all guidance, they still prefer ignorance.

**Who Is Better Judge Than Allah for People of Sure Faith**

Wa man aḥsanu min Allāhi ḥukman li-qawmin yūqinūn — who is better than Allah in judgment for people of sure faith — asserts Allah's judgment is best. Man aḥsanu, who is better, aḥsan from ḥ-s-n, to be good, beautiful, and ḥukman, in judgment, indicates that no one judges better than Allah. Li-qawmin yūqinūn, for people who are certain, yūqinūn from y-q-n, certainty, indicates that those with sure faith recognize Allah's judgment as best, while those without certainty seek jāhiliyyah.
