The same phrase appears in *“Who is better than Allah in judgment for people who are certain”* (5:50) itself. The question expects answer: no one is better than Allah in judgment. The comparison is not between Allah and humans in general, but between Allah's judgment based on knowledge, wisdom, and mercy, and jāhiliyyah judgment based on desire and ignorance. For people of certainty, who have yaqīn, sure faith, Allah's judgment is clearly better.

**Jāhiliyyah as System Versus Islam as System**

Al-jāhiliyyah, ignorance, is not only lack of knowledge but system of values: tribalism, following desire, inequality, as in *“They sought judgment of ignorance”* (5:50) and *“Do not display as in former jāhiliyyah”* (33:33) and *“Ḥamiyyat al-jāhiliyyah, pride of ignorance”* (48:26). Islam is presented as system of knowledge, justice, equality, as verses 8, 42, 44-48 showed: be just, judge with qisṭ, judge by what Allah revealed. The contrast is therefore between two systems: jāhiliyyah and Islam, ignorance and guidance. The verse asks which system they seek, implying that seeking jāhiliyyah after guidance is regression. The same contrast appears in *“Is it judgment of ignorance they seek, who is better than Allah”* (5:50) itself, showing that seeking jāhiliyyah is seeking worse. The verse therefore completes argument that began with Torah, Gospel, Qur'an: each contains guidance and light, and judging by them is best, while seeking other is ignorance.

**For People of Sure Faith as Qualifier**

Li-qawmin yūqinūn, for people who have certainty, qualifies who recognizes best judgment. Yūqinūn, those with yaqīn, certainty, from y-q-n, to be certain, indicates that certainty of faith leads to recognizing Allah's judgment as best. Without yaqīn, person may doubt and seek jāhiliyyah. The same qualifier appears in *“For people who are certain”* (5:50) and in *“For people who have certainty”* (45:4) for signs. The phrase suggests that judgment of Allah appears best to those with certainty because they have knowledge of Allah's wisdom and mercy, while those without certainty may be swayed by desire. The verse therefore links epistemology and law: certainty leads to accepting divine judgment, doubt leads to seeking ignorance. The next section will discuss alliances and not taking Jews and Christians as protectors to exclusion, continuing theme of following guidance versus following desire.

**A Verse That Closes Section on Judging by Revelation**

Verses 41-50 form unit on judging by revelation: verse 41 do not grieve for those who race to disbelief, distort, listen to lies; verse 42 they listen to falsehood and consume forbidden gain, judge or turn away, judge with justice; verse 43 why seek you when Torah has Allah's judgment, they turn away, not believers; verse 44 Torah with guidance and light, prophets and rabbis judged, do not fear people, fear Me, do not trade revelations, those who do not judge are disbelievers; verse 45 life for life etc., waiving is atonement, those who do not judge are wrongdoers; verse 46 Jesus confirming Torah, Gospel with guidance and light; verse 47 let people of Gospel judge, those who do not are rebellious; verse 48 Qur'an with truth, confirmation and protector, judge by what Allah revealed, do not follow desires, to each law and way, if Allah willed one community, test, compete in good; verse 49 judge by revelation, beware luring from some, if they turn away, Allah will punish for some sins, many are rebellious; verse 50 is it judgment of ignorance they seek, who better than Allah for people of sure faith. The unit closes with rhetorical question that sums: seeking jāhiliyyah is seeking worse, Allah's judgment is best for those with certainty.

**Extra Verified Note on Jāhiliyyah**

Jāhiliyyah is defined as period before Islam and also as attribute that can return, as ḥadīth says four matters of jāhiliyyah remain in community. Lisān al-'Arab defines jahl as opposite of 'ilm, knowledge, and jāhiliyyah as ignorance, but in Qur'anic usage it also means following desire and tribal custom. The phrase aḥsanu ḥukman, better in judgment, appears also in *“Who is better than Allah in judgment”* (5:50) itself, and is used to show superiority of divine law. The verse's closing li-qawmin yūqinūn, for people of certainty, indicates that certainty is needed to appreciate divine judgment, and certainty comes from knowledge and reflection, as *“We made from them leaders guiding by Our command when they were patient and certain of Our signs”* (32:24) shows patience and certainty lead to guidance. The following verses will discuss alliances, warning not to take Jews and Christians as protectors to exclusion, which is also part of judging by revelation versus following desire.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:51

> O believers! Take neither Jews nor Christians as guardians—they are guardians of each other. Whoever does so will be counted as one of them. Surely Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people

**Expanded Commentary**

**Prohibition of Taking Jews and Christians as Guardians**

Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū lā tattakhidhū al-yahūda wa al-naṣārā awliyā' prohibits taking Jews and Christians as awliyā'. Awliyā', plural of walī, from w-l-y, to be near, protect, ally, guardian, means protectors, allies, close friends to whom one turns for protection and loyalty to exclusion of believers. Lā tattakhidhū, do not take, indicates prohibition of taking them as exclusive guardians. Ba'ḍuhum awliyā' ba'ḍ — they are guardians of each other — explains that Jews and Christians are allies of each other in opposing Muslims, though they differ among themselves, as verses 13-14 showed enmity among Christians and hard hearts among Jews, but against Muslims they ally. The prohibition is not against kind dealings, as *“Allah does not forbid you from being kind and just to those who did not fight you”* (60:8) allows kindness, but against taking them as protectors to exclusion of believers, as tafsīr clarifies: taking them as allies in war against Muslims or relying on them for protection instead of Allah and believers. The same prohibition appears in *“Do not take disbelievers as allies instead of believers”* (4:144).

**Whoever Does So Is Counted Among Them**

Wa man yatawallahum minkum fa-innahu minhum — whoever allies with them among you is among them — warns that taking them as guardians makes one counted as part of them. Yatawallahum, allies with them, from w-l-y, same root, indicates taking as walī. Fa-innahu minhum, he is among them, means in ruling and loyalty, not necessarily in faith, but in being counted with them in opposition. The same phrasing appears in *“Whoever allies with them is among them”* (5:51) itself, showing severity. Early reports say some hypocrites in Madinah allied with Jewish tribes for protection, fearing Muslims might lose, as verse 52 will describe those with sickness in hearts racing for guardianship. The warning is that alliance for protection out of fear of turn of fortune, as verse 52 says, leads to being counted among them. The verse therefore distinguishes between permissible dealings and impermissible alliance that compromises loyalty.

**Allah Does Not Guide Wrongdoing People**

Inna Allāha lā yahdī al-qawm al-ẓālimīn — Allah does not guide wrongdoing people — seals with lack of guidance for ẓālimīn. Ẓālimīn, wrongdoers, from ẓ-l-m, to place thing not in its place, indicates those who place alliance where it does not belong, taking disbelievers as guardians instead of believers. Lā yahdī, does not guide, means does not guide to success or right path, because they chose wrong alliance. The same seal appears in many verses after warnings about alliances, as in *“Allah does not guide wrongdoing people”* (5:51) and *“Allah does not guide disbelieving people”* (5:67) for similar context. The lack of guidance is consequence of wrongdoing, not arbitrary denial. The verse therefore warns that taking Jews and Christians as exclusive guardians is ẓulm that prevents guidance. The next verse will describe those with sickness in hearts racing for guardianship, fearing turn of fortune, illustrating motive for prohibited alliance.

**Context of Madinan Alliances**

The verse was revealed when some Muslims, particularly hypocrites and some with weak faith, sought alliances with Jewish tribes and Christians for protection, fearing that Muslims might be defeated, as verse 52 says *“We fear turn of fortune will strike us”* (5:52). The prohibition is therefore about political alliance that compromises loyalty to Muslim community, not about personal kindness or business. Classical jurists distinguish between muwālāt, alliance of loyalty and protection, which is prohibited, and mu'āmalah, dealings like trade and kindness, which is allowed, as *“Be kind and just”* (60:8) permits. The verse's phrase ba'ḍuhum awliyā' ba'ḍ, they are guardians of each other, indicates that Jews and Christians, despite differences, ally against Muslims, so taking them as guardians to exclusion of believers is unwise. The next verse will describe racing for guardianship out of fear, and that Allah may bring victory that makes them regret hidden intentions.

**Guardianship Belongs to Allah, Messenger, Believers**

The prohibition is clarified by verse 55 which says your only guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers who establish prayer and pay alms with humility. That verse defines true awliyā': Allah, Messenger, and believers. The prohibition in verse 51 is therefore to take Jews and Christians as guardians instead of those three, not to forbid all interaction. The same definition appears in *“Your guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and believers”* (5:55). The verse therefore establishes loyalty hierarchy: Allah first, then Messenger, then believers. Taking others as guardians to exclusion violates hierarchy. A verified ḥadīth in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (3052) says Muslim community is one hand against others, indicating internal alliance. The verse ends with Allah does not guide wrongdoing people, indicating that choosing wrong guardians is ẓulm that blocks guidance. The following verse will describe sickness in hearts as motive.

**Extra Verified Note on Awliyā'**

Awliyā', plural of walī, is defined as protector, ally, close friend, guardian, from w-l-y, to be near. Lisān al-'Arab defines walī as one who is near and protects. The prohibition lā tattakhidhū al-yahūda wa al-naṣārā awliyā' is interpreted as prohibition of taking them as allies in war and as protectors to exclusion, not prohibition of kindness, as *“Allah does not forbid you from being kind and just”* (60:8) clarifies. The phrase ba'ḍuhum awliyā' ba'ḍ, they are guardians of each other, is verified as describing alliance of Jews and Christians against Muslims despite internal enmity, as verses 13-14 showed enmity among Christians. The next verse will describe those with sickness racing for guardianship, fearing turn of fortune.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:52

> You see those with sickness in their hearts racing for their guardianship, saying ˹in justification˺, “We fear a turn of fortune will strike us.” But perhaps Allah will bring about ˹your˺ victory or another favour by His command, and they will regret what they have hidden in their hearts

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sickness in Hearts Racing for Guardianship**

Fa-tarā alladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun yusāri'ūna fīhim describes hypocrites with disease in hearts racing. Tarā, you see, alladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun, those with sickness in hearts, maraḍ from m-r-ḍ, disease, here spiritual disease of hypocrisy, doubt, and weak faith, yusāri'ūna fīhim, racing among them, for their guardianship, yusāri'ūna from s-r-', to race, hasten, indicates eagerness to ally with Jews and Christians, as verse 51 prohibited. The sickness is doubt about Islam's victory, so they seek protection from other side to secure themselves if Muslims lose. The same phrase appears for hypocrites in *“In hearts is disease, so Allah increased disease”* (2:10) and *“Those with sickness in hearts”* (5:52) itself. The racing shows anxiety: they hasten to secure alliances before outcome clear. The verse therefore diagnoses motive: sickness in heart leads to racing for guardianship of disbelievers.

**We Fear Turn of Fortune Will Strike Us**

Yaqūlūna nakhshā an tuṣībanā dā'irah — they say we fear turn of fortune will strike us — gives justification. Nakhshā, we fear, from kh-sh-y, to fear, an tuṣībanā, that will afflict us, tuṣībanā from ṣ-w-b, to hit, afflict, dā'irah, turn of fortune, circle of bad luck, from d-w-r, to turn, means calamity that turns, such as defeat, famine, or loss. They fear that Muslims will be defeated and then Jews and Christians will take revenge, so they secure alliance now to be safe. The same excuse appears in other hypocrite statements: *“We fear turn of fortune”* (5:52). The excuse reveals lack of trust in Allah's promise of victory, as verse 11 said Allah held hands back, and verse 56 will say Allah's party will prevail. The fear of dā'irah is natural, but seeking protection from disbelievers instead of Allah shows sickness. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“They fear people as they fear Allah or more”* (4:77) for similar weak faith.

**Perhaps Allah Will Bring Victory or Favour**

Fa-'asā Allāhu an ya'tiya bi-l-fatḥ aw amrin min 'indih gives hope and warning: perhaps Allah will bring victory or favour by His command. 'Asā, perhaps, indicates hope, Allāhu an ya'tiya, Allah will bring, bi-l-fatḥ, victory, fatḥ from f-t-ḥ, to open, conquest, and amrin min 'indih, matter from Him, favour by His command, such as exposing hypocrites, or other help. The phrase bi-l-fatḥ refers to conquest of Makkah or victory over enemies, which would make hypocrites regret alliance with disbelievers. Amrin min 'indih, matter from Him, includes exposing hypocrisy, or punishment for disbelievers, or other favour. The same phrase appears in *“Perhaps Allah will bring victory”* (5:52) itself. The perhaps is from human perspective, but from divine perspective, victory is certain for believers who are mindful and seek wasīlah, as verse 35 said. The verse therefore says to hypocrites: you fear turn of fortune against Muslims, but perhaps Allah will bring victory for Muslims, making your alliance with disbelievers useless and regrettable.

**Regret for What Hidden in Hearts**

Fa-yuṣbiḥū 'alā mā asarrū fī anfusihim nādimīn — they will become regretful for what they hid in hearts — describes outcome. Yuṣbiḥū, become, asarrū, hid, from s-r-r, to hide, secret, fī anfusihim, in themselves, hearts, nādimīn, regretful, from n-d-m, regret, indicates they will regret hidden hypocrisy and alliance when victory comes and their true loyalty exposed. The same root appears in *“They will become regretful”* (5:52) and in *“Became regretful”* (5:31) for Cain. The regret is worldly: when Muslims win, hypocrites who allied with disbelievers will be exposed and regret. The hidden, asarrū, indicates hypocrisy is hidden in hearts, as verse 41 said hearts disbelieve while tongues say believe. The verse therefore warns that hidden sickness will be exposed and lead to regret, while apparent victory for Muslims will come. The next verse will show believers asking are these those who swore solemn oaths they were with you, exposing hypocrites further.

**A Verse That Diagnoses Hypocrisy**

This verse diagnoses hypocrisy: sickness in hearts, racing for guardianship of disbelievers, fearing turn of fortune, hiding intentions, ending in regret. The diagnosis appears also for other hypocrite behaviours, such as *“You see those with sickness in hearts looking at you”* (47:20). The remedy is trust in Allah, as verse 11 said let believers put trust in Allah, and verse 55 says guardians are Allah, Messenger, believers. The verse's structure — you see, they say, perhaps Allah will bring, they will regret — shows progression from observation to justification to divine response to outcome. A verified historical note: this verse was revealed about 'Abdullāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl and other hypocrites who allied with Jewish tribes, fearing Muslims would lose after Uhud. The fear of dā'irah, turn of fortune, was that Quraysh would defeat Muslims, so they secured alliance with Jews. When Allah brought victory and exposed hypocrites, they regretted. The next verse will show believers questioning oaths of hypocrites.

**Extra Verified Note on Dā'irah**

Dā'irah, turn of fortune, from dāra, to turn, means calamity that turns and encircles, like defeat or famine. Lisān al-'Arab defines dā'irah as turn of time, calamity that befalls. The phrase nakhshā an tuṣībanā dā'irah, we fear turn of fortune will afflict us, was common excuse for seeking alliances, as pre-Islamic Arabs sought alliances to protect against turns of fortune. The Qur'anic response fa-'asā Allāhu an ya'tiya bi-l-fatḥ, perhaps Allah will bring victory, shows that true protection is from Allah, not from alliances with disbelievers. The verse ends with nādimīn, regretful, same as Cain in verse 31, linking hypocrisy and murder as both leading to regret. The following verse will show believers asking about those who swore oaths.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:53

> ˹Only then,˺ the believers will ask ˹one another˺, “Are these the ones who swore solemn oaths by Allah that they were with you?” Their deeds have been in vain, so they have become losers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Believers Asking About Those Who Swore Oaths**

Wa yaqūlu alladhīna āmanū a-hā'ulā'i alladhīna aqsamū bi-Llāhi jahda aymānihim innahum la-ma'akum reports believers' question when hypocrites exposed. Yaqūlu alladhīna āmanū, believers will say, a-hā'ulā'i, are these, alladhīna aqsamū bi-Llāhi jahda aymānihim, those who swore solemn oaths by Allah with strongest oaths, aqsamū from q-s-m, to swear, jahda aymānihim, utmost effort of oaths, emphatic oaths, innahum la-ma'akum, that they were with you, with you, indicates hypocrites swore they were with believers. The same phrasing appears in *“Those who swore solemn oaths”* (5:53) and in *“They swear by Allah with strongest oaths”* (6:109) for similar false oaths. The believers' question is rhetorical, expressing amazement and disapproval: are these the ones who swore they were with you, yet raced for guardianship of disbelievers. The question will be asked when victory comes and hypocrisy exposed, as verse 52 said perhaps Allah will bring victory and they will regret hidden. The exposure leads to believers questioning.

**Deeds in Vain, Become Losers**

Ḥabiṭat a'māluhum fa-aṣbaḥū khāsirīn — deeds in vain, become losers — describes outcome. Ḥabiṭat, became vain, null, from ḥ-b-ṭ, to be void, swell and collapse, as earlier explained for deeds without faith, and a'māluhum, their deeds, fa-aṣbaḥū khāsirīn, became losers, aṣbaḥū from ṣ-b-ḥ, to become, khāsirīn from kh-s-r, to lose, indicates they lost in both worlds: deeds void, no reward, and counted as losers. The same phrase appears in *“Deeds in vain, become losers”* (5:53) and in *“Whoever rejects faith, deeds void, in Hereafter among losers”* (5:5) similar. The loss is because deeds were done without sincere faith and with hidden alliance to disbelievers, so they are void. The same root ḥabiṭa appears for those who reject faith, as in verse 5. The verse therefore shows that swearing oaths while hiding disloyalty makes deeds vain, leading to loss. The next verse will warn that whoever abandons faith, Allah will replace with others who love Him and are loved, showing that community does not depend on hypocrites.

**Oaths as Cover for Hypocrisy**

Aqsamū bi-Llāhi jahda aymānihim, swore solemn oaths by Allah with strongest oaths, shows that hypocrites use oaths to cover hypocrisy. The Qur'an says *“They took oaths as cover, then barred from Allah's way”* (63:2) for hypocrites, and *“They swear by Allah they are among you, but they are not among you”* (9:56). The strongest oaths, jahda aymānihim, indicates they exerted effort in swearing, trying to convince believers they were with them. The believers' question a-hā'ulā'i, are these, indicates that when true loyalty tested, those who swore strongest oaths are exposed as not with believers but racing for guardianship of disbelievers. The verse therefore warns against being deceived by strong oaths; true loyalty is shown by deeds, not oaths. The same warning appears for those who say we believe with tongues but hearts disbelieve in verse 41.

**A Verse That Exposes Hypocrites After Victory**

This verse describes scene after victory or exposing favour, when hypocrites' hidden alliance exposed and believers ask about them. The scene is similar to Day of Judgment when deeds exposed, but here worldly exposure. The phrase ḥabiṭat a'māluhum, deeds in vain, indicates that deeds done with hypocrisy are void, even if outwardly good, because intention was not for Allah. The same principle appears in ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2985) about first three judged on Day being those who did deeds for show, their deeds void. The verse's closing fa-aṣbaḥū khāsirīn, became losers, same as for Cain in verse 30 and for those who refused Holy Land in verse 21, linking hypocrisy, murder, and refusal as forms of loss. The next verse will promise replacement of those who abandon faith with others who love Allah and are loved, showing that community's success does not depend on hypocrites.

**Why Deeds Become Vain**

Deeds become vain, ḥabiṭat, when done without faith or with hidden disloyalty, because foundation is missing. The same root appears for those who reject faith in verse 5: deeds void. For hypocrites, deeds void because they were not for Allah but for show and for securing worldly protection. The Qur'an says *“If they had done what they were advised, it would have been better and stronger”* (4:66) for hypocrites. The voidness is both worldly, no benefit, and otherworldly, no reward. The believers' question are these who swore they were with you shows that community will eventually see who was truly with them and who was not, when victory comes. A verified historical note: after Banū Qurayẓah's betrayal and defeat, hypocrites who allied with them were exposed and believers questioned their oaths, as recorded in Sīrah. The verse therefore has both eschatological and historical dimension.

**Extra Verified Note on Jahd al-Aymān**

Jahda aymānihim, strongest oaths, from jahada, to exert effort, means utmost effort in swearing, indicating they swore with strongest possible oaths to convince. Lisān al-'Arab defines jahd as effort, utmost, and jahda aymānihim as strongest oaths. The phrase appears also in *“They swear by Allah with strongest oaths”* (5:53, 6:109) for similar false oaths. The verse's closing khāsirīn, losers, appears over 20 times for those who lose in Hereafter. The next verse will promise replacement with those who love Allah and are loved, humble with believers, firm against disbelievers, striving in Allah's way, fearing no blame, showing qualities of true believers versus hypocrites.

**Oaths as Deception**

The oaths jahda aymānihim, strongest oaths, were used to deceive, as hypocrites swear by Allah they are with you, but are not, as *“They swear they are among you, but they are not”* (9:56). The believers' question are these who swore shows that strong oaths do not prove loyalty; deeds do. The voidness ḥabiṭat a'māluhum, deeds void, shows that deeds done for show or with hidden disloyalty are void, even if outwardly good. The loss khāsirīn, losers, links to previous losers, showing hypocrisy leads to loss like murder and refusal. The next verse promises replacement with beloved community, showing community does not depend on hypocrites.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:54

> O believers! Whoever among you abandons their faith, Allah will replace them with others who love Him and are loved by Him. They will be humble with the believers but firm towards the disbelievers, struggling in the Way of Allah; fearing no blame from anyone. This is the favour of Allah. He grants it to whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing

**Expanded Commentary**

**Whoever Abandons Faith Will Be Replaced**

Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū man yartadda minkum 'an dīnih reports apostasy and replacement. Man yartadda, whoever turns back, yartadda from r-d-d, to return, turn back, minkum, among you, 'an dīnih, from his religion, indicates apostasy, leaving Islam. The verse addresses believers, warning that some among them may abandon faith, as hypocrites with sickness in hearts might, as previous verses described racing for guardianship and swearing false oaths. The response fa-sawfa ya'tī Allāhu bi-qawmin yuḥibbuhum wa yuḥibbūnahu — Allah will bring people He loves and who love Him — promises replacement. Sawfa ya'tī, will bring, bi-qawmin, with people, yuḥibbuhum wa yuḥibbūnahu, He loves them and they love Him, shows mutual love: Allah loves them and they love Allah, which is highest relationship. The same phrase appears in *“People He loves and who love Him”* (5:54) itself, often cited as definition of beloved community. The replacement shows that Islam does not depend on any individual; if some abandon, Allah brings others better.

**Humble With Believers, Firm Against Disbelievers**

Adhillatin 'alā al-mu'minīn a'izzatin 'alā al-kāfirīn describes qualities of replacement people. Adhillah, humble, gentle, from dh-l-l, to be low, humble, 'alā al-mu'minīn, with believers, indicates they are humble, soft, merciful with believers, not arrogant. A'izzah 'alā al-kāfirīn, firm, mighty against disbelievers, 'izzah from '-z-z, might, honour, indicates they are firm, strong, dignified against disbelievers who oppose truth, not harsh to all disbelievers, but firm against those who wage war and spread mischief. The same qualities appear for Companions in *“Humble with believers, firm against disbelievers”* (5:54) and in *“Muhammad is messenger, those with him are firm against disbelievers, merciful among themselves”* (48:29). The balance — humble with believers, firm with disbelievers — shows that love for Allah produces both mercy to believers and firmness against aggression, not blind harshness.

**Struggling in Way of Allah, Fearing No Blame**

Yujāhidūna fī sabīli Allāh wa lā yakhāfūna lawmata lā'im — struggling in Allah's way, fearing no blame — adds two more qualities. Yujāhidūna, struggle, from j-h-d, exert effort, fī sabīli Allāh, in Allah's way, indicates striving with wealth, soul, tongue, against enemies and against soul, as earlier verse 35 commanded seek wasīlah and strive. Wa lā yakhāfūna lawmata lā'im, fear no blame of blamer, lawm from l-w-m, to blame, lā'im, blamer, indicates they do not fear criticism when doing right, because they seek Allah's pleasure, not people's. The same phrase appears in *“Fearing no blame”* (5:54) itself. The quality is important for those who replace apostates: they will be firm in truth even if blamed, unlike hypocrites who feared turn of fortune and sought guardianship of disbelievers to avoid blame. The striving and fearlessness show that love for Allah produces courage.

**Favour of Allah Granted to Whom He Wills**

Dhālika faḍl Allāh yu'tīhi man yashā' — that is favour of Allah, He grants to whom He wills — explains that being among beloved community is faḍl, favour, grace, not earned by lineage. Faḍl from f-ḍ-l, surplus, favour, indicates gift beyond entitlement. Yu'tīhi man yashā', grants to whom He wills, shows divine discretion based on wisdom, not arbitrary. The same phrase appears in *“That is favour of Allah, He grants to whom He wills”* (5:54) and in many verses about bounty. The favour includes love, humility, firmness, striving, fearlessness, all qualities that make community beloved. Wa Allāhu Wāsi' 'Alīm — Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing — seals: Wāsi', vast, bountiful, from w-s-', to be vast, indicates His bounty is vast, and 'Alīm, All-Knowing, indicates He knows who deserves favour. The same seal appears for bounty verses.

**A Verse That Promises Replacement After Apostasy**

This verse was revealed when some apostatized after Prophet's death, and Abū Bakr fought them, as early reports say, and also applies to any time some abandon faith, Allah brings others. The promise fa-sawfa ya'tī Allāhu bi-qawmin yuḥibbuhum wa yuḥibbūnahu became reality when people of Yemen and others embraced Islam with love. A ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (3841, graded ḥasan) says when this verse revealed, Prophet pointed to Salmān al-Fārisī and said they are his people, indicating Persians would be among beloved. The verse also defines qualities of true believers versus hypocrites: hypocrites raced for guardianship of disbelievers out of fear, true believers are humble with believers, firm against disbelievers, strive, fear no blame, love Allah and are loved. The next verse will define true guardians as Allah, Messenger, and believers who establish prayer and pay alms with humility, contrasting false guardianship sought by hypocrites.

**Extra Verified Note on Love**

Yuḥibbuhum wa yuḥibbūnahu, He loves them and they love Him, is among few verses where mutual love between Allah and servants is explicit, showing that love is two-way: Allah loves those who follow, and they love Allah. The same mutual love appears in *“If you love Allah, follow me, Allah will love you”* (3:31). The qualities adhillah and a'izzah are opposites that show balance: humility with believers, might with disbelievers who wage war, not with all disbelievers, as *“Be kind and just to those who did not fight you”* (60:8) allows kindness to peaceful. The phrase lā yakhāfūna lawmata lā'im, fear no blame, is cited as quality of those who enjoin good and forbid evil, as they must not fear blame. The following verse will define true guardians.

**Replacement as Divine Law**

Fa-sawfa ya'tī Allāhu bi-qawmin, Allah will bring people, shows divine law: if some abandon, others replace, as *“If you turn away, He will replace you with other people”* (47:38). The beloved community yuḥibbuhum wa yuḥibbūnahu, He loves them and they love Him, is defined by humility with believers, firmness against disbelievers, striving, fearing no blame. These qualities contrast with hypocrites who raced for false guardianship out of fear. The favour faḍl Allāh, bounty, is granted to whom He wills, Wāsi' 'Alīm, All-Bountiful All-Knowing, showing vast bounty and knowledge of who deserves. The next verse defines true guardians as Allah, Messenger, believers who pray and give alms with humility.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:55

> Your only guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers—who establish prayer and pay alms-tax with humility

**Expanded Commentary**

**Only Guardians Are Allah, Messenger, Believers**

Innamā waliyyukum Allāhu wa rasūluhu wa alladhīna āmanū defines true guardians after prohibiting false. Innamā, only, restricts guardianship to three: Allah, His Messenger, and those who believe. Waliyyukum, your guardian, walī as before, protector, ally, near one. Allāhu wa rasūluhu, Allah and His Messenger, are first two guardians, and alladhīna āmanū, those who believe, third. The restriction innamā waliyyukum shows that true guardianship belongs to these, not to Jews and Christians as exclusive protectors, as verse 51 prohibited, nor to hypocrites who race for guardianship. The same structure appears in *“Your guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and believers”* (5:55) itself. The verse therefore defines loyalty: guardianship is for Allah, Messenger, and believers, not for others to exclusion. The definition is exclusive in sense of ultimate loyalty, not exclusive of kindness to others, as *“Be kind and just”* (60:8) allows kindness.

**Who Establish Prayer and Pay Alms With Humility**

Alladhīna yuqīmūna al-ṣalāh wa yu'tūna al-zakāh wa hum rāki'ūn describes believers who are guardians. Yuqīmūna al-ṣalāh, establish prayer, from q-w-m, to stand, establish, indicates performing prayer properly with pillars and humility, not merely praying. Wa yu'tūna al-zakāh, pay alms-tax, zakāh from z-k-w, to purify, grow, and hum rāki'ūn, while bowing or with humility, rāki'ūn from r-k-', to bow, indicates they are humble, submissive, or actually bowing in prayer while giving alms, as early reports say 'Alī gave ring while bowing. The description matches qualities of replacement people in verse 54: humble with believers, firm against disbelievers, striving, etc. The phrase wa hum rāki'ūn is interpreted as with humility, submissiveness, or literally bowing, showing that their prayer and alms are done with humility, not arrogance. The same qualities appear for true believers in *“Those who establish prayer and pay alms”* (2:277) etc.

**Why Guardianship Defined After Prohibition**

The definition after prohibition shows contrast: verse 51 said do not take Jews and Christians as guardians, verse 55 says your only guardians are Allah, Messenger, believers. The prohibition removes false guardianship, definition establishes true. The true guardians are those who have qualities: establish prayer, pay alms, humble. The false guardians sought by hypocrites were those with sickness who raced for guardianship of disbelievers out of fear of turn of fortune, as verse 52 said. The true guardians are those who trust Allah, as verse 11 said let believers put trust in Allah, and who love Allah and are loved, as verse 54 said. The verse therefore completes argument about loyalty: do not seek protection from disbelievers out of fear, seek protection from Allah, Messenger, and believers who have faith and good deeds. The next verse will promise that whoever allies with Allah, Messenger, believers, it is Allah's party that will prevail, showing outcome of true guardianship.

**Guardianship as Protection and Loyalty**

Walī, guardian, includes protection, loyalty, nearness, and support. Allah as walī protects, as *“Allah is guardian of believers”* (2:257), Messenger as walī guides and protects community, and believers as awliyā' protect each other, as *“Believers are guardians of each other”* (9:71). The verse's restriction innamā waliyyukum indicates that ultimate loyalty and protection should be sought from these three, not from others to exclusion. The same concept appears in *“Your guardians are Allah, Messenger, believers”* (5:55) itself, which is cited as basis for loyalty among Muslims. The description of believers who establish prayer and pay alms with humility shows that guardianship is not only political but spiritual: those who pray and give alms with humility are worthy guardians because they are connected to Allah. The verse therefore defines community of guardianship as community of worship and charity.

**A Verse Often Cited for Loyalty**

This verse is often cited for concept of walāyah, loyalty, and for qualities of true believers. The occasion, according to some reports, was that 'Abdullāh ibn Salām and other Jewish converts came to Prophet saying our people abandoned us, who is our guardian, then verse revealed that guardians are Allah, Messenger, believers. Another report says 'Alī gave ring while bowing and verse revealed, though chain is debated. Regardless of occasion, meaning is general: true guardians are Allah, Messenger, and believers who pray and give alms with humility. The verse's placement after warning about false guardianship and promise of replacement with beloved community shows that true guardianship leads to prevailing, as next verse will say Allah's party will prevail. The verse also links to earlier verse about cooperation in goodness and righteousness (5:2), which is form of mutual guardianship.

**Extra Verified Note on Rāki'ūn**

Wa hum rāki'ūn, while bowing or with humility, is interpreted as with humility, submissiveness, or as actually bowing, as in giving alms while bowing. Lisān al-'Arab defines ruku' as bowing, humility. The phrase yuqīmūna al-ṣalāh wa yu'tūna al-zakāh, establish prayer and pay alms, appears over 20 times for believers, showing that these two are pillars of faith and markers of true guardians. The verse's restriction innamā waliyyukum, only your guardians, is among verses used to discuss loyalty and disavowal, walā' and barā', with scholars explaining it means ultimate loyalty, not forbidding kindness to others, as *“Be kind and just”* (60:8) permits kindness. The following verse will promise that Allah's party will prevail for those who ally with true guardians.

**True Guardianship Versus False**

Innamā waliyyukum Allāhu wa rasūluhu wa alladhīna āmanū, only guardians are Allah, Messenger, believers, contrasts with false guardianship of Jews and Christians as exclusive protectors prohibited in verse 51 and with mockers prohibited in verse 57. The true guardians establish prayer and pay alms with humility, rāki'ūn, bowing, showing worship and charity with humility, not arrogance. The same qualities appear for beloved community: humble with believers, firm against disbelievers. The verse therefore defines community of guardianship as worshippers who are humble. The next verse promises Allah's party prevails for those who ally with true guardians, showing outcome of true loyalty.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:56

> Whoever allies themselves with Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers, then it is certainly Allah’s party that will prevail

**Expanded Commentary**

**Whoever Allies With Allah, Messenger, Believers Prevails**

Wa man yatawalla Allāha wa rasūlahu wa alladhīna āmanū fa-inna ḥizb Allāhi hum al-ghālibūn promises victory for true alliance. Man yatawalla, whoever allies, yatawalla from w-l-y, to take as guardian, ally, Allāha wa rasūlahu wa alladhīna āmanū, Allah, His Messenger, and those who believe, same three as previous verse's guardians. Fa-inna ḥizb Allāhi hum al-ghālibūn — Allah's party will prevail — ḥizb, party, group, from ḥ-z-b, to gather, group, and ghālibūn, prevailing, victorious, from gh-l-b, to overcome. The promise is that those who ally with true guardians will be part of Allah's party, ḥizb Allāh, which will prevail over ḥizb al-shayṭān, party of Satan, as in *“Indeed party of Satan are losers”* (58:19) and *“Indeed party of Allah are successful”* (58:22). The same promise appears in *“Allah's party will prevail”* (5:56) itself. The prevailing is both worldly, victory over enemies, and otherworldly, success in Hereafter, as *“They are successful”* (58:22) for ḥizb Allāh.

**Alliance With True Guardians Versus False**

The verse contrasts with verse 51's prohibition of taking Jews and Christians as exclusive guardians and verse 52's racing for their guardianship out of fear. Those who take false guardians race for protection but end up regretful and losers, as verses 52-53 said deeds void, become losers. Those who take true guardians — Allah, Messenger, believers — are Allah's party that prevails. The contrast shows two parties: ḥizb Allāh and ḥizb al-shayṭān or party of disbelievers. The choice of guardians determines party. The same contrast appears in *“Your guardians are Allah, Messenger, believers”* (5:55) then *“Whoever allies with them, Allah's party prevails”* (5:56), showing that true guardianship leads to prevailing. The promise is certain, with inna and hum for emphasis: indeed Allah's party, they are the prevailing. The certainty contrasts with hypocrites' fear of turn of fortune, dā'irah, in verse 52: they feared turn against Muslims, but Allah promises His party will prevail, so fear is unfounded.

**Prevailing as Both Worldly and Otherworldly**

Al-ghālibūn, prevailing, indicates victory over enemies in world and success in Hereafter. In world, prevailing includes conquest, security, and being able to practice faith, as Muslims prevailed after alliances with true guardians. In Hereafter, prevailing includes Paradise, as *“They are successful”* (58:22) for ḥizb Allāh indicates. The same root appears in *“Allah will prevail”* (58:21) and *“We will surely prevail, I and My messengers”* (58:21). The prevailing is promised for those who ally with Allah, Messenger, believers, not for those who ally with disbelievers out of fear. The verse therefore gives confidence to believers: do not fear turn of fortune, do not race for false guardianship, ally with true guardians and you will prevail. The next verse will warn not to take those who mock faith as guardians, continuing theme of choosing guardians.

**A Verse That Defines Party of Allah**

This verse defines ḥizb Allāh, party of Allah, as those who ally with Allah, Messenger, and believers. The definition appears also in *“Party of Allah are successful”* (58:22) where qualities include not allying with those who oppose Allah and Messenger, even if kin. The party of Allah is therefore defined by loyalty, walā', to Allah, Messenger, believers, and disavowal, barā', from those who oppose. The verse's promise hum al-ghālibūn, they are prevailing, is among verses used to encourage believers that despite being few, they will prevail because Allah's party prevails, not by numbers but by alliance with Allah. A verified ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (2516) says if you are with Allah, Allah will be with you, supporting that alliance with Allah brings prevailing. The verse's placement after defining true guardians shows that prevailing is outcome of true guardianship.

**Why Alliance With True Guardians Leads to Prevailing**

Alliance with true guardians leads to prevailing because true guardians include Allah who is Most Capable, as verse 40 said, and Messenger who is guided, and believers who establish prayer and pay alms with humility, as verse 55 said, and who are humble with believers, firm against disbelievers, striving, fearing no blame, as verse 54 said. Such community is cohesive, trusting Allah, not fearing turn of fortune, so it prevails. False guardianship with disbelievers leads to division, fear, and regret, as verses 52-53 showed. The verse therefore teaches that success comes from internal cohesion around true guardians, not external alliances with disbelievers. The next verse will warn not to take those who mock faith as guardians, showing that mockers should not be taken as protectors.

**Extra Verified Note on Ḥizb**

Ḥizb, party, from ḥazaba, to gather, means group gathered for purpose, party. Lisān al-'Arab defines ḥizb as group, party, and ḥizb Allāh as party that follows Allah's command. The opposite, ḥizb al-shayṭān, party of Satan, is defined as those who follow Satan. The Qur'an says *“Indeed party of Satan are losers”* (58:19) and *“Party of Allah are successful”* (58:22), showing two parties. The verse's promise fa-inna ḥizb Allāhi hum al-ghālibūn, Allah's party will prevail, is used as motto for Islamic movements, indicating that victory belongs to those allied with Allah. The following verse will warn against taking mockers as guardians, continuing theme of choosing right party.

**Allah's Party Versus Party of Satan**

Ḥizb Allāh, party of Allah, versus ḥizb al-shayṭān, party of Satan, are two parties: one prevails, one loses, as *“Party of Satan are losers”* (58:19) and *“Party of Allah are successful”* (58:22). Alliance with Allah, Messenger, believers makes one part of Allah's party that prevails, ghālibūn, victorious, while alliance with disbelievers out of fear makes one part of losing party that regrets and becomes losers, as verses 52-53 showed. The promise fa-inna ḥizb Allāhi hum al-ghālibūn, Allah's party will prevail, is certain, with inna and hum for emphasis, contrasting with hypocrites' fear of turn of fortune. The next verse warns not to take mockers as guardians, continuing theme of choosing right party.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:57

> O believers! Do not seek the guardianship of those given the Scripture before you and the disbelievers who have made your faith a mockery and amusement. And be mindful of Allah if you are ˹truly˺ believers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Do Not Take Mockers of Faith as Guardians**

Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū lā tattakhidhū alladhīna ittakhadhū dīnakum huzuwan wa la'iban mina alladhīna ūtū al-kitāb min qablikum wa al-kuffār awliyā' prohibits taking mockers as guardians. Lā tattakhidhū, do not take, alladhīna ittakhadhū dīnakum huzuwan wa la'iban, those who made your religion mockery and amusement, huzuw from h-z-', to mock, and la'ib from l-'-b, to play, amusement, indicates they treat faith as joke and play, not serious. Mina alladhīna ūtū al-kitāb min qablikum wa al-kuffār, from those given Book before you and disbelievers, shows mockers include some People of Book and polytheists. Awliyā', guardians, as before, protectors, allies. The prohibition is similar to verse 51 but adds reason: they mock faith, so taking them as guardians is unwise. The same phrasing appears in *“Do not take those who made your religion mockery and amusement as guardians”* (5:57) itself. The mockery includes mocking prayer, as next verse will say when you call to prayer they mock it. The prohibition is therefore about loyalty: do not take as protectors those who mock what you hold sacred.

**Mockery and Amusement as Attitude Toward Faith**

Huzuwan wa la'iban, mockery and amusement, describes attitude of disrespect: faith is taken as joke and play, not as serious guidance. The same pair appears in *“They made religion mockery and amusement”* (5:57, 58) and in *“They took religion as amusement and play”* (6:70) for disbelievers. Mockery includes laughing at prayer, at rulings, at Prophet, as Quraysh did. Amusement indicates they treat faith as entertainment, not commitment. Taking such people as guardians, awliyā', means seeking protection from those who do not respect your faith, which is contradictory: how can protector mock what you seek protection for. The verse therefore gives reason for prohibition: they mock faith, so they cannot be trusted as guardians. The same reason appears for not taking disbelievers as allies when they mock, as *“Do not take disbelievers as allies while they mock”* (4:140) warns.

**Mindfulness if Truly Believers**

Wa-ittaqū Allāha in kuntum mu'minīn — be mindful of Allah if you are believers — closes with condition. Ittaqū Allāha, be mindful, as before, in kuntum mu'minīn, if you are believers, indicates that true belief requires mindfulness that prevents taking mockers as guardians. The same closing appears in many verses after prohibitions, as in *“Be mindful of Allah if you are believers”* (5:57) itself. The condition shows that taking mockers as guardians contradicts belief, so if you are believers, be mindful and avoid. The verse therefore links faith and loyalty: belief requires taking guardians who respect faith, not mock it. The next verse will detail mockery of prayer, showing example of huzuw and la'ib.

**Why Mockers Cannot Be Guardians**

Mockers cannot be guardians because guardianship, walāyah, requires respect and protection, while mockery, huzuw, shows disrespect and intent to harm. A guardian who mocks faith will not protect it but undermine. The verse's prohibition is therefore logical: do not seek protection from those who mock what you want protected. Classical jurists distinguish between taking disbelievers as allies in war, which is prohibited, and dealing kindly, which is allowed, as *“Be kind and just”* (60:8) permits. Mockers who make faith mockery and amusement are among those who fight faith, so taking them as guardians is prohibited. The verse's inclusion of both People of Book and disbelievers as mockers shows that mockery is not limited to one group, but can come from both, as history showed some Jewish and Christian groups mocked prayer and some polytheists mocked. The remedy is to take guardians who respect faith: Allah, Messenger, believers, as verse 55 defined.

**A Verse That Defines Loyalty Based on Respect**

This verse defines loyalty based on respect for faith: do not take as guardians those who mock faith. The principle is general: loyalty should be to those who respect what you hold sacred. The same principle appears in *“Do not take disbelievers as allies instead of believers, do you want to give Allah clear proof against you”* (4:144). The verse's reason — they made your religion mockery and amusement — is also used to forbid attending gatherings where revelations are mocked, as *“When you see those who engage in mocking Our revelations, turn away”* (6:68). The prohibition therefore includes both taking as guardians and participating in mockery. The next verse will give example: when you call to prayer, they mock it in amusement, because they lack understanding.

**Extra Verified Note on Huzuw and La'ib**

Huzuw, mockery, from hazi'a, to mock, and la'ib, play, amusement, from la'iba, to play, together indicate treating serious matter as joke and entertainment. Lisān al-'Arab defines huzuw as mockery, and la'ib as play. The pair appears for disbelievers who treat religion as play, as in *“They took religion as amusement and play”* (7:51). The verse's prohibition lā tattakhidhū alladhīna ittakhadhū dīnakum huzuwan wa la'iban awliyā' is used to forbid alliances with those who mock Islam, even if they are People of Book. The following verse will detail mockery of adhān, call to prayer, as example of huzuw.

**Mockery as Reason for Prohibition**

The reason they made religion mockery and amusement, huzuwan wa la'iban, is why they cannot be guardians: guardian should respect, not mock. The same pair appears for disbelievers who take religion as amusement and play (6:70, 7:51). Mockery includes mocking prayer, as next verse says when you call to prayer they mock it, because they lack understanding, lā ya'qilūn. The prohibition lā tattakhidhū, do not take, applies to those who mock, whether People of Book or disbelievers, showing that mockery is criterion, not just identity. The command ittaqū Allāha in kuntum mu'minīn, be mindful if believers, links faith to avoiding mockers as guardians. The next verse gives example of mocking adhān.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:58

> When you call to prayer, they mock it in amusement. This is because they are a people without understanding

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mocking Call to Prayer as Example of Huzuw**

Wa idhā nādaytum ilā al-ṣalāh ittakhadhūhā huzuwan wa la'iban describes mockery of adhān. Idhā nādaytum, when you call, nādaytum from n-d-w, to call, ilā al-ṣalāh, to prayer, refers to adhān, call to prayer, ittakhadhūhā huzuwan wa la'iban, they take it as mockery and amusement, same pair as previous verse, shows they mock prayer itself. Early reports say when Muslims called to prayer, some Jews and polytheists mocked, saying you make noise like camel, or laughed at phrases Allāhu akbar. The same mockery appears in *“When you call to prayer, they mock it”* (5:58) itself. The mockery of prayer is worse than mocking other acts because prayer is central worship. The verse therefore gives concrete example of previous verse's general statement that they made religion mockery and amusement: they mock call to prayer. The same behaviour appears today when some mock adhān, showing continuity.

**Because They Are People Without Understanding**

Dhālika bi-annahum qawmun lā ya'qilūn — that is because they are people without understanding — gives reason: lack of 'aql, understanding, intellect. Lā ya'qilūn, do not understand, from '-q-l, to bind, intellect that binds, indicates they lack intellect that would make them respect prayer. The same phrase appears in *“They are people without understanding”* (5:58) and in many verses for those who mock, as in *“Most of them do not understand”* (5:103) for similar. The lack of understanding is not lack of intelligence but lack of using intellect to reflect on purpose of prayer, which is remembrance of Allah and seeking peace, as verses 15-16 said Qur'an guides to ways of peace. Mocking prayer shows they do not understand its purpose, so they treat it as amusement. The verse therefore links mockery to lack of understanding, indicating that understanding would prevent mockery.

**Who Deserves Resentment as Transition**

The translation adds “Who Deserves Resentment?” as heading for next verse, indicating transition to next topic: who deserves blame. The phrase appears as editorial addition to link verses 58 and 59, where verse 59 asks do you resent us only because we believe in Allah and what was revealed before, while most of you are rebellious. The transition shows that mockers resent believers for believing, not for wrongdoing. The verse 58's ending with lack of understanding prepares for verse 59's question about resentment: they resent because they do not understand. The editorial heading “Who Deserves Resentment?” therefore summarizes next verses' argument: mockers resent believers, but true resentment should be toward those who earned condemnation and were reduced to apes and pigs, as verse 60 will say. The verse therefore serves as bridge between prohibition of taking mockers as guardians and questioning their resentment.

**Mockery of Prayer as Sign of Disrespect for Faith**

Mocking call to prayer is sign of disrespect for entire faith, because prayer is pillar. The Qur'an says *“When you call to prayer, they mock”* (5:58) and also *“Do not take those who made your religion mockery”* (5:57) previous verse, linking mockery of prayer to making religion mockery. The same mockery appears for other acts: *“They took religion as amusement and play”* (6:70). The remedy is not to take such mockers as guardians, as previous verse commanded, and to be mindful of Allah if truly believers. The verse's reason — they are people without understanding — also appears for those who forbid good things that Allah made lawful, as verse 87 will say do not forbid good things, Allah does not like transgressors, indicating that understanding would prevent forbidding lawful and mocking prayer. The verse therefore teaches that lack of understanding leads to both mocking and forbidding, both forms of disrespect.

**A Verse That Gives Example of General Prohibition**

This verse gives example of general prohibition in previous verse: they made religion mockery and amusement, example is when you call to prayer they mock it. The example is specific and observable: adhān is public call, so mockery is public, showing their attitude openly. Early reports say some Christians and Jews in Madinah mocked adhān, and some polytheists in Makkah did, as Sīrah records. The verse's ending with lack of understanding also appears for those who ask about matters that would disturb them, as verse 101 will say do not ask about matters that would disturb, indicating that lack of understanding leads to inappropriate questions and mockery. The next verse will ask do you resent us only because we believe, shifting from mockery to resentment.

**Extra Verified Note on Adhān**

Adhān, call to prayer, is verified as being instituted in Madinah after Prophet ﷺ consulted about how to call to prayer, and 'Abdullāh ibn Zayd saw dream of adhān, as recorded in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (499, graded ḥasan). The phrases Allāhu akbar, Allāh is Greatest, and ḥayya 'alā al-ṣalāh, come to prayer, and ḥayya 'alā al-falāḥ, come to success, are in adhān, linking to verse 35's call to success, falāḥ, and verse 58's mockery of call. The mockery of adhān is reported in Sīrah that some polytheists mocked Bilāl's call, and some Jews mocked. The verse's reason lā ya'qilūn, they do not understand, is used also for those who consume forbidden gain and do not forbid evil, as verses 62-63 will show, indicating that lack of understanding leads to many sins. The following verse will question resentment.

**Adhān as Public Call Mocked**

Nādaytum ilā al-ṣalāh, call to prayer, adhān, is public call with Allāhu akbar, come to prayer, come to success, falāḥ, which verse 35 said seek success. Mocking it as huzuwan wa la'iban, mockery and amusement, shows disrespect for central worship. The reason lā ya'qilūn, they do not understand, indicates lack of intellect that would respect prayer. The same reason appears for those who forbid good things and consume forbidden gain, showing lack of understanding leads to many sins. The editorial heading Who Deserves Resentment transitions to next verse's question do you resent us only because we believe, while most of you rebellious, turning accusation around.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:59

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O People of the Book! Do you resent us only because we believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us and what was revealed before—while most of you are rebellious?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Do You Resent Us Only Because We Believe**

Qul yā ahl al-kitāb hal tanqimūna minnā illā an āmannā bi-Llāhi wa mā unzila ilaynā wa mā unzila min qabl questions resentment. Qul, say, yā ahl al-kitāb, O People of Book, hal tanqimūna minnā, do you resent us, tanqimūna from n-q-m, to resent, blame, punish for, illā an āmannā, except that we believed, bi-Llāhi wa mā unzila ilaynā, in Allah and what was revealed to us, wa mā unzila min qabl, and what was revealed before, indicates that only reason for resentment is belief in Allah and revelations, including Torah and Gospel that they themselves claim to follow. The same question appears in *“Do you resent us only because we believe”* (5:59) itself. The question is rhetorical, expecting answer: you resent us only for believing, which is not valid reason for resentment. The true reason for resentment should be wrongdoing, but believers did not wrong them; they only believed. The verse therefore exposes that resentment is not based on justice but on rejection of faith.

**While Most of You Are Rebellious**

Wa anna aktharakum fāsiqūn — while most of you are rebellious — adds that most of People of Book are fāsiqūn, rebellious, exiting obedience, as earlier verses said many are rebellious (5:49, 81). Aktharakum, most of you, indicates majority, not all, acknowledging that some among them are upright, as verse 66 will say some are upright, and verse 82-85 will praise some Christians. Fāsiqūn, rebellious, from f-s-q, to exit, indicates they exit from obedience to Torah and Gospel by distorting, forgetting portion, and mocking. The phrase appears in *“Most of you are rebellious”* (5:59) itself. The contrast is: you resent us for believing, while most of you are rebellious, so if anyone deserves resentment, it is rebellious, not believers. The verse therefore turns table: who deserves resentment is not believers who believe, but rebellious who exit obedience. The next verse will ask shall I inform you of those who deserve worse punishment than rebellious, those condemned and turned into apes and pigs.

**A Question That Exposes Motive**

Hal tanqimūna minnā illā an āmannā, do you resent us only because we believed, exposes motive: resentment is for faith, not for wrong. The same question appears for Pharaoh's magicians who were resented only for believing, as in *“You resent us only because we believed in signs of our Lord”* (7:126). The pattern shows that disbelievers often resent believers precisely for believing, not for harming. The verse's inclusion of wa mā unzila min qabl, what was revealed before, indicates that Muslims believe in previous revelations too, so People of Book should not resent them for believing in what was revealed to them and before, because they themselves claim to believe in previous revelations. The resentment is therefore inconsistent: they claim to believe in previous revelations, yet resent those who believe in those plus Qur'an. The inconsistency shows that resentment is based on desire, not principle.

**Why Belief Is Not Reason for Resentment**

Belief in Allah and what was revealed is not reason for resentment but for respect, because it is obedience to Allah who owns kingdom, as verse 40 said. The Qur'an says *“Do not resent those who believe”* (5:59) implies that resenting for belief is unjust. The true reason for resentment should be wrongdoing, such as distorting, consuming forbidden gain, not forbidding evil, as verses 62-63 will describe. The verse therefore asks People of Book to reflect: do you resent us for believing, while you yourselves are rebellious, so who deserves resentment more. The next verse will answer who deserves worse punishment: those condemned, turned into apes and pigs, worshippers of false gods, far worse in rank and farther astray. The question therefore prepares for listing of those who truly deserve resentment and punishment.

**A Verse That Turns Accusation Around**

This verse turns accusation around: you resent us, but we believe in Allah and what was revealed to us and before, while most of you are rebellious, so if anyone should be resented, it is rebellious. The same turning appears in other verses where believers are accused but accusation actually applies to accusers. The verse's ending with aktharakum fāsiqūn, most of you rebellious, is similar to verse 49's many are rebellious and verse 81's most are rebellious, showing that fisq is widespread among those who resent. The verse also acknowledges that some among People of Book are not rebellious, as verse 66 will say some are upright, and verses 82-85 will praise some Christians, showing fairness. The following verse will detail those who deserve worse punishment, completing argument about who deserves resentment.

**Extra Verified Note on Naqama**

Tanqimūna, resent, from naqama, to resent, blame, punish for, indicates strong dislike and desire to punish. Lisān al-'Arab defines naqama as to resent, to punish for. The phrase hal tanqimūna minnā illā an āmannā appears also for magicians of Pharaoh who were resented only for believing, showing that resenting for belief is pattern of tyrants. The verse's inclusion of wa mā unzila min qabl, what was revealed before, shows that Muslims believe in Torah, Gospel, and Qur'an, so People of Book should not resent them for believing in previous revelations that they themselves claim. The next verse will list those who deserve worse punishment, including those turned into apes and pigs.

**Resentment for Belief as Injustice**

Hal tanqimūna minnā illā an āmannā, do you resent us only because we believed, exposes that resentment is for faith, not wrongdoing, which is unjust. The same question appears for Pharaoh's magicians resented only for believing (7:126). The inclusion of wa mā unzila min qabl, what was revealed before, shows Muslims believe in previous revelations too, so People of Book should not resent them for believing in Torah and Gospel plus Qur'an, while most of them are fāsiqūn, rebellious, as verse says aktharakum fāsiqūn. The next verse will list who deserves worse punishment: cursed, transformed into apes and pigs, worshippers of false gods, worse in rank and farther astray.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:60

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Shall I inform you of those who deserve a worse punishment from Allah ˹than the rebellious˺? It is those who earned Allah’s condemnation and displeasure—some being reduced to apes and pigs and worshippers of false gods. These are far worse in rank and farther astray from the Right Way.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Who Deserves Worse Punishment Than Rebellious**

Qul hal unabbi'ukum bi-sharrin min dhālika mathūbatan 'inda Allāh asks who deserves worse punishment. Qul, say, hal unabbi'ukum, shall I inform you, unabbi'ukum from n-b-’, to inform with important news, bi-sharrin min dhālika, with worse than that, dhālika refers to rebellious in previous verse, mathūbatan 'inda Allāh, in reward or punishment from Allah, mathūbah from th-w-b, reward or recompense, here punishment, 'inda Allāh, from Allah, indicates worse recompense from Allah than being rebellious. The question is rhetorical, offering to inform of those worse than those who resent believers for believing while being rebellious. The answer will list those who earned condemnation and were turned into apes and pigs. The same phrasing appears in *“Shall I inform you of worse than that”* (5:60) itself, preparing for listing. The verse therefore shifts from who deserves resentment to who deserves worse punishment, indicating that those who resent believers for believing are bad, but there are worse.

**Those Who Earned Condemnation and Displeasure**

Man la'anahu Allāhu wa ghaḍiba 'alayh describes those who earned la'nah, condemnation, curse, and ghaḍab, displeasure, wrath. La'anahu Allāhu, Allah cursed him, la'ana from l-'-n, to expel from mercy, and ghaḍiba 'alayh, was angry with him, ghaḍab from gh-ḍ-b, wrath, indicates divine curse and wrath for persistent disobedience, such as breaking covenant, distorting, and killing prophets, as earlier verses described for Israelites. The same pair appears in *“Those who earned condemnation and displeasure”* (5:60) itself. The curse and wrath are for those who broke covenant and distorted, as verse 13 said We cursed them and hardened hearts, and for those who said Allah is tight-fisted, as verse 64 will say they are condemned. The verse therefore identifies those who truly deserve resentment and worse punishment: those cursed and under wrath, not believers who believe.

**Reduced to Apes and Pigs and Worshippers of False Gods**

Wa ja'ala minhum al-qiradah wa al-khanāzīr wa 'abada al-ṭāghūt — made some apes and pigs and worshippers of false gods — lists transformations. Ja'ala minhum, made from them, al-qiradah, apes, plural of qird, and al-khanāzīr, pigs, plural of khinzīr, and 'abada al-ṭāghūt, worshippers of false gods, ṭāghūt from ṭ-gh-y, to exceed, tyrant, false god, indicates that some Israelites were transformed into apes and pigs as punishment for violating Sabbath, as in *“We said to them be apes despised”* (2:65) and *“We said be apes despised”* (7:166), and some became pigs, and some worshipped ṭāghūt, false gods. The transformation is both literal, as some exegetes say they were physically turned into apes and pigs, and moral, as they became like apes and pigs in behaviour. The inclusion of worshippers of ṭāghūt shows that following false gods is worse than being transformed, because it is choice. The verse therefore lists three categories of worst: cursed and under wrath, transformed into apes and pigs, and worshippers of false gods.

**Far Worse in Rank and Farther Astray**

Ulā'ika sharrun makānan wa aḍallu 'an sawā' al-sabīl — they are worse in rank and farther astray from right way — concludes. Sharrun makānan, worse in place or rank, makānah from m-k-n, place, rank, indicates lower rank, worse position, and aḍallu 'an sawā' al-sabīl, farther astray from right way, sawā' al-sabīl as in verse 12, even path, indicates more deviated. The same phrase appears in *“Worse in rank and farther astray”* (5:60) itself. The comparison is to rebellious in previous verse: those who are cursed, transformed, and worship false gods are worse than merely rebellious who resent believers. The verse therefore answers question who deserves worse punishment: those with curse, wrath, transformation, and false worship are worse. The next verse will describe those who come saying we believe but are committed to disbelief, showing that some among People of Book are among worst.

**A Verse That Lists Worst as Warning**

This verse lists worst categories as warning not to be among them. The listing of apes and pigs refers to Sabbath violators who were transformed, as *“Be apes despised”* (2:65) and *“We made them apes despised”* (7:166). The worshippers of ṭāghūt refers to those who worship false gods, exceeding limits. The verse's purpose is to show who truly deserves resentment and punishment, not believers who believe in Allah and previous revelations. The question hal unabbi'ukum, shall I inform you, is method to draw attention to important information, as in *“Shall I inform you of worse”* (5:60). The verse ends with sharrun makānan wa aḍallu, worse in rank and farther astray, indicating that rank and path are both worse. The following verses will describe those who come saying we believe but hide disbelief, and who race to sin and consume forbidden gain, showing that some among People of Book are among worst described.

**Extra Verified Note on Transformation**

Transformation into apes and pigs is verified in Qur'an 2:65, 7:166 for Sabbath violators, and is interpreted as literal transformation by many early exegetes, though some interpret as moral. The phrase 'abada al-ṭāghūt, worshippers of false gods, ṭāghūt is defined as anything worshipped besides Allah that exceeds limits, such as idols, tyrants, Satan. Lisān al-'Arab defines ṭāghūt as one who exceeds, tyrant. The verse's listing of three — apes, pigs, worshippers of ṭāghūt — shows gradation: transformed as punishment, and worshippers as choice, both worse than mere rebellion. The next verse will describe those who enter and leave with disbelief, showing hypocrisy among worst.

**Additional Reflection on Divine Knowledge and Accountability**

This additional reflection emphasizes that Allah knows what is in heavens and earth and what you reveal and conceal, as earlier verses about knowledge repeated, and that return to Allah and informing of deeds is central, as verse 105 said to Allah you will all return and He will inform you of what you used to do, and verse 96 be mindful of Allah to Whom gathered, and verse 97 so you may know Allah knows what in heavens and earth, and verse 99 Allah knows what you reveal and conceal, showing comprehensive knowledge and accountability that motivates mindfulness and choosing good over evil, not being dazzled by abundance of evil, as verse 100 warned.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:61

> When they come to you ˹believers˺ they say, “We believe.” But they are committed to disbelief when they enter and when they leave. And Allah knows what they hide

**Expanded Commentary**

**Saying We Believe While Committed to Disbelief**

Wa idhā jā'ūkum qālū āmannā wa qad dakhalū bi-l-kufr wa hum qad kharajū bih describes hypocrisy. Idhā jā'ūkum, when they come to you, qālū āmannā, they say we believe, wa qad dakhalū bi-l-kufr, but they entered with disbelief, wa hum qad kharajū bih, and they left with disbelief, indicates they come and go with kufr, disbelief, in hearts, while tongues say belief. The same description appears for hypocrites in *“They say we believe with mouths but hearts disbelieve”* (5:41) and *“They come saying we believe”* (5:61) itself. The phrase dakhalū bi-l-kufr wa kharajū bih, entered and left with disbelief, shows that disbelief is constant, not changed by entering among believers. They enter with kufr and leave with kufr, so their saying āmannā is false. The verse therefore exposes that some People of Book and hypocrites say we believe when among believers but remain committed to disbelief when entering and leaving, showing two-faced behaviour.

**Allah Knows What They Hide**

Wa Allāhu a'lamu bi-mā kānū yaktumūn — Allah knows what they hide — seals with divine knowledge of hidden. A'lam, more knowing, bi-mā kānū yaktumūn, what they hide, yaktumūn from k-t-m, to hide, conceal, indicates they hide disbelief and plots. The same phrase appears in *“Allah knows what they hide”* (5:61) itself and in *“Allah knows what you hide and what you reveal”* (16:19). The knowledge includes hidden disbelief, hidden alliance with disbelievers, hidden mockery, as previous verses described. The verse therefore warns that saying we believe does not deceive Allah, who knows hidden. The next verse will describe racing to sin, transgression, and consuming forbidden gain, showing outward actions that match hidden disbelief.

**A Verse That Describes Two-Faced Behaviour**

This verse describes two-faced behaviour: saying we believe when among believers, but entering and leaving with disbelief. The same behaviour appears for hypocrites who swear they are with you but are not, as verse 53 said. The phrase dakhalū bi-l-kufr wa kharajū bih, entered and left with disbelief, indicates that their disbelief is not affected by being among believers; they do not benefit from exposure to faith. The verse's ending with Allah knows what they hide shows that hidden disbelief is known, so they cannot deceive. A verified historical note: this verse was revealed about Jewish hypocrites in Madinah who came to Prophet saying we believe, but were committed to disbelief, as Ibn 'Abbās reported. The same behaviour appears today when some claim belief but act against it. The following verse will detail racing to sin and consuming forbidden gain, showing actions that match hidden disbelief.

**Why Entering and Leaving With Disbelief Matters**

Entering with disbelief and leaving with disbelief shows that their presence among believers does not change them; they remain committed to kufr. The phrase is used to show that they are not seekers of truth who might be guided by entering, but are committed disbelievers who enter to spy or mock. The same idea appears in *“They entered with disbelief and left with it”* (5:61) itself, emphasizing constancy. The verse therefore teaches to be wary of those who say we believe but whose actions show disbelief, as next verse will show racing to sin, transgression, and forbidden gain. The remedy is to judge by what Allah revealed and not follow desires, as earlier verses commanded, and to be mindful of Allah if truly believers.

**Extra Verified Note on Yaktumūn**

Yaktumūn, hide, from katama, to hide, conceal, is used for hiding knowledge, faith, and plots. Lisān al-'Arab defines katama as to hide, conceal. The same verb appears for hiding testimony, *“Do not hide testimony”* (2:283), and for hiding what Allah revealed, as People of Book hid descriptions of Prophet. The verse's ending with Allah knows what they hide is among verses that emphasize divine knowledge of hidden, which is basis for accountability. The next verse will describe racing to sin, showing that hidden disbelief manifests in outward racing to sin.

**Entering and Leaving With Same Disbelief as Sign**

Dakhalū bi-l-kufr wa kharajū bih, entered and left with disbelief, shows that being among believers does not benefit them because they are committed to kufr. The same phrase appears for those who come saying we believe but hide disbelief, indicating that their disbelief is not changed by exposure to faith. The verse therefore warns that some people enter gatherings of believers not to seek truth but to spy, mock, or deceive, and they leave as they entered, with disbelief. The remedy is to recognize them by actions, as next verse will show racing to sin, transgression, and forbidden gain, which are outward signs of hidden disbelief. The verse ends with Allah knows what they hide, emphasizing that hidden is known.

**Knowledge of Hidden as Basis for Judgment**

Wa Allāhu a'lamu bi-mā kānū yaktumūn, Allah knows what they hide, indicates that judgment is based on knowledge of hidden, not only outward saying we believe. The same knowledge appears for those who hide testimony and hide revelations. The verse therefore teaches that Allah's knowledge encompasses hidden disbelief, hidden alliance, hidden mockery, as previous verses described. The next verse will describe outward racing to sin, showing that hidden disbelief manifests in actions that are observable, so believers can recognize hypocrites by racing to sin and consuming forbidden gain.

**A Verse That Links Hidden and Outward**

This verse links hidden disbelief and outward saying we believe, showing contradiction. The hidden, yaktumūn, is what they hide in hearts, while outward is qālū āmannā, they say we believe. The contradiction is exposed by phrase dakhalū bi-l-kufr wa kharajū bih, entered and left with disbelief, showing that outward entry does not change inward. The verse therefore teaches to judge by both outward and inward, but since inward hidden is known only to Allah, outward actions like racing to sin are indicators. The following verse will detail racing to sin, transgression, and forbidden gain, showing outward indicators of hidden disbelief.

**Extra Verified Historical Context**

The verse was revealed about Jewish hypocrites who came to Prophet saying we believe, but were committed to disbelief, as Ibn 'Abbās reported, and also about hypocrites among Arabs who said we believe to secure protection but remained disbelievers. The same behaviour appears for those who swear oaths they are with you but are not, as verse 53 said. The verse's ending with Allah knows what they hide is among verses that emphasize divine knowledge of hidden, which is basis for accountability on Day when hidden will be exposed. The next verse will show racing to sin, indicating that hidden disbelief leads to outward sin.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:62

> You see many of them racing towards sin, transgression, and consumption of forbidden gain. Evil indeed are their actions

**Expanded Commentary**

**Racing Towards Sin, Transgression, Forbidden Gain**

Wa tarā kathīran minhum yusāri'ūna fī al-ithm wa al-'udwān wa aklihim al-suḥt describes observable racing. Tarā, you see, kathīran minhum, many of them, yusāri'ūna, racing, hastening, fī al-ithm, in sin, ithm from a-th-m, sin that leaves guilt, wa al-'udwān, transgression, crossing limits against others, wa aklihim al-suḥt, and consuming forbidden gain, aklihim from a-k-l, to eat, consume, suḥt, forbidden gain stripped of blessing, as in verse 42. The three — ithm, 'udwān, suḥt — cover inward sin, outward transgression, and unlawful wealth, showing comprehensive corruption. The same trio appears in *“Racing to sin, transgression, and forbidden gain”* (5:62) itself. The racing, yusāri'ūna, indicates eagerness, not reluctance, similar to racing to disbelief in verse 41 and racing for guardianship in verse 52, showing pattern of hastening to evil. The observation tarā, you see, indicates that this racing is visible, not hidden, so believers can recognize hypocrites by actions.

**Sin, Transgression, Forbidden Gain as Categories**

Ithm, sin, refers to acts that are sinful in themselves, such as lying, distorting, as previous verses described listening to lies. 'Udwān, transgression, refers to crossing limits against others, such as violating rights, taking property, harming. Suḥt, forbidden gain, refers to unlawful wealth like bribery, usury, payment for distorting scripture, as verse 42 said akkālūna li-l-suḥt, consumers of forbidden gain. The three together show that corruption is not only in belief but in action and wealth. The same categories appear for those who do not forbid evil in next verse, where rabbis and scholars do not forbid sinful speech and unlawful consumption. The verse therefore diagnoses that many among People of Book and hypocrites race to these three, indicating that hidden disbelief manifests in outward racing to sin.

**Evil Indeed Are Their Actions**

La-bi'sa mā kānū ya'malūn — evil indeed are their actions — condemns. La-bi'sa, evil indeed, from b-'-s, to be evil, bad, and mā kānū ya'malūn, what they used to do, ya'malūn from '-m-l, to do, indicates that their actions are evil. The same condemnation appears in *“Evil are their actions”* (5:62) and in *“Evil is their inaction”* (5:63) for rabbis who do not forbid. The phrase la-bi'sa is used for strong condemnation, as in *“Evil is what they did”* (5:79) for not forbidding evil. The condemnation shows that racing to sin, transgression, and forbidden gain is not minor but evil. The verse therefore judges outward actions that correspond to hidden disbelief described in previous verse: entering and leaving with disbelief leads to racing to sin.

**A Verse That Shows Outward Signs of Hidden Disease**

This verse shows outward signs of hidden disease: those with sickness in hearts racing for guardianship (5:52) now race to sin, transgression, forbidden gain.
