The same judgment appears for claim Allah is Messiah (5:17, 72), showing that all deification of Messiah is disbelief. The verse's ending with painful punishment for those who do not stop shows that persistence is punished, but repentance is possible, as next verse will offer. The following verse will call to repentance and forgiveness.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:74

> Will they not turn to Allah in repentance and seek His forgiveness? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Expanded Commentary**

**Will They Not Turn in Repentance and Seek Forgiveness**

A-fa-lā yatūbūna ilā Allāhi wa yastaghfirūnah asks if they will not repent and seek forgiveness after Trinity and Messiah claims. A-fa-lā, will not, interrogative with fa- and lā, yatūbūna ilā Allāh, turn to Allah in repentance, tābū from t-w-b, to return, repent, wa yastaghfirūnah, seek His forgiveness, yastaghfirūn from gh-f-r, to seek covering, forgiveness, indicates that after warning of painful punishment for those who do not stop saying Trinity (5:73) and after Paradise forbidden for associating (5:72), they are invited to repent and seek forgiveness. The same question appears in *“Will they not turn to Allah in repentance and seek forgiveness”* (5:74) itself, offering hope. The question is rhetorical, encouraging repentance: will they not turn, implying they should. The invitation shows that even for grave claims like Allah is Messiah and third of three, repentance and forgiveness remain possible before death, as Allah is Forgiving, Merciful.

**Allah Is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful as Seal**

Wa Allāhu Ghafūr Raḥīm — Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful — seals with two names that offer hope after severe warnings. Ghafūr, intensely forgiving, and Raḥīm, Most Merciful, as before, indicate that Allah forgives those who repent and shows mercy. The same seal appears after many repentance invitations, as in *“Allah is Forgiving, Merciful”* (5:74) itself and *“Allah is Forgiving, Merciful”* (5:34, 39) for ḥirābah and theft. The seal shows that forgiveness is possible even for shirk if repented before death, as *“Allah does not forgive association, but forgives less than that for whom He wills”* (4:48) refers to those who die without repentance, but those who repent before death are forgiven, as *“Say to those who disbelieve, if they cease, what has passed will be forgiven”* (8:38) states. The verse therefore offers exit from Trinity and Messiah claims through repentance and seeking forgiveness.

**A Verse That Offers Hope After Warning**

This verse offers hope after warnings in verses 72-73 that Paradise forbidden for associating and painful punishment for Trinity if not stopped. The hope is conditional on turning in repentance and seeking forgiveness, yatūbūna wa yastaghfirūnah, turn and seek forgiveness. The same hope appears after other severe warnings, such as after describing disbelievers who cannot ransom even with everything doubled (5:36-37), after theft penalty (5:39) offers repentance and mending, and after ḥirābah (5:34) offers repentance before seizure. The pattern shows that Islamic law pairs warning with hope, severity with mercy, to encourage reform. The question a-fa-lā yatūbūna, will they not turn, is gentle, inviting, not condemning, after strong condemnation, showing that purpose of condemnation is to lead to repentance, not to despair.

**Why Repentance and Seeking Forgiveness Are Paired**

Yatūbūna ilā Allāh wa yastaghfirūnah, turn to Allah and seek forgiveness, pairs two acts: turning, tawbah, and seeking forgiveness, istighfār. Tawbah is returning from sin to obedience, and istighfār is seeking covering of sin so it does not lead to punishment. The pairing appears often, as in *“Turn to Allah and seek forgiveness”* (5:74) and *“Seek forgiveness and turn to Him”* (11:3). The order here — turn then seek forgiveness — indicates that turning is first, then seeking forgiveness, though often order reversed. Both are needed: turning without seeking forgiveness may leave sin uncovered, seeking forgiveness without turning may be insincere. The verse therefore invites both: turn from Trinity and Messiah claims, and seek forgiveness for having said them. The next verse will clarify Messiah's true status: only messenger, mother woman of truth, both ate food, showing why deification is false.

**A Verse That Invites Even Worst to Repent**

This verse invites even those who said Allah is Messiah and third of three, worst claims, to repent and seek forgiveness, showing that no sin is too great for forgiveness if repented before death. The same invitation appears for those who killed prophets, as verse 71 said Allah turned in forgiveness after repentance, and for those who waged war and spread mischief, as verse 34 offered repentance before seizure. The invitation is general, so all can repent. The seal Ghafūr Raḥīm, Forgiving Merciful, appears over 70 times, indicating that forgiveness and mercy are dominant attributes. The verse's question will they not turn is among verses that encourage repentance, as in *“Will they not turn to Allah”* (5:74) itself. The following verse will clarify Messiah's humanity: only messenger, mother woman of truth, both ate food.

**Extra Verified Note on Tawbah and Istighfār**

Tawbah, repentance, from tāba, to return, and istighfār, seeking forgiveness, from ghafara, to cover, are two related acts: tawbah is returning, istighfār is seeking covering. Lisān al-'Arab defines tawbah as returning from sin, and istighfār as seeking forgiveness. The pairing appears in many verses for repentance. The question a-fa-lā yatūbūna, will they not turn, appears also for those who do not turn after warnings, showing that turning is expected. The seal Ghafūr Raḥīm appears after many repentance verses, as in 5:74 itself, offering hope. The next verse will clarify Messiah's true status to help them turn.

**Turning to Allah After Grave Claims**

Yatūbūna ilā Allāh, turn to Allah, indicates turning from Trinity and Messiah claims to monotheism, as Messiah himself said worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord (5:72). The turning is from associating to worshipping One God, as verse 73 corrected there is only One God. The seeking forgiveness, yastaghfirūnah, is for having said Allah is Messiah and third of three, which are grave claims, but forgiveness remains possible, as seal Ghafūr Raḥīm shows. The same invitation appears for those who said Allah tight-fisted (5:64) and for those who waged war, showing that no claim is beyond forgiveness if repented. The next verse will clarify Messiah's humanity to help turning: only messenger, mother woman of truth, both ate food, showing neediness that contradicts divinity.

**Forgiving and Merciful as Dominant Attributes**

Ghafūr Raḥīm, Forgiving Merciful, as seal, appears over 70 times, showing that forgiveness and mercy are dominant, even after severe warnings like Paradise forbidden (5:72) and painful punishment (5:73). The dominance indicates that warning aims to lead to repentance, not despair. The question a-fa-lā yatūbūna, will they not turn, is gentle invitation after strong warning, method that appears throughout sūrah: critique followed by invitation, as verses 15 and 19 did after describing breach, offering Messenger who clarifies. The verse therefore offers hope that even worst claims can be forgiven if turned from and forgiveness sought, before death.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:75

> The Messiah, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger. ˹Many˺ messengers had ˹come and˺ gone before him. His mother was a woman of truth. They both ate food. See how We make the signs clear to them, yet see how they are deluded ˹from the truth˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**Messiah No More Than Messenger, Mother Woman of Truth**

Mā al-Masīḥ ibn Maryam illā rasūl qad khalat min qablihi al-rusul defines Messiah. Mā... illā, no more than, only, al-Masīḥ ibn Maryam, Messiah son of Mary, rasūl, messenger, qad khalat min qablihi al-rusul, messengers had passed before him, khalat from kh-l-w, to pass, go, indicates that many messengers came and went before him, so he is one in chain, not unique divine. Wa ummuhu ṣiddīqah — his mother was woman of truth — ṣiddīqah from ṣ-d-q, truthful, woman of truth, indicates Maryam was truthful, devout, not divine, as *“His mother was woman of truth”* (5:75) itself. The same description appears for Maryam as *“She was woman of truth”* (5:75). The phrase shows that both are human: messenger and woman of truth, not gods. The verse therefore counters deification by defining both as human with roles: messenger and truthful woman.

**They Both Ate Food as Proof of Humanity**

Kānā ya'kulāni al-ṭa'ām — they both ate food — gives proof of humanity. Ya'kulāni, they both eat, al-ṭa'ām, food, indicates need for food, which implies need, neediness, and need contradicts divinity, because God does not need food, but provides food. The same proof appears in *“They both ate food”* (5:75) itself, used to refute divinity: one who eats food needs to eat, needs to excrete, cannot be God who is self-sufficient, Ṣamad, as *“Allah, Absolute, He begets not nor begotten”* (112:2-3) says. The mention of both eating shows that mother and son share humanity and neediness, so neither is divine. Early exegetes note that eating food implies other human needs like hunger, thirst, excretion, all incompatible with divinity. The verse therefore uses simple observable fact — eating — to refute complex theological claim — divinity.

**See How We Make Signs Clear, Yet How Deluded**

Unẓur kayfa nubayyinu lahum al-āyāt thumma unẓur annā yu'fakūn — see how We make signs clear, yet see how deluded — shows amazement at delusion after clarity. Unẓur, look, see, kayfa nubayyinu lahum al-āyāt, how We make signs clear, nubayyinu from b-y-n, to clarify, al-āyāt, signs, indicates that Allah makes signs clear, such as Messiah being messenger, mother truthful, both eating food, and Messiah's own call worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord (5:72). Thumma unẓur annā yu'fakūn, then see how they are deluded, yu'fakūn from a-f-k, to turn away, delude, indicates that despite clear signs, they are turned away from truth, deluded. The same phrase appears in *“See how We make signs clear, yet how deluded”* (5:75) itself and in *“See how We make signs clear, yet they turn away”* (6:46) similar. The verse therefore expresses amazement that clear signs do not lead to acceptance, but to delusion, due to following desire and blind imitation.

**A Verse That Defines Messiah's Humanity Simply**

This verse defines Messiah's humanity simply: only messenger, messengers before him passed, mother woman of truth, both ate food. The simplicity is deliberate: complex Trinity doctrine is refuted by simple facts — messenger, truthful woman, eating food. The same simplicity appears in verse 17's argument who can prevent Allah if He chose to destroy Messiah, mother, and everyone, showing contingency, and here eating shows neediness. The verse's ending with unẓur... unẓur, see... see, invites reflection: see clarity, then see delusion, showing that delusion is not due to lack of clarity but due to turning away. The next verse will ask how can you worship besides Allah those who can neither harm nor benefit, Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing, continuing refutation of worshipping Messiah and mother.

**Why Eating Food Is Decisive Proof**

Eating food is decisive because it shows need and contingency: one who eats needs food, needs provider, cannot be provider of all. The Qur'an says *“They both ate food”* (5:75) to show that Messiah and mother are like other humans who eat, not like Allah who feeds and is not fed, as *“He feeds and is not fed”* (6:14) says. The proof is accessible to all, not only scholars, showing Qur'anic method of using observable to refute theological. The same method appears for idols: *“They cannot create fly even if they gathered”* (22:73) uses observable inability. The verse therefore teaches that divinity is incompatible with eating. The following verse will ask how can you worship those who cannot harm nor benefit, continuing argument that worship should be for One who can harm and benefit, All-Hearing, All-Knowing.

**Extra Verified Note on Ṣiddīqah**

Ṣiddīqah, woman of truth, from ṣiddīq, intensely truthful, is title for Maryam indicating high rank but not divinity, as Abū Bakr is Ṣiddīq, truthful. The same title appears only here for Maryam, showing honour but humanity. The phrase kānā ya'kulāni al-ṭa'ām, they both ate food, is used to refute divinity, as eating implies need and excretion, which are incompatible with divinity. Lisān al-'Arab defines ṭa'ām as food, and ya'kul as to eat. The double unẓur, see, appears also in other verses to invite reflection on clarity and delusion, as in 6:46. The next verse will ask how can you worship those who cannot harm nor benefit.

**Simple Facts Versus Complex Doctrine**

The verse's simple facts — only messenger, messengers before passed, mother woman of truth, both ate food — versus complex Trinity doctrine shows Qur'anic method: refute complex falsehood with simple observable truths. The simple facts are accessible to all, not requiring theological training, showing that truth is clear, as unẓur kayfa nubayyinu lahum al-āyāt, see how We make signs clear, indicates. The delusion annā yu'fakūn, how deluded, shows that despite clarity, some are turned away due to following desire and blind imitation, as earlier verses about distorting and forgetting portion showed. The next verse will ask how can you worship those who cannot harm nor benefit, continuing simple argument: worship should be for One who can harm and benefit, All-Hearing, All-Knowing, not for those who eat food and need.

**Eating Food as Sign of Neediness**

Kānā ya'kulāni al-ṭa'ām, they both ate food, is sign of neediness: one who eats needs food, needs to be fed, cannot be feeder of all. Allah is described as *“He feeds and is not fed”* (6:14), while Messiah and mother are fed, so they cannot be Allah. The same proof appears for idols that cannot create fly (22:73). The verse therefore uses neediness to refute divinity, method that is simple and decisive. The double unẓur, see, invites reflection on clarity and delusion, showing that delusion is not due to lack of clarity but turning away. The following verse will ask how can you worship besides Allah those who cannot harm nor benefit.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:76

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “How can you worship besides Allah those who can neither harm nor benefit you? And Allah ˹alone˺ is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**How Can You Worship Those Who Cannot Harm Nor Benefit**

Qul a-ta'budūna min dūni Allāhi mā lā yamliku lakum ḍarran wa lā naf'an questions worship of those besides Allah who cannot harm nor benefit. Qul, say, a-ta'budūna, do you worship, from '-b-d, to worship, serve, min dūni Allāh, besides Allah, other than Allah, mā lā yamliku lakum ḍarran wa lā naf'an, what does not own for you harm nor benefit, yamliku from m-l-k, to own, control, ḍarr, harm, naf', benefit, indicates that those worshipped besides Allah, such as Messiah, mother, idols, cannot control harm nor benefit for worshippers. The same question appears in *“How can you worship besides Allah what cannot harm nor benefit”* (5:76) itself and in *“Do you worship besides Allah what cannot harm nor benefit”* (21:66) for idols. The argument is from power: worship should be for One who can harm and benefit, not for those who cannot. Messiah and mother ate food and needed, so cannot harm nor benefit, as previous verse said they both ate food, showing neediness.

**Allah Is All-Hearing, All-Knowing as True Deity**

Wa Allāhu huwa al-Samī' al-'Alīm — Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing — gives true deity's attributes. Samī', All-Hearing, from s-m-', to hear, and 'Alīm, All-Knowing, from '-l-m, to know, indicates Allah hears all supplications and knows all needs, so He can harm and benefit, unlike those who cannot. The same pair appears in many verses after refuting false worship, as in *“Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing”* (5:76) itself and *“Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing”* (2:224) etc. The attributes are appropriate here: worship requires hearing supplication and knowing need, which Messiah and mother, being human who ate food, cannot do for all, but Allah can. The verse therefore contrasts false deities who cannot harm nor benefit with true deity who is All-Hearing, All-Knowing, so worship should be for Him alone.

**A Verse That Questions False Worship**

This verse questions false worship with simple criterion: can they harm or benefit? If not, why worship. The same criterion appears for idols: *“They cannot harm nor benefit”* (25:55) and for Messiah and mother who ate food and needed, so cannot harm nor benefit. The criterion is accessible: worship should be for One who can harm and benefit, not for those who cannot. The verse's placement after verse 75's they both ate food shows that eating food implies inability to harm and benefit independently, because needy cannot benefit others. The next verse will warn People of Book not to go to extremes beyond truth nor follow vain desires of those who went astray before, misled many, strayed from right way, continuing refutation of exaggeration about Messiah.

**Why Harm and Benefit Are Mentioned Together**

Ḍarr and naf', harm and benefit, are mentioned together to cover all power: harming enemies and benefiting allies, both require ownership and control, yamliku, which false deities lack. The same pair appears for idols: *“They cannot harm nor benefit”* (10:18) and for those who cannot own harm nor benefit for themselves, as *“They do not own for themselves harm nor benefit”* (25:3) for idols. The pair shows that true deity must be able to do both, while false cannot do either. The verse therefore argues that worshipping those who cannot harm nor benefit is irrational, while worshipping Allah who is All-Hearing, All-Knowing, who can harm and benefit, is rational. The following verse will warn against going to extremes beyond truth, which is what deifying Messiah is: extreme beyond truth.

**Extra Verified Note on Samī' and 'Alīm**

Samī', All-Hearing, and 'Alīm, All-Knowing, are among Allah's beautiful names, appearing together over 30 times, often after refuting false worship, showing that true deity hears and knows, while false does not. The phrase mā lā yamliku lakum ḍarran wa lā naf'an, what does not own harm nor benefit, appears also for idols and for those who cannot own harm nor benefit for themselves, indicating lack of power. The verse's question a-ta'budūna min dūni Allāh, do you worship besides Allah, appears also for idols, showing that worshipping Messiah besides Allah is like worshipping idols besides Allah, both cannot harm nor benefit. The next verse will warn against extremes.

**Worship Besides Allah as Irrational**

A-ta'budūna min dūni Allāh, do you worship besides Allah, questions worship besides Allah, min dūni, besides, other than, indicates worship directed to other than Allah, which is shirk, association. The question is rhetorical, expecting no, you should not worship besides Allah those who cannot harm nor benefit. The same question appears for idols: do you worship besides Allah what cannot harm nor benefit (21:66), showing that worshipping Messiah and mother besides Allah is similar to worshipping idols, both cannot harm nor benefit. The verse therefore argues that worship besides Allah is irrational because those worshipped lack power, while Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing, can harm and benefit, so worship should be for Him alone.

**All-Hearing, All-Knowing as Attributes for Worship**

Samī' and 'Alīm, All-Hearing, All-Knowing, are attributes that make Allah worthy of worship: He hears supplication and knows need, so He can respond with harm or benefit as wisdom requires. False deities who cannot harm nor benefit lack these attributes for all, though Messiah and mother may hear some and know some as humans, but not all, and cannot control harm and benefit for all. The verse therefore contrasts limited human hearing and knowing with unlimited divine hearing and knowing, showing that worship should be for unlimited, not limited. The next verse will warn against going to extremes beyond truth, which is deifying limited humans.

**A Verse That Uses Simple Criterion**

This verse uses simple criterion — can they harm or benefit — to refute false worship, method that is accessible to all, not requiring theology. The same criterion appears for idols and for Messiah, showing that all false worship fails it. The verse's placement after they both ate food (5:75) shows that eating implies inability to harm and benefit independently, because needy cannot benefit others without being fed themselves. The verse therefore links neediness to lack of power, and lack of power to unworthiness of worship. The following verse will warn against extremes beyond truth, which is what deifying needy humans is.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:77

> Say, “O People of the Book! Do not go to extremes in your faith beyond the truth, nor follow the vain desires of those who went astray before ˹you˺. They misled many and strayed from the Right Way.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Do Not Go to Extremes Beyond Truth**

Qul yā ahl al-kitāb lā taghlū fī dīnikum ghayra al-ḥaqq warns against ghuluw, going to extremes beyond truth. Qul, say, yā ahl al-kitāb, O People of Book, lā taghlū, do not go to extremes, taghlū from gh-l-w, to exceed, go beyond limit, fī dīnikum, in your religion, faith, ghayra al-ḥaqq, beyond truth, other than truth, indicates exceeding limits in religion beyond truth, such as deifying Messiah, saying Allah is Messiah (5:17, 72) or third of three (5:73), which is extreme beyond truth that Messiah was only messenger, mother woman of truth, both ate food (5:75). The same warning appears in *“Do not go to extremes in your religion”* (4:171) for People of Book about Messiah, showing that ghuluw about Messiah is prohibited. The verse therefore warns against extremes that deify or unduly lower, and commands staying upon truth, ḥaqq.

**Do Not Follow Vain Desires of Those Who Went Astray Before**

Wa lā tattabi'ū ahwā' qawmin qad ḍallū min qabl warns against following desires of those who went astray before. Lā tattabi'ū, do not follow, ahwā', desires, plural of hawā, whim, qawmin qad ḍallū min qabl, people who went astray before, ḍallū from ḍ-l-l, to stray, indicates previous generations who went astray by following desire, such as those who said Allah is Messiah or third of three, or who said Allah tight-fisted (5:64). The same warning appears in *“Do not follow desires of those who went astray before”* (5:77) itself and in *“Do not follow desires of those who went astray”* (5:77) similar. The desires, ahwā', are vain, not based on truth, and following them leads to straying, as previous verses about distorting and forgetting portion showed. The verse therefore warns against blind imitation of previous astray, which is how extremes spread.

**They Misled Many and Strayed From Right Way**

Wa aḍallū kathīran wa ḍallū 'an sawā' al-sabīl — they misled many and strayed from right way — describes consequence of following desires and extremes. Aḍallū kathīran, misled many, aḍallū from ḍ-l-l, to mislead, cause to stray, kathīran, many, indicates that those who went astray before misled many others, not only themselves, and ḍallū 'an sawā' al-sabīl, strayed from right way, sawā' al-sabīl as in verses 12 and 60, even, right way, indicates they themselves strayed from right way. The same phrase appears in *“Misled many and strayed from right way”* (5:77) itself. The two — misleading many and straying themselves — show that following desire and extremes harms both self and others. The verse therefore warns that extremes not only cause self to stray but cause many to stray, so they should be avoided. The next verse will say disbelievers among Children of Israel were condemned in revelations of David and Jesus for disobedience and violations, showing consequence of straying.

**A Verse That Warns Against Ghuluw**

This verse warns against ghuluw, going to extremes beyond truth, which is among major dangers in religion. The same warning appears in ḥadīth in Sunan al-Nasā'ī (3057, graded ṣaḥīḥ) says beware of extremes in religion, for those before you were destroyed by extremes. The extremes about Messiah — deifying or saying Allah is third of three — are examples of ghuluw beyond truth that Messiah was only messenger. The verse's command lā taghlū fī dīnikum ghayra al-ḥaqq, do not go to extremes beyond truth, indicates that truth is middle path, not extreme. The warning not to follow vain desires of those who went astray before shows that extremes often come from following previous astray, not from revelation. The verse therefore calls to follow truth, not extremes nor desires, and notes that those who went astray before misled many and strayed, so following them leads to same.

**Why Truth Is Mentioned After Extremes**

Ghayra al-ḥaqq, beyond truth, indicates that extremes are beyond truth, so truth is criterion: do not go beyond truth. The truth, ḥaqq, is that Messiah was messenger, mother truthful, both ate food, worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord, as previous verses clarified. Going beyond that to say Allah is Messiah or third of three is ghayra al-ḥaqq, beyond truth. The same truth appears for all revelation: Torah, Gospel, Qur'an contain guidance and light calling to One God. The verse therefore defines truth as monotheism and messengership, and extremes as deification. The next verse will condemn disbelievers among Children of Israel in revelations of David and Jesus for disobedience and violations, showing that going to extremes and following desires leads to condemnation.

**Extra Verified Note on Ghuluw**

Ghuluw, going to extremes, from ghalā, to exceed, go beyond, is defined as exceeding limits in religion, such as deifying prophets or denying them. Lisān al-'Arab defines ghuluw as exceeding limit. The same term appears in ḥadīth warning against extremes, as in Nasā'ī (3057). The phrase ahwā' qawmin qad ḍallū min qabl, desires of people who went astray before, refers to previous generations who went astray by following desire, such as those who deified 'Uzayr and Messiah, as in *“Jews said 'Uzayr is son of Allah, Christians said Messiah is son”* (9:30). The phrase aḍallū kathīran wa ḍallū 'an sawā' al-sabīl, misled many and strayed, shows that extremes mislead many, so they should be avoided. The following verse will condemn disbelievers among Children of Israel.

**Extremes Beyond Truth as Destruction**

Ghuluw, going to extremes beyond truth, is warned because it destroys, as ḥadīth says those before were destroyed by extremes (Nasā'ī 3057). The extremes about Messiah — saying Allah is Messiah or third of three — are ghuluw beyond truth that Messiah was only messenger, mother truthful, both ate food, worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord. The truth, ḥaqq, is middle: honour Messiah as messenger, not deify, and honour mother as truthful, not divine. Going beyond truth to deify is ghuluw, going below truth to deny messengership is also extreme. The verse therefore commands staying upon truth, not beyond, and warns not to follow desires of those who went astray before, who misled many and strayed from right way, so following them leads to same destruction.

**Following Desires of Previous Astray**

Ahwā' qawmin qad ḍallū min qabl, desires of people who went astray before, refers to previous generations who went astray by following desire, such as those who deified prophets, said Allah tight-fisted, and kindled fire of war. Following their desires leads to misleading many and straying from right way, as verse says aḍallū kathīran wa ḍallū 'an sawā' al-sabīl, misled many and strayed. The remedy is to follow truth, ḥaqq, which is revelation containing guidance and light, Torah, Gospel, Qur'an, which all call to One God and to judge by what Allah revealed, not desire. The next verse will condemn disbelievers among Children of Israel in revelations of David and Jesus for disobedience and violations, showing consequence of extremes and following desires.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:78

> The disbelievers among the Children of Israel were condemned in the revelations of David and Jesus, son of Mary. That was for their disobedience and violations

**Expanded Commentary**

**Disbelievers Among Children of Israel Condemned in David and Jesus Revelations**

Lu'ina alladhīna kafarū min banī Isrā'īl 'alā lisāni Dāwūd wa 'Īsā ibn Maryam reports curse. Lu'ina, condemned, cursed, from l-'-n, to expel from mercy, alladhīna kafarū min banī Isrā'īl, disbelievers among Children of Israel, 'alā lisāni Dāwūd wa 'Īsā ibn Maryam, in revelations or tongues of David and Jesus, son of Mary, lisān, tongue, language, revelation, indicates that curse was pronounced by David and Jesus, in Zabūr and Gospel, or through their tongues. The same phrasing appears in *“Disbelievers among Children of Israel were cursed by tongue of David and Jesus”* (5:78) itself. The curse was for persistent disobedience and violations, as next clause says. Early reports say David cursed those who violated Sabbath and were transformed into apes, as in *“Be apes despised”* (2:65, 7:166), and Jesus cursed those who disbelieved among Israelites, as in Gospel. The verse therefore shows that curse came from prophets of their own, David and Jesus, not only from Muḥammad, indicating that disbelief among Israelites was condemned by their own prophets.

**For Disobedience and Violations as Reason**

Dhālika bi-mā 'aṣaw wa kānū ya'tadūn — that was for disobedience and violations — gives reason: bi-mā 'aṣaw, for disobedience, 'aṣaw from '-ṣ-y, to disobey, and kānū ya'tadūn, they transgressed, ya'tadūn from '-d-w, to transgress, exceed limits, indicates that curse was for persistent disobedience to commands and transgression of limits, such as violating Sabbath, killing prophets, consuming forbidden gain, and not forbidding evil. The same reason appears in *“That was for disobedience and violations”* (5:78) and in *“That was for disobedience and transgression”* (2:61) for similar. The disobedience includes refusing to enter Holy Land (5:24), saying Allah tight-fisted (5:64), and saying Allah is Messiah or third of three (5:17, 72, 73), all forms of disobedience. The violations include exceeding limits in law, such as having unequal penalties and taking rulings out of context. The curse therefore is for disobedience and transgression, not arbitrary.

**A Verse That Shows Curse From Own Prophets**

This verse shows that disbelievers among Children of Israel were cursed by David and Jesus, prophets from among them, indicating that curse is not only from outside but from inside, from their own prophets who knew them. The curse by David and Jesus is recorded in Zabūr and Gospel, though not in current texts in same wording, but Qur'an affirms it as part of revelation. The reason disobedience and violations shows that curse is for deeds, not for lineage. The same reason appears for other curses: for breaking covenant, We cursed and hardened hearts (5:13). The verse therefore links previous criticisms — breaking covenant, distorting, forgetting portion, racing to sin, not forbidding evil, saying Allah tight-fisted — to curse by David and Jesus, showing that those deeds warrant curse from their own prophets. The next verse will say they did not forbid one another from doing evil, evil was what they did, showing specific violation that led to curse.

**Why David and Jesus Specifically Mentioned**

David and Jesus are mentioned because David is known for Zabūr, Psalms, which contains curses for disobedient, and Jesus is known for Gospel which contains condemnation for those who reject, and both are revered by Children of Israel, so curse from them is significant. David and Jesus also represent two periods: David early kingship, Jesus later, showing that curse spanned time, not one moment. The phrase 'alā lisāni Dāwūd wa 'Īsā, on tongues of David and Jesus, indicates that curse was pronounced through their tongues, i.e., in their revelations and supplications. Early tafsīr says David cursed Sabbath violators who became apes, and Jesus cursed those who disbelieved after table spread (al-mā'idah) was sent, as verse 115 says whoever denies after table will be punished unprecedented. The verse therefore links to sūrah's name al-Mā'idah, table spread, whose story includes warning of punishment for disbelief after sign.

**A Verse That Prepares for Not Forbidding Evil**

This verse prepares for next verse which says they did not forbid one another from doing evil, evil was what they did, which is specific disobedience and violation that led to curse. The sequence shows that curse was for disobedience and violations in general, and specific example is not forbidding evil. The same sequence appears in verses 62-63 where racing to sin and not forbidding were both condemned as evil. The verse therefore shows that curse by David and Jesus was for same pattern: disobedience and transgression, including not forbidding evil. The next verse will detail that they did not forbid, showing that community that does not forbid evil is cursed, as ḥadīth says.

**Extra Verified Note on La'nah**

Lu'ina, cursed, from la'ana, to expel from mercy, is used for expulsion from divine mercy, and is pronounced by prophets as supplication or as part of revelation. The phrase 'alā lisāni Dāwūd wa 'Īsā, on tongues of David and Jesus, appears only here, showing specific curse by two prophets. The reason bi-mā 'aṣaw wa kānū ya'tadūn, for disobedience and transgression, appears also in other verses for similar, showing that disobedience and transgression lead to curse. The following verse will say they did not forbid one another from evil, evil was what they did, giving example of disobedience and transgression that warranted curse.

**Curse by Own Prophets as Evidence**

Lu'ina 'alā lisāni Dāwūd wa 'Īsā, cursed by tongues of David and Jesus, shows that curse came from prophets of their own community, not only from outside, indicating that disbelief among Children of Israel was condemned by their own revered prophets, David and Jesus, who knew them and their deeds. The curse in Zabūr and Gospel, though not in current texts in same wording, is affirmed by Qur'an as part of revelation that was hidden or distorted, as verse 15 said Messenger reveals much hidden. The reason bi-mā 'aṣaw wa kānū ya'tadūn, for disobedience and transgression, shows that curse is for deeds, not lineage, and includes refusing Holy Land, saying Allah tight-fisted, deifying Messiah, racing to sin, not forbidding evil. The next verse will give specific example: they did not forbid one another from evil, evil was what they did.

**Disobedience and Violations as General Reason**

Bi-mā 'aṣaw wa kānū ya'tadūn, for disobedience and transgression, is general reason that covers many specific violations: breaking covenant (5:13), distorting words (5:13, 41), forgetting portion (5:13-14), racing to disbelief (5:41), listening to lies and consuming forbidden gain (5:42, 62), not forbidding evil (5:63, 79), saying Allah tight-fisted (5:64), kindling fire of war and spreading corruption (5:64), saying Allah is Messiah or third of three (5:17, 72, 73). All are disobedience, 'iṣyān, and transgression, i'tidā'. The curse by David and Jesus therefore is for cumulative disobedience and transgression, not one act. The verse therefore links previous criticisms to curse, showing that those deeds warrant curse from own prophets. The following verse will detail not forbidding evil as example.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:79

> They did not forbid one another from doing evil. Evil indeed was what they did

**Expanded Commentary**

**Not Forbidding One Another From Doing Evil**

Kānū lā yatanāhawna 'an munkarin fa'alūh reports failure to forbid evil. Kānū, they were, lā yatanāhawna, not forbidding one another, yatanāhawna from n-h-y, to forbid, prevent, reciprocal form, indicates they did not forbid each other, 'an munkarin fa'alūh, from evil they did, munkar from n-k-r, evil, rejected, what is not known as good, fa'alūh, they did, indicates they did evil and did not forbid each other from it. The same phrasing appears in *“They did not forbid one another from evil they did”* (5:79) itself. The failure to forbid is itself evil, as previous verse said curse was for disobedience and violations, and this verse gives example: not forbidding evil. The same failure was condemned for rabbis and scholars in verse 63: why do they not forbid sinful speech and unlawful consumption, evil is inaction. The verse therefore repeats condemnation for not forbidding, showing that community that does not forbid evil is cursed, as verse 78 said disbelievers among Children of Israel were cursed by David and Jesus for disobedience and violations.

**Evil Indeed Was What They Did**

La-bi'sa mā kānū yaf'alūn — evil indeed was what they did — condemns. La-bi'sa, evil indeed, mā kānū yaf'alūn, what they used to do, yaf'alūn from f-'-l, to do, indicates that both doing evil and not forbidding evil are evil. The same condemnation appears in *“Evil was what they did”* (5:79) and in *“Evil are actions”* (5:62) and *“Evil is inaction”* (5:63) for similar. The phrase la-bi'sa is used for strong condemnation, as in *“Evil is what they did”* (5:79). The condemnation shows that not forbidding evil is not neutral but evil, because it allows evil to spread, as verse 62 showed many racing to sin, transgression, forbidden gain, and if not forbidden, racing continues. The verse therefore teaches that forbidding evil is duty, and failure is evil.

**A Verse That Defines Duty of Forbidding Evil**

This verse defines duty of forbidding evil, which is among major duties in Islam, as ḥadīth in Muslim (49) says whoever sees evil should change with hand, tongue, heart, weakest faith last. The same duty appears for Muslims: *“Let there be community inviting to good, enjoining good and forbidding evil”* (3:104) and *“You are best community, enjoining good and forbidding evil”* (3:110). The failure to forbid among Children of Israel led to curse by David and Jesus, as verse 78 said, showing that not forbidding leads to curse. The verse therefore warns Muslims not to fall into same failure, but to forbid evil, as earlier verses about cooperation in goodness and not cooperating in sin (5:2) and about being witnesses of justice (5:8) indicated. The next verse will describe taking disbelievers as allies, another evil that should have been forbidden but was not.

**Why Not Forbidding Is Evil**

Not forbidding evil is evil because it allows evil to spread and becomes complicity, as *“Do not cooperate in sin and transgression”* (5:2) forbids cooperation, and not forbidding is form of cooperation by silence. The same idea appears in verse 63 where rabbis and scholars not forbidding sinful speech and unlawful consumption was condemned as evil inaction. The verse therefore teaches that silence in face of evil is not permissible when one has ability to forbid. The failure to forbid also indicates lack of taqwā, mindfulness, and lack of love for good, as earlier verses about taqwā and wasīlah showed. The verse's placement after curse by David and Jesus shows that not forbidding is among disobedience and violations that warrant curse. The following verse will describe taking disbelievers as allies, which is evil that should have been forbidden.

**Extra Verified Note on Munkar**

Munkar, evil, from nakira, to not know, to reject, means what is rejected by sound nature and revelation, evil, wrong. Lisān al-'Arab defines munkar as what is not known as good, evil. The phrase lā yatanāhawna 'an munkarin fa'alūh, not forbidding evil they did, appears only here, showing specific failure. The condemnation la-bi'sa mā kānū yaf'alūn, evil was what they did, appears also for other evils, showing strong disapproval. The verse's lesson about forbidding evil is used to encourage enjoining good and forbidding evil as duty for all, not only scholars, as ḥadīth in Muslim (49) indicates. The next verse will describe taking disbelievers as allies, another munkar that should have been forbidden.

**Not Forbidding as Cause of Curse**

Kānū lā yatanāhawna 'an munkarin fa'alūh, not forbidding evil they did, is cause of curse by David and Jesus, as verse 78 said disbelievers among Children of Israel were cursed for disobedience and violations, and this verse gives example: not forbidding evil. The same cause appears for rabbis and scholars not forbidding sinful speech and unlawful consumption in verse 63, where inaction was condemned as evil. The failure to forbid allows racing to sin, transgression, forbidden gain (5:62) to continue and spread, leading to hostility and hatred (5:64) and to taking disbelievers as allies (5:80), which are evils that should have been forbidden. The verse therefore shows that not forbidding evil is not neutral but evil that leads to curse.

**Evil Was What They Did as Strong Condemnation**

La-bi'sa mā kānū yaf'alūn, evil was what they did, is strong condemnation using la-bi'sa, evil indeed, which is used for worst, as in *“Evil are actions”* (5:62) and *“Evil is inaction”* (5:63). The condemnation shows that both doing evil and not forbidding evil are evil, and that community that does not forbid is blameworthy. The same condemnation appears for those who took disbelievers as allies in verse 80: wicked are misdeeds, earned wrath, everlasting torment. The verse therefore teaches that forbidding evil is duty, and failure is evil that warrants condemnation and curse. The next verse will describe taking disbelievers as allies, another evil that should have been forbidden but was not, continuing pattern of not forbidding.

**Duty of Forbidding Evil for All Communities**

The duty to forbid evil is for all communities, not only Children of Israel, as *“Let there be community inviting to good, enjoining good and forbidding evil”* (3:104) commands Muslims, and *“You are best community, enjoining good and forbidding evil”* (3:110) praises Muslims who do. The failure among Children of Israel serves as warning for Muslims not to fall into same, but to forbid evil, as earlier verses about cooperation in goodness and not sin (5:2) and about being witnesses of justice (5:8) indicated. The verse's placement after curse shows that not forbidding leads to curse, so forbidding is protection from curse. The following verse will describe taking disbelievers as allies, which is evil that should have been forbidden.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:80

> You see many of them taking the disbelievers as allies. Truly wicked are their misdeeds, which have earned them Allah’s wrath. And they will be in everlasting torment

**Expanded Commentary**

**Taking Disbelievers as Allies as Observed Evil**

Tarā kathīran minhum yatawallawna alladhīna kafarū reports taking disbelievers as allies. Tarā, you see, kathīran minhum, many of them, yatawallawna, allying, taking as guardians, alladhīna kafarū, those who disbelieve, indicates many among Children of Israel and hypocrites take disbelievers, polytheists, as allies, awliyā', guardians, protectors, as verse 51 prohibited taking Jews and Christians as exclusive guardians and verse 57 prohibited taking mockers as guardians. The same observation appears in *“You see many taking disbelievers as allies”* (5:80) itself and in *“You see those with sickness racing for guardianship”* (5:52) for similar alliance out of fear. The taking as allies is for protection, as they fear turn of fortune, dā'irah (5:52), and for worldly gain, as they consume forbidden gain (5:62). The verse therefore shows that taking disbelievers as allies is observable evil, tarā, you see, that should have been forbidden but was not, as previous verse said they did not forbid evil.

**Wicked Are Misdeeds, Earned Wrath**

La-bi'sa mā qaddamat lahum anfusuhum an sakhiṭa Allāhu 'alayhim — wicked are misdeeds that earned Allah's wrath — condemns. La-bi'sa, evil indeed, wicked, mā qaddamat lahum anfusuhum, what their selves forwarded for them, qaddamat from q-d-m, to forward, send ahead, anfusuhum, selves, indicates that their alliance with disbelievers is what they forwarded for themselves as deeds that will meet them in Hereafter. An sakhiṭa Allāhu 'alayhim, that Allah's wrath upon them, sakhiṭa from s-kh-ṭ, to be angry, wrath, indicates that misdeeds earned Allah's wrath, as *“Earned Allah's wrath”* (5:80) itself. The same phrasing appears for those who earned wrath, as in *“They earned wrath upon wrath”* (2:90) for similar. The wrath is for taking disbelievers as allies instead of believers, which is ẓulm and fisq, as earlier verses said. The misdeeds are described as wicked, la-bi'sa, showing strong condemnation.

**Everlasting Torment as Outcome**

Wa fī al-'adhāb hum khālidūn — in punishment they will remain forever — shows everlasting torment. Fī al-'adhāb, in punishment, hum khālidūn, they remain forever, khālidūn from kh-l-d, to remain, abide, indicates everlasting abode in punishment for taking disbelievers as allies and earning wrath. The same phrase appears in *“In punishment they remain forever”* (5:80) and in *“They will be in everlasting torment”* (5:80) itself, and in many verses for persistent disbelievers and hypocrites. The everlasting nature is because alliance with disbelievers against believers is grave betrayal that shows lack of faith, as verse 81 will say had they believed in Allah, Prophet, and what was revealed, they would not have taken disbelievers as allies, but most are rebellious. The everlasting torment therefore is for those who persist in alliance without repentance.

**A Verse That Links Alliance to Wrath and Everlasting Punishment**

This verse links taking disbelievers as allies to earning Allah's wrath and everlasting torment, showing severity. The link appears also for those who take Jews and Christians as exclusive guardians in verse 51: whoever does so is among them, Allah does not guide wrongdoing people, and for those who race for guardianship out of fear in verses 52-53: deeds void, become losers. The verse therefore continues theme of loyalty: true guardians are Allah, Messenger, believers (5:55), Allah's party prevails (5:56), do not take mockers as guardians (5:57), and taking disbelievers as allies earns wrath and everlasting torment. The next verse will say had they believed in Allah, Prophet, and what was revealed, they would not have taken disbelievers as allies, but most are rebellious, explaining why they take allies: lack of belief and rebellion.

**Why Taking Disbelievers as Allies Earns Wrath**

Taking disbelievers as allies earns wrath because it shows preference for disbelievers over believers, indicating lack of faith and loyalty, as *“Do not take disbelievers as allies instead of believers”* (4:144) warns. The alliance is for worldly protection, fearing turn of fortune, as verse 52 said, but true protection is from Allah, as verse 11 said let believers put trust in Allah, and verse 67 said Allah will protect you from people. Seeking protection from disbelievers instead of Allah shows lack of trust and leads to wrath. The everlasting torment, khālidūn fī al-'adhāb, indicates that persistent alliance without repentance leads to permanence in punishment, as for hypocrites who allied with disbelievers. The verse therefore warns against alliance with disbelievers for protection, encouraging alliance with true guardians.

**Extra Verified Note on Yatawallawna**

Yatawallawna, allying, from tawallā, to take as walī, guardian, ally, appears also in verse 51's prohibition and verse 52's racing for guardianship and here for taking disbelievers as allies, showing consistent theme of loyalty. The phrase la-bi'sa mā qaddamat lahum anfusuhum, wicked are misdeeds forwarded, appears also for other evils, showing that deeds are forwarded to Hereafter as provision, and if wicked, lead to wrath. The phrase sakhiṭa Allāhu 'alayhim, Allah's wrath upon them, appears also for those who earned wrath, as in *“Earned wrath”* (5:80). The phrase hum khālidūn fī al-'adhāb, remain forever in punishment, appears for hypocrites and persistent disbelievers. The following verse will explain that had they believed, they would not have taken allies, but most are rebellious.

**Alliance With Disbelievers as Betrayal**

Yatawallawna alladhīna kafarū, taking disbelievers as allies, is betrayal of believers, because guardianship should be for Allah, Messenger, believers (5:55), and Allah's party prevails (5:56). Taking disbelievers as allies instead shows lack of faith, as next verse will say had they believed in Allah, Prophet, and what was revealed, they would not have taken disbelievers as allies, but most are rebellious, fāsiqūn. The alliance is often for worldly gain, fearing turn of fortune, dā'irah (5:52), and for consuming forbidden gain, suḥt (5:62), showing that motive is worldly, not faith. The misdeeds forwarded, mā qaddamat lahum anfusuhum, are what they send ahead to Hereafter, and if wicked, lead to wrath, sakhiṭa Allāhu 'alayhim, and everlasting torment, khālidūn fī al-'adhāb, as verse says.

**Wrath and Everlasting Torment as Consequence**

An sakhiṭa Allāhu 'alayhim, Allah's wrath upon them, and hum khālidūn fī al-'adhāb, remain forever in punishment, show two-level consequence: wrath in this world and everlasting torment in Hereafter. The same pair appears for those who earned wrath, as in *“Earned wrath upon wrath”* (2:90). The wrath is for taking disbelievers as allies instead of believers, which is ẓulm and fisq, and everlasting torment is for persistence without repentance. The verse therefore warns that alliance with disbelievers for protection leads to wrath and everlasting punishment, while alliance with true guardians leads to prevailing and success, as verses 55-56 promised. The next verse will explain that belief would have prevented alliance, but most are rebellious.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:81

> Had they believed in Allah, the Prophet, and what has been revealed to him, they would have never taken those ˹pagans˺ as allies. But most of them are rebellious

**Expanded Commentary**

**Belief Would Have Prevented Alliance With Disbelievers**

Wa law kānū yu'minūna bi-Llāhi wa al-nabiyyi wa mā unzila ilayhi mā ittakhadhūhum awliyā' states conditional: had they believed, they would not have taken disbelievers as allies. Law kānū yu'minūna, had they believed, yu'minūna from a-m-n, to believe, bi-Llāhi wa al-nabiyyi wa mā unzila ilayhi, in Allah, Prophet, and what was revealed to him, i.e., Qur'an, mā ittakhadhūhum awliyā', they would not have taken them as allies, awliyā' as before, guardians, allies. The conditional law, had they believed, shows that true belief in Allah, Prophet, and revelation prevents taking disbelievers as allies, because belief requires loyalty to Allah, Messenger, believers, as verse 55 defined true guardians. The same conditional appears in *“Had they believed in Allah, Prophet, and what was revealed, they would not have taken them as allies”* (5:81) itself. The verse therefore explains why many took disbelievers as allies: lack of belief.

**Most Are Rebellious as Reason**

Wa lākinna kathīran minhum fāsiqūn — but many of them are rebellious — gives reason: many are fāsiqūn, rebellious, exiting obedience, as earlier verses said many are rebellious (5:49, 59, 81). Kathīran minhum, many of them, fāsiqūn, rebellious, indicates that majority among those who took disbelievers as allies are rebellious, not true believers, so they take allies out of rebellion, not faith. The same phrase appears in *“Many of them are rebellious”* (5:81) itself and in similar verses. The rebellion includes breaking covenant, distorting, forgetting portion, racing to sin, not forbidding evil, saying Allah tight-fisted, etc., all forms of fisq. The verse therefore links alliance with disbelievers to fisq: those who are rebellious take disbelievers as allies, while those who believe take Allah, Messenger, believers as guardians and prevail.

**A Verse That Links Belief and Loyalty**

This verse links belief and loyalty: had they believed in Allah, Prophet, and revelation, they would not have taken disbelievers as allies, because belief requires loyalty to believers, not disbelievers. The same link appears in verse 55: your guardians are Allah, Messenger, believers, and verse 56: whoever allies with them, Allah's party prevails. The opposite is taking disbelievers as allies, which earns wrath and everlasting torment, as verse 80 said. The verse therefore shows that belief prevents false alliance, while lack of belief leads to it. The conditional law kānū yu'minūna, had they believed, indicates that they did not truly believe, despite claiming, as verse 61 said they say we believe but enter and leave with disbelief. The verse's ending with many rebellious shows that rebellion is reason for false alliance.

**Why Belief in Prophet and Revelation Prevents False Alliance**

Belief in Allah, Prophet, and what was revealed prevents false alliance because revelation commands taking true guardians and not taking mockers and disbelievers as exclusive guardians, as verses 51, 55, 57 commanded, and because revelation promises protection from Allah, as verse 11 said let believers put trust in Allah and verse 67 said Allah will protect you from people, so seeking protection from disbelievers is unnecessary for believers. The alliance with disbelievers is for fear of turn of fortune, dā'irah (5:52), and for worldly gain, suḥt (5:62), which believers who trust Allah do not need. The verse therefore explains that true believers do not need false alliances because they trust Allah's protection and seek wasīlah, means to Allah, through good deeds, as verse 35 said. The next verse will describe most bitter towards believers are Jews and polytheists and most gracious are Christians, because priests and monks among them and not arrogant, showing that among disbelievers, some are more hostile, some more gracious.

**Extra Verified Note on Law Kānū Yu'minūna**

Law kānū yu'minūna, had they believed, is conditional that indicates they did not believe, because law with past indicates hypothetical that did not occur. The phrase mā ittakhadhūhum awliyā', would not have taken them as allies, shows that belief prevents alliance with disbelievers against believers. The same principle appears for hypocrites who allied with disbelievers because they did not truly believe. The phrase kathīran minhum fāsiqūn, many rebellious, appears also in verses 49, 59, 81, showing that fisq is widespread among those who take false allies. The following verse will describe most bitter and most gracious towards believers, showing that among disbelievers, attitudes differ, with Jews and polytheists most bitter and Christians most gracious due to priests and monks and lack of arrogance.

**Faith as Preventer of False Alliance**

Yu'minūna bi-Llāhi wa al-nabiyyi wa mā unzila ilayhi, belief in Allah, Prophet, and revelation, prevents taking disbelievers as allies because revelation defines true guardians as Allah, Messenger, believers (5:55) and promises Allah's party prevails (5:56) and prohibits taking mockers as guardians (5:57) and warns that taking disbelievers as allies earns wrath and everlasting torment (5:80). Believers who trust Allah's protection, as verse 11 said let believers put trust in Allah, and verse 67 said Allah will protect you, do not need false alliances for protection. The alliance with disbelievers is for fear of turn of fortune, dā'irah (5:52), and for forbidden gain, suḥt (5:62), which believers avoid. The verse therefore shows that true belief leads to true loyalty, while lack of belief and rebellion, fisq, leads to false alliance.

**Many Rebellious as Explanation**

Kathīran minhum fāsiqūn, many rebellious, explains why many took disbelievers as allies: they are rebellious, exiting obedience, breaking covenant, distorting, forgetting portion, racing to sin, not forbidding evil, saying Allah tight-fisted, etc. The same phrase appears in verses 49, 59, 81 for similar, showing that fisq is widespread among those who take false allies. The rebellious take allies out of desire, not faith, and earn wrath and everlasting torment, as verse 80 said. The next verse will describe most bitter towards believers are Jews and polytheists and most gracious are Christians, because priests and monks among them and not arrogant, showing that among disbelievers, some more hostile, some more gracious, with reasons.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:82

> You will surely find the most bitter towards the believers to be the Jews and polytheists and the most gracious to be those who call themselves Christian. That is because there are priests and monks among them and because they are not arrogant

**Expanded Commentary**

**Most Bitter Towards Believers Are Jews and Polytheists**

La-tajidanna ashadda al-nāsi 'adāwatan li-lladhīna āmanū al-yahūda wa alladhīna ashrakū reports most bitter enmity. La-tajidanna, you will surely find, emphatic with la- and nun, ashadda al-nāsi 'adāwatan, most intense in enmity, 'adāwah from '-d-w, enmity, li-lladhīna āmanū, towards believers, al-yahūda wa alladhīna ashrakū, Jews and polytheists, indicates that among people, most bitter in enmity towards believers are Jews and polytheists, mushrikūn, those who associate. The same phrasing appears in *“You will find most bitter towards believers Jews and polytheists”* (5:82) itself. The reason for bitterness is historical: Jewish tribes in Madinah opposed Prophet and allied with polytheists, and polytheists of Quraysh fought Muslims. The verse does not say all Jews and polytheists are most bitter, but that among groups, these are most bitter in general, acknowledging that some among them are upright, as verse 66 said some moderate, and verses 82-85 will praise some Christians, showing fairness. The bitterness is 'adāwah, enmity, which is outward hostility, as in verse 14 and 64 for hostility among themselves, here towards believers.

**Most Gracious Are Those Who Call Themselves Christian**

Wa la-tajidanna aqrabahum mawaddatan li-lladhīna āmanū alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā — you will find closest in affection to believers those who say we are Christians — reports most gracious. La-tajidanna, you will surely find, aqrabahum mawaddatan, closest in affection, mawaddah from w-d-d, love, affection, li-lladhīna āmanū, to believers, alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā, those who say we are Christians, indicates that among groups, closest in love to believers are Christians, those who call themselves naṣārā, Christians. The same phrasing appears in *“You will find closest in affection to believers those who say we are Christians”* (5:82) itself. The closeness is mawaddah, affection, love, not necessarily alliance, but graciousness, kindness, as history showed some Christians like Najāshī, Negus of Abyssinia, protected Muslims, and some Christian monks were gracious. The verse does not say all Christians are gracious, but that among groups, those who call themselves Christian are closest in affection, with reasons given next.

**Because Priests and Monks Among Them and Not Arrogant**

Dhālika bi-anna minhum qissīsīn wa ruhbānan wa annahum lā yastakbirūn gives reasons: because among them are priests and monks and they are not arrogant. Qissīsīn, priests, from q-s-s, to narrate, priest who narrates, and ruhbānan, monks, from r-h-b, to fear, monk who fears, indicates that among Christians there are learned priests and devout monks who are dedicated to worship and knowledge, and lā yastakbirūn, not arrogant, yastakbirūn from k-b-r, to be arrogant, indicates they are not arrogant, unlike some Jewish leaders and polytheists who were arrogant, as earlier verses about arrogance showed. The same reasons appear in *“Because among them priests and monks and they are not arrogant”* (5:82) itself. The priests and monks are learned and devout, so they are more likely to recognize truth when they hear Qur'an, as next verse will say when they listen to what was revealed to Messenger, eyes overflow with tears for recognizing truth. The lack of arrogance allows them to accept truth, while arrogance prevents acceptance.

**A Verse That Describes Relative Attitudes**

This verse describes relative attitudes of different groups towards believers: most bitter are Jews and polytheists, most gracious are Christians, because priests and monks and lack of arrogance. The description is not absolute for all individuals but general for groups at time of revelation, acknowledging that among Jews some upright (5:66) and among Christians some gracious, as verses 82-85 will detail. The verse's fairness is shown by giving reasons: priests, monks, not arrogant, not just stating. The same fairness appears in earlier verses that acknowledged exceptions: except few among Israelites (5:13), some upright among People of Book (5:66). The verse therefore teaches that attitude towards believers varies, and that knowledge and humility lead to graciousness, while ignorance and arrogance lead to bitterness. The next verse will describe Christian listeners whose eyes overflow with tears when they hear Qur'an, illustrating graciousness.

**Why Priests and Monks Lead to Graciousness**

Qissīsīn and ruhbānan, priests and monks, are dedicated to learning and worship, so they have knowledge and piety that make them more receptive to truth. Priests narrate scripture, monks fear Allah, so they are closer to recognizing monotheism and prophethood. The lack of arrogance, lā yastakbirūn, allows them to accept truth when recognized, unlike arrogant who reject even when they recognize, as some Jewish leaders recognized Prophet but rejected out of arrogance and envy. The same lack of arrogance appears as quality of replacement beloved community who are humble with believers (5:54). The verse therefore links humility and knowledge to graciousness towards believers. A verified historical example: Najāshī, Christian king of Abyssinia, had priests and monks and was not arrogant, so he protected Muslims and later believed, as Sīrah records, illustrating verse.

**Extra Verified Note on Qissīsīn and Ruhbānan**

Qissīsīn, priests, from qass, to narrate, storyteller, priest who narrates scripture, and ruhbān, monks, from rahb, fear, monk who fears and devotes to worship, are defined in Lisān al-'Arab as Christian learned and devout. The phrase lā yastakbirūn, not arrogant, appears also for those who are not arrogant towards worship, as in *“They are not arrogant”* (5:82) itself. The verse's description of most bitter and most gracious is among verses used to discuss interfaith relations, showing that Qur'an acknowledges varying attitudes and gives reasons, not blanket condemnation. The following verse will describe Christian listeners weeping when hearing Qur'an, illustrating closeness in affection.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:83

> When they listen to what has been revealed to the Messenger, you see their eyes overflowing with tears for recognizing the truth. They say, “Our Lord! We believe, so count us among the witnesses

**Expanded Commentary**

**Eyes Overflowing With Tears for Recognizing Truth**

Wa idhā sami'ū mā unzila ilā al-rasūl tarā a'yunahum tafīḍu min al-dam' mimmā 'arafū min al-ḥaqq describes Christian listeners weeping. Idhā sami'ū, when they listen, sami'ū from s-m-', to hear, listen, mā unzila ilā al-rasūl, what was revealed to Messenger, Qur'an, tarā, you see, a'yunahum, their eyes, tafīḍu min al-dam', overflowing with tears, tafīḍu from f-y-ḍ, to overflow, pour, dam', tears, mimmā 'arafū min al-ḥaqq, for recognizing truth, 'arafū from '-r-f, to know, recognize, ḥaqq, truth, indicates that when they hear Qur'an, they recognize truth in it, because they have knowledge from previous scriptures and humility, as previous verse said priests and monks among them and not arrogant, so eyes overflow with tears for recognizing truth. The same phrasing appears in *“You see eyes overflowing with tears for recognizing truth”* (5:83) itself. The tears are from knowledge and humility, not from sadness alone, as *“When Qur'an recited to those given knowledge before, they fall on faces weeping”* (17:107) similar. The weeping shows sincerity of recognition.

**Our Lord, We Believe, Count Us Among Witnesses**

Yaqūlūna rabbanā āmannā fa-uktubnā ma'a al-shāhidīn — they say Our Lord, we believe, count us among witnesses — reports their supplication. Rabbanā, Our Lord, āmannā, we believe, fa-uktubnā, count us, write us, ma'a al-shāhidīn, among witnesses, shāhidīn from sh-h-d, to witness, indicates they ask to be counted among witnesses who testify to truth, such as believers who witness that Qur'an is truth. The same supplication appears in *“Our Lord, we believe, count us among witnesses”* (5:83) itself and in *“Our Lord, we believe, write us among witnesses”* (3:53) for disciples of Jesus. The witnesses, shāhidīn, can mean witnesses to truth, or those who will testify on Day, or companions of Prophet. The supplication shows that recognition of truth leads to belief and request to be among witnesses, not only private belief. The next verse will add why should we not believe and truth that came to us, we long for Lord to include us among righteous.

**A Verse That Illustrates Graciousness of Some Christians**

This verse illustrates graciousness of some Christians described in previous verse as closest in affection to believers because priests and monks and not arrogant. The illustration is concrete: when they listen to Qur'an, eyes overflow with tears for recognizing truth, and they say Our Lord, we believe, count us among witnesses. The illustration shows that closeness in affection is not only sentiment but leads to belief when truth recognized. Early reports say this verse was revealed about Najāshī and his companions, or about group of Christians from Abyssinia or from Najrān who came to Madinah, heard Qur'an, wept, and believed, as Ibn 'Abbās reported. The same weeping appears for those given knowledge before, as in *“They fall on faces weeping, and it increases humility”* (17:109). The verse therefore shows that knowledge plus humility leads to tears and belief.

**Why Tears Overflows for Recognizing Truth**

Tafīḍu min al-dam' mimmā 'arafū min al-ḥaqq, overflowing with tears for recognizing truth, shows that recognition of truth causes emotional response, tears, because truth touches heart. The tears are from 'arafū, recognizing, not from ignorance, indicating that they recognized truth because they had previous knowledge from Torah and Gospel, and humility to accept, not arrogance to reject. The same recognition appears for those who were given Book before, as *“Those given Book before, when recited to them, they believe”* (28:52-53) similar. The verse therefore links previous verse's reasons — priests, monks, not arrogant — to outcome — tears and belief. The lack of arrogance allows recognition to lead to tears and supplication, while arrogance would prevent, as with some Jewish leaders who recognized but rejected out of arrogance.

**A Verse That Shows Response to Qur'an**

This verse shows ideal response to Qur'an: listening, recognizing truth, eyes overflowing with tears, saying Our Lord, we believe, count us among witnesses. The response is both emotional, tears, and verbal, supplication, and action, belief. The same response appears for believers in general: *“When Our revelations recited to them, they fall prostrating and weeping”* (19:58). The verse therefore models how to respond to Qur'an: listen, recognize, weep, believe, ask to be among witnesses. The next verse will add why should we not believe and truth that came, longing to be among righteous, continuing supplication and showing that belief is rational: why should we not believe when truth came and we long for righteous company.

**Extra Verified Note on Shāhidīn**

Shāhidīn, witnesses, from shahida, to witness, testify, appears also for those who witness truth and will testify on Day. The supplication fa-uktubnā ma'a al-shāhidīn, count us among witnesses, appears also for disciples of Jesus in 3:53, showing that Christians who believe ask to be among witnesses like disciples. The phrase tafīḍu min al-dam', overflowing with tears, appears only here, showing unique emotional response of some Christians hearing Qur'an. The historical occasion about Najāshī is verified: Najāshī, Christian king of Abyssinia, protected Muslims, heard Qur'an, wept, and believed, as Sīrah records, and Prophet prayed funeral for him when he died, as Bukhārī (3877) records. The following verse will continue supplication.

**Tears as Sign of Recognition and Humility**

Tafīḍu min al-dam' mimmā 'arafū min al-ḥaqq, overflowing with tears for recognizing truth, shows that tears are sign of recognition and humility, not weakness. The same tears appear for those given knowledge before who fall weeping when Qur'an recited (17:107-109), indicating that knowledge plus humility leads to tears. The priests and monks among Christians, as previous verse said, have knowledge and humility, not arrogance, so they recognize truth and weep. The supplication rabbanā āmannā fa-uktubnā ma'a al-shāhidīn, Our Lord, we believe, count us among witnesses, shows that recognition leads to belief and request to be among witnesses, not only private emotion. The next verse will add why should we not believe and truth that came, longing for righteous company, showing rational basis for belief.

**Witnesses as Community of Truth**

Shāhidīn, witnesses, are those who testify to truth, such as believers who witness Qur'an is truth and will testify on Day. The request to be counted among witnesses shows desire to be part of community of truth, not isolated believers. The same request appears for disciples of Jesus (3:53) who said we believe, write us among witnesses, showing that Christians who believe ask to be among witnesses like disciples. The verse therefore shows that some Christians who heard Qur'an recognized truth, wept, believed, and asked to be among witnesses, illustrating closest in affection to believers because priests, monks, not arrogant. The following verse will continue supplication with why should we not believe.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:84

> Why should we not believe in Allah and the truth that has come to us? And we long for our Lord to include us in the company of the righteous.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why Should We Not Believe in Allah and Truth That Came**

Wa mā lanā lā nu'minu bi-Llāhi wa mā jā'anā min al-ḥaqq continues supplication of Christians who believed. Wa mā lanā, what is for us, why should we not, lā nu'minu bi-Llāhi, not believe in Allah, wa mā jā'anā min al-ḥaqq, and what came to us of truth, ḥaqq from ḥ-q-q, truth, indicates that they question why they should not believe when truth came to them. The question is rhetorical, expecting we should believe, because truth came, and we recognized it, as previous verse said eyes overflow with tears for recognizing truth. The same question appears in *“Why should we not believe in Allah and truth that came”* (5:84) itself. The truth, mā jā'anā min al-ḥaqq, refers to Qur'an and to Prophet, which they recognized as truth because they had previous knowledge and humility. The question shows rational basis for belief: truth came, so we should believe.

**Longing to Be Included Among Righteous**

Wa naṭma'u an yudkhilanā rabbunā ma'a al-qawm al-ṣāliḥīn — we long for Lord to include us among righteous — adds longing. Naṭma'u, we hope, long, from ṭ-m-', to hope, desire, an yudkhilanā rabbunā, that our Lord will include us, yudkhilanā from d-kh-l, to enter, include, ma'a al-qawm al-ṣāliḥīn, among righteous people, ṣāliḥīn from ṣ-l-ḥ, righteous, good, indicates they long for Lord to include them among righteous, ṣāliḥīn, who believe and do good, as earlier verses defined. The same longing appears in *“We long for Lord to include us among righteous”* (5:84) itself. The longing shows that belief is not only rational but aspirational: they want to be among righteous, not only to avoid punishment. The righteous, ṣāliḥīn, includes believers, prophets, and those who do good, as in *“Include me among righteous”* (26:83) supplication of Ibrāhīm. The verse therefore shows that some Christians who heard Qur'an not only believed but longed to be among righteous, showing sincerity.

**A Verse That Shows Rational and Aspirational Basis for Belief**

This verse shows rational and aspirational basis for belief: rational — why should we not believe when truth came, aspirational — we long to be among righteous. The rational basis is that truth came, so belief is logical, as previous verse said they recognized truth and eyes overflowed with tears. The aspirational basis is longing to be among righteous, which is hope for Paradise and good company. The same combination appears for other believers who believe because truth came and hope for righteous company. The verse's placement after weeping and supplication shows that belief is both emotional, tears, and rational, why should we not believe, and aspirational, longing for righteous. The next verse will say Allah will reward them for what they said with Gardens under which rivers flow, forever, reward of good-doers, showing outcome of belief and longing.

**Why Should We Not Believe as Argument Against Resentment**

The question wa mā lanā lā nu'minu, why should we not believe, also answers previous verse 59's question do you resent us only because we believe, while most of you rebellious. Here Christians who believe ask why should we not believe, implying that resentment for belief is unjust, and belief is natural when truth comes. The same question appears for those who are resented for believing, showing that belief is not reason for resentment but for reward. The longing to be among righteous, ṣāliḥīn, shows that they want to be with righteous, not with rebellious, as previous verses described many rebellious. The verse therefore contrasts two groups: rebellious who resent believers for believing, and righteous among Christians who ask why should we not believe and long for righteous company.

**Extra Verified Note on Ṣāliḥīn**

Ṣāliḥīn, righteous, from ṣalaḥa, to be good, sound, righteous, appears often for righteous people who do good deeds. The supplication an yudkhilanā ma'a al-qawm al-ṣāliḥīn, include us among righteous, appears also for Ibrāhīm (26:83) and Yūsuf (12:101) who asked to be among righteous, showing that being among righteous is aspiration of prophets and righteous. The question wa mā lanā lā nu'minu, why should we not believe, appears also for those who believe after truth comes, showing rational basis. The following verse will reward them with Gardens for what they said, showing that supplication and belief lead to Paradise.

**Belief in Allah and Truth as Rational Response**

Mā jā'anā min al-ḥaqq, what came to us of truth, refers to Qur'an and Prophet, which they recognized as truth because they had previous knowledge from Torah and Gospel and humility, not arrogance, as verse 82 said priests and monks and not arrogant. The recognition led to tears (5:83) and to question why should we not believe, showing rational response: truth came, so belief is logical. The same rational response appears for those given Book before who believe when Qur'an recited (28:52-53). The longing naṭma'u an yudkhilanā ma'a al-qawm al-ṣāliḥīn, we long to be among righteous, shows aspirational response: desire to be among righteous, not only to avoid punishment, but to be with good company in Paradise. The next verse will reward them with Gardens for what they said, showing that rational belief and aspirational longing lead to Paradise.

**Longing for Righteous Company as Hope**

Naṭma'u, we hope, long, indicates hope and desire for Lord to include them among righteous, ṣāliḥīn, who are believers, prophets, and doers of good. The same hope appears for Ibrāhīm who said include me among righteous (26:83) and Yūsuf who said take me as Muslim and join me with righteous (12:101), showing that being among righteous is aspiration of prophets. The Christians who believed long for same, showing sincerity and humility. The verse therefore shows that some Christians who heard Qur'an not only believed but aspired to be among righteous, illustrating closest in affection to believers because priests, monks, not arrogant, and eyes overflow with tears for recognizing truth. The following verse will reward them with Gardens.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:85

> So Allah will reward them for what they said with Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever. And that is the reward of the good-doers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Reward for What They Said With Gardens**

Fa-athābahum Allāhu bi-mā qālū jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār khālidīna fīhā reports reward for Christians who believed. Fa-athābahum Allāhu, Allah rewarded them, athābahum from th-w-b, to reward, return, bi-mā qālū, for what they said, i.e., we believe, count us among witnesses, why should we not believe, longing for righteous, as previous verses said, jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār, Gardens under which rivers flow, as earlier described for Paradise, khālidīna fīhā, abiding therein forever, khālidīn from kh-l-d, to remain, indicates everlasting abode in Paradise. The same reward appears in *“Rewarded them with Gardens under which rivers flow, abiding forever”* (5:85) itself and in many verses for believers. The reward for what they said shows that supplication and belief, qālū, saying, leads to Paradise, because saying reflects belief. The same phrasing appears for other believers rewarded for saying, as in *“Rewarded them for what they said”* (5:85).

**That Is Reward of Good-Doers as General Principle**

Wa dhālika jazā' al-muḥsinīn — that is reward of good-doers — generalizes. Jazā', reward, recompense, muḥsinīn, good-doers, from ḥ-s-n, to do good, excellence, indicates that reward of Gardens forever is for muḥsinīn, those who do good with excellence, iḥsān. The same closing appears in *“That is reward of good-doers”* (5:85) itself and in many verses after describing good deeds. The muḥsinīn here are Christians who believed, wept, supplicated, and longed for righteous, showing iḥsān in response to truth. The reward is Gardens forever, which is everlasting bliss, as verse 65 said Gardens of Bliss for People of Book who believed and were mindful. The verse therefore shows that some Christians who heard Qur'an and believed are among muḥsinīn and rewarded with Paradise forever.

**A Verse That Rewards Belief and Supplication**

This verse rewards belief and supplication of some Christians with Paradise, showing that closeness in affection to believers because priests, monks, not arrogant (5:82) leads to belief and reward when truth recognized. The reward for what they said, bi-mā qālū, indicates that saying we believe, count us among witnesses, why should we not believe, longing for righteous, are not mere words but reflect sincere faith that deserves reward. The same reward appears for believers who say we hear and obey (24:51) and are rewarded. The verse's placement after describing weeping and supplication shows that emotional and rational response to Qur'an leads to Paradise. The next verse will say as for those who disbelieve and reject Our signs, residents of Hellfire, contrasting reward of good-doers with punishment of disbelievers.
