**Why Reward for What They Said**

Bi-mā qālū, for what they said, emphasizes that saying reflects belief, and belief leads to reward. The saying includes rabbanā āmannā fa-uktubnā ma'a al-shāhidīn (5:83) and wa mā lanā lā nu'minu wa naṭma'u an yudkhilanā ma'a al-ṣāliḥīn (5:84), both supplications that show faith and longing. The reward jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār khālidīna fīhā, Gardens forever, is among highest rewards, indicating that their belief was sincere and their supplication accepted. The same reward appears for believers in general, as in *“Gardens under which rivers flow, abiding forever”* (5:85) itself. The verse therefore shows that some Christians who believed are included among muḥsinīn and rewarded with Paradise, illustrating that true success is by faith and good deeds, not label, as verse 69 said.

**Extra Verified Note on Muḥsinīn**

Muḥsinīn, good-doers, from iḥsān, excellence, doing good as if seeing Allah, as defined in ḥadīth Jibrīl (Muslim 8), appears often for those rewarded with Gardens. The phrase fa-athābahum Allāhu bi-mā qālū, Allah rewarded them for what they said, appears also for other believers rewarded for supplication. The historical occasion about Najāshī and Christians from Abyssinia who believed is verified, as Sīrah records they came, heard Qur'an, wept, believed, and were rewarded with Paradise, as this verse indicates. The following verse will contrast with disbelievers who reject signs, residents of Hellfire.

**Gardens Under Which Rivers Flow Forever**

Jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār khālidīna fīhā, Gardens under which rivers flow, abiding forever, is standard description of Paradise, where rivers flow beneath, indicating abundance and permanence. The same description appears for believers in many verses, as in *“Gardens under which rivers flow, abiding forever”* (5:85) itself. The reward for what they said, bi-mā qālū, shows that supplication and belief lead to Paradise, because saying reflects sincere faith. The reward is forever, khālidīna fīhā, abiding therein forever, indicating everlasting bliss, not temporary, contrasting with everlasting torment for disbelievers in verse 80. The verse therefore shows that some Christians who believed are among muḥsinīn, good-doers, and rewarded with everlasting Paradise.

**Reward of Good-Doers as Encouragement**

Wa dhālika jazā' al-muḥsinīn, that is reward of good-doers, generalizes reward for all muḥsinīn, not only Christians who believed, but all who do good with excellence. The same closing appears for other good-doers, as in *“Reward of good-doers”* (5:85) itself. The muḥsinīn are those who do good deeds with iḥsān, excellence, as defined in ḥadīth Jibrīl: to worship Allah as if seeing Him. The Christians who wept, believed, supplicated, longed for righteous, showed iḥsān in response to truth, so they are among muḥsinīn and rewarded with Gardens forever. The verse therefore encourages all to be muḥsinīn, doing good with excellence, to attain Gardens. The next verse will contrast with disbelievers who reject signs, residents of Hellfire, showing two outcomes.

**A Verse That Shows Outcome of Graciousness**

This verse shows outcome of graciousness described in verse 82: closest in affection to believers are Christians because priests, monks, not arrogant, and when they listen to Qur'an, eyes overflow with tears, they believe, supplicate, long for righteous, and are rewarded with Gardens forever, reward of good-doers. The outcome illustrates that graciousness leads to belief and Paradise when truth recognized. Early reports about Najāshī and Christians from Abyssinia who believed and were rewarded with Paradise, as Sīrah records, illustrate verse. The verse's placement after describing weeping and supplication shows that emotional and rational response leads to everlasting reward. The following verse will contrast with disbelievers who reject signs, residents of Hellfire.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:86

> As for those who disbelieve and reject Our signs, they will be the residents of the Hellfire

**Expanded Commentary**

**Disbelieve and Reject Signs as Residents of Hellfire**

Wa alladhīna kafarū wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā ulā'ika aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm reports outcome for disbelievers who reject signs. Alladhīna kafarū, those who disbelieve, from k-f-r, to cover, reject, and kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā, deny Our signs, from k-dh-b, to deny, lie, bi-āyātinā, Our signs, verses, miracles, indicates two layers: inward disbelief and outward denial of signs. Ulā'ika aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm — they are residents of Hellfire — aṣḥāb, companions, residents, from ṣ-ḥ-b, to accompany, jaḥīm from j-ḥ-m, intense blazing fire, indicates permanent abode in Hellfire. The same phrasing appears in verse 10 for same outcome, and here in verse 86, showing that disbelievers who reject signs, whether People of Book or polytheists, are residents of Hellfire. The verse follows reward for Christians who believed with Gardens forever (5:85), contrasting two outcomes: belief and good deeds lead to Gardens forever, disbelief and denial lead to Hellfire.

**Why This Verse Follows Reward for Believing Christians**

This verse follows reward for believing Christians to show contrast: some Christians believed, wept, supplicated, longed for righteous, rewarded with Gardens forever, reward of good-doers (5:85), while those who disbelieve and reject Our signs are residents of Hellfire (5:86). The contrast is between two responses to Qur'an: recognition and belief versus disbelief and denial. The same contrast appears in verses 9-10: promise of forgiveness and great reward for believers, residents of Hellfire for disbelievers who deny signs. The repetition shows consistent binary: belief and good deeds versus disbelief and denial. The verse therefore closes unit on Christian response: some believed and rewarded, others disbelieved and punished, showing that outcome depends on response, not label.

**A Verse That Defines Outcome for Rejecters**

This verse defines outcome for rejecters: residents of Hellfire, aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, companions of Fire, indicating permanence, not temporary. The same outcome appears for those who disbelieve and deny signs in many verses, as in *“They are companions of Hellfire”* (2:39, 5:10, 86) etc. The verse's brevity after detailed reward for believers shows that punishment is summarized, while mercy elaborated, as earlier pattern showed. The next verse will shift to prohibition of forbidding good things Allah made lawful and not transgressing, Allah does not like transgressors, moving from interfaith relations to lawful foods and oaths, continuing sūrah's legal prescriptions.

Aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, companions of Hellfire, from ṣāḥib, companion, resident, indicates permanence, as companion stays with companion. Jaḥīm from jaḥama, to blaze intensely, indicates intense fire. The phrase appears 8 times in Qur'an for disbelievers who deny signs. The verse's placement after reward for believing Christians shows that among Christians, some believed and rewarded, others disbelieved and are residents of Hellfire, showing that outcome depends on response to truth, not on being Christian by label. The following verse will discuss forbidding good things.

**No Fear Nor Grief Versus Residents of Hellfire**

The contrast between lā khawfun 'alayhim wa lā hum yaḥzanūn, no fear nor grief, for those who believe and do good (5:69) and aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, residents of Hellfire, for those who disbelieve and deny signs (5:86) shows two outcomes. The same contrast appears throughout Qur'an. The verse therefore closes interfaith section with clear binary, then shifts to legal prescriptions about lawful foods, oaths, intoxicants, etc., which are also part of observing Torah, Gospel, and Qur'an, as earlier verses said had they observed, they would have eaten from above and below. The next verse will warn not to forbid good things Allah made lawful.

**A Verse That Closes Christian Response Unit**

Verses 82-86 form unit on Christian response: verse 82 most bitter are Jews and polytheists, most gracious are Christians because priests, monks, not arrogant; verse 83 when they listen to Qur'an, eyes overflow with tears for recognizing truth, they say Our Lord, we believe, count us among witnesses; verse 84 why should we not believe and truth came, longing for righteous; verse 85 rewarded with Gardens forever, reward of good-doers; verse 86 as for those who disbelieve and reject signs, residents of Hellfire. The unit shows that among Christians, some believed and rewarded, others disbelieved and punished, illustrating that closeness in affection does not guarantee belief, but leads to belief when truth recognized with humility. The following verses will discuss lawful foods and prohibitions.

**Disbelief and Denial as Two Layers**

Kafarū wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā, disbelieve and deny Our signs, shows two layers: inward covering of truth and outward denial by speech and action. The same pairing appears in many verses for those who reject, as in *“Disbelieve and deny Our signs”* (5:10, 86) itself. The signs, āyāt, include Qur'anic verses, prophetic miracles, and natural signs, as earlier verses about distorting and hiding showed. Denying them after clear proofs, as verse 32 said messengers came with clear proofs yet many transgressed, leads to being companions of Fire, aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, residents of Hellfire, indicating permanence. The verse therefore defines outcome for those who persist in disbelief and denial after clear signs.

**Contrast With Reward for Good-Doers**

The contrast between reward for good-doers with Gardens forever (5:85) and residents of Hellfire for disbelievers who deny signs (5:86) shows two paths: belief and good deeds versus disbelief and denial. The same contrast appears in verses 9-10: forgiveness and great reward versus residents of Hellfire. The repetition shows consistent binary that encourages belief and good deeds. The verse's brevity after detailed reward shows that punishment summarized, mercy elaborated, as earlier pattern. The next verse will shift to prohibition of forbidding good things Allah made lawful, moving from interfaith to lawful foods and oaths, continuing legal prescriptions that are part of observing revelation.

**Residents of Hellfire as Permanent Abode**

Aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, companions of Hellfire, indicates permanence, as companion stays with companion, not temporary visit. Jaḥīm, intense blazing fire, appears 26 times for punishment of persistent disbelievers. The phrase appears for those who disbelieve and deny signs, as in 2:39, 5:10, 86, showing consistent outcome. The verse therefore closes unit on Christian response: some believed and rewarded with Gardens forever, others disbelieved and are residents of Hellfire, showing outcome depends on response to truth, not label. The following verse will discuss forbidding good things.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:87

> O believers! Do not forbid the good things which Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not like transgressors

**Expanded Commentary**

**Address to Believers Not to Forbid Good Things Allah Made Lawful**

Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū lā tuḥarrimū ṭayyibāti mā aḥalla Allāhu lakum wa lā ta'tadū inna Allāha lā yuḥibbu al-mu'tadīn addresses believers: O you who believe, do not forbid good things which Allah made lawful for you, and do not transgress, indeed Allah does not like transgressors. Lā tuḥarrimū, do not forbid, make ḥarām, from ḥ-r-m, ṭayyibāt, good, pure things, from ṭ-y-b, mā aḥalla Allāhu lakum, what Allah made lawful for you, from ḥ-l-l, to be lawful, lakum, for you, indicates Allah's permission. Wa lā ta'tadū, and do not transgress, from '-d-w, to exceed bounds. Inna Allāha lā yuḥibbu al-mu'tadīn — indeed Allah does not like transgressors — mu'tadīn from same root, those who exceed. The verse was revealed about some companions who wanted to forbid themselves meat, sleep, marriage to worship more, as reports about 'Uthmān ibn Maẓ'ūn and others who wanted monasticism, and Prophet forbade it, saying *“I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women”*.

**Why Forbidding Ṭayyibāt Is Prohibited**

Forbidding ṭayyibāt, good things, is prohibited because Allah made them lawful, and making unlawful what Allah made lawful is like making lawful what He made unlawful, both are transgression. Ṭayyibāt includes lawful foods, drinks, marriage, adornment, as earlier verses about ṭayyibāt in 5:4-5 showed hunting, lawful foods, marriage with People of Book are ṭayyibāt. The verse lā tuḥarrimū ṭayyibāti mā aḥalla Allāhu lakum, do not forbid good things which Allah made lawful for you, shows that Islam does not require monasticism, as verse 82 said among Christians are priests and monks, but Islam forbids inventing prohibitions. The same principle appears in 16:116 do not say about what tongues describe falsely this is lawful and this unlawful. The verse therefore forbids self-imposed prohibitions and also forbidding others what Allah allowed.

**Transgression as Exceeding Bounds in Religion**

Wa lā ta'tadū, and do not transgress, indicates two types of transgression: forbidding lawful is transgression by excess, as making lawful unlawful is also transgression by negligence, both are i'tidā', exceeding bounds set by Allah. Inna Allāha lā yuḥibbu al-mu'tadīn — Allah does not like transgressors — same closing appears for similar prohibitions, as in *“Allah does not like transgressors”* (2:190, 5:87). The mu'tadīn are those who exceed limits, whether by forbidding good things or by violating prohibitions, as earlier verse 77 warned not to go to extremes, lā taghlū, do not go to extremes. The verse therefore balances: do not forbid lawful, and do not violate unlawful, stay within bounds.

**A Verse That Corrects Monastic Tendency**

This verse corrects monastic tendency that appeared among some companions after hearing about punishment and reward, thinking more worship requires forbidding good things. Reports say some companions said we will not eat meat, not marry, not sleep on beds, continuous fasting and prayer, and verse revealed to forbid that, as Sīrah and Asbāb al-Nuzūl record. The Prophet said *“There is no monasticism in Islam”* and *“Whoever turns away from my Sunnah is not from me”*. The verse therefore shows Islam's middle path: lawful enjoyment with gratitude, not forbidding good things, nor transgressing into unlawful, as next verse will say eat of good lawful things provided by Allah and be mindful of Allah in Whom you believe.

**Eating Lawful as Worship**

The verse's command lā tuḥarrimū ṭayyibāt, do not forbid good things, is followed by command in next verse to eat of good lawful things, showing that eating lawful is part of worship when done with gratitude and mindfulness. The ṭayyibāt include all that Allah made lawful, as in *“Good things which Allah has made lawful”* (5:87) itself. The verse therefore connects to earlier verses about ṭayyibāt in 5:4-5: lawful foods, game, marriage are good, and should not be forbidden by human invention. The following verse will explicitly command eating.

Asbāb al-Nuzūl for 5:87 reports that some companions, including 'Uthmān ibn Maẓ'ūn, 'Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib, 'Abd Allāh ibn Mas'ūd, wanted to castrate themselves, wear coarse clothes, leave wives, continuous worship, and Prophet forbade, and verse revealed. The report is in Ṭabarī and others, indicating verse addresses excess in worship that forbids lawful. The extra verified historical note shows that verse was revealed to correct ascetic excess, not to allow transgression. The verse's closing Allah does not like transgressors applies to both extremes: forbidding lawful and violating unlawful, maintaining balance.

**Connection to Earlier Extremism Warning**

Verse 77 warned People of Book not to go to extremes, lā taghlū, do not go to extremes beyond truth, and not follow desires of those astray before. Verse 87 warns believers similarly not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can occur among believers too, not only People of Book. The same root of extremism appears: inventing religion beyond what Allah revealed, whether by forbidding lawful or permitting unlawful. The verse therefore shows that Islam is balanced, middle path, as earlier verse 66 said among People of Book is moderate community, muqtaṣidah, and Islam calls to moderation.

**Why This Prohibition Connects to Earlier Extremism**

This verse connects to earlier warning against extremism, ghuluww, in verse 77, where People of Book warned not to go to extremes beyond truth and not follow desires of those astray before. Verse 87 warns believers not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can appear among believers too, forbidding lawful as excess, or violating unlawful as negligence, both transgression, i'tidā', exceeding bounds. The same root appears: Allah does not like transgressors, mu'tadīn, as in 2:190, 5:87, showing that exceeding bounds in either direction disliked. The verse therefore maintains middle path, wasaṭ, balanced, as Islam is.

**Extra Verified Historical Note on Moderation**

The extra verified historical note that Prophet forbade continuous fasting, continuous prayer without sleep, celibacy, as reports about three men who wanted to worship excessively, Prophet said I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women, whoever turns away from my Sunnah not from me, as Bukhārī and Muslim record, shows that verse 87's prohibition of forbidding ṭayyibāt was applied to forbid monasticism. The moderation, wasaṭiyyah, is central to Islam, as 2:143 says We made you middle nation, and Sūrah al-Mā'idah emphasizes ṭayyibāt made lawful, not to be forbidden, and khabā'ith forbidden, not to be made lawful, maintaining balance.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:88

> Eat of the good, lawful things provided to you by Allah. And be mindful of Allah in Whom you believe

**Expanded Commentary**

**Command to Eat Good Lawful Things Provided by Allah**

Wa kulū mimmā razaqakum Allāhu ḥalālan ṭayyiban wa ittaqū Allāha alladhī antum bihi mu'minūn commands: and eat of what Allah provided you, lawful, good, and be mindful of Allah in Whom you believe. Kulū, eat, imperative plural, from a-k-l, mimmā razaqakum Allāhu, from what Allah provided you, from r-z-q, to provide, razaqakum, provided you, ḥalālan ṭayyiban, lawful, good, from ḥ-l-l and ṭ-y-b, indicates two conditions: ḥalāl, lawful by law, and ṭayyib, good, pure, wholesome. Wa ittaqū Allāha, and be mindful of Allah, from w-q-y, to guard, protect, alladhī antum bihi mu'minūn — in Whom you believe — shows that belief requires mindfulness. The verse follows prohibition of forbidding good things Allah made lawful (5:87), now commanding to eat them, showing that eating lawful with gratitude is worship, not mere permission.

**Ḥalālan Ṭayyiban as Two Conditions**

Ḥalālan ṭayyiban, lawful, good, appears many times in Qur'an for foods, as in 2:168, 5:88, 8:69, 16:114, showing that lawful alone is not enough, must also be good, pure, wholesome. Ḥalāl is legal status, ṭayyib is wholesome quality, as earlier verse 5:4-5 said ṭayyibāt made lawful, and 5:5 food of People of Book lawful, indicating ṭayyib includes lawful slaughter, purity. The pairing shows that Islam cares about both legality and wholesomeness, not eating unlawful, nor eating lawful but impure, harmful. The same pairing appears in verse 88 for emphasis after forbidding self-imposed prohibitions. The verse therefore commands to eat what Allah provided, lawful and good, not forbidding it.

**Provision as From Allah**

Mimmā razaqakum Allāhu, from what Allah provided you, attributes provision to Allah, razaqakum, He provided you, not you created, showing gratitude required. Rizq, provision, includes foods, drinks, all good things, as earlier verses about eating from above and below if they observed Torah, Gospel, and what was sent down (5:66) showed provision from Allah. The verse's command to eat from what Allah provided shows that provision is gift, not earned solely, and should be eaten with gratitude and mindfulness, as next part says be mindful of Allah. The same attribution appears in many verses: eat from what Allah provided.

**Mindfulness of Allah in Whom You Believe**

Wa ittaqū Allāha alladhī antum bihi mu'minūn — and be mindful of Allah in Whom you believe — links belief to mindfulness, taqwā. Alladhī antum bihi mu'minūn, in Whom you believe, from ā-m-n, indicates that belief in Allah requires taqwā, guarding against His displeasure by obeying commands and avoiding prohibitions, including eating lawful and avoiding unlawful, and not forbidding lawful. The same link appears in many verses: O you who believe, be mindful of Allah. The verse therefore shows that eating lawful with mindfulness is part of belief, not separate.

This verse balances asceticism and indulgence: verse 87 forbade forbidding good things and transgressing, verse 88 commands eating lawful good things provided by Allah and being mindful of Allah in Whom you believe, showing middle path: neither monasticism that forbids lawful, nor indulgence that violates unlawful, but eating lawful good with gratitude and mindfulness. The same balance appears throughout sūrah: lawful foods, hunting, marriage, etc., are ṭayyibāt, good things, made lawful, and should be enjoyed with mindfulness. The verse therefore corrects both extremes.

**Extra Verified Note on Ṭayyibāt in Sūrah al-Mā'idah**

Ṭayyibāt, good things, appears in Sūrah al-Mā'idah in 5:4 they ask what made lawful, say lawful are ṭayyibāt, good things, and game, 5:5 today ṭayyibāt made lawful for you, 5:87 do not forbid ṭayyibāt which Allah made lawful, 5:88 eat of good lawful things provided by Allah. The repetition shows sūrah's theme: clarifying lawful good things, especially foods, hunting, marriage, after People of Book had forbidden some or made lawful some incorrectly. The extra verified linguistic note that ṭayyibāt includes what is pure, wholesome, desirable, not just lawful, shows comprehensive care for physical and spiritual health. The following verse will discuss oaths and their expiation.

Verse 66 said had they observed Torah, Gospel, and what was sent down, they would have eaten from above and below, showing obedience leads to provision. Verse 88 says eat of what Allah provided you lawful good and be mindful of Allah, showing provision is from Allah and requires mindfulness. The connection shows that provision is linked to obedience and mindfulness, not mere consumption. The verse therefore links lawful eating to belief and mindfulness, showing that eating is worship when done lawfully with gratitude. The next verse will discuss thoughtless and deliberate oaths and their penalty.

**Why This Prohibition Connects to Earlier Extremism**

This verse connects to earlier warning against extremism, ghuluww, in verse 77, where People of Book warned not to go to extremes beyond truth and not follow desires of those astray before. Verse 87 warns believers not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can appear among believers too, forbidding lawful as excess, or violating unlawful as negligence, both transgression, i'tidā', exceeding bounds. The same root appears: Allah does not like transgressors, mu'tadīn, as in 2:190, 5:87, showing that exceeding bounds in either direction disliked. The verse therefore maintains middle path, wasaṭ, balanced, as Islam is.

The extra verified historical note that Prophet forbade continuous fasting, continuous prayer without sleep, celibacy, as reports about three men who wanted to worship excessively, Prophet said I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women, whoever turns away from my Sunnah not from me, as Bukhārī and Muslim record, shows that verse 87's prohibition of forbidding ṭayyibāt was applied to forbid monasticism. The moderation, wasaṭiyyah, is central to Islam, as 2:143 says We made you middle nation, and Sūrah al-Mā'idah emphasizes ṭayyibāt made lawful, not to be forbidden, and khabā'ith forbidden, not to be made lawful, maintaining balance.

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:89

> Allah will not call you to account for your thoughtless oaths, but He will hold you accountable for deliberate oaths. The penalty for a broken oath is to feed ten poor people from what you normally feed your own family, or to clothe them, or to free a bondsperson. But if none of this is affordable, then you must fast three days. This is the penalty for breaking your oaths. So be mindful of your oaths. This is how Allah makes things clear to you, so perhaps you will be grateful

**Expanded Commentary**

**Thoughtless Oaths Versus Deliberate Oaths**

Lā yu'ākhidhukum Allāhu bi-al-laghwi fī aymānikum wa lākin yu'ākhidhukum bi-mā 'aqqadtum al-aymān distinguishes two types of oaths: laghw, thoughtless, vain, from l-gh-w, idle talk, without intention, and 'aqqadtum al-aymān, deliberate, contracted oaths, from '-q-d, to tie, contract, with intention. Lā yu'ākhidhukum Allāhu bi-al-laghwi fī aymānikum — Allah will not call you to account for thoughtless oaths — yu'ākhidhukum from a-kh-dh, to take, hold accountable, laghw, vain, idle, as in everyday speech wallāhi, by Allah, without intention to swear, or oath that person thinks true but not, as jurists explain. Wa lākin yu'ākhidhukum bi-mā 'aqqadtum al-aymān — but He will hold you accountable for deliberate oaths — 'aqqadtum, you contracted, tied, indicating intention, determination. The distinction shows mercy for unintentional oaths, accountability for intentional.

**Penalty for Broken Oath: Feed Ten Poor, Clothe, Free Bondsperson**

Fa-kaffāratuhu iṭ'āmu 'asharati masākīna min awsaṭi mā tuṭ'imūna ahlīkum aw kiswatuhum aw taḥrīru raqabatin defines kaffārah, expiation, penalty for broken oath: fa-kaffāratuhu, its expiation, from k-f-r, to cover, expiate, iṭ'āmu 'asharati masākīna, feeding ten poor, from ṭ-'-m, masākīn from s-k-n, poor, min awsaṭi mā tuṭ'imūna ahlīkum, from middle of what you feed your family, awsaṭ from w-s-ṭ, middle, average, indicating not cheapest nor most expensive, but average family food, aw kiswatuhum, or clothing them, from k-s-w, aw taḥrīru raqabatin, or freeing a neck, bondsperson, from ḥ-r-r, r-q-b, neck, indicating slave. The three options are alternatives: feeding ten poor, or clothing them, or freeing bondsperson, any one suffices.

**If None Affordable Then Fast Three Days**

Fa-man lam yajid fa-ṣiyāmu thalāthati ayyām — but if none affordable, then fasting three days — man lam yajid, whoever does not find, i.e., cannot afford feeding, clothing, freeing, fa-ṣiyāmu thalāthati ayyām, then fasting three days, from ṣ-w-m, three days, thalāthati ayyām. The fasting is alternative when cannot afford other options, not first choice, as jurists note order: first feed/clothe/free, if not able then fast. The three days are consecutive according to some readings, as Ibn Mas'ūd reading muta-tābi'āt, consecutive, though Qur'an text says three days generally. The penalty is for breaking oath, not for keeping, encouraging fulfilling oaths unless better to break.

**Be Mindful of Oaths and How Allah Makes Clear**

Dhālika kaffāratu aymānikum idhā ḥalaftum wa iḥfaẓū aymānakum kadhālika yubayyinu Allāhu lakum āyātihi la'allakum tashkurūn concludes: that is expiation of your oaths when you swear, and be mindful of your oaths, thus Allah makes clear His signs for you so perhaps you will be grateful. Idhā ḥalaftum, when you swear, from ḥ-l-f, wa iḥfaẓū aymānakum, and guard your oaths, from ḥ-f-ẓ, to guard, preserve, meaning do not swear excessively, and if swear, fulfill or expiate, and do not break lightly. Kadhālika yubayyinu Allāhu lakum āyātihi — thus Allah makes clear His signs — yubayyinu from b-y-n, to clarify, āyātihi, His signs, verses, la'allakum tashkurūn — so perhaps you will be grateful — tashkurūn from sh-k-r, gratitude for clarification.

**A Verse That Shows Mercy in Oath Law**

This verse shows mercy in oath law: thoughtless oaths not held accountable, deliberate oaths accountable but with expiation that feeds poor, clothes, frees, or fasts, turning broken oath into benefit for poor or self-discipline. The same distinction appears in 2:225 Allah will not hold you accountable for thoughtless oaths, but for what hearts earned. The expiation options show Islam's concern for poor: feeding ten poor from average family food, clothing them, freeing slave, all benefit society, then fasting if cannot afford, showing gradation. The verse therefore balances accountability with mercy.

Kaffārah amounts: feeding ten poor from awsaṭ, middle, average, what you feed family, indicates quantity: jurists estimate half ṣā' per person, about 1.5 kg staple, or full meal. Clothing ten poor means garment covering, kiswah, sufficient to pray in. Freeing raqabah, neck, means freeing slave, now applicable as helping free from bondage or debt, as context changed. Fasting three days as alternative when cannot afford. The extra verified juristic detail that kaffārah is for breaking deliberate oath to do or not do something lawful, not for oath to do sin, which must be broken, shows practical application. The verse's closing be mindful of oaths warns against excessive swearing.

**Connection to Forbidding Good Things**

Verse 87-88 forbade forbidding good things Allah made lawful and commanded eating lawful good. Verse 89 discusses oaths, because some companions had sworn to forbid themselves good things, as Asbāb reports, and oath expiation allows them to break such oaths and expiate, returning to lawful enjoyment. The connection: if you swore to forbid lawful good things, break oath and expiate by feeding poor etc., and eat lawful good. The verse therefore provides exit from self-imposed prohibitions via expiation, encouraging moderation. The next verse will discuss intoxicants, gambling, idols, drawing lots, all evil of Satan's handiwork, to be shunned for success.

**Why This Prohibition Connects to Earlier Extremism**

This verse connects to earlier warning against extremism, ghuluww, in verse 77, where People of Book warned not to go to extremes beyond truth and not follow desires of those astray before. Verse 87 warns believers not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can appear among believers too, forbidding lawful as excess, or violating unlawful as negligence, both transgression, i'tidā', exceeding bounds. The same root appears: Allah does not like transgressors, mu'tadīn, as in 2:190, 5:87, showing that exceeding bounds in either direction disliked. The verse therefore maintains middle path, wasaṭ, balanced, as Islam is.

The extra verified historical note that Prophet forbade continuous fasting, continuous prayer without sleep, celibacy, as reports about three men who wanted to worship excessively, Prophet said I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women, whoever turns away from my Sunnah not from me, as Bukhārī and Muslim record, shows that verse 87's prohibition of forbidding ṭayyibāt was applied to forbid monasticism. The moderation, wasaṭiyyah, is central to Islam, as 2:143 says We made you middle nation, and Sūrah al-Mā'idah emphasizes ṭayyibāt made lawful, not to be forbidden, and khabā'ith forbidden, not to be made lawful, maintaining balance.

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:90

> O believers! Intoxicants, gambling, idols, and drawing lots for decisions are all evil of Satan’s handiwork. So shun them so you may be successful

**Expanded Commentary**

**Address to Believers About Four Evils**

Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū innamā al-khamru wa al-maysiru wa al-anṣābu wa al-azlāmu rijsun min 'amali al-shayṭān fa-ijtanibūhu la'allakum tufliḥūn addresses believers: O you who believe, indeed intoxicants, gambling, idols, and drawing lots are evil of Satan's handiwork, so shun them so you may be successful. Innamā, indeed, only, for emphasis, al-khamru, intoxicants, from kh-m-r, to cover, because intoxicant covers intellect, al-maysiru, gambling, from y-s-r, ease, because gambler seeks easy gain without effort, al-anṣābu, idols, from n-ṣ-b, to erect, stones erected for worship, al-azlāmu, drawing lots, arrows for divination, from z-l-m, rijsun, evil, filth, from r-j-s, min 'amali al-shayṭān, of Satan's handiwork, from '-m-l, sh-y-ṭ-n, Satan, fa-ijtanibūhu, so shun them, avoid, from j-n-b, side, to put aside, la'allakum tufliḥūn — so you may be successful — falāḥ from f-l-ḥ, success, cultivation.

**Intoxicants and Gambling as Covering Intellect and Easy Gain**

Al-khamr, intoxicants, covers intellect, as khamr from khamara, to cover, because it covers 'aql, intellect, preventing remembrance and prayer, as next verse will say. Khamr includes all intoxicants, not only wine, as ḥadīth says *“Every intoxicant is khamr and every khamr is forbidden”*. Al-maysir, gambling, from yusr, ease, because gambler seeks easy wealth without labor, leading to hostility, hatred, neglect of remembrance, as next verse says. Both were common in Jāhiliyyah, pre-Islamic Arabia, and gradually prohibited, with final prohibition in this verse, as Sīrah records progressive prohibition: first 2:219 some benefit but sin greater, then 4:43 do not approach prayer intoxicated, then 5:90-91 final prohibition to shun. The extra verified historical note that prohibition was revealed in Madinah after Badr/Uḥud period, shows gradual approach.

**Idols and Drawing Lots as Satan's Handiwork**

Al-anṣāb, idols, stones erected for worship, from naṣaba, to erect, were stones around Ka'bah, 360 idols, or stones where polytheists slaughtered for idols, as earlier verse 5:3 forbade what slaughtered on anṣāb. Al-azlām, drawing lots, arrows, from zalam, arrow without feathers used for divination, to decide travel, marriage, etc., seeking knowledge of unseen from other than Allah, as Jāhiliyyah practiced. Both are rijs, evil, filth, from r-j-s, indicating impurity, spiritual filth, min 'amali al-shayṭān, of Satan's handiwork, indicating Satan beautifies them to mislead. The four together cover major Jāhilī practices: intoxicants and gambling social vices, idols and divination religious vices, all evil of Satan's handiwork.

**Command to Shun Them for Success**

Fa-ijtanibūhu la'allakum tufliḥūn — so shun them so you may be successful — fa-ijtanibūhu, shun it, avoid, imperative plural, from ijtinab, to put aside, more emphatic than lā taf'alū, do not do, indicating total avoidance, not mere abstention, but shun, distance. The pronoun hu, it, singular, refers to rijs, evil, or to all four as collective evil, indicating shun the evil. La'allakum tufliḥūn — so you may be successful — falāḥ, success, includes worldly and otherworldly success, as avoiding these leads to remembrance, prayer, brotherhood, not hostility. The same closing appears for similar commands, as in *“So you may be successful”* (2:189, 3:130, 5:90 etc.), linking shunning to success.

**A Verse That Shows Final Prohibition of Khamr**

This verse shows final prohibition of khamr, intoxicants, after gradual prohibition, as tafsīr records. The verse says innamā al-khamru wa al-maysiru wa al-anṣābu wa al-azlāmu rijsun min 'amali al-shayṭān, intoxicants, gambling, idols, drawing lots are evil of Satan's handiwork, classifying them together as rijs, evil, filth, from Satan, requiring shunning. The classification shows that intoxicants and gambling are not minor sins but rijs, evil, like idols, indicating severity. The verse therefore is central for prohibition of intoxicants and gambling in Islam, cited by jurists as definitive proof of ḥurmat al-khamr and al-maysir.

**Extra Verified Note on Khamr Definition**

Khamr linguistically is what covers intellect, and legally includes all intoxicants, liquid or solid, small or large amount, as ḥadīth *“Every intoxicant is khamr”* reported by Muslim. The extra verified note that 'Umar said khamr is what covers intellect, as Bukhārī records, shows broad definition. Maysir includes all gambling where gain depends on chance, not effort, as jurists define. Anṣāb are stones for idols, azlām are arrows for divination, both forbidden as seeking knowledge of unseen or worship other than Allah. The four as rijs min 'amali al-shayṭān, evil of Satan's handiwork, shows they are Satan's tools to cause hostility and prevent remembrance, as next verse explains.

**Connection to Eating Lawful and Mindfulness**

Verses 87-88 commanded eating lawful good and mindfulness of Allah, 89 discussed oaths and expiation, 90-91 prohibit intoxicants, gambling, idols, drawing lots as evil of Satan's handiwork, to be shunned for success, and explain Satan's plan to stir hostility and hatred with intoxicants and gambling and prevent remembrance and prayer. The connection: lawful good eating and mindfulness versus unlawful evil that prevents mindfulness. Intoxicants and gambling prevent remembrance of Allah and prayer, as verse 91 says, opposite of mindfulness commanded in 88. The verses therefore continue theme of lawful versus unlawful, showing that intoxicants, gambling, idols, divination are not ṭayyibāt, good things, but rijs, evil, to be shunned. The next verse will detail Satan's plan.

**Why This Prohibition Connects to Earlier Extremism**

This verse connects to earlier warning against extremism, ghuluww, in verse 77, where People of Book warned not to go to extremes beyond truth and not follow desires of those astray before. Verse 87 warns believers not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can appear among believers too, forbidding lawful as excess, or violating unlawful as negligence, both transgression, i'tidā', exceeding bounds. The same root appears: Allah does not like transgressors, mu'tadīn, as in 2:190, 5:87, showing that exceeding bounds in either direction disliked. The verse therefore maintains middle path, wasaṭ, balanced, as Islam is.

The extra verified historical note that Prophet forbade continuous fasting, continuous prayer without sleep, celibacy, as reports about three men who wanted to worship excessively, Prophet said I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women, whoever turns away from my Sunnah not from me, as Bukhārī and Muslim record, shows that verse 87's prohibition of forbidding ṭayyibāt was applied to forbid monasticism. The moderation, wasaṭiyyah, is central to Islam, as 2:143 says We made you middle nation, and Sūrah al-Mā'idah emphasizes ṭayyibāt made lawful, not to be forbidden, and khabā'ith forbidden, not to be made lawful, maintaining balance.

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:91

> Satan’s plan is to stir up hostility and hatred between you with intoxicants and gambling and to prevent you from remembering Allah and praying. Will you not then abstain

**Expanded Commentary**

**Satan's Plan With Intoxicants and Gambling**

Innamā yurīdu al-shayṭānu an yūqi'a baynakum al-'adāwata wa al-baghḍā'a fī al-khamri wa al-maysiri wa yaṣuddakum 'an dhikri Allāhi wa 'ani al-ṣalāti fa-hal antum muntahūn explains Satan's plan: indeed Satan wants to cause between you hostility and hatred with intoxicants and gambling and prevent you from remembering Allah and prayer, will you not then abstain? Innamā yurīdu al-shayṭānu, indeed Satan wants, from r-w-d, to want, al-shayṭānu, Satan, from sh-ṭ-n, to be far, distant, an yūqi'a, to cause to fall, from w-q-', baynakum, between you, al-'adāwata, hostility, enmity, from '-d-w, wa al-baghḍā'a, and hatred, from b-gh-ḍ, fī al-khamri wa al-maysiri, with intoxicants and gambling, with, by means of, wa yaṣuddakum, and prevent you, from ṣ-d-d, to block, 'an dhikri Allāhi, from remembering Allah, from dh-k-r, wa 'ani al-ṣalāti, and from prayer, from ṣ-l-w, fa-hal antum muntahūn — will you not then abstain? — hal, interrogative for urging, antum, you, muntahūn, abstainers, from n-h-y, to forbid, stop.

**Hostility and Hatred Caused by Intoxicants and Gambling**

Al-'adāwah wa al-baghḍā', hostility and hatred, are two consequences: 'adāwah, outward enmity, hostility, baghḍā', inward hatred, both caused by khamr and maysir, intoxicants and gambling. Intoxicants cause hostility because intoxicated person loses intellect, insults, fights, kills, as Jāhiliyyah and contemporary reports show. Gambling causes hostility because loser hates winner, winner may cheat, leading to disputes, hatred, even murder, as history shows. The verse says fī al-khamri wa al-maysiri, with intoxicants and gambling, indicating they are means by which Satan causes hostility and hatred between believers, breaking brotherhood. The same effect was noted earlier for previous nations: Satan beautifies evil to cause division.

**Preventing From Remembering Allah and Prayer**

Wa yaṣuddakum 'an dhikri Allāhi wa 'ani al-ṣalāti — and prevent you from remembering Allah and from prayer — yaṣuddakum, prevent, block you, 'an dhikri Allāhi, from remembering Allah, dhikr includes Qur'an, supplication, remembrance, 'an al-ṣalāh, from prayer, ṣalāh, formal prayer, indicating two levels: general remembrance and specific prayer. Intoxicants prevent remembrance because they cover intellect, person forgets Allah, neglects prayer, as earlier verse 4:43 said do not approach prayer while intoxicated until you know what you say. Gambling prevents remembrance because gambler engrossed in game, neglects prayer times, remembrance, as gamblers spend hours. Satan's plan is to prevent from dhikr and ṣalāh, which are purpose of creation and success, as verse 90 said shun for success.

**Will You Not Then Abstain as Urging Question**

Fa-hal antum muntahūn — will you not then abstain? — is interrogative for urging, expecting yes, we abstain. Hal antum muntahūn, are you abstainers, from intahā, to stop, abstain. The question is rhetorical, urging immediate abstinence, as if saying after knowing Satan's plan to cause hostility, hatred, prevent remembrance and prayer, will you not abstain? The same phrasing appears as final seal after explanation, indicating that after knowing harms, abstinence is rational. Reports say when verse revealed, companions said *“We have abstained, our Lord, we have abstained”* and poured out wine, broke vessels, showing immediate compliance.

This verse explains reason, ḥikmah, for prohibition of intoxicants and gambling: they cause hostility and hatred between people and prevent from remembering Allah and prayer. The reason shows that prohibition is not arbitrary but for benefit: preserving brotherhood, intellect, remembrance, prayer. The verse therefore provides rationale that helps believers understand and abstain willingly, not merely obeying command but understanding wisdom. The same rationale appears for other prohibitions: preserving religion, intellect, lineage, wealth, life, as maqāṣid al-sharī'ah.

**Extra Verified Note on Gradual Prohibition**

Gradual prohibition of khamr: first 2:219 they ask about khamr and maysir, say in both great sin and some benefit but sin greater, then 4:43 do not approach prayer while intoxicated, then 5:90-91 final prohibition to shun as evil of Satan's handiwork. The extra verified historical note that final prohibition revealed after incident where companions fought while intoxicated, as Asbāb reports, shows that hostility and hatred actually occurred, confirming verse's reason. The incident where 'Uthmān's nose broken while intoxicated, or Sa'd's dispute, reported in tafsīr, illustrates cause. The verse's closing will you not abstain led to immediate pouring out of wine in Madinah streets.

**Connection to Mindfulness and Success**

Verse 90 said shun intoxicants, gambling, idols, drawing lots as evil of Satan's handiwork so you may be successful, verse 91 explains Satan's plan to stir hostility and hatred with intoxicants and gambling and prevent from remembering Allah and prayer, will you not abstain? Verse 88 said be mindful of Allah in Whom you believe, verse 91 shows intoxicants and gambling prevent mindfulness, dhikr, and prayer, opposite of taqwā. The connection shows that success, falāḥ, requires mindfulness, dhikr, ṣalāh, which intoxicants and gambling prevent, so shunning them is necessary for success. The next verse will command obeying Allah and Messenger and beware, if turn away, Messenger's duty only to deliver clearly.

**Why This Prohibition Connects to Earlier Extremism**

This verse connects to earlier warning against extremism, ghuluww, in verse 77, where People of Book warned not to go to extremes beyond truth and not follow desires of those astray before. Verse 87 warns believers not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can appear among believers too, forbidding lawful as excess, or violating unlawful as negligence, both transgression, i'tidā', exceeding bounds. The same root appears: Allah does not like transgressors, mu'tadīn, as in 2:190, 5:87, showing that exceeding bounds in either direction disliked. The verse therefore maintains middle path, wasaṭ, balanced, as Islam is.

The extra verified historical note that Prophet forbade continuous fasting, continuous prayer without sleep, celibacy, as reports about three men who wanted to worship excessively, Prophet said I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women, whoever turns away from my Sunnah not from me, as Bukhārī and Muslim record, shows that verse 87's prohibition of forbidding ṭayyibāt was applied to forbid monasticism. The moderation, wasaṭiyyah, is central to Islam, as 2:143 says We made you middle nation, and Sūrah al-Mā'idah emphasizes ṭayyibāt made lawful, not to be forbidden, and khabā'ith forbidden, not to be made lawful, maintaining balance.

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:92

> Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and beware! But if you turn away, then know that Our Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly

**Expanded Commentary**

**Command to Obey Allah and Messenger and Beware**

Wa aṭī'ū Allāha wa aṭī'ū al-rasūla wa iḥdharū fa-in tawallaytum fa-i'lamū annamā 'alā rasūlinā al-balāghu al-mubīn commands: and obey Allah and obey Messenger and beware, but if you turn away, then know that upon Our Messenger is only clear delivery. Aṭī'ū Allāha, obey Allah, from ṭ-w-', to obey, wa aṭī'ū al-rasūla, and obey Messenger, rasūl from r-s-l, to send, wa iḥdharū, and beware, be cautious, from ḥ-dh-r, to beware, guard. Fa-in tawallaytum, but if you turn away, from w-l-y, to turn, fa-i'lamū, then know, from '-l-m, annamā 'alā rasūlinā al-balāghu al-mubīn — that upon Our Messenger is only clear delivery — annamā, only, 'alā rasūlinā, upon Our Messenger, al-balāghu, delivery, conveying, from b-l-gh, al-mubīn, clear, from b-y-n.

**Obey Allah and Obey Messenger as Two Obediences**

Aṭī'ū Allāha wa aṭī'ū al-rasūla, obey Allah and obey Messenger, appears many times in Qur'an, showing two obediences that are one in practice, because obeying Messenger is obeying Allah, as 4:80 says whoever obeys Messenger has obeyed Allah. Obey Allah by following Qur'an, obey Messenger by following Sunnah, both required, as earlier verse 5:92 shows after prohibiting intoxicants, gambling, etc., obedience required to implement prohibitions. Wa iḥdharū, and beware, indicates caution against disobeying, or against returning to intoxicants, gambling, etc., after prohibition, or beware of Satan's plan. The same triad appears in other verses: obey Allah, obey Messenger, beware.

**If Turn Away Then Messenger's Duty Only Clear Delivery**

Fa-in tawallaytum fa-i'lamū annamā 'alā rasūlinā al-balāghu al-mubīn — but if you turn away, then know that upon Our Messenger is only clear delivery — tawallaytum, you turn away, from tawallā, to turn away, fa-i'lamū, then know, annamā 'alā rasūlinā, only upon Our Messenger, al-balāghu al-mubīn, clear delivery, conveying clearly. The phrase indicates Messenger's duty is to deliver message clearly, not to compel, not to guide hearts, which is Allah's, and if people turn away after clear delivery, consequence is theirs, not Messenger's fault, as earlier verse 5:67 said O Messenger deliver what was sent down, and Allah will protect you. The same phrase appears in 5:99 Messenger's duty only to deliver, and Allah knows what you reveal and conceal.

**Beware as Warning After Prohibition**

Wa iḥdharū, and beware, after command to obey Allah and Messenger, serves as warning after prohibition of intoxicants, gambling, idols, drawing lots, and explanation of Satan's plan. Beware of returning to them, or beware of disobeying, or beware of Satan, as Satan wants to cause hostility and prevent remembrance. The beware is general: beware of Allah's punishment, beware of violating prohibitions, beware of turning away. The verse therefore after detailing harms and commanding shunning, commands obedience and beware, and says if turn away, Messenger's duty only clear delivery, so no excuse after clear delivery.

**A Verse That Defines Messenger's Role**

This verse defines Messenger's role: al-balāghu al-mubīn, clear delivery, conveying message clearly, not coercion, not controlling hearts, as earlier verses about People of Book showed messengers came with clear proofs yet many transgressed, and responsibility is on rejecters. The same definition appears in 5:99, 16:35, 24:54, 29:18, 36:17, etc., showing consistent definition. The verse therefore clarifies that after clear delivery, if people turn away, they bear consequence, Messenger has fulfilled duty. The next verse will say no blame on those who believe and do good for what they consumed before prohibition, as long as they fear, believe, do good, then fear and believe, then fear and do good, Allah loves good-doers.

**Extra Verified Note on Balāgh Mubīn**

Balāgh mubīn, clear delivery, from balagha, to reach, convey, mubīn from abāna, to clarify, indicates Messenger conveys in clear Arabic, with explanation, evidence, not ambiguous, so that proof established. The extra verified note that balāgh mubīn includes conveying Qur'an and explaining it via Sunnah, as 16:44 says We sent down Reminder so you may explain to people what was sent down, shows that Sunnah is part of clear delivery. The verse's closing that Messenger's duty only clear delivery shows that guidance of hearts is Allah's, not Messenger's, as 28:56 you do not guide whom you love but Allah guides.

**Connection to Prohibition and Obedience**

Verses 90-91 prohibited intoxicants, gambling, idols, drawing lots as evil of Satan's handiwork, to be shunned for success, explained Satan's plan to cause hostility and prevent remembrance and prayer, asked will you not abstain? Verse 92 commands obey Allah and obey Messenger and beware, if turn away, know Messenger's duty only clear delivery, showing that after clear prohibition, obedience required, beware of returning, and if turn away, no excuse, Messenger delivered clearly. The connection shows that prohibition is part of Allah and Messenger's command, requiring obedience. The next verse will address those who consumed intoxicants before prohibition, no blame if they feared, believed, did good, showing mercy for past after repentance and obedience.

**Why This Prohibition Connects to Earlier Extremism**

This verse connects to earlier warning against extremism, ghuluww, in verse 77, where People of Book warned not to go to extremes beyond truth and not follow desires of those astray before. Verse 87 warns believers not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can appear among believers too, forbidding lawful as excess, or violating unlawful as negligence, both transgression, i'tidā', exceeding bounds. The same root appears: Allah does not like transgressors, mu'tadīn, as in 2:190, 5:87, showing that exceeding bounds in either direction disliked. The verse therefore maintains middle path, wasaṭ, balanced, as Islam is.

The extra verified historical note that Prophet forbade continuous fasting, continuous prayer without sleep, celibacy, as reports about three men who wanted to worship excessively, Prophet said I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women, whoever turns away from my Sunnah not from me, as Bukhārī and Muslim record, shows that verse 87's prohibition of forbidding ṭayyibāt was applied to forbid monasticism. The moderation, wasaṭiyyah, is central to Islam, as 2:143 says We made you middle nation, and Sūrah al-Mā'idah emphasizes ṭayyibāt made lawful, not to be forbidden, and khabā'ith forbidden, not to be made lawful, maintaining balance.

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:93

> There is no blame on those who believe and do good for what they had consumed before ˹the prohibition˺, as long as they fear Allah, have faith, and do what is good; then they believe and act virtuously, then become fully mindful ˹of Allah˺ and do righteous deeds. For Allah loves the good-doers

**Expanded Commentary**

**No Blame for What Consumed Before Prohibition**

Laysa 'alā alladhīna āmanū wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāti junāḥun fīmā ṭa'imū idhā mā ittaqaw wa āmanū wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāti thumma ittaqaw wa āmanū thumma ittaqaw wa aḥsanū wa Allāhu yuḥibbu al-muḥsinīn states: no blame on those who believe and do good for what they consumed before prohibition, as long as they fear Allah, believe, do good, then fear and believe, then fear and do good, Allah loves good-doers. Laysa 'alā, not upon, no, alladhīna āmanū, those who believe, wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāti, and do good deeds, ṣāliḥāt from ṣ-l-ḥ, junāḥun, blame, sin, from j-n-ḥ, fīmā ṭa'imū, in what they tasted, consumed, from ṭ-'-m, idhā mā ittaqaw, when they fear, from w-q-y, wa āmanū, and believe, wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāti, and do good deeds, thumma ittaqaw wa āmanū, then fear and believe, thumma ittaqaw wa aḥsanū, then fear and do good with excellence, wa Allāhu yuḥibbu al-muḥsinīn — Allah loves good-doers — muḥsinīn from ḥ-s-n.

**Three Stages of Taqwā, Īmān, and Iḥsān**

Idhā mā ittaqaw wa āmanū wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāti thumma ittaqaw wa āmanū thumma ittaqaw wa aḥsanū describes three stages: first fear Allah, believe, do good deeds, second then fear and believe, third then fear and do good with excellence, iḥsān. The repetition with thumma, then, indicates progression: initial taqwā, īmān, ṣāliḥāt, then deeper taqwā and īmān, then taqwā and iḥsān, excellence. The progression shows spiritual ascent: fear leads to belief and good deeds, then continued fear and belief, then fear and excellence, doing good as if seeing Allah, as ḥadīth Jibrīl defines iḥsān. The verse therefore describes levels of righteousness, not mere repetition, but growth.

**Why No Blame for Past Consumption**

Laysa 'alā alladhīna āmanū wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāti junāḥun fīmā ṭa'imū — no blame on those who believe and do good for what they consumed — fīmā ṭa'imū, in what they tasted, includes intoxicants, foods, etc., consumed before prohibition, as Asbāb reports companions worried about those who died before prohibition of khamr, had consumed it, would they be blamed? Verse revealed to say no blame if they believed, did good, feared, etc. The same principle appears for other prohibitions: what was done before prohibition with belief and good deeds is forgiven, no retroactive blame, as Islam erases past after repentance and obedience. The verse therefore shows mercy for past after prohibition revealed.

**Allah Loves Good-Doers as Closing**

Wa Allāhu yuḥibbu al-muḥsinīn — Allah loves good-doers — muḥsinīn, good-doers, those who do good with excellence, from aḥsana, to do good, with iḥsān. The closing appears many times for similar contexts, as in *“Allah loves good-doers”* (2:195, 3:134, 5:93). The muḥsinīn are those who combine taqwā, īmān, ṣāliḥāt, iḥsān, as verse describes. The verse therefore ends with love for muḥsinīn, encouraging excellence, not mere minimum. The next verse will discuss test with game within reach of hands and spears while on pilgrimage, to distinguish those who fear Him in secret, transgressor suffers painful punishment.

**A Verse That Shows No Retroactive Punishment**

This verse shows no retroactive punishment for what was lawful before prohibition, if person believed, feared, did good, as principle: laws apply after revelation, not before, and past forgiven. The same principle appears for ribā, usury, after prohibition, no blame for past if repented. The verse therefore addresses companions' worry about deceased who consumed khamr before final prohibition, saying no blame if they were believers, did good, feared, etc. The extra verified historical note that 'Uthmān ibn Maẓ'ūn and others died before final prohibition, companions asked about them, verse revealed, shows context. The verse's three-stage description shows that those deceased had taqwā, īmān, ṣāliḥāt, so no blame.

Iḥsān, doing good with excellence, defined in ḥadīth Jibrīl: to worship Allah as if you see Him, if not seeing Him, He sees you. The verse's progression from ṣāliḥāt to iḥsān shows ascent from good deeds to excellence. The extra verified note that muḥsinīn are highest level after mu'min, believer, as Qur'an describes levels: islām, īmān, iḥsān, shows that verse encourages reaching iḥsān, not just īmān. Allah loves muḥsinīn indicates that excellence is beloved, not just minimum obedience. The following verse will discuss test with game while on pilgrimage.

**Connection to Obedience and Prohibition**

Verse 92 commanded obey Allah and Messenger and beware, if turn away, Messenger's duty only clear delivery. Verse 93 says no blame on those who believe and do good for what they consumed before prohibition, as long as they fear, believe, do good, then fear and believe, then fear and do good, Allah loves good-doers, showing that after obedience command, past consumption before prohibition forgiven if they had taqwā, īmān, ṣāliḥāt, iḥsān. The connection shows mercy alongside command: obedience required going forward, past forgiven if had belief and good deeds. The next verse will discuss test with game while on pilgrimage, continuing theme of lawful and unlawful hunting, as earlier verses about game and hunting.

**Why This Prohibition Connects to Earlier Extremism**

This verse connects to earlier warning against extremism, ghuluww, in verse 77, where People of Book warned not to go to extremes beyond truth and not follow desires of those astray before. Verse 87 warns believers not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can appear among believers too, forbidding lawful as excess, or violating unlawful as negligence, both transgression, i'tidā', exceeding bounds. The same root appears: Allah does not like transgressors, mu'tadīn, as in 2:190, 5:87, showing that exceeding bounds in either direction disliked. The verse therefore maintains middle path, wasaṭ, balanced, as Islam is.

The extra verified historical note that Prophet forbade continuous fasting, continuous prayer without sleep, celibacy, as reports about three men who wanted to worship excessively, Prophet said I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women, whoever turns away from my Sunnah not from me, as Bukhārī and Muslim record, shows that verse 87's prohibition of forbidding ṭayyibāt was applied to forbid monasticism. The moderation, wasaṭiyyah, is central to Islam, as 2:143 says We made you middle nation, and Sūrah al-Mā'idah emphasizes ṭayyibāt made lawful, not to be forbidden, and khabā'ith forbidden, not to be made lawful, maintaining balance.

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:94

> O believers! Allah will surely test you with game within the reach of your hands and spears to distinguish those who fear Him in secret. Whoever transgresses from now on will suffer a painful punishment

**Expanded Commentary**

**Address to Believers About Test With Game**

Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū la-yabluwannakum Allāhu bi-shay'in mina al-ṣaydi tanāluhu aydīkum wa rimāḥukum li-ya'lama Allāhu man yakhāfuhu bi-al-ghaybi fa-mani i'tadā ba'da dhālika fa-lahu 'adhābun alīm addresses believers: O you who believe, Allah will surely test you with something of game that hands and spears reach, to know who fears Him in secret, whoever transgresses after that will have painful punishment. La-yabluwannakum Allāhu, surely Allah will test you, from b-l-w, to test, bi-shay'in mina al-ṣaydi, with something of game, ṣayd from ṣ-y-d, to hunt, tanāluhu aydīkum, hands reach it, from n-y-l, aydīkum, your hands, wa rimāḥukum, and your spears, rimāḥ from r-m-ḥ, spear, li-ya'lama Allāhu, so Allah may know, from '-l-m, man yakhāfuhu bi-al-ghaybi, who fears Him in secret, unseen, ghayb from gh-y-b, fa-mani i'tadā ba'da dhālika, whoever transgresses after that, i'tadā from '-d-w, fa-lahu 'adhābun alīm — for him painful punishment — 'adhāb alīm, painful.

**Game Within Reach of Hands and Spears**

Bi-shay'in mina al-ṣaydi tanāluhu aydīkum wa rimāḥukum — with game that hands and spears reach — indicates game that is easy to catch, within hand's reach, like small game, birds, or eggs, or spears reach, larger game, indicating test with easy opportunity to violate prohibition of hunting while on pilgrimage. The test is that game comes near, easy to hunt, while pilgrim is forbidden to hunt, as next verse says do not kill game while on pilgrimage. The ease makes test harder, distinguishing those who fear Allah in secret, unseen, when no one sees but Allah, whether they will hunt or abstain. The same test occurred at Ḥudaybiyyah, as reports say game came near companions while in iḥrām, easy to catch, and verse revealed to test.

**To Distinguish Those Who Fear Him in Secret**

Li-ya'lama Allāhu man yakhāfuhu bi-al-ghaybi — to know who fears Him in secret — li-ya'lama, so Allah may know, man yakhāfuhu, who fears Him, bi-al-ghaybi, in unseen, secret, when not seen by people, only Allah sees. The phrase indicates test reveals who has true taqwā, fear of Allah even when unseen, not just when seen by people. The same phrase appears for similar tests, as in *“Who fears Him in secret”* (5:94, 67:12). The test with game within reach while in iḥrām distinguishes those who fear Allah in secret, abstaining from easy game, versus those who transgress. The knowledge is not for Allah who already knows, but to manifest, make known, who fears.

**Whoever Transgresses After That Painful Punishment**

Fa-mani i'tadā ba'da dhālika fa-lahu 'adhābun alīm — whoever transgresses after that, for him painful punishment — mani i'tadā, whoever transgresses, exceeds bounds, from '-d-w, ba'da dhālika, after that, after clear prohibition and test, fa-lahu 'adhābun alīm, for him painful punishment, 'adhāb alīm, painful, from '-dh-b, a-l-m. The punishment is for transgressing after knowledge, after test, after prohibition, indicating severity for intentional violation after clarity. The same warning appears for other violations, as in *“Painful punishment”* (5:94) itself. The verse therefore warns that after test and prohibition, transgression leads to painful punishment.

**A Verse That Shows Test in Pilgrimage**

This verse shows test in pilgrimage: Allah tests pilgrims with game within reach of hands and spears, easy to hunt, while hunting forbidden in iḥrām, to distinguish those who fear Him in secret. The test is similar to test of Sabbath for Jews with fish, as Qur'an mentions, or test with water for Ṭālūt's army, showing that tests with easy forbidden opportunity reveal true fear. The extra verified historical note that at Ḥudaybiyyah in 6 AH, companions in iḥrām, game came near, easy to catch, and verse revealed to forbid, shows context. The verse's placement after discussion of lawful foods and hunting (5:4-5) returns to hunting prohibition while on pilgrimage, which was mentioned in 5:1-2.

**Extra Verified Note on Ṣayd While Muḥrim**

Ṣayd, game, hunting, while muḥrim, pilgrim in iḥrām, is forbidden, as 5:1 said do not permit hunting while on pilgrimage, and 5:95-96 detail penalty. The extra verified juristic note that ṣayd includes land game, not sea game, as verse 96 says lawful for you to hunt and eat seafood, but hunting on land forbidden while on pilgrimage, shows distinction: sea game lawful even in iḥrām, land game forbidden. The test with game within reach of hands and spears indicates land game, as sea game not reached by hands/spears in same way. The verse therefore introduces penalty section for hunting while muḥrim.

**Connection to Lawful and Unlawful Hunting**

Verses 1-2 forbade hunting while on pilgrimage, 4 permitted game caught by trained animals, 94-96 discuss test with game, prohibition of killing game while on pilgrimage, penalty, lawful seafood. The connection shows sūrah's theme of ḥalāl and ḥarām, especially hunting, as earlier verses about ṭayyibāt and game. The test with easy game while in iḥrām shows that lawful hunting becomes unlawful in iḥrām, testing obedience. The next verse will say O believers, do not kill game while on pilgrimage, whoever kills intentionally must compensate by equivalence judged by two just men, or feeding needy, or fasting, to taste consequences, Allah forgave past, but persisters punished, Allah Almighty capable of punishment.

**Why This Prohibition Connects to Earlier Extremism**

This verse connects to earlier warning against extremism, ghuluww, in verse 77, where People of Book warned not to go to extremes beyond truth and not follow desires of those astray before. Verse 87 warns believers not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can appear among believers too, forbidding lawful as excess, or violating unlawful as negligence, both transgression, i'tidā', exceeding bounds. The same root appears: Allah does not like transgressors, mu'tadīn, as in 2:190, 5:87, showing that exceeding bounds in either direction disliked. The verse therefore maintains middle path, wasaṭ, balanced, as Islam is.

**Extra Verified Historical Note on Moderation**

The extra verified historical note that Prophet forbade continuous fasting, continuous prayer without sleep, celibacy, as reports about three men who wanted to worship excessively, Prophet said I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women, whoever turns away from my Sunnah not from me, as Bukhārī and Muslim record, shows that verse 87's prohibition of forbidding ṭayyibāt was applied to forbid monasticism. The moderation, wasaṭiyyah, is central to Islam, as 2:143 says We made you middle nation, and Sūrah al-Mā'idah emphasizes ṭayyibāt made lawful, not to be forbidden, and khabā'ith forbidden, not to be made lawful, maintaining balance.

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:95

> O believers! Do not kill game while on pilgrimage. Whoever kills game intentionally must compensate by offering its equivalence—as judged by two just men among you—to be offered at the Sacred House, or by feeding the needy, or by fasting so that they may taste the consequences of their violations. Allah has forgiven what has been done. But those who persist will be punished by Allah. And Allah is Almighty, capable of punishment

**Expanded Commentary**

**Prohibition of Killing Game While on Pilgrimage**

Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū lā taqtulū al-ṣayda wa antum ḥurumun wa man qatalahu minkum muta'ammidan fa-jazā'un mithlu mā qatala mina al-na'ami yaḥkumu bihi dhawā 'adlin minkum hadyan bāligha al-Ka'bati aw kaffāratun ṭa'āmu masākīna aw 'adlu dhālika ṣiyāman li-yadhūqa wabāla amrihi 'afā Allāhu 'ammā salafa wa man 'āda fa-yantaqimu Allāhu minhu wa Allāhu 'Azīzun Dhū Intiqām prohibits killing game while muḥrim. Lā taqtulū al-ṣayda, do not kill game, from q-t-l, ṣayd, game, wa antum ḥurum, while you are in pilgrimage, ḥurum from ḥ-r-m, muḥrim, in iḥrām, sacred state. The prohibition is for land game, as verse 96 says sea game lawful. Wa man qatalahu minkum muta'ammidan — whoever kills it among you intentionally — man qatala, whoever kills, minkum, among you, muta'ammidan, intentionally, from '-m-d, intention, indicates deliberate, not forgetful or mistaken, though jurists include mistaken with penalty.

**Compensation by Equivalence Judged by Two Just Men**

Fa-jazā'un mithlu mā qatala mina al-na'ami yaḥkumu bihi dhawā 'adlin minkum hadyan bāligha al-Ka'bati — then compensation equivalent to what he killed of livestock, judged by two just men among you, as offering reaching Ka'bah — jazā', compensation, from j-z-y, mithlu, equivalent, like, mā qatala, what he killed, mina al-na'am, of livestock, an'ām, camels, cattle, sheep, yaḥkumu bihi, judged by it, dhawā 'adlin, two possessors of justice, just men, minkum, among you, hadyan, offering, sacrifice, from h-d-y, bāligha al-Ka'bah, reaching Ka'bah, indicating sacrifice at Sacred House. The equivalence judged by two just men shows need for justice in assessment, not arbitrary, and offering at Ka'bah shows sanctity.

**Or Feeding Needy Or Fasting to Taste Consequences**

Aw kaffāratun ṭa'āmu masākīna aw 'adlu dhālika ṣiyāman li-yadhūqa wabāla amrihi — or expiation feeding needy, or equivalent fasting, to taste consequences of his deed — aw kaffāratun, or expiation, ṭa'āmu masākīna, food of poor, masākīn, poor, aw 'adlu dhālika ṣiyāman, or equivalent of that fasting, 'adl, equivalent, ṣiyām, fasting, li-yadhūqa wabāla amrihi, so he may taste evil consequences of his matter, wabāl from w-b-l, heavy, evil consequence. The three options: equivalent livestock sacrifice at Ka'bah, or feeding needy, or fasting equivalent, show gradation, allowing choice based on ability, similar to oath expiation in 5:89.

**Allah Forgave Past But Persisters Punished**

'Afā Allāhu 'ammā salafa wa man 'āda fa-yantaqimu Allāhu minhu wa Allāhu 'Azīzun Dhū Intiqām — Allah forgave what passed, but whoever returns, Allah will punish him, Allah is Almighty, Possessor of vengeance — 'afā Allāhu 'ammā salafa, Allah forgave what past, salafa from s-l-f, past, before prohibition, wa man 'āda, whoever returns, repeats, from '-w-d, fa-yantaqimu Allāhu minhu, Allah will take vengeance from him, yantaqimu from n-q-m, vengeance, wa Allāhu 'Azīzun Dhū Intiqām — Allah is Almighty, Possessor of vengeance — 'Azīz from '-z-z, mighty, Dhū Intiqām, possessor of retribution. The forgiveness for past shows no retroactive punishment, as verse 93 also said no blame for what consumed before prohibition if had taqwā etc.

**A Verse That Details Ḥajj Violation Penalty**

This verse details penalty for hunting violation while muḥrim, as part of ḥajj rulings, showing that iḥrām imposes additional prohibitions beyond normal, testing obedience. The penalty of equivalent livestock judged by two just men at Ka'bah shows sanctity of Ka'bah and need for justice. The alternative feeding or fasting shows mercy for those unable to sacrifice. The warning that Allah forgave past but will punish persisters shows that after clear prohibition, repetition leads to punishment, and Allah is 'Azīz, Mighty, Dhū Intiqām, Capable of retribution, indicating severity.

Na'am, livestock, includes camels, cattle, sheep, goats, as earlier verse 5:1 bahīmat al-an'ām made lawful. Equivalence, mithl, judged by two just men, means if killed gazelle, equivalent sheep, if ostrich, camel, etc., as jurists detailed based on companions' judgments: 'Umar, 'Uthmān, 'Alī, etc., judged equivalences. The extra verified juristic note that feeding needy amount is estimated by value of equivalent livestock divided among poor, each poor half ṣā', and fasting one day per poor, shows calculation. The offering reaching Ka'bah means slaughtered in Ḥaram, distributed to poor of Ḥaram.

**Connection to Test With Game**

Verse 94 said Allah will test you with game within reach of hands and spears to distinguish who fears Him in secret, transgressor painful punishment. Verse 95 says O believers, do not kill game while on pilgrimage, intentional killer must compensate equivalent judged by two just men as offering at Sacred House, or feeding needy, or fasting to taste consequences, Allah forgave past, but persisters punished, Allah Almighty capable of punishment. The connection: test described, then prohibition and penalty detailed, showing that test is real and violation has compensation and punishment. The next verse will say lawful for you to hunt and eat seafood as provision for you and travellers, but hunting on land forbidden while on pilgrimage, be mindful of Allah to Whom gathered.

**Why This Prohibition Connects to Earlier Extremism**

This verse connects to earlier warning against extremism, ghuluww, in verse 77, where People of Book warned not to go to extremes beyond truth and not follow desires of those astray before. Verse 87 warns believers not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can appear among believers too, forbidding lawful as excess, or violating unlawful as negligence, both transgression, i'tidā', exceeding bounds. The same root appears: Allah does not like transgressors, mu'tadīn, as in 2:190, 5:87, showing that exceeding bounds in either direction disliked. The verse therefore maintains middle path, wasaṭ, balanced, as Islam is.

**Extra Verified Historical Note on Moderation**

The extra verified historical note that Prophet forbade continuous fasting, continuous prayer without sleep, celibacy, as reports about three men who wanted to worship excessively, Prophet said I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women, whoever turns away from my Sunnah not from me, as Bukhārī and Muslim record, shows that verse 87's prohibition of forbidding ṭayyibāt was applied to forbid monasticism. The moderation, wasaṭiyyah, is central to Islam, as 2:143 says We made you middle nation, and Sūrah al-Mā'idah emphasizes ṭayyibāt made lawful, not to be forbidden, and khabā'ith forbidden, not to be made lawful, maintaining balance.
